## 📚 Paper List

- **[Reset Dependence Is Embodied: Training Protocol Changes Which Morphologies Appear Optimal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29518)**  `arXiv:2611.29518`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Kevin Qiu, Zhao Yang, Marek Cygan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot co-design benchmarks rank bodies after training their controllers under a fixed episode protocol, but that protocol can change the ranking itself. Consequently, two studies can select different bodies from the same corpus even when they use the same task and evaluator. We study one part of that protocol, the reset rule, which ends a training episode when the robot's torso falls below a height threshold. We train proximal policy optimization (PPO) controllers for 100 DERL-evolved UNIMAL bodies with this threshold at 20% of each body's starting torso height (Standard), at 50% (Strict), or with no threshold (Reset-free), and score every controller with the same evaluator. The threshold changes which bodies look best: the rankings after Strict and Reset-free training correlate at only 0.21, against about 0.89 if seed noise were the only difference. Removing fall resets costs some bodies far more than others. Bodies evolved on flat ground lose 45% of their per-step reward relative to Standard training, and those evolved on variable terrain only 16%. Permutation tests over bodies support this difference, and on 24 bodies, flat-ground bodies gain the most from Strict over Reset-free training under both PPO and soft actor-critic (SAC). Choosing bodies by their worst reward across the three protocols costs little: in sample, they keep at least 91% of the reward of each protocol's best bodies, and over 80% averaged across held-out seeds. Co-design benchmarks should state their reset rule, test whether their rankings depend on it, and report bodies that stay strong across protocols. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Test-Time Training Corrects in Latent World Models? A Slow-Fast Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29453)**  `arXiv:2611.29453`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Shuaicheng Niu, Guohao Chen, Kevin Z. Shen, Yunbei Zhang, Peilin Zhao, Yinan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models plan toward a goal by predicting the outcomes of candidate actions, but under the distribution shift, prediction errors can lead the planner astray. Test-time training using executed actions helps, yet what it corrects and whether its updates should persist across episodes remains unclear. We address both by analyzing the world model's prediction error. On PushObj, an object-pushing task, we probe multiple actions from the same state and decompose the error into an offset shared across actions and an action-dependent component. The offset effectively shifts the planner's goal, and AdaJEPA mainly tracks it: the common shift alone recovers 91% of its planning gain. Part of the offset is episode-specific, so carrying its correction into the next episode does not help and may even hurt. The remaining offset recurs across episodes and action-dependent error can be or needs to be learned and retained from many episodes. Our sft-JEPA therefore uses two learners on different timescales: a fast learner that tracks the offset and resets each episode, and a slow LoRA learner that accumulates recurring knowledge across episodes. Across PushObj, PointMaze, zero-shot planning on RoboCasa (with DINO-WM), and OGBench (with LeWAM), sft-JEPA leads all benchmark averages, improving planning success over AdaJEPA by up to 22 points.
  </details>

- **[SWEEP-Bench: Do Embodied Policies Act on the Speed They See?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30637)**  `arXiv:2611.30637`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Hiroaki Honda, Koya Sakamoto, Shu Morikuni, Yusuke Iwasawa, Taiki Miyanishi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot policies must adapt to the speed of moving objects, yet existing benchmarks rarely measure this ability across diverse tasks and speeds. We introduce SWEEP-Bench, a simulation benchmark with 80 conveyor-belt manipulation tasks spanning 16 skill categories, each evaluated at 13 belt speeds (0.10-0.40 m/s). Objects are transported through physical contact with a visibly moving belt. Each task has a static counterpart, and each evaluation episode uses the same randomized scene at all 13 speeds. We fine-tune eight vision-language-action and world-action models on demonstrations spanning the full speed range, comparing observations from the current frame alone with those including two past frames. For six models, the success rate peaks at intermediate speeds, and their failures indicate that they tend to produce actions suited to the average demonstration speed rather than to the actual one. Past frames raise the success rate by 10-40 percentage points for three models, and linear probes show that the belt speed is also most linearly decodable from these three. Adding ground-truth speed input to four of the models improves all of them, and a speed estimated from the images keeps most of this gain. These results show that SWEEP-Bench exposes speed-dependent failures and suggest that past frames help only policies from which the speed is most decodable, such as those pretrained on multiple frames. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[HIVE-Bench: Evaluating Patch-Level Visual Representations for Egocentric Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30813)**  `arXiv:2611.30813`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Qiwei Liang, Zhengtu Liang, Minghao Lai, Yikeng Chen, Yiming Wang, Boyang Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained vision is a common start for robot learning, but most comparisons still score one pooled vector from a third-person image. Bimanual policies instead read dense patch tokens from cameras on the robot. We introduce HIVE-Bench: one shared flow-matching policy reads 20 encoders from seven families on 24 egocentric bimanual tasks, across adaptation, task sharing, and sweeps of data, model size, and layer. Keeping the patches matters. Averaging each view to one token removes most of the success, adaptation helps every pair we test, and one shared policy keeps most of the success of separate policies at a fraction of the updates. The same tokens are then scored with statistics, state and object readouts, published vision scores, and an inverse-dynamics readout. That readout of the commanded motion is one of the strongest measurements available before a policy is trained. Recognition, depth, and correspondence do not transfer reliably. Fine-tuning can improve the action readout even when other measurements move the other way, and no statistic is stable enough to replace a rollout before any deployment.
  </details>

- **[Action Windows Are Inherently Temporal: A Benchmark for Time-Sensitive Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29558)**  `arXiv:2611.29558`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoxiang Zhan, Haoqin Hong, Congcong Zhu, Jingrun Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language model (VLM) agents have surpassed learned-policy baselines on existing robotic manipulation benchmarks. However, these agents still struggle with time-sensitive everyday tasks that existing benchmarks for VLM agents cover only partially. In these tasks, evolving physical processes create finite windows for successful intervention. These action windows are inherently temporal and impose a shared time budget on decision making and physical execution. Coordinating intervention within this budget requires agents to determine when the window opens. We introduce AWAIT, a simulation benchmark that evaluates task completion within action windows while accounting for decision and execution time. Our evaluation reveals difficulties in recognizing window onset and shows that successive decisions and movements can exhaust an action window even when each decision takes less than the window duration. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[RSLaA: Self-Reconfiguring Roadside LiDARs as Agents for 3D Traffic Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29294)**  `arXiv:2611.29294`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yang Ma, Yichen Sun, Yubing Zheng, Zhongxiang Feng, Zeyang Cheng, Said M. Easa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative perception with roadside LiDARs (RSLs) extends the sensing range of autonomous vehicles and resolves onboard-sensor occlusions, particularly at curves and intersections. Because infrastructure deployment is costly, existing work optimizes RSL configurations once, against road geometry and aggregated traffic statistics, and treats them as fixed thereafter. Traffic flow, however, is inherently dynamic, and a placement optimal on average can be far from optimal at any given moment. We introduce RSLaA (Roadside LiDARs as Agents), a framework in which each RSL adaptively adjusts its pose to the evolving traffic scene over decision-time windows. Since ground truth is unavailable at decision time, the framework centers on surrogate metrics computed solely from a probabilistic occupancy grid of historical observations, which predicts the next-window perception performance of candidate poses. We construct a co-simulation benchmark pairing five candidate surrogates with detector-agnostic ground-truth perception labels at every decision window, identifying the multi-view coverage metric J_mv as the most reliable predictor. Upon this validated surrogate we build two adaptive configuration methods, a receding-horizon (MPC) optimizer and a lightweight MAPPO-based multi-agent reinforcement learning scheme with centralized training and decentralized execution. Across 10 sites and 89 six-hour traffic scenarios (890 evaluation runs), adaptive configuration consistently improves the composite perception score over static deployment. Our results indicate that RSLs could be benefitted from being treated as agents.
  </details>

- **[DriveTTO: Understanding Search and Selection in Test-Time Trajectory Optimization for End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29205)**  `arXiv:2611.29205`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Xinyao Rao, Zecheng Tang, Zheyuan Lai, Liuxuan Sun, Minjun Yu, Xun Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In difficult driving scenes, a learned scorer can select a poor trajectory from a fixed pool even when a better candidate is available. Nearby vehicles and road boundaries can also leave only a narrow range of high-scoring maneuvers. These constraints vary with the scene and are difficult to cover exhaustively during training. We introduce DriveTTO, a training-free method that searches around native proposals and checks each proposed refinement against a retained trajectory. A frozen scorer evaluates completed paths at the endpoints they actually reach, guiding resampling in progressively smaller neighborhoods. From a shared reference, one branch draws on planner proposals and another perturbs vehicle controls. DriveTTO blends their poses at matching times and accepts a branch or fusion update only when both frozen scorers predict a gain. On Navtest, DriveTTO recovers nonzero predictive driver model scores (PDMS) in 51.35% of native zero-score cases on average. Accounting for new failures, the zero-score rate falls from 1.63% to 1.13%. Benchmark-specific configurations achieve 95.54 PDMS and 92.30 extended PDMS (EPDMS) on Navtest, 57.10 EPDMS on Navhard, and 40.1 HD-Score on HUGSIM under observed commands. With a single frozen scorer, DriveTTO-S improves all six evaluated planners on both Navtest and Navhard.
  </details>

- **[Tripwire: Robot Agents that Program Their Own Interrupts under Human Disturbance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27182)**  `arXiv:2611.27182`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sunli Chen, Yun Zong, Qinhong Zhou, Xiaowen Qiu, Jiaben Chen, Zeyuan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses are turn-based: the agent acts, waits for the result, then reasons. When the action invokes a pretrained robot policy, such as a vision-language-action (VLA) or world action model (WAM), the policy may react continuously to observations, but the agent cannot revisit its decision until the call returns. Meanwhile, a person may step into the robot's path or move the object it is about to grasp. Invoking the agent more often narrows this blind interval, but a reasoning model takes seconds per decision and most control steps need no new one. We propose TRIP, a training-free, event-driven harness in which the agent programs its own interrupts. With each call, the agent writes a tripwire: a lightweight, stateful Python program that runs alongside the policy, can query a compact VLM (for example, to locate a person and measure their distance), and decides whether the call continues or control returns to the agent. When it fires, the harness halts the policy and returns control with the reason; the agent then reassesses, adapts, and continues under a rewritten tripwire. We evaluate TRIP with three VLAs and one WAM on RoboHD, a benchmark of 168 RoboCasa365 scenes in which a simulated person disturbs the robot and any human-robot contact counts as failure. Compared with a turn-based harness using the same agent and tools, TRIP halves the rate of human contact with the strongest policy, raising safety from 61.3% to 81.3%, without sacrificing task success (38.7% vs. 31.0%). Real-robot experiments show that TRIP completes two manipulation tasks under human interference where the turn-based harness and the policy alone largely fail.
  </details>

- **[CoReMatch: Recurrent Continuous Coordinate Refinement for Real-Time Robotic Feature Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03932)**  `arXiv:2611.03932`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Robert J. Wang, Charles Ling, Boyu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feature matching underpins many robotic perception tasks, including visual localization, mapping, SLAM, and autonomous navigation. Existing semi-dense matchers predict confidence on fixed grids, limiting correspondences to predefined locations and requiring costly high-resolution grids for precise localization. We present CoReMatch (Coordinate Refinement Match), a recurrent framework that refines correspondences directly in continuous coordinate space rather than predicting discrete confidence maps. CoReMatch progressively improves correspondence estimates by propagating information across iterations. At the coarse stage, a Deep Aggregated Transformer captures global context and iteratively updates correspondence coordinates, while a lightweight regression head further sharpens their localization. Experiments on indoor and outdoor benchmarks show that CoReMatch strikes a strong balance between accuracy and efficiency, outperforming Efficient LoFTR in accuracy while running more than 10x faster on embedded edge hardware. These results demonstrate that recurrent coordinate refinement offers an efficient and scalable approach to real-time feature matching for robotic perception.
  </details>

- **[Self-Supervised Online Robot-Agnostic Traversability Estimation for Open-World Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28442)**  `arXiv:2605.28442`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Julia Hindel, Simon Bultmann, Houman Masnavi, Daniele Cattaneo, Abhinav Valada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised online traversability estimation enables robots to continuously learn from unlabeled open-world experiences and adapt their navigation behavior toward safe and efficient trajectories. Existing approaches either rely on handcrafted proprioceptive traversability scores, limiting robot-agnosticism, or cluster prior data, preventing online learning. Moreover, many continual learning methods incur substantial memory and computational costs, hindering onboard deployment. We introduce COTRATE, an online learning framework for continuous traversability estimation from multimodal, unlabeled robot experience. Our method first infers robust traversability scores using a robot-agnostic, learning-based online terrain assessment module operating on proprioceptiveand inertial signals. These scores then supervise a visual traversability network through a novel alignment loss that associates visual embeddings with online terrain assessments. To mitigate forgetting during continual learning with minimal overhead, we propose a diversity-aware feature selection strategythat preserves performance using a compact replay memory. We further show that the learned traversability representation supports knowledge transfer across different robot platforms with different locomotion kinematics. We evaluate COTRATE on a dataset of $\approx$ 50,000 images collected with two robotic platforms across 11 outdoor terrains, and benchmark it on navigation tasks in three representative outdoor environments. We make the dataset, code, and trained models publicly available.
  </details>

- **[RoboUniverse-10K: Toward the Scaling Era of Agentic Robotics via Real-to-Sim Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31002)**  `arXiv:2611.31002`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Jinkun Liu, Yingze Wang, Situ Wang, Deyao Hong, Mingju Gao, Wenyi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic Robotics, in which foundation models act as coding agents to control robots through executable programs, has emerged as a compelling paradigm for embodied intelligence. Yet unlike software engineering, where massive executable environments power post-training, robotics remains constrained by small, manual benchmarks and in-distribution evaluation. To bridge this gap, we introduce RoboUniverse-10K, 10,807 interactive, resettable, and verifiable physics environments built from real-world videos via an automated Real2Sim and quality assurance pipeline. Each runs in an isolated, Harbor-compatible harness that hides privileged states and grading logic to mirror real-world sensing. From the same pipeline, we hold out 500 unseen tasks to establish RoboUniverse-Verified, an expert-verified zero-shot benchmark. Evaluating 11 closed- and open-source models reveals a huge gap: frontier models reach 85.8\% success, while open-source baselines lag at 2.6\% to 10.0\%, bottlenecked by low-level grasping, deformable-object manipulation, and post-contact physical control. Finally, we collect about 17K multi-turn trajectories totaling 0.79B tokens with GPT-6 Astra and perform full-parameter supervised fine-tuning on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, yielding RoboUniverse-35B-A3B. Our model achieves substantial zero-shot gains on RoboUniverse-Verified, lifting the success rate from 0.8\% to 21.2\%, while achieving zero-shot task completions across LIBERO-MAX, RoboCasa, RoboTwin 2.0, and a real Franka arm. We open-source the RL-ready environments and model to lay the foundation for large-scale reinforcement learning in Agentic Robotics.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Continuous-Time MAPF and Industrial AGV Fleet Coordination with Lazy Priority-Based Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30874)**  `arXiv:2611.30874`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Federico Signorile, Raffaele Carli, Alessandro Bertogli, Roberto Olmi, Sven Koenig, Mariagrazia Dotoli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time multi-agent path finding on roadmaps (MAPF$_R$) offers a principled way to coordinate robot fleets with geometric collision avoidance, yet its deployment on industrial automated guided vehicle (AGV) infrastructures remains challenging, as highly connected and redundant roadmaps amplify both safe-interval reasoning and multi-agent interaction handling. To bridge this gap, we formalize AGV-MAPF$_R$-CTC, a configuration-space variant of MAPF$_R$ with precomputed continuous-time conflicts that enables the reuse of MAPF$_R$ solvers for AGV fleet coordination with minimal changes. We then revisit continuous-time priority-based search (CPBS), identifying the boundary conditions required by its safe interval path planning (SIPP) low-level planner under priority-induced constraints and enabling earlier solution detection. As a central contribution, we analyze the structure of the priority search space, clarifying which agents are affected by each priority refinement and when replanning can be avoided. Building on this analysis, we introduce a novel algorithm, Lazy-CPBS, which materializes high-level nodes lazily: a child's plans are computed only when it is expanded, so siblings that are never expanded cost no low-level search. Experiments on standard MAPF benchmarks and on industrial AGV roadmaps imported from a proprietary digital-twin simulator show that CPBS-based solvers remain effective well beyond the agent counts reachable by optimal MAPF$_R$ solvers, while Lazy-CPBS substantially reduces CPBS overhead and offers the most consistent robustness/runtime trade-off across industrial layouts.
  </details>

- **[SayDo: Benchmarking the Safety of Frontier Models from Chat to Closed-Loop Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30957)**  `arXiv:2611.30957`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Benjamin Alt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as high-level robot controllers, yet their safety is evaluated almost entirely in text. We introduce SayDo, a benchmark that assesses the propensity of frontier models to cause harm in embodied contexts. SayDo comprises eleven laboratory and household tasks that pair a benign instruction with a hazard latent in the scene. Tasks are implemented in six settings along an embodiment gradient, from chat questions about an imagined robot to closed-loop control of a simulated robot arm. Benign controls, safe-alternative variants and scripted operator pushback complement each task. Across six open- and closed-weight VLMs and 11,480 episodes, hazardous-action rates rise from 14% in chat to 47% in the simulator, and from 2% to 80% for the most affected model. The increase is largely driven by open-weight models. We find that models flag hazards before acting in 73% of unsafe episodes and give way to pushback about twice as often when controlling robots as in chat. The safest models also show signs of overcaution on benign controls. Safety evaluations run in text therefore do not predict the safety behavior of the same models when used as robot controllers.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Is Not Choosing: What a World Model Needs to Keep a VLA Policy Safe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29553)**  `arXiv:2611.29553`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Thanish Bolla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies complete most benchmark tasks but often violate safety constraints. Since they are stochastic, one remedy is to sample several candidate action chunks and execute one that a world model predicts is safe. We ask what such a model must do. In simulation, a lookahead safety filter with the simulator as an exact world model raises the safety rate of three VLA policies on LIBERO and of pi0.5 on LIBERO-Plus by 11.5 to 25.7 points. It also helps pi0.5 on three RoboCasa tasks. Learned critics trained on one policy's data detect dangerous states (pooled AUROC up to 0.864) but rank candidates at one state close to chance (within-decision AUROC 0.551-0.560), and the learned dynamics and video models we test do no better. When the simulator's state is perturbed, selection degrades long before detection. In the loop, a median position error of 3.18 mm removes 70% of the gain on LIBERO-Spatial, partly because the error also corrupts the displacement check. On LIBERO-10 the loss is smaller, and the intervals overlap. The median does not predict the cost: a pixel reader with a 0.56 mm median error keeps 6.5 points less of the gain than Gaussian error of a similar median. A world model for safety should thus be judged by within-state ranking, and its state must be accurate in the tail and on handled objects, not only in the median.
  </details>

- **[NAT: Scaling Causal Neural Action Tokenizers Across Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28873)**  `arXiv:2611.28873`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ahad Jawaid, Alexander Swerdlow, Yu Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action tokenizers let robot policies treat control as autoregressive next-token prediction. As these policies are trained on larger and more diverse datasets, they will need a universal tokenizer that can encode actions from many embodiments and data sources, including ones it has not seen. We study action tokenization at scale, training on 49k hours of action data from real-world robots, handheld grippers, simulation, and humans. We introduce Neural Action Tokenizer (NAT), a causal tokenizer whose decoder lets actions be executed as their tokens are generated. Across four simulation benchmarks, NAT outperforms FAST and prior neural action tokenizers when tokenizing unseen robot embodiments, and in vision-language-action models it outperforms FAST+ across four benchmarks and on a real robot when acting through its tokens. NAT tokens also close the performance gap to a flow-matching action head on some benchmarks while lowering the inference latency for first actions. Our experiments also find that robot data may not be necessary to learn an effective action space: a NAT trained only on human hand motion performs close to tokenizers trained on robot data.
  </details>

- **[Fast World Planner: Advantage-Aware Action-Chunk Policy for Online Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28616)**  `arXiv:2611.28616`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiangtao Kong, Yuchen Wang, Yandong Luo, Rongchao Xu, Guang Wang, Yanbing Mao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control is central to online planning for model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL), but its iterative refinement incurs planning latency. Reducing refinement iterations often degrades performance, motivating stronger policy guidance learned from the planner. However, existing methods typically learn from the planner through action-level alignment, which does not directly capture the complete $H$-step planner proposal. Moreover, planner imitation leaves further target improvement to online refinement. To address these limitations, we propose FaSt World PlANner (SWAN), an advantage-aware MPPI-based MBRL framework that explicitly represents and improves the complete $H$-step planner proposal to improve policy guidance for efficient online planning. For proposal representation, SWAN combines k-MPPI, a multimodal MPPI extension, with a mixture of factor analyzers (MFA) action-chunk policy to capture multiple modes and correlations across time and action dimensions. For proposal optimization, we introduce Advantage-Guided Proposal Optimization (APO), which reuses MPPI evaluations to construct an improved target using advantage estimates and trains the policy toward this target while preserving planner alignment. Experiments across 31 high-dimensional continuous-control tasks from DMControl, HumanoidBench, and MyoSuite show that SWAN substantially improves control performance and sample efficiency while using 50% fewer MPPI refinement iterations than state-of-the-art planning-based MBRL methods. Finally, SWAN demonstrates zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a physical Unitree Go2, achieving stable velocity-tracking locomotion with high-frequency control. (see our demo atthis https URL)
  </details>

- **[Embodied-Harness: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27689)**  `arXiv:2611.27689`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhenxuan Fan, Zhuoya Wang, Yuqian Yuan, Wenqiao Zhang, Juncheng Li, Jun Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language model (VLM)-based agents offer a promising approach to robotic manipulation, yet remain limited by inadequate memory organization, uncertain failure attribution, and limited reuse of execution experience. We propose Embodied-Harness, a dual-agent framework that improves manipulation by refining the execution harness without updating model parameters. The Actor interacts with the environment through a robot-control interface, while the Reflector converts execution evidence into reusable operational guidance. Hierarchical Memory Architecture maintains short-term execution context and long-term experiential knowledge to support execution and reflection. Evidence-based Experience Reflection grounds failure diagnosis in observed interactions, while Recursive Harness Self-Improvement refines operational guidance across successive attempts. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0, RoboSPA, and RMBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Embodied-Harness in generalization under environmental variations, spatial reasoning, long-horizon planning, and memory-dependent manipulation. Our method achieves success rates of 82.4%, 76.7%, and 94.4% on the three benchmarks, respectively. Real-world evaluation further yields an average success rate of 96.7% across three manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of Embodied-Harness to improve robotic manipulation through experience-driven harness self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[LeadVLA: Learning Follower-Aware Route Execution for Robot Leading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30149)**  `arXiv:2611.30149`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Tianle Zeng, Hanjing Ye, Bingyi Xia, Yuchen Zhou, Zibo Zhang, Yufei Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot leading requires executing a route while continuously adapting to a designated follower. Existing robot-leading systems often mediate follower understanding and physical route execution through explicit states, planners, or modular interfaces; directly learning this coupling through end-to-end trajectory prediction can instead entangle route execution with follower response. We introduce LeadVLA, a structured multimodal policy that preserves the route as an explicit Path reference while learning a Pace for route progress and a Residual for local follower- and scene-conditioned adaptation. To make this problem trainable and measurable, we develop LeadInfra, a configurable Unreal Engine-based closed-loop data-generation infrastructure from which we instantiate an approximately 4M-sample training corpus, and LeadBench, a standardized benchmark with 108 fully held-out closed-loop episodes. LeadVLA achieves the highest task success (39.6%) among evaluated robot policies on LeadBench while maintaining strong route fidelity and follower responsiveness, and transfers to unseen real-world environments without real-world policy fine-tuning. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[World Models for Ocean Engineering: A Critical Review of Maritime Autonomy, Adaptive Latent Prediction, Digital Twins, and Biodiversity Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28978)**  `arXiv:2611.28978`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Basma Dokkara, Esteban W. G. Cluab, Luiz M. G. Goncalves, Paolo Spagnolo, Cosimo Distante_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Marine robots face partial observability, non-stationary dynamics, and sparse communication, motivating WMs that learn compact predictive states for planning. This review develops a functional taxonomy of WMs for ocean engineering, focusing on Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) and AdaJEPA's test-time adaptation within model predictive control, mapping them to navigation, adaptive sampling, digital twins, and biodiversity monitoring, and separating demonstrated evidence from prospective applications. The synthesis identifies key Gaps: benchmarks, uncertainty calibration, safe adaptation and proposes a research agenda for physics-informed, deployment-adaptive marine WMs validated through simulation and field missions.
  </details>

- **[RoboInF: A Benchmark for Instruction Fidelity in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30555)**  `arXiv:2611.30555`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Guo, Yu Huang, Dijiang Dong, Yue Liu, Peiran Ren, Wei Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise language control requires embodied models to follow instructions governing both task outcomes and execution. Although recent methods train policies to follow increasingly detailed commands, existing benchmarks often assess instruction following alongside perception, planning, and generalization to unfamiliar scenes. Higher task success therefore offers limited evidence of closer adherence to instructions. We introduce RoboInF, a benchmark measuring how reliably manipulation policies adapt their behavior to changes in an instruction. For each of eight tasks, six to twelve expert-validated instruction branches vary the requested goal, procedure, or manipulation detail while holding the initial scene and robot state fixed. Task-specific checkers verify the terminal goal and use recorded trajectories, contacts, and event sequences to assess compliance throughout execution. The accompanying RoboInF-4K dataset pairs 4,242 expert demonstrations with simulator-derived annotations of motion and contact. We evaluate leading manipulation policies ($\pi_{0.5}$ and OpenWAM) and inference-only agents (GPT-6 Astra and Qwen3.8-Flash). All four models exhibit a gap between task completion and faithful execution, indicating substantial room for fine-grained instruction following.
  </details>

- **[RoboLiveMem: Is Robot Memory Solved When the World Does Not Wait?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30047)**  `arXiv:2611.30047`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunbei Zhang, Yuyou Zhang, Janet Wang, Ming Liu, Yijiang Li, Yikai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot agents built on frontier vision-language models now solve most robot memory benchmarks. Does this mean they can remember what they need in a home, where people and machines keep moving things? Existing memory benchmarks cannot answer this question, because they pause the simulator while the model thinks: nothing can change during a decision, so whatever the robot remembers is still true when it acts. We introduce RoboLiveMem, a benchmark that runs the same memory tasks twice, once with the world paused while the model thinks and once with the world running. It contains 29 task families in three tiers, from 16 realistic BEHAVIOR-1K scenes of homes, workshops and a restaurant to controlled ManiSkill tasks. Every task mixes real changes with harmless look-alikes and runs without any change, so that no fixed strategy succeeds, and most tasks have a twin that keeps the change in view, which separates forgetting from acting too late. Testing 16 models on 34,176 episodes, we find three surprises. (1) Paused results hide the problem: when a remembered object changes in a realistic scene, the 8 Claude and GPT models succeed in 97% of episodes on average with the world paused, but in only 50% with the world running, and their ranking changes: Claude Opus 5.5, which never fails when paused, succeeds in 44%. (2) Speed is not the fix: slower models do no worse, and models given only 2 s per decision still fall at least 36 points below their paused success. (3) Models see the change but act on camera frames from before it ended. Looking again just before acting closes most of the realistic gap, yet when identical objects are swapped several times, all nine models we test still lose points to memory.
  </details>

- **[Spiking World-Action Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30041)**  `arXiv:2611.30041`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingquan Liu, Peng Zhou, Shanming Yao, Shuai Wang, Simo Wu, Malu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) predict how the world responds to actions and offer a promising path toward general embodied intelligence, but they are large and run the network many times per plan, so they are hard to deploy on robots. Spiking neural networks (SNNs) compute with sparse binary spikes and use additions instead of multiplications, which saves power. However, existing WAMs use dense videos as the world state, so a spiking version would spend most of its computation on the background. We therefore introduce SpikingWAM, the first spiking WAM, which uses a sparse task skeleton as the world state. Inspired by biological motor control, which attends only to the key positions of a task, the Task Skeleton State (TSS) keeps the keypoints of the arm and the task objects, and a small spiking front end estimates the object keypoints from images. Body Frame Spike Coding (BFSC) and Grid Coincidence Attention (GCA) keep millimetre precision, and the Persistent Single Step Readout (PSR) runs the network once per plan. On the simulated benchmarks LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin 2.0, SpikingWAM achieves competitive success rates with far less energy than ANN methods and surpasses all compared methods on LIBERO-Plus. Real robot experiments further confirm its effectiveness. These results show that a task skeleton lets spiking networks implement WAMs at very low energy, offering a new solution for low power embodied intelligence. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[How Should a Home Robot Update Its Memories When Human Routines Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29818)**  `arXiv:2611.29818`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Oliver Wang, Mani Srivastava_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A home robot that helps with chores needs to know where objects are, but people move objects according to routines that change and later change back. The robot's memory must adapt to the new routine without erasing experience that may become useful again. One approach is to have a language model maintain written summaries of the home from the robot's own observations, but existing evaluations of such memories use static worlds. We explore simulated households where a resident gets sick, recovers, and later gets sick again. We compare a rule that keeps every room an object has been seen in, language-model memories that store only written summaries, and language-model memories that can also read the raw history of sightings. The rule outperforms every summary-only memory, and giving language models the sighting history closes the gap. Surprisingly, writing summaries on top of the history adds nothing we can measure. When the illness returns, methods that keep the history search better than they did the first time, while summary-only methods show no consistent improvement. Deleting the first illness from memory removes all of the rule's gain and part of theirs. The summaries recorded where objects went during the illness but rarely distinguished sick-day patterns from normal routines. Our findings suggest that common approaches to summarizing robot memories struggle to remember facts about a routine that might return, highlighting the need for future benchmarks to evaluate this aspect of long-term robot deployment.
  </details>

- **[TASC: Time-to-Collision Anticipation and Selective Crowd Attention for Non-Holonomic Service Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29637)**  `arXiv:2611.29637`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammadhossein Malmir, Mohammadali Rahmati, Mohammad Reza Kolani, Alois Knoll_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Service robots that share corridors and lobbies with people must keep moving without endangering them. Reactive planners can stop indefinitely in dense crowds (the freezing robot problem), whereas the attention-based learned navigators that dominate the standard crowd benchmark assume holonomic motion or emit discrete actions unsuited to a differential-drive base. This paper presents TASC, a navigation system for a non-holonomic service robot that integrates an attention-based crowd encoder, an anticipatory collision feature, and a continuous-control policy. We adapt the dual-selection attention module, originally developed for discrete-action value learning, to proximal policy optimization for continuous velocity control, and add a closing-speed time-to-collision feature, a conservative estimate of the time to contact that serves as both an observation and a reward term. The policy is trained on a high-fidelity 3D robot model in NVIDIA Isaac Lab with 10,000 parallel environments, social-force-model pedestrians, and randomized crowd sizes. In 30,000 evaluation episodes, each run to its end, the policy timed out in fewer than 0.5% at every tested crowd density. This liveness comes at a cost: pedestrian collisions rise from 4.5% with five pedestrians to 17.4% with ten. In an ablation with four seeds per variant, policies retrained without the time-to-collision feature succeed 4 to 7 percentage points less often. The policy runs in Gazebo without retraining and on a commercial differential-drive service robot through a ROS 2 package with an inference latency below 10 ms, verified in a hardware-in-the-loop test with virtual pedestrians.
  </details>

- **[JEPA-Shield: Safe Diffusion Planning with Latent World Models via Conformal Shielding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29609)**  `arXiv:2611.29609`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Taekyung Kim, Ege Yuceel, Steven Hong, Georgios Fainekos, Dimitra Panagou, Bardh Hoxha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models enable planning from images, but their predictions do not directly establish whether a robot will satisfy physical constraints. We present JEPA-Shield, which connects a frozen action-conditioned Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world model to task-space safety enforcement. Two downstream models predict relative motion from imagined latent rollouts and decode unsafe geometry from the current image. Split conformal prediction turns end-to-end motion-prediction errors into multi-horizon uncertainty tubes. A backup-based shield checks these tubes against physical constraints and screens both candidate trajectories and final proposals in diffusion-inspired sampling-based model predictive control. Under the stated exchangeability and initial-backup assumptions, we establish probabilistic constraint satisfaction at action-block boundaries for a returned plan executed without replanning. We also introduce VParkBench, with geometric car and tractor-trailer parking tasks and a editable photorealistic Gaussian-splatting environment. In closed-loop evaluation, JEPA-Shield achieves success rates of 84.4%, 82.3%, and 99.0%, respectively, with no observed constraint violations. Ablations show complementary benefits from shielding and uncertainty calibration. The results demonstrate the importance of grounding latent world-model predictions in task-space quantities and explicitly accounting for prediction uncertainty in safety-critical planning.
  </details>

- **[MAWAM: Joint Imagination for Collaborative Robot Manipulation with a Multi-Agent World Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29501)**  `arXiv:2611.29501`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Rongze Tang, Chenyu Zhou, Jianjie Fang, Ziming Liu, Zhaolu Wang, Ziyou Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) excel at robot manipulation by imagining future observations and decoding actions from them, yet remain confined to a single embodied agent. In practice, manipulation often requires multiple robots to collaborate across disjoint workspaces, coupled timing, and heterogeneous embodiments. Such coordination demands both spatiotemporal alignment of future interactions and awareness of individual embodiment and role identities, whereas existing decentralized per-robot policies, flat centralized models, and multi-robot VLAs fail to unite both. To bridge this gap, we present MAWAM, a multi-agent world action model that jointly predicts the future observations of multiple embodiments and decodes each agent's actions from them for collaborative manipulation. Through an agent-axis curriculum, MAWAM expands a single-agent WAM along an agent dimension to coordinate teams under shared weights. Within one unified causal sequence, joint self-attention spans all agents to spatiotemporally align their imagined futures, while hierarchical language, identity encodings, and embodiment masks bind each stream to its role, identity, and embodiment. We further formalize multi-agent-necessary manipulation along three axes (why, when, and who) and develop MA-Task Harness, a coding-agent pipeline where mutually constraining generators and evaluators co-evolve customizable simulation-data and evaluation packages. Using this pipeline, we build MA-Bench across all axes with seven tasks, pairing joint success rate with role and collaboration scores for fine-grained analysis. On MA-Bench, MAWAM reaches 82.7% mean joint success, 30.7% above the strongest baseline, and achieves real-time closed-loop collaboration on a real heterogeneous robot team.
  </details>

- **[Fast-JEPA: Advancing the Success-Latency Frontier for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25927)**  `arXiv:2611.25927`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Zhang, Bin Yu, Shijie Lian, Haishan Liu, Changti Wu, Yu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models predict the outcomes of action sequences, but comparing candidate plans through recursive rollout remains expensive at test time. We introduce Fast-JEPA, a latent world model planning framework that advances the success-latency Pareto frontier of predictive search. Fast-JEPA reorganizes predictive computation to support effective action-sequence search at low planning cost. It constructs a goal-directed plan to guide search and directly predicts the outcomes of complete candidates for efficient comparison, avoiding recursive state rollout during refinement. Across PushT, Reacher, Cube, and TwoRoom, Fast-JEPA achieves 96.50% average success rate with 2.41x faster planning than INTACT. Search-budget sweeps show that its competitive success and low latency extend across candidate counts and refinement depths. We further introduce DynaGoal, a benchmark for evaluating world-model planning under dynamic out-of-distribution conditions, execution deadlines, and controlled response delays. Fast-JEPA achieves average success rates of 73.83% on DynaGoal-Shift and 87.67% on DynaGoal-Deadline, demonstrating the practical value of low-latency predictive planning under changing control conditions and execution-time constraints. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TipDex-VTLA: Learning to Refine Actions with Visual Reliability and Tactile Feedback for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25683)**  `arXiv:2611.25683`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xudi Ge, Yepeng Liu, Haifeng Liu, Enze Shi, Liangqi Zhang, Yongchao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Changes in illumination can induce action drift in dexterous manipulation even when the physical state remains unchanged. Although existing vision--tactile--language--action (VTLA) policies incorporate tactile feedback, when and how touch refines actions under changing visual conditions remains challenging. We introduce \textbf{TipDex-VTLA}, a VTLA policy that combines estimated visual action reliability and tactile forecast verification to regulate execution-time refinement. Specifically, Visual Action Sensitivity Estimation (VASE) learns visual reliability from a frozen reference policy's action sensitivity to illumination perturbations. Tactile Anticipation and Correction during Execution (TACE) compares predicted and measured touch at matched times and combines the resulting discrepancy with visual reliability to gate bounded feature corrections for unexecuted actions. This effectively helps to refine actions under challenging unseen visual conditions. We evaluate TipDex-VTLA on four real-world dexterous manipulation tasks and the UniVTAC simulation benchmark. TipDex-VTLA trained on data collected under normal visual conditions, achieves average full-task success rates of 87.5% (resp. 73.8%) under normal (resp. challenging unseen) lighting in real-world tasks, significantly outperforming some state-of-the-art methods. We will release the collected data and source code.
  </details>

- **[RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850)**  `arXiv:2611.03850`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Deng-Ke Wu, Yifan Jin, Yida Yang, Lei Qian, Zhaoye Zhou, Hongru Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating embodied-AI policies in closed loop across simulation benchmarks such as LIBERO and RoboTwin requires repeated integration, as each benchmark defines its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention. Evaluation can also be slow: with a StarVLA checkpoint, the 5,000 episodes of RoboTwin Easy take 17.5 hours on four RTX PRO 5000 GPUs. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluators identifies two bottlenecks: alternating between one simulator and inference leaves the GPU at only 14.3% average utilization on LIBERO-Long, while rendering at every control step on RoboTwin means an action-chunk policy reads only one of every 16 rendered observations. We present RoboScope Kit, an evaluation toolkit that integrates each model and benchmark once through a unified interface, keeping each benchmark's native evaluator and scoring, and accelerates evaluation through two mechanisms. (1) Scheduling: simulator workers and policy replicas scale independently, with autotuning sizing both pools to each workload and host. (2) Runtime optimization: the simulation runtime skips unconsumed simulation and rendering work without changing the policy's inputs or outcomes. On a single RTX 4090, RoboScope Kit runs LIBERO-Long 6.2$\times$ faster and 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes 13.2$\times$ faster than StarVLA, and 2.1-7.3$\times$ faster than RLinf and vla-eval. Scaling to four RTX PRO 5000 GPUs, it evaluates all 5,000 RoboTwin Easy episodes in 66 minutes (15.8$\times$ faster). LIBERO-Long success rates stay within 0.3 percentage points of StarVLA, and every RoboTwin episode matches its exact outcome and terminal step.
  </details>

- **[PAC-ACT: Post-training Actor-Critic for Action Chunking Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09590)**  `arXiv:2607.09590`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Pang, Zudong Li, Leran Chen, Yongsheng Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precision industrial contact manipulation requires reliable robot policies under pose perturbations and contact-force constraints. Vision-language-action models offer broad generalization but often introduce high inference latency and GPU-memory cost, while vision-action chunking policies are more suitable for real-time industrial control. However, these policies are usually trained by behavior cloning and suffer from distribution shift in contact-rich tasks. This paper proposes PAC-ACT, a reinforcement-learning post-training framework for pretrained Action Chunking Transformer policies. PAC-ACT reformulates policy optimization at the chunk level, constructs an ACT-transferred actor-critic architecture, and introduces a hybrid behavior-prior constraint to preserve the pretrained action distribution during online fine-tuning. Experiments on industrial precision-contact benchmarks show that PAC-ACT improves task success, contact stability, and force safety while retaining low latency and low GPU-memory usage. On the Contour task, PAC-ACT significantly reduces peak contact force and decreases the proportion of force readings above 60 N by 46 times. Sparse-reward ablations further show that the proposed behavior-prior constraint enables effective exploration under randomized initial poses.
  </details>

- **[EmboEagle: An Efficient Agentic Embodied Framework for Long-horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31173)**  `arXiv:2611.31173`  `cs.RO`  
  _Fan Xu, Zhongtao Wang, Hao Wu, Yitong Zhu, Wenzhuo Ma, Long Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robotic manipulation demands reliable execution across interdependent stages, yet standalone World-Action Models (WAMs) suffer from compounding physical errors, while stateless Vision-Language Model (VLM) supervision incurs prohibitive chunkwise latency and lacks 3D spatial persistence. We present EmboEagle, a closed-loop agentic framework that unifies subtask-conditioned WAM control, latent-space adaptive supervision, and memory-guided geometric recovery. First, we adapt sequential WAMs via decoupled two-stage flow matching that preserves cross-stage visual history while calibrating truncated action supervision at subtask boundaries. Second, without an auxiliary vision encoder, we distill teacher intervention decisions into a lightweight routing gate driven solely by the WAM Action Expert's pre- and post-chunk latent transitions and subtask embeddings. Third, upon invocation, the VLM decouples diagnostic status from discrete routing actions and executes parameterized recovery primitives grounded in structured memory (initial references, active grasp bindings, and tool traces), followed by mandatory post-repair verification. Across RMBench and LIBERO-Mem, EmboEagle achieves 91.3\% and 90.6\% average success, surpassing the strongest baselines by 8.0 and 9.8 percentage points, while boosting real-world success over VoLoAgent under identical executor weights. Moreover, adaptive gating cuts VLM calls by 74.2\% versus chunkwise monitoring (91.3\% vs. 93.7\% success).
  </details>

- **[Generalizing Agentic Robotics via Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30997)**  `arXiv:2611.30997`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinxin Liu, Anh Dao, Wenrui Bao, Rafid Ahmed, Bingxin Xu, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building general-purpose robots is a central goal in robotics. Agentic robotics pursues this goal with planners that control robots through tool calls. However, existing methods either rely on proprietary models accessed through APIs, which incur high costs and latency, or post-train a planner with reinforcement learning (RL) in a single domain, requiring a separate specialist for each domain with incompatible tools. In large language model (LLM) post-training, multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) has been used to consolidate domain specialists into a single generalist. Yet whether MOPD can build a generalist planner for agentic robotics, how well it performs, and how it compares with other consolidation methods remain unexplored. In this paper, we systematically study these questions: we introduce Robo-MOPD, which trains a single student on closed-loop rollouts in simulation under token-level supervision from matching frozen specialists, and compare it with weight merging, off-policy specialist supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and multi-domain online RL. Experiments on LIBERO-PRO and RoboCasa365 show that a single 9B Robo-MOPD planner reaches 72.9% and 44.8% success (58.9% average), surpassing all consolidated baselines in average success and both 9B specialists on their respective benchmarks. Robo-MOPD excels on long-horizon tasks, outperforming multi-domain online RL by 13.0 percentage points on LIBERO-10.
  </details>

- **[WorldLeague: Benchmarking Physical Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30989)**  `arXiv:2611.30989`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinyang Zhou, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems can now operate Blender, follow instructions in complex video games, and manipulate objects with robot arms. What range of physical goals can today's AI systems achieve autonomously? Answering this question requires testing systems on tasks that demand different combinations of perception, memory, planning, and action. We introduce \textbf{WorldLeague}, a benchmark that compares AI systems on a common set of \textbf{60 simulated robot tasks} by measuring progress toward each task's physical goal from the robot's trajectory. Robots explore scenes to gather evidence in \textbf{WorldSeek}, rearrange objects in \textbf{WorldShape}, and adapt to changes in \textbf{WorldShift}. Tying these goals to a single control interface limits which AI systems can be compared. In WorldLeague, vision-language models (VLMs) choose targets and motions, while vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs) use their native controls. For each of six capability dimensions, we measure how much progress a system makes toward its goals (Interactive Score) and how often it achieves them (physical success rate). Together, these measures form the system's \emph{Physical Agency Profile}. Among 13 representative AI systems, Claude Fable 5.2 achieves 13 of the 60 goals and earns the highest mean Interactive Score (28.58/100). Fable leads in four dimensions, while GPT-6.1-Sol and GPT-6-Astra tie for first on rule-following tasks. Even when VLMs answer every final question correctly, they receive an Interactive Score of zero in 71.8\% of those episodes. WorldLeague gives Embodied AI, Physical AI, and Agentic AI a shared basis for comparing what AI systems achieve autonomously, from partial progress toward a physical goal to full goal completion. Project Webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Place-Revisit Consistency Evaluation of Reference Poses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30741)**  `arXiv:2611.30741`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jianzhu Huai, Yujia Zhang, Grzegorz Jozkow_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference trajectories are widely used to evaluate odometry and simultaneous localization and mapping systems. However, local inconsistencies in benchmark reference poses can bias such evaluations. This paper presents a place-revisit consistency evaluation method for reference poses in mobile sensing datasets. When a platform revisits a previously observed place after a sufficient temporal gap, the relative pose implied by the reference trajectory should agree with relative poses estimated directly from onboard sensors. We instantiate this idea with lidar scan registration and stereo-image pose estimation, and apply it to the KITTI Odometry and SNAIL Radar. The KITTI analysis identifies several revisit regions where the public reference poses disagree with sensor-derived relative poses, with median translation discrepancies up to 6.60 m. Representative point-cloud overlays further localize these discrepancies and support the use of place revisits as a benchmark-validation tool.
  </details>

- **[DexDuet: Learning Contact-Preserving Bimanual Dexterity from Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30495)**  `arXiv:2611.30495`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haoran Zhang, Haokai Ding, Han Zhou, Zuxing Lu, Heng Zhang, Xingxing Zuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human demonstrations provide a rich source of bimanual dexterous skills, yet transferring them to robot hands remains challenging. Existing methods primarily optimize object trajectory tracking, often overlooking how human hands interact with the object. Even with accurate tracking, robots may contact incorrect object parts or slide their fingers, resulting in unstable manipulation. We present DexDuet, a framework that learns contact-preserving bimanual dexterity from a single human demonstration. DexDuet employs two interaction rewards to preserve human hand-object configurations and contacts. To enable coordinated manipulation, we factorize the bimanual policy into two weight-sharing per-hand policies that condition on each other's states. We further propose assisted reference-state initialization (A-RSI), an adaptive curriculum that adjusts object assistance and training starts across demonstration segments to overcome difficult manipulation phases. Across four benchmarks spanning three simulators and five robot embodiments, DexDuet improves object-tracking ADD-AUC by 11 to 41 points over the corresponding baselines while consistently achieving better contact preservation. Notably, among 122 paired runs with comparable object-tracking accuracy, DexDuet preserves the human's contacts better in 117 cases. Code and videos are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[ROSE-Drive: Runtime Orchestration and System Evolution for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30438)**  `arXiv:2611.30438`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qihang Huang, Zhidong Wang, Zirui Li, YuJie Yan, Jingsong Liang, Shiyu Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end driving models have made substantial progress, yet reliable closed-loop driving in complex traffic remains challenging. Beyond improving individual models, system-level orchestration offers a complementary approach by coordinating driving capabilities and computation. We introduce ROSE-Drive (Runtime Orchestration and System Evolution), a model-agnostic framework that jointly orchestrates driving execution and computation. It coordinates frozen driving models with driving skills, perception tools, and feedback controllers. A distilled vision-language model (VLM) proposes driving tasks and scene relations. Shared physical state and persistent tasks support capability composition, task continuity, and adaptive scheduling. The orchestrator combines model and skill outputs across control channels, maintains task responsibility through handover, and uses execution feedback to select controllers, reuse valid motion references, and schedule model inference. Offline revisions use execution records to refine skills and their coordination through a stable task interface. Successive system stages inherit these revisions while model, perception, and VLM weights remain fixed. Evaluations with four driving models on Bench2Drive v0.0.3, Bench2Drive v0.0.4, and Fail2Drive show accumulated capability and transfer before target-benchmark revision. On held-out Longest6, driving score (DS) rises from 25 to 52 for AutoMoT and from 22 to 47 for SimLingo across system stages, while both systems skip new model inference on over 71% of control steps.
  </details>

- **[AgentMoT: An Agentic Mixture-of-Transformers for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30389)**  `arXiv:2611.30389`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qihang Huang, Collister Chua, YuJie Yan, Shiyu Fang, Zhan Chen, Haoran Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving models need to choose which capabilities to use and when to reconsider their decisions. We introduce AgentMoT, a Mixture-of-Transformers with built-in agent reasoning and system scheduling. Combinations of agent, world, and action experts form three jointly trained pathways for direct-action, vision-language-action, and world-action computation. The Router selects a native pathway, while the agent expert supplies reasoning and selects skills. Execution feedback raises wake-up requests, and the agent self-scheduler learns whether to reconsider the task decision or keep it. Mandatory requests invoke the agent directly. The relative-capability principle guides these choices by relating the benefit of additional computation or intervention to its cost. After open-loop initialization, dual-loop evolution trains the Router, agent, and self-scheduler from closed-loop experience while failure-driven updates refine skills and execution feedback. AgentMoT achieves state-of-the-art results on Bench2Drive v0.0.3 and v0.0.4, with 94.48/92.33 driving score and 85.45/82.73% success rate, respectively. With skills disabled, learned routing improves over the strongest fixed pathway by 1.79 driving-score points and 4.09 percentage points of success rate on v0.0.3. Ablation studies further show gains from closed-loop agent learning, two-view queries, and event-triggered wake-up.
  </details>

- **[ConsisX: Cross-View Consistency Thinking in 3D for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30308)**  `arXiv:2611.30308`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yunhe Zhang, Long Gao, Yuedong Fu, Gang He, Li Xu, Chang Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models integrate visual perception and language understanding from vision-language models (VLMs) with action prediction for robotic manipulation. Although multi-view observations provide complementary scene information, VLMs model cross-view interactions based on 2D feature similarity, which can lead to physical inconsistency in the form of cross-view incorrect association between distinct entities and cross-view information fragmentation about the same entity. To address these challenges, we propose ConsisX, a novel VLA framework that integrates cross-view association and entity coherence to enable cross-view consistency thinking in 3D for physically consistent robotic manipulation. Specifically, Cross-View Association Modeling (CAM) is proposed to introduce cross-view spatial proximity constraints in a shared 3D space to reduce cross-view incorrect association. Entity Coherence Organization (ECO) is designed to organize cross-view local information into coherent 3D entity evidence, thereby addressing cross-view information fragmentation. Consistent Action Conditioning (CAC) is introduced to condition action prediction on task-relevant 3D entity evidence for cross-view consistent action execution. Experiments on 3 real-world manipulation tasks and 4 simulation benchmarks establish ConsisX as an effective and competitive framework for robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Which: Where, How, and When in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30291)**  `arXiv:2611.30291`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yunhe Zhang, Long Gao, Yuedong Fu, Gang He, Li Xu, Chang Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models typically rely on vision-language model (VLM) features as the primary perceptual source for action prediction, showing strong generalization across diverse robotic manipulation tasks. Although VLM features can identify which entities are relevant to action prediction, precise manipulation also requires understanding where task-relevant entities are located within the spatial layout, how their geometric structure constrains physical interaction, and when spatial or geometric information should make a greater contribution to action prediction. To this end, we introduce {\mathcal{W}^{3}\mathcal{H}}-VLA to organize VLA perception and action prediction around which, where, how, and when. Specifically, {\mathcal{W}^{3}\mathcal{H}}-VLA retains VLM-based semantic entity perception which and further introduces spatial distance perception where and geometric structure perception how with adaptive spatial-geometric guidance when to enrich the perceptual information available for action prediction. Experiments across real-world settings and simulation benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and superior performance of {\mathcal{W}^{3}\mathcal{H}}-VLA.
  </details>

- **[MoMa-NMP: Scene-Scale Neural Motion Planning for Mobile Manipulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29913)**  `arXiv:2611.29913`  `cs.RO`  
  _Adam Kan, Jason Jingzhou Liu, Jiahui Yang, Kenneth Shaw, Deepak Pathak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile manipulators operating in homes and other complex environments must coordinate their base and arm over long horizons, navigating around obstacles, through narrow passages, and between constrained workspaces. Neural motion planners can provide fast closed-loop planning from partial observations, but scaling them to room-sized environments is difficult because generating high-quality training trajectories is itself a challenging motion-planning problem. To address this, we introduce MoMa-DemoSmith, a demonstration-generation pipeline that separates the problem into global path discovery for large complex scenes and local motion refinement for collision avoidance. This key separation enables the generation of millions of challenging whole-body trajectories across our procedurally generated scenes. We train MoMa-NMP on these demonstrations through behavior cloning (BC) followed by privileged on-policy distillation, achieving 87.0% success on our simulation benchmark and 77.5% across 80 trials in unseen real-world scenes. See video results atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SuperNav-1.5: Structured Spatial Memory For Agentic Navigation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29912)**  `arXiv:2611.29912`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiarui Guo, Jinkai Zhang, Yuanhong Yu, Yongchen Zai, Futang Zhu, Ruizhen Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper addresses the challenge of scene-level memory design for efficient navigation across sessions and successive object goals. Recent methods commonly represent a scene using scene graphs or image--text records. However, dense scene-graph representations impose substantial reasoning and context overhead on multimodal large language models, while image--text records cannot be directly converted into executable geometric routes. To address these limitations, we propose SuperNav-1.5, an agentic navigation system equipped with a structured spatial memory constructed by the agent. The memory consists of an area-segmented occupancy map annotated with agent-maintained, map-grounded memory nodes. This map guides the agent toward unvisited areas and provides executable routes to known locations. For multi-session navigation, we introduce agent-guided relocalization, whereby the agent infers an area prior from current observations and stored memory records to constrain geometric matching and localize the robot within the stored map. Together, these designs enable efficient navigation across session and successive object goals. Experiments show that our method outperforms baselines on single-object, multi-object, and demand-driven navigation benchmarks, with particularly strong gains in path efficiency. Multi-session evaluation demonstrates that the constructed memory can be reused, while deployment on a real quadruped robot further shows the system's applicability.
  </details>

- **[CarlHOI: Counterfactual Predictive Imagination for Generalizable Multi-Humanoid Object Interaction under Constrained Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29749)**  `arXiv:2611.29749`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wenjie Lin, Jin Wei-Kocsis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in physics-based humanoid control enable teams of varying sizes to coordinate their movements and cooperatively transport shared objects. However, adapting learned cooperative behaviors to unseen environmental constraints remains challenging, particularly when obstacles require coordinated detours and changes in team configuration. We propose CarlHOI, a counterfactual predictive imagination framework built upon a counterfactual-anchored relational latent world model (CarlWM) for human-object interaction (HOI), enabling multi-humanoid teams to adapt to unseen environmental constraints without retraining their underlying cooperative control policies. CarlHOI explicitly represents the shared object, individual humanoids, and environmental obstacles as interacting entities. Trained offline using counterfactual simulator rollouts, the model imagines alternative collective futures under different shared waypoint commands, predicting how the coupled humanoid team and object would respond. By evaluating these imagined outcomes through predicted progress, failure, and contact risks, and long-horizon value estimates, CarlHOI selects adaptive guidance commands while preserving the pretrained cooperative controller. We introduce a benchmark comprising three challenging obstacle-constrained transport tasks to evaluate adaptation and generalization. Despite being trained exclusively with four humanoids, our model generalizes without retraining to teams of two to ten humanoids, unseen obstacle geometries and placements, unseen objects, and novel combinations of environmental constraints. Extensive experiments demonstrate improved cooperative transport success over evaluated baselines across diverse configurations. Results highlight the potential of counterfactual predictive imagination to extend pretrained multi-humanoid cooperative skills beyond their original training conditions.
  </details>

- **[A Glimpse of Training VLMs as Foundation Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29338)**  `arXiv:2611.29338`  `cs.RO`  
  _Meng-Hao Guo, Yi Zhang, Zhe-Han Mo, Han-Zheng Wang, Jia-Jun Wang, Xin-Sheng Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, GPT-6 Astra demonstrates strong generalization in robot control, yet limited disclosure of its training leaves open how such capabilities can be reproduced. We introduce nanoAstra, an approach to training vision-language models (VLMs) for generalizable manipulation that combines semantic action modeling with capability-preserving supervised fine-tuning (CP-SFT). nanoAstra represents robot behavior as semantic steps composed of reusable, parameterized action primitives, guided by explicit progress descriptions. Meanwhile, CP-SFT uses an explicit robot-specific system prompt to constrain manipulation outputs to a structured action space, aiming to focus adaptation on robot-specific contexts and promote specialization while minimizing interference with broader pretrained capabilities. With this formulation, we train nanoAstra on more than 5,000 hours of robot data together with general VQA data. On RoboTwin 2.0's randomized benchmark, nanoAstra-9B achieves a 17.8% zero-shot success rate, substantially outperforming advanced VLA and WAM baselines as well as similarly sized VLMs. With inference faster than the corresponding motion execution on a single H20 GPU, our model supports action-chunk-like streaming execution that overlaps inference with motion, reducing the robot's idle time from 35.0% to 6.9%.
  </details>

- **[Tacinf: A Unified Tactile Infrastructure for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29240)**  `arXiv:2611.29240`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qiwei Wu, Xiangyu Chen, Xin Xiang, Rui Zhang, Yihang Kang, Weiran Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Force-aware manipulation requires robots to infer appropriate interaction forces from task context and tactile feedback. We present Tacinf, a low-cost, modular infrastructure that enables policies to predict robot actions and fingertip forces from task language, vision, and touch. Task instructions can optionally specify the desired force level. Tacinf integrates simulation data synthesis and tactile benchmarking (Tabero), vision-tactile-language-action (VTLA) policy learning, handheld data collection using the same gripper as robot deployment (Tacumi), and task-aligned sim-to-real evaluation (Taco). Together, these components form an integrated workflow connecting data collection from multiple sources, foundation-model training, and hardware deployment. We validate each component, including the simulation data, benchmark, controller, and Tacumi, and are releasing the full stack as open source. A foundation model fine-tuned solely on tactile simulation data transfers zero-shot to different real-robot tasks. Across eight policy variants, mean task success rates are strongly correlated between simulation and the real robot (Pearson r=0.998, mean maximum rank violation MMRV=0.010). For the four policies with reported force measurements, mean grip force is higher under firm instructions than under gentle instructions on all three tasks in both domains. When both object positions and instances are randomized, a mixture of teleoperation, simulation, and Tacumi data raises mean real-robot task success from 20% with teleoperation alone to 60%.
  </details>

- **[Force2Act: A Force-Aware Visuomotor Policy for Generalizable Compliant Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29208)**  `arXiv:2611.29208`  `cs.RO`  
  _Keerthi Vasan Murugan, Fangguo Zhao, Jiafei Duan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contact-rich manipulation requires a robot to regulate the force it applies, not only where it moves. Existing force-aware policies learn this force from demonstrations and therefore reproduce the force levels they were trained on, which fails once the interaction demands more force than any demonstration. We present Force2Act, a hybrid force-position policy built on two complementary mechanisms. A global force policy plans the motion and the interaction force once per action chunk, with force represented relative to the measured interaction, so that the planned force scales with the load. Between planning steps, a reactive compliance policy, termed Mixture of Force Experts, identifies at every control step the force expert that governs the interaction and regulates the force through it, so that the contact holds when it changes within a chunk. We further introduce Force2Act-Bench, a simulation benchmark of five contact-rich tasks that gives the community a common and repeatable way to test force-aware policies and measure how well they regulate the interaction force. Force2Act achieves an overall success rate of 93.8%, compared with 77.0% for the strongest baseline, and its reactive regulation reduces the failures on block pivoting from 30 to 10 of 150 trials. On a real robot, Force2Act reaches an average success rate of 94.2% over four tasks, against at most 27.5% for the baselines.
  </details>

- **[FunGrasp-Zero: Field-Conditioned Graph Attention Policy for Zero-Shot Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Functional Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29195)**  `arXiv:2611.29195`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanhan Lin, Yuliang Wu, Yihao Guo, Wei-Jin Huang, Jianwen Li, Jiawei Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous functional grasping is critical for robotic tool use, yet current dexterous hands exhibit substantial morphological diversity. This hardware heterogeneity makes cross-embodiment functional grasping a fundamental but largely unexplored problem. Existing functional grasping methods use retargeted hand poses as guidance, tying task representation to a specific embodiment and failing to explicitly capture object-centric functional contact intent, limiting generalization across hands. To address this, we propose FunGrasp-Zero, a reinforcement learning framework for zero-shot cross-embodiment functional grasping. Instead of relying on embodiment-specific joint configuration, FunGrasp-Zero captures functional intent via an object-centric, cross-embodiment-consistent contact map, adapted to heterogeneous hands through morphological mapping. We construct a hand-object potential field from mapped contact regions, yielding attractive and repulsive guidance vectors. A Field-Conditioned Graph Attention Network (FC-GAT) leverages these vectors to coordinate multi-finger motion across hands, while a potential field reward guides fingers toward target regions and penalizes non-target collisions to enforce functional alignment and stability. We further establish X-FunBench for cross-embodiment functional grasping, evaluating both physical grasp success and functional intent preservation across hands. Evaluated on X-FunBench, our unified policy, co-trained on four embodiments, achieves 86% average grasp success rate across five unseen hands on novel objects. Deployed on four unseen physical hands (Leap, Inspire, Sharpa, Wuji) for novel object grasping, the policy achieves 80% average success rate without fine-tuning. To our knowledge, FunGrasp-Zero is the first RL-based framework to achieve zero-shot cross-embodiment functional grasping across unseen hands in both simulation and the real world.
  </details>

- **[Your Diffusion-Based Driving Planner Only Needs a Nudge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29123)**  `arXiv:2611.29123`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zilin Wang, Guozheng Ma, Saeed Rahmani, Jiong Yang, Antyanta Bangunharcana, Yiluan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
