  Diffusion-based planners capture the multimodality of human driving behavior well, yet covariate shift in closed-loop driving can lead them to generate trajectories that result in collisions or road departures. Reinforcement learning (RL) finetuning offers a promising way to tackle this issue, but applying it to diffusion-based planners is complicated by their iterative denoising process and receding-horizon execution. We find that small adjustments to the planned trajectories can prevent many of these unsafe outcomes. Motivated by this observation, we propose PlanNudge, which preserves the learned planning capabilities of a pretrained diffusion-based planner by freezing it and trains a lightweight policy that we call the nudge policy. At each planning step, the nudge policy outputs a nudge, a two-dimensional adjustment of the speed and curvature of the plan from the frozen planner. The nudge policy is a multilayer perceptron that predicts the nudge in a single forward pass, and both dimensions directly change the motion of the vehicle, so standard off-policy RL can train it in closed-loop simulation. We evaluate PlanNudge on nuPlan, a large-scale autonomous driving benchmark based on real-world driving data. In all six settings, PlanNudge matches or exceeds the final scores of the previous state-of-the-art RL finetuning method while using about one sixth as many environment steps and less than one fifth as much model compute. We also provide guidelines for reward shaping, value learning, and exploration when using off-policy RL to post-train autonomous driving planners. Our results are reported with means and standard deviations over six training runs. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LIBERO-MOR: A Multimodal Robustness Benchmark Across the Vision-Language-Action Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28867)**  `arXiv:2611.28867`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qihang Chen, Tengjie Li, Hongwei Lu, Jason Wu, Chyna Qu, Shikui Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models have achieved strong performance on robot manipulation benchmarks. Translating this progress into reliable deployment calls for systematic evaluation of how policies respond to variations throughout the robot interaction loop. We introduce LIBERO-MOR, a multimodal robustness benchmark across the vision-language-action pipeline. Built on standard LIBERO, it covers five deployment-facing domains: vision, language, proprioception, action execution, and environment. The benchmark comprises 22 deployment-inspired perturbations, each evaluated at three severity levels across 40 tasks under a paired clean-perturbed protocol, enabling controlled and reproducible comparisons. We evaluate seven representative VLA models spanning diverse action-generation mechanisms. Our experiments reveal substantial robustness gaps behind high clean-task success, together with shared vulnerabilities and distinct model-specific failure patterns across domains. Severity analysis further distinguishes progressive degradation, collapse at stronger interventions, and failures triggered at the mildest tested level. By connecting aggregate performance to pipeline domains, individual perturbations, and intervention strength, LIBERO-MOR provides a systematic framework for diagnosing VLA robustness and guiding the development of more reliable robot policies.
  </details>

- **[LoKiTO: Local Kinodynamic Trajectory Optimization for UAVs based on Memory-Efficient Neural Distance Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28438)**  `arXiv:2611.28438`  `cs.RO`  
  _Guillermo Gil-Garcia, J. A. Cobano, L. Merino, F. Caballero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces LoKiTO, a 3D local kinodynamic trajectory optimizer that combines a Chebyshev polynomial parameterization with an online neural Euclidean Signed Distance Field (ESDF) representation for aerial robotics. While current gradient-based planners often struggle with the high memory requirements of explicit volumetric maps or fail to strictly enforce dynamic limits, LoKiTO embeds kinodynamic constraints directly into a modular cost function and exploits the numerical conditioning properties of the Chebyshev basis. Collision awareness is provided by a continuous, memory-efficient neural ESDF updated online from LiDAR measurements, which drastically reduces map memory consumption to approximately 1.0 MB. Benchmarking in highly cluttered simulated environments against state-of-the-art baselines (Fast-Planner and EGO-Planner) demonstrates the superiority of our approach. Experimental results show that LoKiTO achieves over 99.4% dynamic feasibility compliance, significantly outperforming the baselines, and maintaining higher median clearance from obstacles. By coupling a well-conditioned continuous parameterization with explicit kinodynamic reasoning, LoKiTO provides a practical and extensible basis for local replanning in aerial robotics.
  </details>

- **[BeyondEgo: Learning to Act Beyond Egocentric Perception via Humanoid Ego-Exo Foresight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27047)**  `arXiv:2611.27047`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhaoshan Yang, Chenglei Li, Haoyu Lei, Yutao Zhang, Yuyu Yang, Zhuo Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinating whole-body motion to perform manipulation tasks in complex environments. Yet egocentric vision-language-action (VLA) policies can miss critical body--scene relationships, and action imitation alone provides no explicit supervision for future scene evolution. We propose BeyondEgo to extend VLA perception in space and time. Spatially, we encode a synchronized exocentric view with frozen DINOv3 and concatenate the resulting tokens with egocentric VLM tokens to condition the action expert. Temporally, we use future observations from both views as privileged supervision for a residual adapter on task-conditioned VLM features. A foresight expert regresses DINOv3 representations of both views 0.6s ahead. At deployment, the foresight expert is discarded and the 4.2M-parameter adapter is retained. We also introduce HELM, to our knowledge, the first closed-loop humanoid loco-manipulation benchmark with synchronized egocentric and exocentric views across five whole-body tasks. On HELM, BeyondEgo improves success from 48.0% to 92.0% in distribution and from 23.5% to 65.0% on 200 held-out scenes with layout, asset, material, and lighting shifts. Ablations show that dual-view DINOv3 foresight yields the best transfer results.
  </details>

- **[RoboColiseum: Co-evolution of Simulation, Evaluation, and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27042)**  `arXiv:2611.27042`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chenghao Yin, Da Huang, Di Yang, Jichao Wang, Junhui Wu, Nanshu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation-based evaluation of embodied models requires calibrated interactions, diverse tasks, and explicit execution constraints. We present RoboColiseum, a platform combining system identification, composable physics and rendering, and multidimensional benchmarking. Recorded-command replay characterizes robot dynamics and object interactions, while a shared interface connects rigid and deformable simulation to rendering. Across 22 paired real--simulation robot--task configurations, task scores have a reported Pearson correlation of 0.9686. Eight evaluation tracks span instruction following, spatial reasoning, general manipulation, robustness, dexterous manipulation, precision manipulation, whole-body coordination, and execution across robot embodiments. Budget-aware scoring combines task progress, resource use, and completion rewards. We report 12 selected Season~I policies and all 11 systems on the public Season~II leaderboard, comprising eight Agent systems and three VLA policies. Agent2Act connects language models to robot-control execution; the VLA study uses mixed-task supervised fine-tuning. The results reveal substantial variation across tracks within each evaluation category. RoboColiseum connects simulation development with capability-oriented evaluation and provides a foundation for extending robot interactions and model assessment.
  </details>

- **[RecallFlow: Episodic Memory as the Source Distribution of Generative Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23731)**  `arXiv:2611.23731`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuji Zhang, Qilin Zhong, Zichen Zhang, Wei Zhai, Jian Di, Kun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow-matching policies have become a standard choice for visuomotor imitation learning, owing to their ability to model multimodal action distributions. In memory-dependent manipulation, the robot must act on information that is no longer visible. Existing methods feed the observation history to the policy as an additional condition, while action generation still starts from Gaussian noise. However, the policy must then pick out the relevant target from a long and redundant history, leading to spurious correlations and unreliable decisions as the memory load grows. Additionally, the initial noise carries no information about what the robot has seen, leaving generation without guidance toward the remembered target. To address these issues, we propose RecallFlow, a flow-policy framework that builds the source distribution from episodic memory. Recall-Relocalization relates the memory to the current view through a few recall slots, so that the source reflects where the remembered targets are now. Recall Anchors then turns each slot into a Gaussian component of the source, enabling generation to start close to the intended action instead of from noise. The transport network then completes the action from the current observation alone, keeping inference nearly as cheap as a memoryless policy. We further build a suite of memory-dependent tasks grouped by memory load and compare all policies under a matched protocol. Extensive experiments show that RecallFlow achieves a state-of-the-art average success rate of 80.4% on the public MIKASA-Robo memory benchmark, and outperforms the strongest baseline by 20.3 points on our suite and by 18.8 points on four real-robot tasks.
  </details>

- **[ReuseMem: One Memory, Multiple Goals in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21115)**  `arXiv:2611.21115`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yoonseok Kang, Dayeon Jung, Seunghoon Jeon, Wonjib Kim, Dongchul Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots that work alongside people should be able to use what they have seen when new requests arise. The same experience may later support identifying an object or returning it to its original location, although these uses were unknown during observation. ReuseMem learns to use one visual memory for multiple later manipulation goals. It records visual features independently of future goals, retrieves complementary evidence for each request, and combines it with the current scene through a lightweight action interface. Retrieval is supervised by how well candidate evidence supports expert-action prediction across different current states, linking memory selection to its use in manipulation. Repeated visual features are stored once, and memory computations that remain unchanged during action generation are reused. Experiments on four simulation tasks and two physical scenarios demonstrate reuse of a common recording across different requests. With matched action adaptation, ReuseMem improves joint goal completion on the benchmark tasks by 24.5 percentage points over uniform memory. These findings show that one memory can support multiple later manipulation goals as the scene changes. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[STVLA: Spatial-aware and Temporal-memory forVision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16589)**  `arXiv:2611.16589`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhuo Han, Yeqiang Wang, Xinran Wang, Hongwei Han, Yi Xiong, Zunian Wan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation often requires both spatial scene understanding and task history. However, many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely primarily on current observations, without explicitly integrating spatial structure and task history. Appearance-based memories can be sensitive to occlusion and viewpoint changes, while spatial representations of the current scene alone do not preserve how the scene has changed during task execution. We introduce STVLA, a framework that incorporates features from a pretrained visual geometry foundation model into temporal memory alongside visual representations. This allows the policy to recall past scene structure as context for current action decisions. To select informative history, STVLA uses a mathematically grounded scoring rule that balances relevance to the current observation with complementary evidence from memory. Across evaluations on LIBERO, LIBERO-Mem, MIKASA-Robo, and SimplerEnv Bridge, STVLA achieves the highest average success rate among the compared methods on each benchmark. On real-world block stacking, object organization, and phone packaging, it improves success over $\pi_{0.5}$ by $58.33$, $30.00$, and $35.00$ percentage points, respectively. These results support integrating scene geometry into temporal memory to improve manipulation requiring both spatial precision and task history. Project website: \href{this https URL}{this https URL}
  </details>

- **[RoboARCade: Benchmarking Agentic Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08209)**  `arXiv:2611.08209`  `cs.RO`  
  _Satvik Sharma, Hengyuan Hu, Mihika Kamat, Samrat Sahoo, Dorsa Sadigh, Jeannette Bohg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improvements in multi-modal capabilities have enabled agents to act directly as robot policies, a setting we refer to as agentic robot control (ARC). We introduce RoboARCade, a benchmark of long-horizon manipulation tasks across three embodiments designed to test in-context adaptation, memory, and reasoning for precise control. The tasks build on existing open-source benchmarks, which we make harder by widening the range of initial conditions. RoboARCade is easy to use and extend: all tasks run on a single simulation backend, MuJoCo, each task is a self-contained data pack, and agents connect through interchangeable interfaces, so new tasks, agents, and interfaces can be added independently. The benchmark separates leading agents: GPT-6 Astra achieves 49.8% compared with 43.6% for Claude Fable 5.1, leaving substantial room for improvement. Comparing two interfaces, we find that a richer visual interface adds only 1.5 to 6.8 points at 1.7 to 2.3 times the cost. Failures stem mostly from choosing a poor manipulation strategy or executing a good one imprecisely, rarely from misunderstanding the scene, and agents incorrectly claim success on 24% to 39% of attempted subtasks. Agents can also improve without human input: one successful demonstration in context raises Astra's score from 53.5% to 73.0% on a five-task subset, and a prompt the agent writes and revises itself reaches 72.0%. Code, videos and full traces are available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[When the Simulator Fails, Not the Policy: Auditing Contact Artifacts in Simulated Robot Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41857)**  `arXiv:2610.41857`  `cs.RO`  
  _Alex Zhou, Junhao Wu, Xueyang Zhou, Yixiao Huo, Hongcheng Gao, Di Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation benchmarks score robot policies by success rate, and every failed episode counts against the policy. Testing over ten vision language action and world action models on five benchmarks, we find that failures in which the policy has placed the object and the simulator then throws it out are frequent, and that the benchmark's own scripted demonstrations fail the same way. We present SimuGuard, an auditing plugin for LIBERO, RoboTwin, RoboCasa, RoboDojo and RoboLab. At every physics step, it records the state and flags an object that starts moving at a contact faster than the robot's contact or gravity account for. It replays a recorded episode bit for bit, and it reruns the episode with one solver parameter changed. With it we trace each such episode to a false contact. A fixed threshold in PhysX's collision detection makes the engine report an overlap of about two centimeters between the placed object and the thin wall of its container, where the two do not touch. The solver removes the whole overlap in one step and throws the object out at several meters per second. With a smaller threshold, LingBot-VA succeeds in 98 of 100 new episodes against 89, and no episode has an artifact. Across five models, the ranking in the repaired engine follows the ranking on a real robot. SimuGuard also finds two other simulation errors: objects that start inside their support, and objects stuck to an open gripper. SimuGuard lets a benchmark separate what the policy did from what the solver did.
  </details>

- **[Vector-Quantized World Model: Latent Feedback Motion Planning for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25600)**  `arXiv:2610.25600`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuezhou Xu, Chung-Pang Wang, Zhenzhen Li, Benjamin Riviere_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual world models have shown strong potential for robot planning, yet long-horizon manipulation remains challenging due to compounding prediction errors, large search complexity, and latent plans that the low-level controller cannot reliably reach. In this work, we introduce Vector-Quantized World Model (VQ-WM), a latent feedback motion planning framework that integrates discrete long-horizon search with continuous visual feedback control: a discrete latent predictor models transitions between vector-quantized latent states, allowing the planner to look far ahead and find a shortest path to the goal through discrete search and a continuous learned controller sequentially reaches the planned states through visual feedback, correcting deviations to mitigate compounding errors. Whereas most vector quantization uses Euclidean distance, we use a novel estimator of the first-passage-time as a quasi-metric, which aligns the planning abstraction with the controller's capabilities. We evaluate on the physical robot manipulation of the Tower of Hanoi puzzle: whereas existing state-of-the-art foundation models (VLAs, WAMs) only succeed when the solution trace in their training dataset, our method achieves 100\% success rate with only short-play data, demonstrate its ability to compose long-horizon solutions beyond the short demonstrated trajectories and execute them reliably. Our solution also outperforms strongest world-models by 13.3--85.3 percentage points on simulation benchmarks spanning manipulation and locomotion, demonstrating consistent progress and new capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Safe Task Completion via Graph-Based Planning for LLM-Powered Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603)**  `arXiv:2610.24603`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ali Taheri, Alireza Taban, Qizhou Wang, Wanhua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are used to drive robotic manipulation and planning, yet deploying them on physical systems exposes risks, including jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. Existing inference-time defenses are mostly evaluated on short instructions with a single hazard. Real tasks may mix safe and unsafe sub-tasks, or combine individually safe actions into an unsafe outcome. For such compound instructions, a single safety decision either misses the hazard or rejects the whole instruction. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-powered robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. To address this, we propose RuleGraph, a multi-agent Graph-of-Rules framework that verifies safety at multiple stages rather than relying on a single one. Five agents ground safety principles into world-specific rules, judge each sub-task in context, and derive a safe goal from the legitimate ones. Planning toward this goal is organized as a graph in which each node handles its assigned rules. Candidate plans are verified and aggregated level by level until one plan satisfies every rule. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions from two benchmarks, measuring Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). RuleGraph reaches 0% ASR on both benchmarks and highest TSR among all methods, with 98.57% on the RoboGuard benchmark and 83.51% on our long-horizon compound instructions.
  </details>

- **[NEXUS: Perceptive Whole-Body Control for Terrain-Adaptive Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39000)**  `arXiv:2609.39000`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xiangyu Miao, Junsong Wu, Jiyuan Shi, Weiji Xie, Jinrui Han, Xingyi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole-body teleoperation requires a humanoid robot to reproduce a human operator's behavior even when their terrains differ. This demands that the robot perceive local terrain and adapt its posture and contacts accordingly, rather than copy the operator's motion frame by frame. However, paired motion data linking the same behaviors across flat ground and different terrains remain scarce, limiting supervision for learning terrain-adaptive control. To enable whole-body teleoperation across mismatched terrains, we introduce NEXUS, a perceptive whole-body control framework that combines human motion commands with onboard sensory feedback. We first develop a scalable terrain-aware adaptation algorithm that efficiently generates high-quality motion pairs across motions and terrains without per-motion or per-terrain tuning. Using a paired motion corpus totaling nearly 1,000 hours, we train a perceptive whole-body controller through teacher-student learning to reproduce commanded behaviors across terrains. Experiments demonstrate efficient, scalable generation of high-quality motion data and show that NEXUS combines broad behavioral coverage with terrain adaptability and tracking fidelity, outperforming existing whole-body controllers on the evaluated benchmarks. Zero-shot real-world deployment enables real-time whole-body teleoperation on diverse unseen terrains, further validating the generalization of our method. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Latent Reasoning VLA: Latent Thinking and Prediction for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01166)**  `arXiv:2602.01166`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shuanghao Bai, Jing Lyu, Wanqi Zhou, Zhe Li, Dakai Wang, Lei Xing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models benefit from chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but existing approaches incur high inference overhead and rely on discrete reasoning representations that mismatch continuous perception and control. We propose Latent Reasoning VLA (LaRA-VLA), a unified VLA framework that internalizes multi-modal CoT reasoning into continuous latent representations for embodied action. LaRA-VLA performs unified reasoning and prediction in latent space, eliminating explicit CoT generation at inference time and enabling efficient, action-oriented control. To realize latent embodied reasoning, we introduce a curriculum-based training paradigm that progressively transitions from explicit textual and visual CoT supervision to latent reasoning, and finally adapts latent reasoning dynamics to condition action generation. We construct two structured CoT datasets and evaluate LaRA-VLA on both simulation benchmarks and long-horizon real-robot manipulation tasks. Experimental results show that LaRA-VLA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art VLA methods while reducing inference latency by up to 90\% compared to explicit CoT-based approaches, demonstrating latent reasoning as an effective and efficient paradigm for real-time embodied control. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PRIMA: Preference-Informed Multimodal Trajectory Planning with Vision-Language Model for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.04209)**  `arXiv:2412.04209`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ruoyu Yao, Yubin Wang, Haichao Liu, Rui Yang, Zengqi Peng, Lei Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision-making and motion planning constitute critical components for ensuring the safety and efficiency of autonomous vehicles (AVs). Existing methodologies typically adopt two paradigms: decision then planning or generation then scoring. However, the former architecture often suffers from decision-planning misalignment that incurs risky situations. Meanwhile, the latter struggles to balance short-term operational metrics (e.g., immediate motion smoothness) with long-term tactical goals (e.g., route efficiency), resulting in myopic or overly conservative behaviors. To address these issues, we introduce PRIMA, a preference-informed multimodal trajectory planning framework assisted by a Vision-Language Model (VLM). Our approach employs a tri-stage cognitive decomposition protocol, encompassing scene understanding, action selection, and confidence assessment, to generate diverse candidate actions paired with verbalized confidence scores. This reasoning mechanism exposes alternative tactical choices and provides an empirical preference signal for balancing them with downstream planning quality. Furthermore, we propose a novel planning module that integrates a diffusion model for trajectory generation and a hierarchical refinement process to find the optimal trajectory. This enables the selection over trajectory candidates accounting for both low-level solution quality and high-level tactical preferences, avoiding the risks within one-shot decisions and overcoming the limitations of short-sighted scoring mechanisms. Comprehensive simulation-based closed-loop evaluations on nuPlan and InterPlan demonstrate competitive performance across common and long-tail benchmarks. A paired controlled intervention further evaluates the incremental decision utility of the verbalized confidence signal. The code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[The Evaluation Substrate Matters: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Serving Substrates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28671)**  `arXiv:2611.28671`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haoyuan Zheng, Dongting Li, Hancheng Ye, Qinsi Wang, Mingyuan Ma, Yuncheng Yao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) usually coordinate heterogeneous models and tools to solve complex tasks, creating a growing need for reliable evaluation. However, a multi-agent workflow that is fastest in one serving environment can lose its advantage in another, even when its models, prompts, and task remain fixed. This makes a benchmark result conditional on how the workflow is executed. We introduce MASSys, a benchmark for measuring this dependence in multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems. MASSys executes the same candidate-task pairs across serving substrates and load regimes, using five workload families spanning software repair, research synthesis, financial analysis, interactive control, and diagram generation. It records task quality alongside delivery, latency, cost, resource pressure, and failure evidence. In a controlled motivation diagnostic, the Star workflow falls from first to fourth in mean-latency rank under KV-cache pressure, while Chain rises from fourth to first. The substrate sweeps further show that increasing load creates long runtime tails and timeout pressure. In several task families, quality among evaluated outputs changes less than effective quality over submitted jobs: the system delivers fewer usable results even when the surviving outputs remain competitive. Tool and runtime traces reveal that these outcomes involve both model serving and non-model execution. Together, the results motivate evaluating MAS designs over explicit operating conditions and reporting which tasks are delivered, how well they are solved, and where execution breaks down.
  </details>

- **[From Local Errors to System Failures: Modeling and Benchmarking Error Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31063)**  `arXiv:2611.31063`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenlong Yu, Bingxu Lu, Qilong Wang, Qinghua Hu, Qianru Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs), errors can propagate through intermediate messages and compromise final answers. However, outcome-based evaluation obscures how agents amplify, attenuate, or correct these errors. We propose Response-Aware Error Propagation among Agents (REPA), a structural model that connects measured local responses to system-level error propagation. REPA separately estimates input assembly and agent processing across multiple error channels, while theoretical analysis relates local modeling errors to endpoint prediction error. Experiments span three Qwen base models, six MAS workflows, and ten knowledge and reasoning settings in ErrPropBench-10. REPA achieves a mean condition-level Spearman correlation of 0.84 across base models, compared with 0.47 for direct regression. Controlled experiments also demonstrate its utility for targeted recovery. REPA-guided retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves accuracy over no retrieval by an average of 19 percentage points with 16% additional tokens. By linking local agent behavior to system susceptibility, REPA provides an interpretable foundation for studying error propagation and developing targeted optimization in MAS.
  </details>

- **[Anchor and Perturb: Lazy Agent Remediation by Exploration Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27365)**  `arXiv:2609.27365`  `cs.MA`  
  _Chengxi Zhong, Yongzhe Chang, Shiqing Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In MARL, teammates sharing a team reward propagated via $\partial Q_{\mathrm{tot}} / \partial Q_i$ can all receive credit for positive outcomes, even when some agents contribute near zero and never explore higher-return actions. Under standard uniform $\epsilon$-greedy exploration, concurrent exploratory noise across all $N$ agents can obscure trivial contributions from individual agents, driving QMIX into late-stage policy regression. Current remedies heavily rely on auxiliary models, intrinsic reward shaping, or pre-trained offline value anchors. Therefore, we propose Anchor and Perturb (AnP), an online framework for remedying lazy agents without altering QMIX's network architecture, loss function, or task reward. Its automated variant, AnP-Auto, monitors a real-time Laziness Disparity Index $\mathcal{L}_i(t)$ built from relative Q-value spreads and empirical action entropy. Once training reaches a point where lazy agents may surface while team win rate remains below a mastery ceiling, exceeding the $\mathcal{L}_i(t)$ threshold triggers a bounded rank-1 ($M = 1$) exploration pulse with $\phi$-annealed decay ($\phi > 1$) on the lagging agent while holding all $N - 1$ teammates strictly greedy ($\epsilon_{-i} = 0$). Following this, a $\phi$-ramped cooldown gradually releases the teammates into uniform exploration without major disruption to the replay buffer. As a proof of concept, within a tightly budgeted regime covering up to 4,500 episodes, a heterogeneous 10-agent SMAC MMM benchmark across five paired random seeds revealed that AnP-Auto remedied vanilla QMIX's late-stage regression and improved terminal performance on all five seeds. The method raised the terminal 500-episode greedy win rate from 30.20% $\pm$ 4.28% to 37.20% $\pm$ 4.55% (+7.00 percentage points; paired $t = 3.65$, $p = 0.0217$) and evaluation return by +0.71 ($p = 0.0037$).
  </details>

- **[The price of decentralization in managing engineering systems through multi-agent reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11884)**  `arXiv:2603.11884`  `cs.MA`  
  _Prateek Bhustali, Pablo G. Morato, Konstantinos G. Papakonstantinou, Charalampos P. Andriotis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inspection and maintenance (I&M) planning involves sequential decision making under uncertainties and incomplete information, and can be modeled as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP). While single-agent deep reinforcement learning provides approximate solutions to POMDPs, it does not scale well in multi-component systems. Multi-agent deep reinforcement learning (MADRL) improves scalability by decentralizing decision-making across agents that locally control individual components. However, this decentralization can induce cooperation pathologies that degrade the optimality of the learned policies. To examine these effects in I&M planning, we introduce a set of deteriorating systems in which redundancy is varied systematically. These benchmark environments are designed such that computation of centralized (near-)optimal policies remains tractable, enabling us to quantify the price of decentralization. We implement and benchmark a broad set of MADRL algorithms spanning fully centralized and decentralized training paradigms, from value-factorization to actor-critic methods. Our results show a clear effect of redundancy on coordination: MADRL algorithms achieve near-optimal performance in series-like settings, whereas increasing redundancy amplifies coordination challenges and leads to optimality losses averaging 10.3% in the parallel configuration. Nonetheless, decentralized agents learn policies that consistently outperform optimized heuristic baselines. While both remain suboptimal relative to the centralized optimum, decentralized policies narrow the gap, highlighting the promise and current limitations of decentralized learning for maintenance planning.
  </details>

- **[SGPC: Safety-Guiding Policy Constraint for Stable Off-Policy Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30265)**  `arXiv:2611.30265`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Yifan Liu, Nan Zhang, Yang Cong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy safe reinforcement learning (RL) faces a challenging trade-off between task optimality and constraint feasibility. In primal-dual methods, the coupled optimization of cumulative reward and constraint penalties through adaptive Lagrange multipliers may induce oscillatory policy updates near the safety boundary and amplify the effect of unreliable reward estimates outside the safe set. To address these issues, we formulate a new constrained optimization problem (COP) for safe RL by introducing a Safety-Guiding Policy (SGP) and a corresponding Safety-Guiding Policy Constraint (SGPC). The proposed COP augments the standard COP in safe RL with a conditional safety-guidance constraint. During actor updates, the current actor is evaluated using the learned cost critic. If it is predicted to be outside the safe set, SGP provides a nearby lower-cost training reference, and SGPC guides actor optimization toward this reference. Once the policy enters the safe set, the added constraint becomes inactive, and the formulation reduces to the original COP. This formulation leads to a plug-in actor-training constraint that can be integrated into off-policy safe RL algorithms. By applying primal-dual optimization to the auxiliary COP, we finally derive an SGPC-based off-policy safe RL algorithm and implement it in two representative off-policy algorithms, yielding SAC-SGPC and TD3-SGPC. Experiments on multiple Safety-Gymnasium benchmarks demonstrate that SGPC achieves a better optimality-safety trade-off by substantially reducing constraint violations and cost oscillations while maintaining competitive or better task returns.
  </details>

- **[Object-Centric Differentiable Logic Networks for Abstract Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30736)**  `arXiv:2611.30736`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Hanne Say Eren, Ana Ezquerro, Ozan Özdenizci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human visual intelligence enables efficient reasoning capabilities on abstract visual problems with bounded cognitive resources and minimal experience, and yields remarkable generalization to novel problems with its ability to draw relational analogies and modular abstractions. In contrast, deep learning models require exhaustive task-specific training on large datasets, making them prone to exploiting visual shortcuts and resulting in poor generalization. While neuro-symbolic approaches attempt to bridge this gap by decoupling perception from reasoning with dedicated computation units, existing solutions rely either on explicit rule supervision or parameter-heavy transformer architectures that reintroduce the computational inefficiencies they aim to solve. We introduce Slot-Dwn to address these limitations, a novel neuro-symbolic architecture that unifies unsupervised object-centric perception with logic-based symbolic reasoning. Our model utilizes a Multi-Head Differentiable Weightless Network (MH-DWN) that performs efficient reasoning entirely in Boolean space using look-up tables (LUTs). Our framework reduces visual perception complexity and introduces a LUT-based relational bottleneck with sparse input routing to minimize computational overhead. We evaluate Slot-Dwn across various abstract visual reasoning benchmark tasks targeting systematic rule generalization, sample-efficient anomaly detection, and multi-relational reasoning. We demonstrate that Slot-Dwn yields competitive performance with respect to the state-of-the-art, under extreme out-of-distribution regimes, while requiring up to 52% fewer trainable parameters, with significantly faster convergence.
  </details>

- **[Asymmetric Multimodal Learning: The Alignment Redundancy Bound and Decomposed JEPAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16639)**  `arXiv:2611.16639`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Yusuf Shihata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-modal alignment is the default objective for multimodal representation learning, but it rests on an implicit assumption: that the two modalities are roughly symmetric views of the same underlying state. We show that this assumption fails on real multimodal data, and that alignment as a sole objective is provably lossy under informational asymmetry. We formalize the failure through Partial Information Decomposition, proving that cross-modal alignment retains at most the redundant information atom and annihilates the modality-unique and synergistic structure on which asymmetric tasks depend. We give the exact PID decomposition of the alignment information bottleneck, characterize its failure regime, and derive a sufficiency condition that any encoder objective must satisfy to preserve all four informational atoms. We then introduce Decomposed JEPA (D-JEPA), which realizes this condition by factorizing each modality's latent space into an aligned shared core and a private fiber anchored by contextual masked prediction. On compositional reasoning benchmarks, alignment-only training undergoes catastrophic semantic collapse on relational queries; D-JEPA prevents this collapse, recovering the referent structure alignment discards, restoring most of the mutual information available from a frozen backbone, and producing positive multimodal synergy where alignment inverts it. Information-theoretic and causal audits confirm that the recovered information is causally localized in the private fibers.
  </details>

- **[Unlocking Compositional Generalization in Continual Few-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11710)**  `arXiv:2605.11710`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Phu-Quy Nguyen-Lam, Phu-Hoa Pham, Dao Sy Duy Minh, Chi-Nguyen Tran, Huynh Trung Kiet, Long Tran-Thanh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object-centric representations promise a key property for few-shot learning: Rather than treating a scene as a single unit, a model can decompose it into individual object-level parts that can be matched and compared across different concepts. In practice, this potential is rarely realized. Continual learners either collapse scenes into global embeddings, or train with part-level matching objectives that tie representations too closely to seen patterns, leaving them unable to generalize to truly novel concepts. In this paper, we identify this fundamental structural conflict and pioneer a new paradigm that strictly decouples representation learning from compositional inference. Leveraging the inherent patch-level semantic geometry of self-supervised Vision Transformers (ViTs), our framework employs a dual-phase strategy. During training, slot representations are optimized entirely toward holistic class identity, preserving highly generalizable, object-level geometries. At inference, preserved slots are dynamically composed to match novel scenes. We demonstrate that this paradigm offers dual structural benefits: The frozen backbone naturally prevents representation drift, while our lightweight, holistic optimization preserves the features' capacity for novel-concept transfer. Extensive experiments validate this approach, achieving state-of-the-art unseen-concept generalization and minimal forgetting across standard continual learning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Lowering the Bar for Everyone: Hidden Validation-Seeking Goals in Empathetic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30702)**  `arXiv:2611.30702`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinche Zhang, Shanghong Xie, Zhiyuan Ma, Jinhao Li, Sen Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When RL learns from the reactions of many users, the policy usually cannot see what each user wants. We ask what it learns from users who privately want validation: to validate them alone (targeting), or to lower its bar for endorsing everyone (a threshold shift). We answer this in one setting, empathetic RL against LLM-simulated users (RLVER, GRPO, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct): we vary only the share of validation-seeking users (0--50%; RLVER's default: 24.6%) and evaluate on 1,000 Reddit AITA posts with community fault verdicts.The policy shifts its threshold. Endorsement of at-fault posters rises by 3--4 percentage points per 10 points of share; without such users the Qwen model stays at base, and the effect persists at matched KL and under a goal-blind reward. Signal-detection analysis shows that the criterion for endorsing falls while discrimination between at-fault and not-at-fault posters does not decline. An explicit request to be told if one is wrong gives no measurable protection: at-fault posters who make it receive nearly the same rise as those who do not (+0.11 vs. +0.13). When the goal is stated during training, the policy instead largely targets, endorsing seekers regardless of fault.Standard sycophancy and empathy benchmarks miss the shift; five of six usable released empathetic-RL checkpoints also endorse at-fault posters who ask for honesty more than their base models, though a pre-registered prediction for two held for only one. The user mix is a training hyperparameter to audit and report.
  </details>

- **[PackLoop: Row-Packed Self-Speculative Decoding for Looped Language Models under Concurrency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30205)**  `arXiv:2611.30205`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Runguo Li, Dezhen Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models reuse one block of weights T times per token, trading parameters for test-time depth, but autoregressive decoding then pays T sequential passes per token. Self-speculative decoders recover 2-10x for a single request, yet their gains fade as requests share a GPU, and current practice is to speculate less as load grows. We show that what fails under load is the layout of speculative work, not speculation. In concurrent decoding the unit of cost is the row, one request advancing on one loop, which reads that loop's key-value (KV) cache once; additional positions inside a row are nearly free. Pipelined schedules such as the wavefront place every speculative position on a different loop, hence in a row of its own, so each token is still read T times. We present PackLoop, built on one principle: speculate only inside rows that are read anyway. Deep loops accumulate positions and advance them as a block in one row, context-copy proposals join the row that the first loop already reads, and a closed-form admission rule, the economic order quantity of inventory theory, sets how long to accumulate from a cost model calibrated once per engine. PackLoop is exact: in FP32 its output is token-for-token identical to autoregressive decoding, also under sampling and early exit. Across 164 load points spanning three GPUs, four Ouro models, six benchmarks, and contexts up to 32K tokens, one PackLoop configuration is faster than the best existing schedule, chosen per point, at 161 points (median 1.25x, up to 1.76x) and never significantly slower. Its lead grows with concurrency and context length, the regime in which existing self-speculation gains least.
  </details>

- **[On the Recall Scaling Laws in Mamba: A Theoretical and Mechanistic Study via Hashing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07681)**  `arXiv:2609.07681`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuval Koren, Assaf Ben-Kish, Raja Giryes, Lior Wolf, Itamar Zimerman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Associative Recall (AR) is the cognitive ability to learn and retrieve links between items in memory. In NLP, AR is used as a benchmark for evaluating the in-context memory capacity of architectures such as Mamba, and has been found to strongly correlate with language modeling performance. This paper explores AR from the perspective of mechanistic interpretability, aiming to reverse-engineer the exact internal algorithm used by Mamba to perform recall. Our key insight is that Mamba performs recall by implicitly learning linear hash functions, and we identify the low-level circuit that enables this behavior. Building on these findings and inspired by theoretical tools in similarity-preserving hashing, such as the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma, we develop a theoretical framework for analyzing AR, which we term Recall Scaling Laws. Given the vocabulary size and the number of facts in context, this framework allows us to (1) predict the embedding and state dimensions required for Mamba to achieve perfect recall, (2) predict recall success probability given the model dimensions, and (3) analyze multi-layer models and multi-head SSM patterns. Empirical results show that our theoretical findings are accurate and predictive, offering insights into how AR capacity scales with vocabulary, state, embedding size, and architecture.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Model Unlearning Under the Microscope: A Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07626)**  `arXiv:2510.07626`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Chongyu Fan, Changsheng Wang, Yancheng Huang, Soumyadeep Pal, Sijia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning for large language models (LLMs), known as LLM unlearning, aims to remove undesired data, knowledge, and behaviors while preserving model capabilities. Despite rapid progress over the past two years, research in LLM unlearning remains fragmented, with limited clarity on what constitutes effective unlearning and how it should be rigorously evaluated. In this work, we present a principled taxonomy of twelve recent stateful unlearning methods, grouped into three methodological families: divergence-driven optimization, representation misalignment, and rejection-based targeted unlearning. Building on this taxonomy, we revisit the evaluation of unlearning effectiveness (UE), utility retention (UT), and robustness (Rob), focusing on WMDP for hazardous knowledge and associated model capability removal. Our analysis shows that current evaluations, dominated by multiple-choice question (MCQ) accuracy, offer only a narrow perspective, often overstating success while overlooking model generation behavior. To address this gap, we introduce open question answering (Open-QA) metrics to better capture generative performance and reveal the strengths and limitations of different unlearning method families. Furthermore, we demonstrate that robustness requires finer-grained analysis. For example, vulnerabilities differ substantially between in-domain relearning and out-of-domain fine-tuning, even though both fall under model-level attacks. Through this study, we provide a full-stack re-examination of LLM unlearning and actionable guidance for designing and evaluating future methods. Code and model checkpoints are publicly available on GitHub and Hugging Face.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30986)**  `arXiv:2611.30986`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yihong Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the \emph{trainable policy} velocity onto rollouts from a \emph{behavior distribution}. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This \emph{behavior--policy mismatch} breaks baseline invariance and injects \emph{baseline-induced gradient drift}, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose \textbf{I}nstantaneous \textbf{T}rajectory \textbf{D}istribution \textbf{M}atching (\textbf{iTDM}), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly $3\times$ faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to $0.87$ without collapse. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[State of Mind: Learning and Evaluating World Models of Naturalistic Brain Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28508)**  `arXiv:2611.28508`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuxiang Wei, Zundong Wu, Weiyu Sun, Qizhen Lan, Yanteng Zhang, Vince D. Calhoun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding naturalistic brain activity requires going beyond the stimulus alone, as external inputs continuously interact with an individual's evolving brain state. A world model captures such dynamics in a latent state by inferring it from neural observations, predicting stimulus-driven evolution, and updating with new observations. However, existing brain world models fail to maintain a persistent latent state over time, and standard metrics of neural prediction accuracy cannot tell whether a model tracks an individual's brain state or relies solely on the stimulus. To directly test state inference, prediction, and updating, we introduce BrainStateBench, which evaluates a model's latent state through a frozen linear probe on whole-cortex, movie-driven fMRI. Its State task tests whether a model captures trial-to-trial variability in an individual's responses to repeated stimuli, while Forecast and Updating evaluate future activity predictions without and with new observations, respectively. To track individual brain state across time, we propose Spectator, a linear latent world model trained with a joint-embedding predictive objective. Spectator encodes short- and long-horizon observation histories into a two-timescale latent state and predicts state transitions linearly from the current state and stimulus. Stimulus-only encoders reach 0.320 in fMRI prediction correlation without observing the brain (0% on State), while prior deep brain world models reach 0.239 yet score at most 0.4% on State. In contrast, Spectator achieves 0.349 correlation alongside 5.5% on State, 16.0% on Forecast, and 20.3% on Updating, outperforming all baselines while forecasting over 20x faster than deep forecasting models.
  </details>

- **[Yggdrasil: Tree-Routed Recurrent Experts for State Tracking and Fast CPU Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29954)**  `arXiv:2611.29954`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Cosmo Santoni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel-trainable recurrences such as Mamba, linear attention, and DeltaNet offer constant-cost streaming inference, but they suffer from two limitations: 1) they struggle to track discrete state, and 2) they read every parameter at every step, tying model capacity directly to CPU latency. We introduce Yggdrasil, a recurrent architecture that decouples capacity from per-step compute by looking up each layer's transition in a large, tree-routed bank of recurrent experts. Each router addresses its bank using the current token and the state of the layer below. We prove that this cross-layer context is necessary---a single token-indexed layer of non-expanding transitions with finitely many reachable states cannot perform conditional resets---while avoiding feedback from the layer's own past preserves parallel-scan training. Because each step traverses one tree path and reads a single expert per layer, active weights stay within a few hundred kilobytes in CPU L2 cache while the full bank resides in RAM. Among the parallel-trainable models we evaluate, Yggdrasil is the only one to solve all five state-tracking tasks. At matched active parameter counts, it achieves the lowest test bits on enwik8 (1.540 bpb) and the human genome hg38 (1.567 bpb), while stepping $3.0\times$ and $3.3\times$ faster on a single CPU core ($6.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and $5.8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$) than the fastest streaming baseline, and delivering the highest GPU throughput of all models at batch 1024. On the Genomic Benchmarks, it is the strongest causal model at matched active parameters (86.0% mean accuracy).
  </details>

- **[Beyond Accuracy: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Hypothesis-Driven Experiment Planning in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29467)**  `arXiv:2611.29467`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ryo Kuroki, Amin Mansouri, Philippe Schwaller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating large language model (LLM) agents on scientific tasks typically reduces to final-task accuracy, which obscures where reasoning succeeds or breaks down. We introduce a benchmark and diagnostic evaluation framework for hypothesis-driven adaptive experimental planning in closed-world system identification, instantiated in kinetic mechanism identification. The framework goes beyond final accuracy in two ways. First, we evaluate exploration behavior by quantifying the extent to which agents exhibit space-filling or corner-seeking patterns in the experimental design space. Second, we decompose the reasoning process into four interpretable steps: belief update, hypothesis discrimination, discriminative experiment design, and predictive reasoning under interventions, each scored separately on reasoning traces. Across ten LLM agents, we find that stronger agents outperform both adaptive (Bayesian optimization) and non-adaptive (Latin hypercube) baselines and exhibit exploration patterns inconsistent with naive heuristics. Decomposing reasoning reveals that the stronger agents score consistently well across all four steps, whereas weaker agents often succeed at belief update and hypothesis discrimination but are less reliable at translating them into actionable designs and sharp contrastive predictions. Independent expert annotations support predictive reasoning under our stated validation criteria, while evidence for discriminative experiment design is positive but less consistent. The results suggest a gap between recognizing plausible hypotheses and translating them into contrastive predictions, with more tentative evidence for a corresponding experiment-design bottleneck. The benchmark website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Which Failure Does an Intervention Repair? When Agent Interventions Help, and Where Self-Evolving Skills Stall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29290)**  `arXiv:2611.29290`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zihan Jin, Gaoyuan Du, Jiate Li, Dongze Ye, Shawn Li, Yue Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An intervention that improves one agent can break another, and adding a stronger harness on top need not repair the damage. Agent interventions such as skills, harnesses, and extra inference budget are nonetheless usually credited with one average gain, measured under one setup. We ask instead when each intervention helps and which failure it repairs. A matched factorial study over nine benchmarks, four models, four skill conditions, four harness levels, and three budgets, with nearly 0.9 million trajectories, changes one axis at a time. No configuration is best everywhere, interventions interact rather than add, and a harness can absorb a skill's gain, rescue a failure the skill causes, or leave the gain intact. The failure left by the current system points to the fix: missing knowledge to a stronger model, execution errors to a richer harness. Yet feedback is not recovery: a matched recovery repairs mainly failures that leave a visible error signal. Skills that evolve from the agent's own experience hit a bottleneck: they rarely beat a short human-written skill, can learn dataset shortcuts, break when moved to another model, and cannot supply missing facts, which outside information does. A benchmark-level switch to the model favored by a 20-task labeled pilot achieves both higher performance and lower cost than always using the stronger model. Agent interventions should be judged by which failure they repair and under which configuration.
  </details>

- **[VeriLogic: Reinforcement Learning over Verifiable Logical Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29147)**  `arXiv:2611.29147`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _ZeLiang Chen, Yunyao Zhang, Xinglang Zhang, Wenbing Li, Junqing Yu, Zikai Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical reasoning derives conclusions from premises through explicit intermediate inference steps, providing a natural basis for process-level verification and supervision. However, existing learning methods often rely on response-level outcome signals, which overlook heterogeneous correctness within the reasoning trace: a correct answer may contain invalid reasoning, while valid intermediate steps may also appear in an incorrect response. To address this issue, we introduce Reinforcement Learning on Verifiable Logical Reasoning (VeriLogic), which turns internal reasoning traces into explicit and verifiable learning objects. Rather than treating a response as an indivisible learning unit, VeriLogic decomposes logical reasoning into explicit intermediate judgments, proofs, and final decisions, and organizes them into a unified "Grounding - Optimization - Refinement" pipeline: (1) Verification-Grounded Reasoning Environment Construction grounds logical reasoning into explicit intermediate states and formally checkable proof obligations, making the internal reasoning process accessible to verification and learning; (2) Logical Structured Policy Optimization learns from verifier feedback through complementary global and local rewards, jointly optimizing the overall reasoning outcome while providing targeted optimization signals to different intermediate reasoning paths; and (3) Verifier-Guided Iterative Refinement acts on verifier feedback by leveraging proof states and error messages to iteratively diagnose and repair failed reasoning. Experiments on six logical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over strong baselines, with average improvements of 6.39% and 5.00% on Qwen3 and Llama-3.1. These results highlight the value of making logical reasoning traces verifiable, learnable, and refinable beyond final-answer supervision.
  </details>

- **[Don't Train It, Write It: An AI Agent Writes New Capabilities into Language Model Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28483)**  `arXiv:2611.28483`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Santiago Rivadeneira Quintero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We show that an AI agent can write new capabilities into a pretrained language model by adding gated attention heads and MLP neurons inside its existing layers, without backpropagation and without fitting any weight to the answers of task examples. It has a budget of 50 model passes and sees no validation or test instance. Composed in one model, its modules for six BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) subtasks raise the normalized BBH score of Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct from 19.95 to 41.05 (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct: at most 35.01) and that of Gemma-3-1B-it from 10.25 to 32.86. Against the unmodified model at the same precision, both composites leave the next-token distribution on 1,023,000 WikiText tokens unchanged (KL exactly 0), give identical answers on 15,845 items of five other Leaderboard tests and identical scores on the other 18 BBH subtasks. The agent also writes exact multiplication up to 20 digits, running the model only up to 6 (100.0% on the absent lengths of the sealed test in both models; a LoRA with the same budget: at most 2.1%). Sets, rules and report sentences were sealed in git before the first session (manifests timestamped in Bitcoin), with dated amendments listed in Appendix D; a frozen evaluator ran the sealed test once. Verdicts (H5: tracking; H6: composite; H8: one pass vs. chain of thought; H9: fresh and rewritten instances; H10: side effects; H12: multiplication), on the preregistered sealed test, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct: H5 success, H6 success, H8 (web_of_lies) success, H9 success, H10 (composite) success on (i) to (iii), (iv) confirmed, H12 success; Gemma-3-1B-it (H7: H5, H6, H8 and H9 in this model): H5 success, H6 success, H8 (web_of_lies) success, H8 (tracking 3) success, H9 partial, H10 (composite) success on (i) to (iii), (iv) confirmed, H12 success. Not everything worked: 4 of 45 single modules show small side effects and 5 of 50 sessions hit the 12-hour limit without a module.
  </details>

- **[Measuring and Teaching Language Model Agents to Act on Delayed Consequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28158)**  `arXiv:2611.28158`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shardul Bansal, Seth Schilbe, Jarrod Barnes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model agents are increasingly asked to run ongoing multi-day operations rather than finish isolated single-turn tasks. In daily operations, today's choices reshape later observations and cash constraints because empty shelves hide lost walk-in demand, short-shipped customers quietly shrink future orders weeks later, and emergency buying drains working capital. We introduce SMB Bench, a calibrated 90-day wholesale simulator with 18 causally paired tasks and an 859-run frontier trace dataset, to ask what experience and learning signal make an agent better at noticing, attributing, and acting on the delayed consequences of its own decisions in unseen businesses. Across 16 frontier models, only 3 outperform a fixed buying rule; models often record a relevant diagnosis in their notes without changing later purchases in time, showing that diagnosing a problem, changing behavior, and improving a business are separate achievements. To study how experience improves those failures in an open model (Qwen3.8-27B), we used training-business records to identify operational rules and archived frontier quarters as starting states to continue from rather than transcripts to copy. On an unseen test business under identical evaluation instructions, the trained policy followed customer-protection commitments more consistently and reduced severe-loss quarters, while the additional profit benefit remained uncertain. Carrying those commitments through the quarter exposed the cost of fulfilling them, as early-quarter stocking habits left the buyer relying on emergency supply and a deteriorating supplier. The results separate recognizing a delayed effect, acting on it consistently, and coordinating surrounding decisions so that the response improves profit. SMB Bench connects these observations to a learning method by turning archived decisions and their consequences into reusable training experience.
  </details>

- **[Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18001)**  `arXiv:2610.18001`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Miao Li, Hanyang Jiang, Hengyu Fu, Baihe Huang, Yating Liu, Yuhang Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. This flexibility, however, introduces training choices absent from standard autoregressive teacher forcing: which reference tokens are visible when a target is supervised and how much generation space is allocated. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Code will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Unsolvability Ceiling in Multi-LLM Routing: An Empirical Study of Evaluation Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07395)**  `arXiv:2605.07395`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Saloni Garg, Amit Sagtani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient routing across multiple LLMs enables cost-quality tradeoffs by directing queries to the cheapest capable model. Prior work attributes routing headroom to an "unsolvability ceiling", queries no model in the pool can solve. We present a large-scale study of multi-tier LLM routing with 206,000 query-model pairs across six benchmarks (MMLU, MedQA, HumanEval, MBPP, Alpaca, ShareGPT) using the Gemma 4 and Llama 3.1 families. Evaluating with both LLM-as-a-judge and exact-match metrics, we show that a substantial portion of reported unsolvability stems from evaluation artifacts: (i) systematic judge biases favoring verbosity over correctness, (ii) truncation under fixed generation budgets, and (iii) output format mismatches. Through dual-judge validation and exact-match grounding, we reduce measured unsolvability across tasks. We introduce a decomposition framework attributing failures to these artifacts, revealing consistent patterns across domains and model families. These artifacts also distort router training signals: standard routers collapse to majority-class prediction (~79% smallest-tier optimal), confirmed via random-feature and shuffled-label controls, incurring a 13-17 percentage point opportunity cost. We provide actionable recommendations including dual-judge validation, exact-match anchoring, and cost-sensitive objectives. Our findings suggest existing routing headroom estimates are substantially inflated, underscoring the need for reliable evaluation protocols in multi-LLM systems.
  </details>

- **[TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30916)**  `arXiv:2611.30916`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bradley Segal, William Bolton, Philip Torr, David Clifton, Lei Clifton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We publicly release nearly 3,500 tasks, varying from analysing a single trial to managing multi-phase programmes with budget and time constraints. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. Model generations show ongoing improvements in handling data and evidence, with substantial variation. The strongest models can act as competent analysts, but do not yet make reliable long-horizon decisions.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Rare-Mode Collapse in Recursive Generative Training from Single-Generation Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30897)**  `arXiv:2611.30897`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyao Zhang, Mingyu Du, Lu Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive generative training can preserve sample counts in a rare region while silently eroding its characteristic core. Mapping this finite-horizon collapse across real-data quotas, rare prevalences, and feedback perturbations via exhaustive multi-generation chains is computationally prohibitive. We develop a decoupled framework that forecasts multi-generation collapse probabilities and locates operating risk boundaries from independent single-generation conditional fits alone. Pairing an exact three-source multinomial count law with single-generation response banks propagates count marginals in closed form, backed by explicit calibration-error and boundary-resolution bounds. For additive Gaussian perturbations, we further prove that equal-variance all-row and sparse policies reverse their boundary-crossing order at a unique hyperplane distance. Across a 2,178-setting count benchmark, exact propagation cuts risk mean absolute error (MAE) from 1.85 to 0.49 percentage points (pp). Across ten two-dimensional learners, calibration achieves 2.60--5.69~pp six-round core MAE on nine stationary learners (65--81\% below an unperturbed baseline), diagnoses non-stationary outlier drift in standard DDIM (16.38~pp) via a stability probe, and resolves the predicted six-round geometric reversal. On Fashion-MNIST latent flow-matching chains, calibration outperforms direct-chain Gaussian-process interpolation at lower fit budgets and tracks the 20\% latent-perturbation boundary shift (3.14 versus 10.78~pp MAE for proportion copying) driven by opposing inflow to the rare region and outflow from its core.
  </details>

- **[PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30785)**  `arXiv:2611.30785`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xianglong Shi, Nicu Sebe, Bernhard Schölkopf, Ziheng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperbolic representations have shown remarkable success on hierarchical and tree-like data, and Transformers have become a central architecture for representation learning. However, a principled construction of hyperbolic Transformers remains challenging because the self-attention layer decomposes into three coupled primitives, transformation, similarity, and aggregation, that should all respect the underlying geometry. Existing hyperbolic Transformer designs are typically built in the Poincaré or Lorentz models, where bounded domains, time-like constraints, tangent-space detours, or projection steps make it difficult to keep the full block intrinsic and scalable. To address this gap, we propose PVFormer, to the best of our knowledge, the first Transformer architecture that operates natively in proper velocity (PV) space. The proper velocity model provides an unconstrained representation of hyperbolic geometry, and PVFormer exploits this structure to redesign the Transformer block in PV space. On the transformation side, we use PV homomorphism transformations to update query and value representations. On the similarity side, we generalize Euclidean inner-product attention with a PV Busemann score inspired by Busemann-based hyperbolic learning and a PV point-to-hyperplane key transformation. On the aggregation side, we replace Euclidean averaging with PV midpoint aggregation and further derive a scalable PV linear attention variant for large graphs. Extensive experiments support the effectiveness of PVFormer across graph, text, and vision benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[HEART: Hierarchical Expert Adaptive Refinement Transformer for ECG Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30298)**  `arXiv:2611.30298`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Junho Song, Jong-Hwan Jang, Joon-Myoung Kwon, Yong-Yeon Jo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning robust representations from electrocardiograms (ECGs) is challenging because repeated cardiac cycles coexist with subtle and sparse diagnostic cues in local morphology and rhythm-level context. Existing ECG foundation models commonly rely on Transformerbased patch encoders with uniformly shared dense refinement pathways, limiting adaptive processing across heterogeneous ECG tokens. We propose HEART (Hierarchical Expert Adaptive Refinement Transformer), an ECG foundation model that introduces stage-specific conditional expert refinement during self-supervised pre-training. HEART places a local-level MoE over patch-level representations before temporal contextualization and a global-level MoE over context-integrated token representations after self-attention. Token-level Top-1 routing allows different patches or contextual tokens within the same ECG to follow different expert pathways. On public ECG benchmarks, HEART achieves strong performance under both linear probing and fine-tuning, with particularly large frozen-encoder gains on PTB-XL Form and Rhythm (+6.76 AUROC points each over the strongest protocol-reproduced baseline). Routing analyses provide qualitative evidence of complementary local and global expert specialization. We release our code and pretrained models atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[QASparse: Query-Adaptive Sparse Attention with Tailored Budgets and Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29280)**  `arXiv:2611.29280`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Chen, Zihao Yang, Quanjiang Li, Haijiang Li, Jiancheng Pan, Qichao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-sequence inference in large language models is constrained by the high cost of prefilling, where dense attention scales quadratically with context length. Exploiting similarity across attention heads offers a way to reduce redundant computation. Prior approaches in this direction have relied on localized query observations to determine head-level computation reuse or budget allocation. However, inter-head similarity does not imply uniform query requirements, leaving adaptation to varying budget needs and selection preferences across query regions a key challenge. We introduce QASparse, a Query-Adaptive Sparse attention mechanism with hardware-efficient execution. QASparse preserves the efficiency of shared cross-head importance estimation while adapting computational budgets and token selections to query requirements, and it executes these dynamic sparse decisions through dedicated kernels. Its query adaptivity stems from two complementary innovations: Local Budget Regression models local variations in computational demand along query positions to estimate budgets for different query regions and attention heads; Boundary Voting lets queries within each head vote over candidates near the selection cutoff using actual query/key scores, refining the retained set while preserving the initial selection size. To reduce the execution overhead of dynamic budgets, we further design length-specialized selection kernels that efficiently support query-adaptive sparse patterns across context lengths. Together, these designs efficiently combine shared estimation with local decisions while adapting both how much attention is computed and which interactions are retained. Experimental results demonstrate improved benchmark scores and faster prefill attention computation compared with existing sparse attention baselines.
  </details>

- **[Hidden Sensitivity in LLM Pre-training: A Dynamic System View](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28830)**  `arXiv:2611.28830`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Liu, Yuan Xie, Junan Chen, Fujun Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Large Language Model (LLM) pre-training, loss curves can conceal substantial instability, i.e., even small perturbations may lead to dramatically different predictions while leaving loss nearly unchanged. To investigate this phenomenon and reveal the underlying mechanisms, we first formulate LLM pre-training as a non-autonomous dynamical system that jointly evolves model weights and Adam optimizer states. Based on this formulation, we introduce a normalized sensitivity score in optimizer-relative coordinates to identify sensitive training checkpoints. At one such checkpoint, a weight perturbation of only 10^(-5) leads to 35.7% disagreement in top-1 predictions and 16.2% disagreement in benchmark answers after 200 training steps, with only 0.8% of greedy generations remaining identical despite similar validation losses. Furthermore, we find that perturbations to Adam's first and second moments induce comparably large effects, with similar sensitivity patterns observed in OLMo-1B. Finally, guided by our diagnostic, targeted stabilization reduces top-1 disagreement to 0.83% and increases generation exact match to 88.3%, at the cost of a 0.11-nat increase in validation loss. Our findings reveal a hidden form of optimizer-state-dependent sensitivity in LLM pre-training that conventional loss-based evaluations may overlook, while demonstrating its potential for diagnosis and mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Action-Conditioned Transition Irreversibility for Out-of-Distribution Detection in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28611)**  `arXiv:2611.28611`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`
