  _Emil Mittag, Richard Dazeley, Peter Vamplew_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement-learning agents shape the observations they encounter; consequently, detecting out-of-distribution (OOD) states requires analysing action-conditioned trajectories rather than treating inputs as independent samples. We propose Action-Conditioned Transition Irreversibility (ACTI), a detector that evaluates forward and backward transition likelihoods, calibrates this comparison against the in-distribution profile at each timestep, and smooths the score over a short window, using no future information. We tested ACTI across six environments and fifteen detectors within OOD-RL-Bench, using the four anomaly mechanisms that qualified in every environment, with ten policy replicates per environment. All primary metrics are matched-time: each score after anomaly onset is compared with a clean twin rollout's score at the same timestep. Our method secured the top macro matched-time AUROC, outperforming the dynamics-model baseline (PEDM), while scoring transitions roughly two orders of magnitude faster. Compared to the state-only variant, ACTI achieved a small but consistent AUROC gain and a shorter detection delay. The capacity-matched forward-only baseline, ACTI-Forward, secured a statistically significant advantage in AUPRC alongside a reduced detection delay. The discrepancy in AUROC did not reach statistical significance. Ablation studies suggest instead that performance improvements stem from action conditioning, calibration at each timestep, and smoothing of the transition-likelihood scores. Based on these results, we recommend using ACTI-Forward.
  </details>

- **[HiPPO Functional Memory: Coordinate-Queryable Attention for Irregular Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28465)**  `arXiv:2611.28465`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaodong Guo, Haoyun Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular time series are recorded at nonuniform times and are often queried at other times: between observations, after the last one, or on a regular grid. Continuous-time models answer such queries by integrating latent dynamics, and time-aware attention models answer them by revisiting the observation tokens. We introduce HiPPO Functional Memory (HiPPO-FM), an attention operator that writes an irregular history into a rank-M function space with explicit time coordinates. Masked self-attention first exchanges content among the observations; the updated values are then contracted against kernel-smoothed HiPPO-Legendre features and solved through a regularized Gram matrix, so the retained coefficients define a function that is read at any time through the same features. The write and read have the form of an interdomain sparse Gaussian-process mean: every readout lies in an M-dimensional reproducing-kernel subspace, and a pointwise bound separates the approximation error of this subspace from the error of the learned memory. Removing the functional memory from the encoder raises Spiral reconstruction RMSE 18-fold. The full model has the lowest error in 11 of 12 Spiral settings, the highest overall accuracy and best average rank on 20 irregular UEA datasets, the highest event-type accuracy on all five multi-type event benchmarks, and a 21% lower extrapolation MSE than CRU on PhysioNet. Because the memory has fixed rank, the state retained after encoding keeps a fixed size as the history grows and is 102 times smaller than a token cache at 1,024 observations.
  </details>

- **[WingBench: Benchmarking How AI Agents Search for Better Designs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28418)**  `arXiv:2611.28418`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yiming Yang, David Xia, Jisen Li, Yuqing Jian, Yunjia Yang, Koray Aydin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Design search often uses cheap learned predictions to choose which candidates deserve expensive tests, but these tests can fail, especially on the unusual designs that optimization favors. We introduce WingBench, a multifidelity benchmark in which optimizers and AI agents design transonic wings with computational fluid dynamics (CFD) inside the search. They can query a learned aerodynamic surrogate and spend a fixed budget of sequential CFD evaluations, each returning a score or a failure type. WingBench grades the design each method recommends, reporting separately whether it receives a valid score and how good that score is. Using WingBench, we contribute a study of five baseline optimizers and a comparison of three AI agent methods, which produced 6,629 CFD evaluation records. Feedback helps baseline optimizers deliver valid designs when it changes their later proposals, but the differential-evolution variant does so with lower aerodynamic quality. An open-ended research agent finds designs of similar quality to the strongest baselines over 32 CFD queries, with different prior information and unmatched total computation. In early search, our reflection and planning implementations do not consistently improve on it. We release the data, code, agent prompts, and analysis scripts for reproducibility.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Site Transformers Are Robust Heart Rate Estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28366)**  `arXiv:2611.28366`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mason Kadem, Rong Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-site heart-rate estimation from photoplethysmography (PPG) degrades sharply under real-world motion. Multi-site PPG provides a natural solution, but existing models fuse sites as generic sensor channels rather than exploiting their hemodynamic structure: each heartbeat propagates along the arterial tree, appearing across body sites as delayed and transformed copies of the same cardiac event. We propose the Cross-Site Transformer, which introduces a hemodynamically informed inductive bias by restricting multi-sensor fusion to cross-attention between PPG sites. On WildPPG, the largest in-the-wild benchmark available, our model establishes a new single-model state of the art, reducing error by 21% over the previous best and 35% over the best prior transformer. The same cross-site restriction makes the trained network directly auditable. Because each cross-attention head lies on one directed pathway between sites, causal resample ablation can isolate and test each direction of information flow. Ablating the chest-to-wrist pathway doubles error, whereas the reverse costs far less. Encoding hemodynamic structure into attention routing thus improves real-world sensing robustness and enables rigorous auditing of multi-sensor fusion.
  </details>

- **[Dual Representation Enhanced Diffusion Policy for Flexible Job Shop Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15246)**  `arXiv:2611.15246`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunyi Zhao, Wei Zhang, Cheng Xiang, Vadakkepat Prahlad, Zuming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) is a fundamental and challenging optimization problem in smart manufacturing. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches can learn effective scheduling policies through interaction with the environment, their performance often degrades as problem scale and processing-time uncertainty increase. In this paper, we propose DredRL, a dual representation enhanced diffusion-policy reinforcement learning scheduler. DredRL first constructs enhanced dual representations of operations and machines through self- and cross-attention mechanisms to capture both intra-entity and inter-entity dependencies. A diffusion model is further employed as the policy network, generating scheduling decisions through iterative denoising conditioned on the encoded state to enhance policy expressiveness and robustness. Extensive experiments on synthetic, public, large-scale, and stochastic FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that DredRL outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based schedulers across most configurations. Compared with SOTA learning-based methods, DredRL reduces makespan by up to 5.44% on synthetic instances. It also achieves the best learning-based performance on three of four public benchmarks and outperforms the stochasticity-aware algorithms on stochastic benchmarks. These results demonstrate that combining enhanced dual representations with diffusion-based policies provides an effective and robust approach for large scale scalable FJSP scheduling in complicated environment.
  </details>

- **[Entropy-Gated Latent Recursion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16620)**  `arXiv:2606.16620`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Soham Bhattacharjee, Dushyant Singh Chauhan, Salem Lahlou, Martin Takac, Nils Lukas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inference-time scaling has become the dominant lever for improving language-model reasoning, but existing methods often derive rollout diversity from a single source: stochastic token-level sampling. We identify a second, fully \emph{deterministic} and \emph{complementary} axis: the layer span $L$ at which a frozen model's top decoder layers are recursively re-applied at high-uncertainty tokens. Different choices of $L$ produce distinct rollouts that solve different subsets of problems, with no stochasticity. We instantiate this axis through \emph{Entropy-Gated Latent Recursion} (EGLR), a training-free decoding procedure that, combined with temperature sampling, turns a single-axis rollout pool into an $L\!\times\!T$ Cartesian sampling space at almost the same per-rollout cost. Across $8$ instruction-tuned models and $6$ math benchmarks, majority voting over the $L$ axis matches or improves on greedy decoding in nearly all settings and outperforms FLOP-matched beam search, and the gains carry over to a code benchmark. On MATH-500, the oracle ceiling of the joint pool exceeds that of either axis alone by $8$ to $10$ points. In a preliminary study, using the pool for GRPO rollouts with importance-sampling correction improves over standard GRPO. The code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[A Theory on Flow Matching with Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10089)**  `arXiv:2606.10089`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yihan He, Qishuo Yin, Yuan Cao, Jianqing Fan, Han Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we develop theoretical foundation for flow matching with neural-network-parameterized conditional velocity fields. We establish convergence guarantees for gradient descent in the over-parameterized 2-layered ReLU neural network regime. We derive generalization bounds for the conditional velocity-field matching objective. Building on these results, we provide Wasserstein-distance guarantees for the samples generated by the induced flow. Our analysis is based on generalization bound for multi-task representation learning with unbounded losses, which may be of independent interest beyond flow-based generative modeling. These theoretical results are validated through extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world image benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OpenRFM: Democratizing Frontier Predictive Intelligence on Relational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04320)**  `arXiv:2606.04320`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhikai Chen, Junyu Yin, Jialiang Gu, Siheng Xiong, Xiaoze Liu, Ruowang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relational databases hold most enterprise data, and predicting on them has long required hand-written feature joins and a model trained for each task. Relational foundation models (RFMs) remove this per-task engineering by predicting on an unseen database from a few labelled examples in one forward pass. However, the strongest RFMs are closed, and open RFMs pre-trained on synthetic data lag far behind. Prior work attributes this gap to the lack of real pre-training data. We find that much of this gap comes from an incomplete synthetic prior and from a sparse context. Existing synthetic priors lack history-dependent labels, so models pre-trained on them do not learn the temporal and relational dynamics behind real tasks. A bounded context also often misses the evidence that a task needs. We present OpenRFM, an open recipe for building an RFM from synthetic databases alone, whose data generator, model and training code are all open. OpenRFM has three components. (1) A relational adapter, pre-trained on synthetic event streams with history-dependent labels, encodes the sampled context of each entity into a feature row and performs in-context learning along the relation axis, over the history of the entity and its related entities. (2) A frozen tabular foundation model predicts from labelled support rows and performs in-context learning along the batch axis. (3) An optional harness, which an LLM agent builds for each task, rewrites the schema so that the evidence a task needs enters the bounded context. On 32 benchmark tasks, OpenRFM achieves the best mean rank, 3.00 against 3.63 for RelAgent and 5.34 for KumoRFM-2. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PepSpecBench: A Unified Evaluation Benchmark for Peptide Tandem Mass Spectrometry Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01945)**  `arXiv:2605.01945`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiwen Yang, Pan Liu, Yifan Li, Yunhua Zhong, Jun Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tandem mass spectrometry provides a high-throughput framework for identifying and quantifying proteins in complex biological samples. In computational proteomics, predicting peptide MS/MS spectra is a critical task, enabling downstream applications such as large-scale peptide identification and quantification. While deep learning architectures have substantially improved prediction accuracy, three evaluation challenges obscure the true progress of the field. First, inconsistent data preprocessing and incompatible model output spaces hinder fair model comparison. Second, flawed data splitting strategies can permit hidden sequence leakage and inflate reported performance. Third, existing evaluations typically lack systematic assessment of heterogeneous zero-shot domain shift and robustness to collision energy and precursor charge. To address these challenges, we propose PepSpecBench, a unified benchmark for peptide MS/MS spectrum prediction. The main leaderboard scores all six predictors on a shared b/y fragmention intensity subtask, so their outputs are directly comparable. PepSpecBench standardizes data preprocessing across complementary public datasets, enforces a strict backbone-disjoint splitting strategy to prevent exact backbone overlap across partitions, and evaluates diverse architectures within a shared fragment-ion representation space. It further introduces a heterogeneous zero-shot domainshift suite and metadata perturbation probes to assess robustness and whether models respond to fragmentation-relevant experimental conditions. Across six representative models, we uncover changes in model rankings across training sources and external test subsets, along with differences in their responses to collision-energy and precursor-charge perturbations. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AI Alignment via Incentives and Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01643)**  `arXiv:2605.01643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Rohit Agarwal, Joshua Lin, Mark Braverman, Elad Hazan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study AI alignment through the lens of law-and-economics models of deterrence and enforcement, which treats misconduct as a rational response to incentivizes, where actors weigh the expected gain from aligning against the expected loss from being caught. We argue that the same logic arises naturally in foundational language models: an agent trained by RLHF can be modeled as deriving intrinsic reward from producing a persuasive yet incorrect response, while an auditor can be viewed as balancing monitoring (which incurs some cost) against identifying solver misalignment. Therefore, alignment can be viewed as a mechanism design problem, where stronger penalties can deter solver misbehavior but also reduce auditing pressure.Under this framework, we can characterize misalignment as an adversarial post-training problem. While standard post-training optimizes against a stationary reward signal, our mechanism constantly adapts to mitigate reward hacking against a fixed monitor. We formalize this interaction as follows: a principal (e.g., a human) defines a value function over joint alignment outcomes, and a meta-optimizer adaptively chooses granular rewards for the solver and auditor to optimize the principal's value. In particular, we propose a bandit-based meta-optimizer for searching over a discrete set of reward profiles using noisy interaction feedback, and we show from experimental results on Qwen2.5-7B that this mechanism can reduce hallucinations on programming benchmarks by as much as 52\%.
  </details>

- **[The Override Gap: A Magnitude Account of Knowledge Conflict Failure in Hypernetwork-Based Instant LLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23750)**  `arXiv:2604.23750`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuaizhi Cheng, Xiang Shi, Zhiwei Zhang, Mingwei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hypernetwork-based methods such as Doc-to-LoRA internalize a document into an LLM's weights in a single forward pass, but struggle when the document contradicts pretraining knowledge. We study this override gap through the competition between the pretrained answer margin and the shift induced by the generated adapter. On 194 Gemma conflicts, baseline accuracy falls from 65.3% in the weakest-prior quartile to 16.3% in the strongest. We introduce two training-free methods: Selective Layer Boosting (SLB), which scales the adapter at its highest-activity layers, and Conflict-Aware Internalization (CA), which chooses between standard and stronger adaptation using a short base-model probe. Strong boosting raises Gemma deep-conflict accuracy from 46.4% to 71.0%, with conflict gains also observed on Mistral-7B and Qwen-4B. Matched-norm controls show that both update magnitude and allocation matter: SLB benefits from the generated layer and direction structure, while global scaling is competitive at the same increment budget. CA offers a workload-dependent recall-latency tradeoff, reaching 98.4% novel recall on Gemma while retaining the strong path's conflict accuracy. KID-Bench provides 486 questions spanning novel recall, mixed knowledge, and prior-graded conflicts, with answer-level semantic evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Attention Latent Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31154)**  `arXiv:2611.31154`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xuyuan Xiong, Siyu Chen, Mengzhao Chen, Yue Su, Zhuoran Yang, Ping Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  During autoregressive decoding, the deeper representations of the previous token have already been computed, yet shallow layers at the current token cannot read them directly because standard self-attention connects positions only at the same depth. We introduce Attention Latent Feedback (ALF), an architecture that allows every sublayer to attend to the outputs of the deeper layers at the previous position. Training unrolls this cross-position recurrence into two parallel passes, whereas decoding remains single-pass using cached previous-token states. Across mixture-of-experts models with up to 4.0B parameters, ALF lowers held-out loss by 0.022-0.024 nats over the standard residual with about 1.1% decoding FLOP overhead, and consistently outperforms prior final-state feedback across all three scales on both standard and attention residual backbones. On MoE-1.6B, ALF matches a $1.38\times$ larger baseline token budget, and on MoE-4.0B it lifts average accuracy across seventeen downstream benchmarks from 45.1% to 47.0%. Analysis further reveals that intermediate and nearby deeper layers drive most of this benefit, allowing a fixed nearest-window variant to retain 89%-94% of the gain at linear read cost while learned attention widens its margin with scale.
  </details>

- **[AnchorCredit: Output-Anchored Credit Assignment for Structured Generation with Multiple Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30881)**  `arXiv:2611.30881`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gen Li, Pengyang Xie, Xiao Xiao, Chuanzhi Tong, Ziyang Ji, Zhigang Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and related methods typically compute group-relative advantages from response rewards and broadcast the same advantage to every generated token in a response to guide policy updates. However, we identify a systematic problem of cross-component credit misassignment in tasks with multiple verifiable results, such as joint multi-attribute prediction: low rewards for some components can penalize a well-performing component and its associated reasoning. To address this problem, we propose AnchorCredit. The method uses the policy model's attention from output anchors to preceding reasoning sentences to estimate sentence-component associations and assign component-specific advantages to the relevant reasoning, without additional relevance annotations or a separate relevance discriminator. To support the study of credit assignment with multiple verifiable rewards, we construct ProductAttr, a benchmark of 7,200 real product image-text samples. Experiments show that AnchorCredit outperforms existing baselines on this benchmark. Experiments on the public Nutrition5k dataset further support the method's effectiveness in this setting.
  </details>

- **[Potential Games for Self-Neighborhood Channel Mixing in Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30829)**  `arXiv:2611.30829`  `cs.LG`  
  _Marco Lavorini, Davide Bacciu, Giancarlo Bigi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph message passing must balance two competing objectives: incorporating neighborhood signals while preserving node-specific information. We model this trade-off as a game in which nodes choose how to mix their own representations with strict-neighborhood aggregates. For fixed channel representations, task supervision defines a common-payoff game, while local distinctness payoffs induce an exact potential game whose potential is the degree-normalized strict Dirichlet energy. We introduce CG2, which amortizes these choices through a shared six-parameter policy per layer based on local channel statistics and can be trained using either objective. Controlled comparisons show that the shared task-trained policy improves mean performance over fixed mixing on three of five heterophily benchmarks, with significant gains on two, while free per-node actions trained with either objective rarely improve on the same baseline. On the ECHO long-range benchmark, the clearest contribution of the gate appears on eccentricity, where the task-trained policy substantially reduces prediction error.
  </details>

- **[Vasicek Diffusion Bridge for Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30605)**  `arXiv:2611.30605`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junyi Tang, Chang Liu, Kun Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based graph generation models progressively transform Gaussian noise into structured graphs through stochastic dynamics. We observe that the effectiveness of this transformation is fundamentally determined by the evolution of the diffusion trajectory. Existing graph diffusion models often suffer from excessive uncertainty during diffusion and delayed growth of the signal-to-noise ratio, causing useful structural information to emerge only in the later stages of generation. To address this issue, we propose the Vasicek Graph Diffusion (VGD), a graph diffusion process derived from the Vasicek dynamics. The key insight of VGD is data-anchored mean dynamics with a closed-form solution, which analytically establishes a continuous transition from Gaussian noise to the target graph distribution. Unlike conventional graph diffusion processes that rely predominantly on stochastic evolution, VGD introduces a data-anchored drift that continuously steers the latent state toward the target distribution throughout diffusion. As a consequence, VGD maintains a smoothly evolving uncertainty while rapidly increasing the signal-to-noise ratio throughout diffusion, allowing informative graph structures to emerge much earlier during generation. Extensive experiments on multiple graph generation benchmarks demonstrate that VGD outperforms existing diffusion-based graph generative models in terms of generation quality and structural fidelity.
  </details>

- **[Optimistic Online Learning for Coding Agents with Aggregate Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30405)**  `arXiv:2611.30405`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Luan, Hao Qiu, Zhi Zheng, See-Kiong Ng, Zhiyong Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) coding agents operating on Web development platforms can interact with repositories, issue trackers, and continuous integration services to carry out long-horizon software engineering tasks. However, behavioral drift remains a major bottleneck in such multi-turn reasoning and action trajectories. To mitigate this, activation steering has emerged as a promising approach for regularizing agent behaviors by intervening on the LLM's transformer residual streams. Nonetheless, existing methods typically apply a static steering policy across all tasks, overlooking the fact that a single policy is rarely effective across heterogeneous tasks and varying execution turns. This limitation motivates dynamic online selection among candidate steering policies. Yet, doing so in long-horizon agentic settings is challenging: verifiable task-level feedback is only revealed upon task completion, leaving the contributions of individual steering turns unobserved. To address this challenge, we formulate online steering-policy selection as a multi-armed bandit problem with delayed task-level feedback and turn-level predictive hints derived from residual-stream activations. Within this formulation, we introduce Online Optimistic Activation Steering, a meta-framework that uses hints to guide within-task steering policy selection and leverages terminal task-level feedback to correct cumulative reward estimates. Theoretically, against the best fixed policy in hindsight, we prove regret bounds that tighten with increasing hint accuracy and establish a matching lower bound with respect to prediction error and candidate policy count. Empirical evaluations on realistic coding benchmarks demonstrate that our framework outperforms both static steering policies and naive online adaptive baselines.
  </details>

- **[FMSNO: Scaling Surface Neural Operators by Learned Flow Matching Reparametrisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30341)**  `arXiv:2611.30341`  `cs.LG`  
  _Vasilii Liamin, Oleg Bakhteev, Vladimir Vanovskiy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose the Flow Matching Surface Neural Operator (FMSNO), which combines learning a shared map from irregular surface meshes to a regular two-dimensional grid on the sphere and training a neural operator on the regular grid. Constructing a separate map for each new geometry can dominate inference time on large meshes. We initially pretrain the mapping by flow matching using precomputed optimal transport maps as targets and then continue its training using combined geometry and CFD objectives. We apply it to unseen geometries in a single forward pass. The learned flow deforms the surface points, and we project its endpoints onto the sphere. We then splat geometric features onto the spherical grid, apply a neural operator (we tested both Fourier and Spherical Fourier Neural Operators), and interpolate its output back to the surface. For a mesh with $N$ points, evaluating the map costs O(N), and the full prediction costs O(NlogN) with the FNO backbone. We evaluate surface pressure prediction on ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, and drag coefficient prediction on DrivAerNet++. Across the three pressure benchmarks, our accuracy is on par with that of the most accurate baseline. On a surface with one million points, our method achieves a 354x end-to-end inference speedup over that baseline while keeping the same memory usage asymptotics of O(N).
  </details>

- **[Learning Teacher Output Preferences from Final Responses for Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30338)**  `arXiv:2611.30338`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongli Shen, Shaopeng Fu, Qinbo Zhang, Jian Li, Di Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many large language models (LLMs) use chain-of-thought (CoT) to solve complex problems, but hide their CoT traces to prevent reasoning capabilities from being distilled. While recent studies have shown that hidden CoTs can be reconstructed, existing methods typically require training a separate attacker for each target LLM. This paper studies model-agnostic CoT reconstruction, aiming to reconstruct hidden CoT traces from arbitrary target LLMs using only input prompts and visible final responses. We propose Final-Output Preference Learning (FOPL), which trains a single reconstruction model that generalizes to unseen LLMs without target-specific adaptation. To achieve this, the reconstruction model must learn which reasoning patterns match a given final response without knowing the target LLM's identity. We address this using preference optimization, training the reconstruction model to prefer native CoTs over synthetic alternatives for the same question and final response. By learning these preferences from LLMs with accessible CoTs, the reconstruction model learns to distinguish reasoning patterns based on visible responses. Experiments show that reconstructed CoTs capture distinct reasoning patterns across LLM families, including a family unseen during training. Moreover, students trained on reconstructed CoTs match or outperform those trained on native CoTs across several reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Verifiable On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30325)**  `arXiv:2611.30325`  `cs.LG`  
  _Juntong Shi, Stefano Ermon, Jure Leskovec, Minkai Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a powerful approach for improving language-model reasoning, but its supervision is inherently sparse: a sequence-level verifier reward is propagated only through sampled trajectories and provides no direct signal for alternative actions. On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a complementary form of supervision by providing dense teacher feedback over the full next-token distribution, but this signal is not grounded in verified task outcomes. Simply co-optimizing the two objectives does not couple these signals: verifier feedback remains sparse and does not inform the dense teacher supervision. We introduce Verifiable On-Policy Distillation (VOPD), which transfers sparse verifier feedback into dense, verifier-grounded supervision. Our key observation is that the implicit-reward view of OPD places teacher supervision and verifier feedback in a common reward space while retaining a reward signal available for every next-token action. Under this view, VOPD projects verifier preference onto the teacher's implicit-reward direction and Rao--Blackwellizes the resulting signal over the full vocabulary, propagating verifier-grounded supervision to actions that were never sampled. The resulting objective can also be interpreted as adaptively extrapolating the distillation target according to teacher--verifier agreement. Across two teacher--student configurations and eight mathematical reasoning benchmarks, VOPD consistently improves both final performance and learning efficiency over vanilla OPD and other on-policy distillation baselines. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Topology-Prior-Guided Regional Latent Dynamics for Transient Structural Response Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30293)**  `arXiv:2611.30293`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuan Zhang, Mohammad Amin Nabian, Shaotong Fu, Yiding Liu, Chengcheng Chu, Xuelin Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting the transient nonlinear responses of complex engineering structures remains challenging due to large finite element meshes, heterogeneous structural components, and history-dependent mechanical behavior. This study proposes a topology-prior-guided regional latent dynamics (TPLD) surrogate model that combines physically structured regional representations with dynamics-aware latent learning for full-field time-history prediction. Based on mesh topology and engineering priors, structural responses are encoded into spatially coupled regional latent variables through region-aware feature extraction and topology-constrained interactions. An auxiliary one-step prediction task enhances latent-space dynamical predictability by introducing state-transition supervision. A load-conditioned spatiotemporal Mamba model then predicts regional latent trajectories by integrating temporal causal scanning and bidirectional spatial scanning, while a shared coordinate-conditioned decoder reconstructs full-field nodal responses. The framework is evaluated on three finite element datasets: a parametric stiffened panel, a partitioned pressure impact plate, and a Toyota Yaris full-vehicle crash model. The relative L2 errors for the three displacement components range from 4.00% to 8.21% on the stiffened panel and from 1.41% to 12.22% on the approximately 1.49-million-node vehicle model. On the BIW crash benchmark, TPLD achieves a relative L2 position error of 0.620%, representing a 30.7% reduction compared with the best-performing GeoTransolver configuration. Ablation studies demonstrate the advantages of topology-guided regional representations over global latent and learnable soft-slice alternatives, while auxiliary one-step prediction particularly improves the prediction of accumulated plastic strain.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Leakage Distorts the TGB Node Affinity Leaderboard: A Point-in-Time Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30289)**  `arXiv:2611.30289`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuhang Wang, Dongmei Li, Yuze Sun, Xiaolong Qu, Yanxiang Wang, Lei Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Node affinity prediction in the Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB) asks how a node's future interactions will be distributed over other nodes. On this task, methods that reuse past ground-truth labels often outperform temporal graph neural networks, and the state of the art builds on them. Such comparisons assume a point-in-time correct evaluation, in which a model receives only information available at prediction time. Drawing on as-of joins and bitemporal databases, we formalize this requirement by distinguishing when a label must be predicted, its query time, from when it is fully determined, its availability time, and we audit the four TGB node affinity datasets and their evaluation loop with model-agnostic tools. None of the four meets it. On three datasets, each label summarizes a single day about five days after its timestamp, not the documented following week, yet the loop feeds it back right after that timestamp; on the fourth, the loop processes the edges from which each label is computed before predicting it. Under a drop-in leakage-free protocol, every method whose inputs leak loses up to 17% of its NDCG@10, the advantage of past labels over the interaction stream nearly vanishes or reverses, and both the best configuration of the state of the art and the rankings change; a blend of training-free long-memory statistics, with weights fit on validation data, matches or exceeds the state of the art under both protocols. Point-in-time correctness should therefore be built into benchmarks, and our tools carry over to other benchmarks whose labels aggregate event streams over time windows.
  </details>

- **[Composing Independently Distilled Memory and Retrieval Policies for Long-Term Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30230)**  `arXiv:2611.30230`  `cs.LG`  
  _Grigorii Davydenko, Artem Sakhno, Igor Pavlov, Julia Belikova, Andrey Savchenko, Maksim Makarenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-lived Web assistants require effective memory and retrieval mechanisms to access information from past user interactions. While these capabilities can be learned through specialized policies, it remains unclear whether specialists trained independently in different environments can be effectively composed without joint optimization. This paper investigates compositional transfer through policy distillation. Memory-writing and sequential retrieval policies are independently distilled from frontier-model action trajectories into 3B models and combined with a fixed answer generator without downstream training. On a LongMemEval-based benchmark, the distilled composition achieves 73.1% accuracy, compared with 36.2% for a composition of existing RL-trained specialists, and also outperforms the evaluated RL-trained compositions on PersonaMem-v2. Component ablations reveal that improvements in individual policies depend on their composition, highlighting the importance of cross-specialist compatibility. Further task-specific adaptation improves accuracy to 75.2% on LongMemEval through RL and 48.9% on PersonaMem-v2 through supervised distillation. These findings demonstrate the effectiveness of independent specialist distillation for modular multi-agent systems. Source code and datasets of teacher-generated memory-editing and retrieval trajectories are released to facilitate reproducibility and further research.
  </details>

- **[FToken: Factors as Tokens for Dual-Axis Cross-Sectional Return Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30112)**  `arXiv:2611.30112`  `cs.LG`  
  _Juntang Wang, Yihan Wang, Sheng'en Li, Dongmian Zou, Shixin Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On a given trading day, a stock-factor panel describes both how stocks differ across factors and how each factor varies across stocks. Most stock Transformers model relationships among stocks or across time. We introduce FToken, which also models relationships among factors. Its factor branch encodes all stocks' values for one factor as a token, applies self-attention across factors, and maps the result back to one return forecast per stock. A second branch applies attention across stocks in the same panel. A gate combines their forecasts using trailing factor covariance and market volatility. We evaluate both branches on NASDAQ, NYSE, and S&P 500 with the benchmarks' five price features and with 158 factors computed from the same data. On the 158-factor NASDAQ panel, averaging the two forecasts yields an information coefficient (IC) of 0.0421, compared with 0.0392 for the strongest external baseline. The gate raises IC to 0.0443 and gives the highest annualized excess return after costs. The equal-weight ensemble has the higher portfolio information ratio. Both combinations also improve on the strongest external baseline in mean IC on the other two 158-factor panels. On the five-feature NYSE panel, the differences are within the variation across seeds. ```
  </details>

- **[Beyond Fixed Prompt Configuration: Alternating Policy Learning for Adaptive Topology-Based Prompting in Few-Shot Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29950)**  `arXiv:2611.29950`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yizhuo Ma, Yihong Huang, Rongzheng Wang, Jiakai Li, Ke Qin, Shuang Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Prompt Learning (GPL) adapts frozen pre-trained graph models to few-shot graph tasks through a small set of trainable prompts that encode task-specific information. Topology-based prompting instantiates this idea by augmenting an input graph with new prompt nodes and edges, yielding promising performance. However, prompt content and propagation structure are coupled, making it difficult to assess their individual effects and learn compatible configurations. In this paper, we empirically analyze two challenges in learning adaptive policies for topology-based prompting: asymmetric performance effects and coupled configuration selection. We then propose TTPR, a two-stage reinforcement framework that alternately optimizes two policies to generate adaptive configurations, with each policy conditioned on configurations generated by the other fixed policy. These conditional updates separate policy evaluation while preserving configuration compatibility. Specifically, TTPR models an edge-level policy to construct connection patterns and optimizes it with Connection Relative Policy Optimization (CRPO) through group-wise comparisons of complete connection patterns. The other is a node-level policy that generates prompt-node schemes and is optimized with Incremental Node Proximal Policy Optimization (INPPO) through incremental prompt-count decisions and feature generation. Across diverse datasets and pre-training strategies, TTPR ranks first in 8/10 few-shot node classification settings and attains the highest average performance across graph classification benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When Source-Domain Validation Fails to Transfer: A Staged Study of Hyperparameter Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29846)**  `arXiv:2611.29846`  `cs.LG`  
  _Veet Zaveri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperparameters for an unseen domain are often selected using held-out examples from the available source domains. Withholding entire source domains during validation seems to better reflect deployment shift, but does this produce better selections on independent targets? We investigate this question through two complementary studies. A preliminary factorial experiment crosses particle swarm optimization and random search with pooled source validation and a leave-one-source-domain-out mean-minimum fitness on PACS and Multi-Domain Sentiment. The domain-aware fitness does not provide a consistent target advantage. We then separate selector development from confirmation using a shared pool of 64 configurations per task across four image benchmarks. Complete target-performance landscapes on VLCS and Office-Home inform one rule: retain the top 16 configurations by pooled source validation, then select the largest leave-one-source-domain-out mean-minus-standard-deviation score. The rule is frozen before evaluation on TerraIncognita and SVIRO. Across their 14 target domains, its equal-dataset-weighted balanced-accuracy difference from pooled validation is +0.097 percentage points (95% bootstrap interval [-0.685, +0.960]), with five wins and nine losses. Exploratory analyses reveal substantial variation across datasets, target domains, and training seeds. These results do not establish equivalence or a general failure of domain-aware validation. They show that the evaluated source-domain criteria did not establish reliable target gains under the tested protocols, and motivate assessing selection rules on datasets independent of those used to develop them.
  </details>

- **[What Should World Models Plan Over? Learning Geometry-Aligned Representations of Body Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29759)**  `arXiv:2611.29759`  `cs.LG`  
  _Renhao Zhang, Abhranil Chandra, Bruno Castro da Silva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce MotionPort, which learns low-dimensional latent representations of body motion for accurate dynamics prediction and effective long-horizon planning. Existing world-model planners often search over action representations learned for prediction, yet we show that predictive accuracy alone does not ensure that optimization can discover successful trajectories. MotionPort addresses this mismatch by representing body trajectories rather than action chunks and organizing the search space by motion similarity, helping predicted improvements translate into successful execution. Without fine-tuning state encoders, MotionPort raises average goal-reaching success from 37.9% to 87.8% across three backbones and seven settings in four domains. On the evaluated AntMaze-OGBench tasks, it achieves 72% success, compared with 4% for the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[CrysLDM: Latent Diffusion Model for Crystal Material Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29675)**  `arXiv:2611.29675`  `cs.LG`  
  _Subhojyoti Khastagir, Kishalay Das, Pawan Goyal, Seung-Cheol Lee, Satadeep Bhattacharjee, Niloy Ganguly_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating new crystal materials with desirable chemical properties has long been a challenging task. Existing diffusion models operate in feature space, requiring complex diffusion architectures to model the joint distribution of atom types, coordinates, and lattice structures. This complexity increases the number of diffusion steps, leading to higher training and sampling costs. In this work, we aim to generate novel crystal materials within a time- and resource-constrained setup, where existing models are not well-suited. To address this, we propose CrysLDM, a novel latent diffusion model for 3D crystal materials, which integrates a variational autoencoder and a diffusion model. The VAE encoder maps 3D crystal structures into a latent space, where the diffusion model operates. Since CrysLDM leverages a smooth, lower-dimensional latent space, it simplifies the generative process and accelerates both training and inference. Through extensive experiments on benchmark datasets and tasks, we show that CrysLDM generates stable and valid materials with quality comparable to state-of-the-art methods, while being significantly more efficient in terms of computational resources and time.
  </details>

- **[Exact on Paper: Which LLM Serving Shortcuts Flip Graded Answers, Measured Against the Noise of Reordering the Same Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29663)**  `arXiv:2611.29663`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sharven Rane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Teams enable serving features such as prefix caching, chunked prefill and speculative decoding after checking that benchmark accuracy is unchanged, because the engine documents them as exact. Accuracy is an aggregate, so a feature can hold it while flipping which requests are answered correctly. We serve one 4B instruction model in vLLM under nine single feature configurations and five replicas of a reference configuration, grade 3,380 requests from five benchmarks in every run, and compare each candidate with a restart floor (fresh process) and a scheduling floor (same requests in a different order). With default kernels every configuration keeps accuracy within one point, and the seven exact features flip the graded outcome of 1.47% (95% CI 1.13 to 1.85) to 1.78% (1.39 to 2.20) of requests, none above the scheduling floor of 1.72%, while the FP8 KV cache flips 3.36% (2.80 to 3.96), as many as FP8 weights. With batch invariant kernels all seven exact features, speculative decoding included, reproduce the reference text on every request, while the FP8 KV cache still flips 3.91% (3.28 to 4.53) of graded outcomes. A gate that compares against a single replica pair rejected an unchanged configuration, so we recommend certifying a serving change under batch invariant kernels, where the floor is zero, or against a scheduling floor measured from at least three replicas.
  </details>

- **[BenchMint: From Real-World Tasks to Executable Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29367)**  `arXiv:2611.29367`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zining Huang, Zuo Wang, Mengyao Zhang, Yilan Piao, Zhe Wang, Shuohan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) need evaluations of everyday reasoning and tool use, such as planning itineraries under user constraints and grounding numerical answers in evidence. We introduce BenchMint, an automated benchmark-construction workflow, and Daily-Life, its collection of 100 selected tasks across 11 real-world domains. BenchMint jointly constructs tasks and scoring criteria from the same source documents. Language-model judges assess possible answers against these criteria using tool traces and private evidence. We report 26 model/harness configurations, including GPT, Claude and Gemini families; 17 have complete domain profiles. These are descriptive configuration results, not a controlled model ranking. In one selected travel task, we corrected the reference total from 320 to 380 minutes while keeping both answers unchanged. The incorrect answer's arithmetic verdict switched from pass to fail; the correct answer's switched from fail to pass. With one ballot per cell, this exposes a reference error without establishing repeatability or collection-wide validity. Incomplete construction comparisons establish no general advantage of joint authoring or feedback-guided revision.
  </details>

- **[S$^2$LMR: Supervised Sequential Long-Context Multi-Step Retriever](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28995)**  `arXiv:2611.28995`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qiang Hu, Kaiyi Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step retrieval over long contexts is a key capability for retrieval-augmented generation, but strong existing approaches are costly and complex to train. Supervised retrievers are far simpler and cheaper, yet they fall well behind in this setting. We show that this gap stems not from supervised training itself, but from three misalignments between training and inference: easy training negatives, noisy retrieval prefixes, and position-agnostic scoring. Guided by this analysis, we introduce S$^2$LMR, a simple yet effective supervised sequential retriever that models multi-step retrieval as the evolution of an explicit textual state encoded by a single shared Transformer. On long-context reasoning, multi-hop QA, and needle-in-a-haystack benchmarks with context lengths ranging from 4K to 10M tokens, S$^2$LMR achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art methods, substantially outperforming prior supervised retrievers. Notably, it requires only $\sim$2 hours of training on a single A6000 for contexts of up to 10M tokens, while remaining robust across a wide range of hyperparameter settings.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Model Merging with Multi-Layer Aggregated Routing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28855)**  `arXiv:2611.28855`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxi Li, Jiacong Hu, Jie Song, Mingli Song, Yuanyu Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic model merging aims to retain the efficiency of a unified model while better preserving task-specific expertise through input-dependent routing at both the task and head levels. However, even the routing components of the state-of-the-art approach remain limited: the task routing is highly sensitive to the choice of routing layer, whereas the head routing relies on simple max-logit competition. To address these limitations, we propose multi-layer aggregated routing evidence (MATE) as a unified mechanism for improving both task and head routing. Specifically, we first introduce a metric to quantify task routing separability across different layers. Empirical analysis of this metric reveals that different layers provide complementary evidence for identifying the tasks relevant to an input. Motivated by this observation, MATE selects multiple informative layers according to their routing separability and aggregates their layer-wise routing scores to produce a more robust signal that can be directly used for task selection. Moreover, we observe that this signal also encodes useful information about the suitability of the corresponding classification heads. This motivates us to further combine the MATE signal with the max-logit score to guide classification head selection. Extensive experiments on vision benchmarks demonstrate that MATE consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[AgriManager: A Framework and Benchmark for LLM-RL Generalization in Agricultural Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28844)**  `arXiv:2611.28844`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chi Gui, Junwen Zheng, Ritwik Nigam, Qianlan Yang, Meagan Lang, Vardhan Dongre, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agricultural management is heterogeneous along two axes. The first is distributional shift within a fixed task interface: weather, crops, and prices vary across years and locations while sensors, actions, and objective stay fixed. The second is structural shift across interfaces: different farms expose different observed variables, available actions, and management objectives. We formalize the second axis as schema shift: variation in the policy-facing schema $\Sigma = (\Sigma_O, \Sigma_A, \Sigma_R)$ between training and deployment. We test whether LLM-RL generalizes across both axes with AgriManager, to our knowledge the first framework that trains LLM policies with RL across multiple process-based crop simulators, unifying DSSAT, WOFOST, and Cycles. We design a three-tier generalization ladder: Tier 1 within-schema distributional shift, Tier 2 single-axis schema shift along $\Sigma_O / \Sigma_A / \Sigma_R$, Tier 3 compositional schema shift across simulators, all scored on a frozen held-out test suite with three training seeds per method. Within fixed schemas, Qwen3-4B trained by RL with explicit reasoning exceeds neural-network PPO trained with the same interaction budget; without reasoning, training stalls near no-action where the reward penalizes input use. Under single-axis schema shift, $\Sigma_O$ transfer needs decision-critical state, $\Sigma_A$ results are consistent with joint action-menu coverage, and with reasoning a policy trained on yield alone transfers to unseen objectives. Under compositional schema shift, single-source training mostly hurts zero-shot transfer across simulators, and a unified policy trained only on other combinations of the same components recovers about 86% of the gain of a specialist trained on the target combination on DSSAT and stays below the specialists on the WOFOST and Cycles profit targets. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CLAIR: Consistent LLM Adaptation via Index-free Reprogramming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28721)**  `arXiv:2611.28721`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dongkuk Kim, Jungkyu Shin, Yoonhyuk Choi, Jong Wook Kim, Beakcheol Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series forecasting with pretrained large language models (LLMs) uses contextualized patch representations, raising the question of how to align them. Matching patches at the same temporal position is natural, but self-attention allows each patch state to incorporate information from elsewhere in the input window, suggesting that useful correspondences can extend across positions. This flexibility, however, does not necessarily translate into consistent forecasts: similar patch states may play different roles when used in their original temporal order. To address both issues, we propose CLAIR (Consistent LLM Adaptation via Index-free Reprogramming), a teacher-guided framework that combines patch-level transport alignment (PTA) and forecast-level consistency (FC). A clean-view exponential moving average (EMA) teacher guides a mildly perturbed student, with PTA establishing soft, representation-dependent correspondences and FC encouraging agreement at corresponding future steps. The forecasting head retains the original patch order. We test this combination through controlled comparisons: while PTA and index-wise alignment perform similarly without FC, PTA+FC under EMA supervision reduces horizon-averaged mean squared error (MSE) by 2.1% on ETTh1 and 2.6% on ETTm1 relative to Index+FC. The same preference for PTA+FC emerges in comparisons with alternative matching strategies on Weather and ETTh2. Beyond these controls, CLAIR achieves the lowest horizon-averaged MSE on five of six long-term benchmarks and the lowest aggregate overall weighted average (OWA) on M4 among evaluated methods, with competitive few-shot performance. As the teacher and transport are used only during training, inference requires only the student.
  </details>

- **[Peel, Don't Whack: Partial Coverage, Imperfect Validators, and Intervention Based Shortcut Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28701)**  `arXiv:2611.28701`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Alavi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Without group labels, shortcut mitigation increasingly relies on interventions : label-preserving edits, such as background or watermark replacement, that are proposed automatically and accepted by a validator. Invariance to a complete family of valid edits yields robustness, but in practice coverage is partial and validators err.We analyze both in a stylized linear model. For the population logistic solution and the max-margin limit of gradient descent, the decision on every shortcut-flip group follows one additive rule. Hence worst-group accuracy never decreases with coverage, while individual groups can provably degrade, a mechanism for the Whac-a-Mole effect; full coverage is not always needed. In the logistic regime the validator's false-accept rate must stay below a closed-form tolerance that grows with coverage. With noiseless training data, one false accept can make the max-margin solution ignore the core. In this model, sensitivity-based discovery removes shortcuts in decreasing salience. We also give a model-agnostic certificate, valid under content-independent validation, whose validator-error term comes from an audit.Simulations match the theory, and deep networks on synthetic and real images show the predicted Whac-a-Mole signs, monotone worst-group accuracy and validator fragility. The resulting procedure, Peel (Propose-Edit-Evaluate-Learn), can propose candidate edits from the model's own mistakes. Given an oracle library of the benchmarks' intervention operators, it selects the right ones and beats group-label methods on Waterbirds and UrbanCars. With fully generated edits, run end to end from three discovery seeds, it improves on empirical risk minimization (ERM) on five of six benchmarks but trails exact edits.
  </details>

- **[TabK: Amortized Bayesian Estimation of the Number of Clusters in Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28335)**  `arXiv:2611.28335`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammadreza Bakhtyari, Bogdan Mazoure, Renato Cordeiro de Amorim, Guillaume Rabusseau, Vladimir Makarenkov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the number of clusters, $k$, is a fundamental prerequisite for most clustering algorithms. Traditionally, this estimation has relied on computationally expensive grid searches over internal cluster validity indices (CVIs) or adaptive clustering algorithms, both constrained by specific statistical assumptions. We introduce TabK, a zero-shot, permutation-invariant transformer architecture that frames the estimation of $k$ as an amortized Bayesian inference problem for tabular data. TabK leverages a novel Quantile Feature Encoder (QFE) to extract position-free, content-derived representations from empirical marginal distributions. By learning a generalized structural bias over a diverse synthetic prior, TabK estimates the number of clusters in a single forward pass per ensemble member. Evaluated across 50 diverse real-world benchmarks, TabK outperforms all competing methods, achieving a mean absolute error (MAE) value of 1.04 compared to 2.14 for the closest competitor. TabK also achieves the lowest average inference time, producing estimates in less than one second on average and running $2\times$ faster than TabClustPFN and $30\times$ faster than traditional CVIs such as Silhouette and Calinski-Harabasz. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Beckmann Maps Improve One-step Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28286)**  `arXiv:2611.28286`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ulrik Unneberg, Shiyi Wang, Lee Cheuk-Kit, Felix Berg, Boris Kozinsky, Yilun Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Beckmann Transport Models (BTM) learn one-step generative maps end-to-end from data, without a teacher, a distillation stage, or pretrained features. We introduce Scalable Beckmann Maps (SBM), which bring to BTM the two ingredients behind the quality of modern multistep generators: classifier-free guidance (CFG) and a training recipe that scales, while keeping sampling at a single network evaluation. CFG combines conditional and unconditional velocity or score predictions, which a Beckmann map does not have. Our guidance rests on one observation: the BTM current is a positive multiple of an autonomous denoising field, to which CFG applies as it does to a velocity. With exact denoisers this gives guided BTM. In practice we substitute the map's own conditional and null predictions for the denoisers, which requires no additional network and introduces a bias that we characterize. We show that the guided update is consistent with a guided conservation equation, and that guidance can move the boundaries between basins, sending more noise to the modes a condition favors. Trained from scratch in the latent of a representation autoencoder, SBM reaches a one-step FID of 1.6 on ImageNet 256x256 without a teacher or a feature loss, outperforming the Improved-MeanFlow baseline with 3.2x fewer FLOPs. In text-to-image generation it reaches a GenEval score of 0.86 with one network evaluation. As a world model, where we train without guidance, the recipe alone outperforms DriftWorld on all five metrics of two robot-manipulation benchmarks, with 1.9-2.8x less training compute and no pretrained auxiliary feature encoder.
  </details>

- **[RefineKV: Query-Adaptive KV Cache Compression via Attention-Error Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28171)**  `arXiv:2611.28171`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kewei Lian, Jiayi Zhai, Anji Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeatedly accessing the full key--value (KV) cache is a major bottleneck in long-context language model decoding. We find that small query-specific subsets can accurately approximate full attention, yet their union covers nearly the entire cache, motivating query-adaptive access for the KV cache. A natural approach is to split the KV cache into clusters, using compact representatives for clusters that incur negligible error while expanding the rest to their original KV entries. We derive a selection criterion from an upper bound on attention-output error, which reveals two complementary factors governing the error: importance measures the impact of a cluster, while distortion measures how faithfully the cluster can be represented. Based on this criterion, we propose RefineKV, a decoding-time query-adaptive KV compression method. Experiments on two models and three benchmarks demonstrate that RefineKV retains near-full-attention quality while achieving up to 7.6x speedup in the attention phase and 1.66x faster decoding, establishing a new quality-latency Pareto frontier.
  </details>

- **[Lift Out, Kick In: Decoupling Event Effects for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27191)**  `arXiv:2611.27191`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunying Zhu, Zichun Cao, Siwei Sun, Xiaocen Guo, Shengsheng Lin, Jing Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world time series are often shaped by exogenous events whose effects are superimposed on the system's underlying temporal dynamics. Historical observations therefore entangle persistent dynamics with event-induced deviations, making it difficult to determine which patterns should be extrapolated and which should be attributed to specific event contexts. Existing event-aware forecasting methods can incorporate event information or model event-related variation, but typically treat historical event attribution and future event generation separately. We propose Loki (Lift Out, Kick In), a general event-aware forecasting framework built on a shared Event Response Function (ERF). The ERF models event effects according to event type and relative temporal position and is shared across the historical and forecast windows. Loki lifts out estimated event-induced deviations from historical observations before forecasting, then uses the same ERF to kick in responses to known future events. This formulation separates event-response modeling from temporal forecasting while coupling historical attribution and future generation through a shared mechanism. Across five forecasting benchmarks, Loki achieves the best standardized MAE on all five datasets, reducing error by approximately 2.2%-11.7% over the strongest non-Loki baseline. Ablations and controlled analyses further support the roles of Lift Out, Kick In, shared response modeling, and compatibility across forecasting backbones.
  </details>

- **[RSIBench-Infra: Can Agents Optimize Training and Inference Infrastructure at Production Scale?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24395)**  `arXiv:2611.24395`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hanlin Wu, Yifei Wang, Zhicheng Cai, Wenxuan Tan, Jiahao Li, Huan-ang Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving the efficiency of training and inference infrastructure is a practical component of recursive self-improvement. Existing benchmarks for agent-driven optimization largely focus on individual software layers such as kernel, leaving unclear whether agents can deliver end-to-end performance gains under production workloads. We introduce RSIBench-Infra, a benchmark of 20 training and inference tasks spanning diverse model families and modalities, with deployments of up to 32 accelerators on NVIDIA GPUs and Ascend NPUs. Agents start from runnable baselines derived from official recipes and can modify the software stack across engine, kernel, and instruction levels. An OCI image-layer submission protocol captures these changes for reproducible deployment, while independent evaluation measures end-to-end performance under task-specific quality constraints. Across all task results, 66.2% exceed $1.10\times$ validated speedup. These results demonstrate that current agents can improve production-scale training and inference performance, but identifying effective optimization strategies and consistently translating local improvements into end-to-end gains remain challenging.
  </details>

- **[Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation: Evaluating Hierarchical Policies from Non-Hierarchical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10543)**  `arXiv:2611.10543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunfu Deng, Josiah P. Hanna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the expected return of a target policy from previously collected data without additional environment interaction. While OPE methods for flat Markovian policies are well studied, little work has addressed evaluating hierarchical policies in which a high-level policy selects temporally extended options that generate primitive actions until a termination condition is met. The primary existing approach applies per-decision importance sampling at the option level, but requires option-annotated trajectories and exhibits variance that still grows with the horizon. For non-hierarchical policies, fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) typically achieves lower error than importance sampling by leveraging the classic Bellman equation for policy evaluation; however, a naive extension of FQE to hierarchical policies results in a biased policy value estimate. In this paper, we introduce Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation (IO-FQE), which estimates a value function in the augmented state-option space; this approach enables OPE of hierarchical policies even when we lack annotations of what option was run in the data. We develop two continuous-action instantiations of IO-FQE and show on long-horizon hierarchical continuous control tasks (AntMaze navigation and OGBench Puzzle manipulation) that IO-FQE eliminates the bias incurred by a naive application of FQE to hierarchical policies and substantially lowers mean squared-error compared to option-level importance sampling.
  </details>

- **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571)**  `arXiv:2611.04571`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea Cenzato, Lukas Karch, Andre Luckow, Yoran Pichon, Francesco Pasqua, Abouzar Ghasemi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Feature Selection Using Local Intrinsic Dimensionality -- Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40736)**  `arXiv:2610.40736`  `cs.LG`  
  _Oussama Chelly, Michael E. Houle, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Muhammad Rajabinasab, Arthur Zimek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High dimensionality degrades both the efficiency and the effectiveness of learning algorithms, and in many applications no class labels are available to guide dimensionality reduction. This paper proposes LIDFS, an unsupervised feature selection method based on Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID). The method builds on the formal equivalence between LID and the indiscriminability of distance measures. The data matrix is transposed so that each feature is treated as a point, and the LID of each feature is estimated by maximum likelihood from the distances to its $k$ nearest neighboring features. Features with lower LID exhibit greater local discriminability and receive higher importance scores. A refinement step then retains one representative per feature neighborhood and demotes locally redundant features. The result is a complete ranking from which any number of features can be selected without rerunning the procedure. LIDFS does not use any information about labels or the number of classes, and runs in $\mathcal{O}(d^2 n)$ time for $n$ instances and $d$ features. LIDFS is compared with classic and state-of-the-art methods and a random baseline on 23 high-dimensional datasets, the classic benchmark collection. The evaluation uses classification accuracy and AUC with five downstream classifiers, as well as clustering accuracy and normalized mutual information, over both full and aggressive selection ranges. LIDFS ranks first in the supervised evaluations and first or tied second in the clustering-based evaluations. It belongs to the top statistical group together with methods that require the number of classes, and it runs faster than its closest competitor. Several recent methods, by contrast, fail to outperform the random baseline. A sensitivity analysis shows that small neighborhood sizes yield the best results.
  </details>

- **[LatentWeave: Test-Time Latent Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39557)**  `arXiv:2610.39557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fanci Meng, Kun Wang, Guibin Zhang, Xinlei Yu, Jiaming Zhang, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in test-time optimization have shown that the multimodal reasoning performance of large multimodal language models (MLLMs) can be improved during inference without modifying backbone model parameters. However, existing approaches primarily operate at the prompt or output level, leaving the latent reasoning process largely uncontrolled and insufficient to prevent error accumulation during autoregressive generation. To address this limitation, we propose LatentWeave, a test-time framework that treats multimodal reasoning as a controllable latent trajectory. LatentWeave performs structured Latent Weaving, progressively refining intermediate hidden states segment-wise to correct emerging deviations, and further introduces Reasoning Anchors via lightweight WEAVE tokens to stabilize reasoning structure and guide answer-oriented generation. These mechanisms are designed to enable fine-grained, step-aware intervention in the latent reasoning process while keeping the main MLLM backbone parameters fixed. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks report improvements over the evaluated baselines; the contribution of latent optimization, anchor training, and additional test-time computation is analyzed separately in the ablation and efficiency studies.
  </details>

- **[ALDER: Discovering the Laws of a World by Acting in It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33728)**  `arXiv:2609.33728`  `cs.LG`  
  _Teng Cao, Yu Deng, Quentin Delfosse, Kristian Kersting_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable world models should not only predict future states but express how actions change the world in an explicit, transparent and testable form, such as equations. Yet methods that rely on a fixed set of trajectories cannot distinguish equally good competing hypotheses, while searches over a fixed set of predefined candidates cannot discover equations outside the initial hypothesis space. We introduce ALDER (Action-guided Law Discovery, Evaluation, and Revision), a method that actively proposes novel experiments to test and revise models. Specifically, ALDER proposes parametric equations; a numerical optimizer fits their coefficients; an independent verifier tests these candidates on held-out data. To distinguish between competing valid hypotheses, a cost- and safety-aware selector queries interventions, in the form of novel experiments. The resulting counterexamples update the evidence ledger and guide the next structural revision, while incompatible laws are discarded. Across an in-house benchmark, ODE equation discovery tasks, and robotic experiments, ALDER discovers laws beyond its initial formula set, repairs failed model proposals, distinguishes fixed candidate models with fewer interactions, and improves out-of-distribution prediction. Furthermore, given a current state and a target, ALDER selects control actions by solving the inverse problem defined by its validated world model. Together, these results show that explicit equation-based world models can be tested and revised through interaction, then naturally used to guide goal-directed control.
  </details>

- **[Can Circuit Alignment Predict OOD Generalization?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31996)**  `arXiv:2609.31996`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ayan Banerjee, Abhra Chaudhuri, Josep Llados, Umapada Pal, Anjan Dutta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization be predicted from a trained model's weights alone, without any target-domain data? Existing representational similarity metrics (CKA, SVCCA, RSA) compare activations rather than forecast generalization. We show they are provably insensitive to structural rerouting in the computational graph, the very change distribution shift induces. We close this gap with the Circuit Alignment Score (CAS), which compares class-specific circuits across domains via graph kernels, decomposed into same-class coherence and cross-class confusion. Casting CAS as a Lebesgue integral over the domain distribution, we prove its Monte Carlo estimate recovers the ground-truth ranking of learners by OOD accuracy, with pairwise inversion error vanishing at rate $O(1/M)$, where $M$ is the number of sampled domains. Across $48$ learners on PACS, CAS attains $0.88$ rank correlation with OOD accuracy, versus $0.58$ (CKA), $0.23$ (SVCCA), and $0.14$ (RSA), with similar trends on other benchmarks and even against data-dependent methods, making it the first provably consistent predictor of distributional robustness requiring neither target-domain data nor labels. The code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SAVOR: Score Once, Learn Deeper in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24141)**  `arXiv:2609.24141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Keye Zheng, Hanyu Li, Zhan Cheng, Yuan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own samples with token-level scores from a stronger teacher, and it effectively transfers reasoning ability to small language models. Each training step pays for student rollouts and teacher scoring, yet common OPD configurations make a single pass over the scored batch and then discard it. We observe that reusing the batch alone is not enough, because no single fixed teacher target suits every batch. In our runs, a fixed $\alpha$ of 0.3 exceeds a KL envelope at every training step, while 0.2 leaves over a quarter of it unused. We propose SAVOR, which selects for each batch the strongest teacher target inside the KL envelope, freezes the scored batch, and reuses it for a fixed number of student updates without further teacher calls. On five math reasoning benchmarks with a 1.7B student and a 30B-A3B teacher, SAVOR raises macro accuracy over TOP-D from 15.41 to 17.78 with two passes and to 19.36 with three passes at about the same teacher budget. With three passes, SAVOR nearly matches the final accuracy of TOP-D after one third of training while using 67.2% fewer teacher-scored tokens. These results suggest budgeting reuse depth explicitly in OPD.
  </details>

- **[TiRex-2: Generalizing TiRex to Multivariate Data and Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01204)**  `arXiv:2607.01204`  `cs.LG`  
  _Patrick Podest, Marco Pichler, Elias Bürger, Levente Zólyomi, Bernhard Voggenberger, Wilhelm Berghammer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce TiRex-2, a recurrent xLSTM-based time series foundation model that generalizes the univariate TiRex to multivariate forecasting with both past and future covariates. Real-world forecasting is inherently sequential: observations arrive continuously, variables evolve jointly, and a subset of covariates is known ahead of time. Existing Transformer-based time series foundation models capture cross-variate dependencies, but the cost of ingesting each new observation grows at least linearly with context length, even with KV caching. TiRex-2 addresses these limitations with a memory-centric recurrent design built around an asymmetric group-attention variate mixer: paired with a bidirectional time mixer, it integrates future-known covariates while keeping target variables strictly causal. To our knowledge no other time series foundation model does both, and it is what makes streaming at constant per-patch cost possible. For scalable multivariate pretraining, we propose a synthetic coupling pipeline that composes multivariate samples on the fly from large univariate corpora. On GIFT-Eval, TiRex-2 improves on the zero-shot Chronos-2-Synth on all four statistics, and on Chronos-2 under a matched corpus. On fev-bench Chronos-2 stays ahead on skill score at $2.2\times$ the mean active parameters. TiRex-2 uses 38.4M active parameters in univariate mode, plus 44.1M for multivariate forecasting. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>
