- **[ZEBRA: Zero-shot Budgeted Resource Allocation for LLM Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20485)**  `arXiv:2605.20485`  `cs.LG`  
  _May Hamri, Inbal Talgam-Cohen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As autonomous agents increasingly execute end-to-end tasks under fixed monetary budgets, the pressing open question shifts from whether the budget is respected, to how to spend it effectively. Existing budget-aware methods typically control reasoning step-by-step within a single agent, or learn resource allocation policies via RL. None address how to split a budget across the composing phases of a multi-agent pipeline at inference time. We propose ZEBRA, a zero-shot framework that reduces multi-phase budget allocation to a continuous nonlinear knapsack problem: an LLM controller estimates per-phase utility curves, and a water-filling search on the Lagrange multiplier returns the per-phase split. Additive and multiplicative aggregations are unified under the same solver. On a 150-task APPS coding benchmark, both ZEBRA variants outperform LLM-direct (budget allocation directly by an LLM) on every aggregate metric. At a budget of $\alpha = 0.5$ of the unconstrained spend, ZEBRA recovers 94.4% of unconstrained quality, versus 88.1% for LLM-direct. The advantage is statistically significant and transfers beyond coding: on a 3-phase HotpotQA pipeline, ZEBRA beats LLM-direct by 9.5pp, with allocations empirically robust to curve-estimation noise. On HotpotQA, ZEBRA arrives at a different budget split (near-balanced) compared to the APPS one (skewed towards a refinement phase), showing adaptation to the pipeline structure. More broadly, we show that lightweight algorithmic guidance at inference time can improve the economic behavior of autonomous multi-agent systems.
  </details>

- **[ModelLens: Finding the Best for Your Task from Myriads of Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07075)**  `arXiv:2605.07075`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rui Cai, Wenjie Jacky Mo, Xiaofei Wen, Qiyao Ma, Wenhui Zhu, Xiwen Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The open-source model ecosystem now contains hundreds of thousands of pretrained models, yet picking the best model for a new dataset is increasingly infeasible: new models and unbenchmarked datasets emerge continuously, leaving practitioners with no prior records on either side. Existing approaches handle only fragments of this in-the-wild setting: AutoML and transferability estimation select models from small predefined pools or require expensive per-model forward passes on the target dataset, while model routing presupposes a given candidate pool. We introduce ModelLens, a unified framework for model recommendation in the wild. Our key insight is that public leaderboard interactions, though scattered and noisy, collectively trace out an implicit atlas of model capabilities across heterogeneous evaluation settings, a signal rich enough to learn from directly. By learning a performance-aware latent space over model--dataset--metric tuples, ModelLens ranks unseen models on unseen datasets without running candidates on the target dataset. On a new benchmark of 1.62M evaluation records spanning 47K models and 9.6K datasets, ModelLens surpasses baselines that either rely on metadata alone or require running each candidate on the target dataset. Its recommended Top-K pools further improve multiple representative routing methods by up to 81% relative EM gain across diverse QA benchmarks. Case studies on recently released benchmarks further confirm generalization to both text and vision-language tasks.
  </details>

- **[Visual Latents Know More Than They Say: Unsilencing Latent Reasoning in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02735)**  `arXiv:2605.02735`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Zhang, Qiqi Tao, Jiawei Du, Moyun Liu, Joey Tianyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous latent-space reasoning offers a compact alternative to textual chain-of-thought for multimodal models, enabling high-dimensional visual evidence to be integrated without explicit reasoning tokens. However, we identify a previously overlooked optimization pathology in existing latent visual reasoning methods: although visual latents become semantically enriched during training, their contribution to final answer prediction is systematically suppressed. Within the shared parameter space, the autoregressive objective favors shortcut reliance on direct visual input, driving latent tokens toward transition-like states rather than informative reasoning content. We term this phenomenon Silenced Visual Latents. To address it, we disentangle the two conflicting objectives by directly optimizing the latent reasoning at inference time, keeping backbone parameters frozen. In Stage I, visual latents are warmed up via query-guided contrastive latent--visual alignment, improving semantic quality while preventing latent collapse. In Stage II, the latent reasoning is further optimized via a confidence-progression reward, which incentivizes predicted token distributions along the latent span to become progressively more concentrated, routing predictions through the latent reasoning rather than bypassing it. Experiments across eight benchmarks and four model backbones show that inference-time latent optimization, without any parameter updates, effectively unleashes the suppressed reasoning capacity of visual latents.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Computational Barrier: Provably Efficient Actor-Critic for Low-Rank MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01242)**  `arXiv:2605.01242`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruiquan Huang, Donghao Li, Yingbin Liang, Jing Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is a fundamental framework for sequential decision-making, in which an agent learns an optimal policy through interactions with an unknown environment. In settings with function approximation, many existing RL algorithms achieve favorable sample complexity, but often rely on computationally intractable oracles. In this paper, we use supervised learning as a computational proxy to establish a clear hierarchy of commonly adopted RL oracles under low-rank Markov Decision Processes (MDPs). This hierarchy shows that policy evaluation is the most computationally efficient oracle, provided that supervised learning can be efficiently solved. Motivated by this observation, we propose a novel optimistic actor-critic algorithm that relies solely on a truncated policy evaluation oracle. We prove that our algorithm outperforms the existing sample complexity guarantees for low-rank MDPs, while avoiding computationally expensive planning or optimization oracles commonly assumed in prior works. We further demonstrate that approximately low-rank MDPs capture a broad class of real world environments. Finally, we evaluate a practical variant of our algorithm with experiments on several standard Gym benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Informed Machine Learning with Knowledge Landmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00256)**  `arXiv:2604.00256`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chuyi Dai, Witold Pedrycz, Suping Xu, Ding Liu, Lin Shang, Xianmin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Informed Machine Learning (ML) aims to improve prediction by using both data and prior knowledge. In scientific and engineering applications, accurate measurements may occupy only a small operating region of the input space, whereas available knowledge is global, coarse, and not expressed as equations. Existing physics-informed methods embed governing equations, residuals, simulators, operators, or mathematically specified constraints. However, they do not directly address learning from localized numeric data together with non-equational knowledge. We develop a Knowledge-Data (KD) ML framework for this setting. The KD-model represents coarse global knowledge as input-output information granules, termed knowledge landmarks. The method uses a landmark-guided objective that converts these landmarks into differentiable compatibility terms and balances them against local data fitting through a trade-off parameter $\lambda$. Experiments on two physics-based benchmarks show that the KD-model predicts more accurately across the full domain than a model trained on the local data alone. For most observation windows, the gain holds over a broad range of $\lambda$. The advantage persists when the sample size or label noise changes, with $\lambda$ re-selected without full-domain labels. These results show that approximate expert knowledge can guide training when dense labels or explicit equations are unavailable.
  </details>

- **[Actor-Accelerated Policy Dual Averaging for Reinforcement Learning in Continuous Action Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10199)**  `arXiv:2603.10199`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ji Gao, Caleb Ju, Guanghui Lan, Zhaohui Tong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Policy Dual Averaging (PDA) offers a principled Policy Mirror Descent (PMD) framework that more naturally admits value function approximation than standard PMD, enabling the use of approximate advantage (or Q-) functions while retaining strong convergence guarantees. However, applying PDA in continuous state and action spaces remains computationally challenging, since action selection involves solving an optimization subproblem at each decision step. In this paper, we propose \textit{actor-accelerated PDA} (AA-PDA), which uses a learned policy network to approximate the solution of the optimization subproblems, significantly reducing runtime. We provide a theoretical analysis that quantifies how actor approximation error impacts the convergence of PDA under suitable assumptions. We then evaluate its performance on several benchmarks in robotics, control, and operations research problems. AA-PDA achieves better performance compared to on-policy baselines such as Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Overall, our results take a step toward bridging the gap between the theoretical advantages of PDA and its practical deployment in continuous-action problems with function approximation. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[When and Why Does Multi-Agent Debate Fail and Does It Really Underperform?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20963)**  `arXiv:2510.20963`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yongqiang Chen, Gang Niu, James Cheng, Bo Han, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate (MAD) was proposed as a promising approach for ensembling the wisdom of multiple large language models (LLMs) to improve reasoning and provide effective supervision to superhuman LLMs. However, increasing empirical evidence suggests that MAD may not outperform or even underperform single-agent approaches (SA), raising doubts about the benefits of MAD. In this work, we investigate this issue by analyzing the incentive structures of popular MAD paradigms: (i) competitive MAD (CopMAD) where agents compete by holding opposing positions; (ii) consensus-seeking MAD (CosMAD) where agents are driven to seek consensus. We show that both paradigms suffer from debate hacking: CopMAD reduces to a cheap-talk game, where agents produce misleading messages to win the game, while CosMAD filters out informative disagreements for premature consensus. Consequently, agents in both CopMAD and CosMAD fail to jointly resolve the ambiguity and seek the truth. To this end, we introduce ColMAD, a collaborative protocol that reframes MAD as a non-zero-sum game to encourage agents to provide informative while truthful messages. Through extensive benchmarking on challenging tasks such as error detection, we show that ColMAD consistently outperforms previous MAD protocols under multi-seed evaluation across heterogeneous LLM pairs. Under the same budgets, ColMAD effectively brings non-trivial improvements over SA methods, implying that the protocol design is critical to realizing the potential of MAD. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Tables to Time: Extending TabPFN-v2 to Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.02945)**  `arXiv:2501.02945`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shi Bin Hoo, Samuel Müller, David Salinas, Frank Hutter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in foundation models has enabled strong zero-shot performance for time series forecasting. In this work, we show that such capabilities can also emerge from tabular foundation models. We introduce TabPFN-TS, a simple method that treats forecasting as a tabular regression problem by combining lightweight temporal featurization with the pretrained TabPFN-v2. This formulation requires no time-series-specific pretraining and naturally supports both univariate and covariate-informed forecasting. Despite its compact size (11M parameters), TabPFN-TS achieves state-of-the-art performance on covariate-informed forecasting and competitive accuracy on univariate forecasting across the GIFT-Eval and fev-bench benchmarks. We further provide controlled analyses examining how the model interprets temporal structure, how featurization choices affect accuracy, and how forecasts change under alternative tabular backbones. Together, our results demonstrate that tabular foundation models--when paired with suitable temporal features--offer an efficient and versatile alternative for forecasting, bridging tabular and time-series learning within a unified framework. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EgoDepth-Bench: Benchmarking Metric Depth for Egocentric Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30734)**  `arXiv:2611.30734`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yuan Gao, Dongchen Wei, Xinyang Lin, Shuhua Cao, Xueliang Guo, Zhuohan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce EgoDepth-Bench, comprising 941,233 synchronized samples collected across 37 distinct physical environments. Each sample combines six calibrated RGB views with metric depth in the native fisheye view, reconstructed from five active-infrared stereo modules. A calibrated ball-bar confirms millimeter-level geometric accuracy, with a median error of 1.27 mm on a known sphere spacing. Environment-held-out splits enable evaluation of generalization to unseen environments. Across monocular, stereo, and multi-view models, the benchmark measures both depth accuracy and prediction coverage, including on hands and objects. Fine-tuning MapAnything on EgoDepth-Bench reduces metric AbsRel from 0.708 to 0.091 and scale-aligned AbsRel from 0.327 to 0.072 on unseen environments. These results demonstrate that EgoDepth-Bench provides effective supervision and enables rigorous evaluation of metric and relative depth estimation in egocentric manipulation. The project page provides the evaluation code and interactive RGB-depth sequences.
  </details>

- **[STAR: Structure-Aware Temporal Action Recovery for Point Supervised Temporal Action Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30354)**  `arXiv:2611.30354`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yawar Rehman, Min Young Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Point supervised temporal action localization is becoming the need of time in this era of ever growing and non-stop video data. Existing method mainly rely on snippet level action response to generate pseudo labels. However, these labels often suffer from boundary ambiguity, over extended proposals, and weak temporal structure. To address these issues, we propose STAR, a structure-aware Temporal Action Recovery framework for point supervised temporal action localization. STAR works by enhancing the base S model with temporal difference interaction with attention module to capture forward and backward snippet changes, and introduces the learning of start, core, and end roles to model the internal temporal structure of actions. In addition, STAR further improves the pseudo label generation through All point proposal ranking and Rolebank based structural re-ranking, which suppress neighbor crossing and structurally inconsistent proposals. Finally, temporal action detector to STAR feedback supervision transfers high quality detector prediction back to the STAR model through soft start, core, end, action, and background targets. This helps to further refine the pseudo labels. Extensive experiments on four standard benchmarks shows the effectiveness of STAR. By using I3D features, STAR achieves 67.2% at AVG@0.1:0.7 on THUMOS'14, 58.1% on GTEA, 74.6% on BEOID, and 31.7% at AVG@0.5:0.95 on ActivityNet-1.3, outperforming previous point supervised methods across multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[MOVER: Planning Beyond Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30022)**  `arXiv:2611.30022`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Xiangyu Li, Yu Jiang, Tianyi Wang, Liang He, Qixing Huang, Zhaomiao Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Residual world models forecast a driving scene through its temporal change, so feature components that stay constant over time vanish from the residual pathway. We ask whether evidence with weak temporal change, such as a waiting pedestrian or a stopped vehicle, can be used more effectively for planning, and introduce MOVER, a complementary event-routing mechanism. A training-time teacher measures how each spatially grounded event query responds when a past frame is removed from shared history and contrasts that sensitivity with the event's pooled temporal residual, prioritizing events that depend on history but receive weak residual evidence. A student predicts the resulting priorities from unperturbed history, so the teacher adds no inference cost. Under fixed total routing mass, one distribution scales a low-rank future bird's-eye-view update and biases trajectory-conditioned event reading. On nuScenes, MOVER reduces ResWorld's UniAD-style average L2 from 0.65 to 0.55 m and its collision rate from 0.23% to 0.12% without direct planner ego status (0.50 m and 0.09% with it), adding 4.1 ms per frame. Matched controls show that the complementary teacher outperforms either signal alone and that the allocation acts mainly during training. Adapted to BridgeDrive-LEAD, MOVER reaches a 96.52 +/- 0.65 driving score over three seeds on the 220-route Bench2Drive closed-loop benchmark, exceeding matched controls in each paired seed by a margin within seed variation.
  </details>

- **[SpatialDuet: Bayesian task routing between a metric expert and a geometry expert for spatial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29168)**  `arXiv:2611.29168`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Duy Tran Thanh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied assistants and wearable devices need 3D spatial reasoning, and their limited hardware restricts them to small vision-language models (VLMs). Small spatial VLMs acquire different abilities from their training: a model trained on large spatial question sets estimates object and room sizes well, whereas a model with a 3D geometry encoder is better at relative directions and absolute distances. Instead of training a single model for all spatial tasks, as published methods do, we build one system that uses each frozen model for the tasks it handles best. We propose SpatialDuet, which pairs a metric expert with a geometry expert under a Bayesian task router. The metric expert is a data-driven spatial VLM run with three training-free modules: training-aligned sampling, metric-aware minimum Bayes risk decoding and per-type metric calibration. The geometry expert is a VLM with a 3D geometry encoder. For each question type, the router estimates from labelled development questions how likely each expert is to be better, and pools their answers with that probability as weight, in log space for numeric answers and over the option distributions for multiple-choice answers. Extensive experiments on four spatial benchmarks covering video and single images validate SpatialDuet; it outperforms the best published small VLMs by 3.17% on VSI-Bench and, excluding a model trained on CV-Bench-style data, by 1.44% on CV-Bench, without training any model weight.
  </details>

- **[Div3DAfford: Diverse 3D affordance grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28917)**  `arXiv:2611.28917`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Haomeng Zhang, Raymond A. Yeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D affordance grounding aims to localize 3D regions specified by an interaction query. Existing methods predict a single affordance mask, overlooking the inherent diversity of human-object interactions. We introduce diverse 3D affordance grounding, a new task that requires predicting multiple plausible regions for a given object and affordance query. We then propose Div3DAffordNet, a transformer-based set prediction model capable of generating diverse affordance regions. To address the scarcity of diverse 3D affordance data, we develop an automated pipeline leveraging 2D generative models and 3D reconstruction techniques to synthesize 3D affordances, followed by lightweight human verification (reducing the annotation time by 72X), yielding the Div3DAfford benchmark. Extensive experiments show Div3DAffordNet effectively captures multi-mask affordance regions. Our method achieves the SOTA affordance grounding performance in the multi-mask setting, reducing GED-SIM from 0.745 to 0.492, while maintaining competitive single-mask performance on the standard benchmark of 19.0 mIoU.
  </details>

- **[Mem-X: A Memory-Dependent Cross-Episode Benchmark for Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27166)**  `arXiv:2611.27166`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhikang Shi, Yuk Tung Samuel Fang, Hongyu Ding, Zixuan Chen, Yujin Wang, Junhui Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables embodied agents to reuse past observations across tasks. However, existing cross-episode vision-and-language navigation (VLN) benchmarks often leave it unclear whether successful navigation relies on historical memory or current exploration. We introduce Mem-X, a benchmark designed to evaluate memory-dependent cross-episode VLN. Mem-X organizes episodes into sessions in which subsequent instructions refer to objects or routes encountered in earlier episodes. Its three levels evaluate historical-object recall, in-memory localization and episodic indexing, and off-memory relocalization and historical-route reproduction. For the latter two levels, agents receive episode-local poses but no transformations between episode frames, requiring them to establish spatial correspondence with historical memory. Mem-X comprises 2,071 episodes across 710 sessions in 34 scenes, including 1,204 memory-dependent episodes. We also provide MemBridge, a modular baseline that combines semantic scene memory, cross-episode localization, and coarse-to-fine retrieval to support existing VLN agents. Experiments with state-of-the-art VLN methods demonstrate the benefits of MemBridge while revealing substantial room for improvement on memory-dependent navigation. The benchmark and baseline code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25595)**  `arXiv:2611.25595`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Anh Dao, Xinxin Liu, Hang Yu, Jeffrey A Chan Santiago, Yuzhang Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.
  </details>

- **[Mesh-Free Keypoint Learning for Category-Level 6D Object Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25513)**  `arXiv:2611.25513`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Junghoon Seo, Hakjin Lee, Jaehoon Sim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Category-level 6D pose estimation relates partial RGB-D observations of unseen instances to a canonical frame shared across a category. Object-aware reconstruction helps adaptive keypoints retain the geometry of the visible surface. Common reconstruction targets select observed points by their distance to a registered instance surface. We present MORSE-Pose, which derives this support directly from standard pose and metric-size annotations. We map observed 3D points to normalized object coordinates and retain those inside a tolerance-dilated pose-and-size envelope. This construction is consistent across camera frames and metric scales. When the annotated extent bounds the object surface, the envelope conservatively contains the support of a conventional surface-distance selector. We integrate this target with multi-level RGB-D features, adaptive keypoints, and learnable category context. Experiments on REAL275 and HouseCat6D show that the envelope closely matches surface-based supervision at both point and task levels, remains stable under boundary perturbations and viewpoint changes, and supports competitive category-level pose accuracy across benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[World Models as Native Latent Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30898)**  `arXiv:2611.30898`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Qinyue Li, Weijie Qiao, Qingyi Tao, Zerui Gong, Zhe Yang, Quanzhou Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large and diverse video sources, such as egocentric human video and heterogeneous robot recordings, carry rich dynamics but come either without actions or without a shared action space. Latent action models (LAMs) can recover these dynamics, but they train their inverse- and forward-dynamics models (IDM, FDM) from scratch, trading off pixel reconstruction against latent capacity and accuracy. Pretrained video world models natively generate high-fidelity dynamics, yet represent them densely, entangled across all layers. Viewing the world model as a LAM, we show that these dynamics can be extracted with minimal changes to the model. Native LAM operates one pretrained video diffusion transformer as both IDM and FDM, with learnable dynamics registers as the only causal bridge between the two, and trains on counterfactual temporal pairs that differ in motion alone, so that the registers distill a video's dynamics into a compact native latent action. We further propose a pretraining paradigm for vision-language-action (VLA) policies, Pretraining Actions from World models (PAWlicy), in which Native LAM serves as a dynamics teacher on large-scale video; unlike world action models, it never generates video. Native latent actions outperform existing LAMs on the LARY benchmark in both action classification and control regression, and replay the same motion on new robots, in new scenes, and from human hands to robots, although no transfer objective is used in training. With a 3.8B model, PAWlicy reaches a 93.6% average success rate on RoboTwin 2.0 trained with domain-randomized demonstrations, the highest among VLAs. On RoboDojo, it is competitive on every dimension we evaluate, and its Precision, evaluated by us, is on par with the leading leaderboard entries. On real robots, it improves over the pi_0.5 baseline on both tasks. Both stages use only public data and a publicly released world model.
  </details>

- **[Self-Supervised Dataset Distillation via Mutual Information Maximization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31225)**  `arXiv:2611.31225`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Hossein Moslemi, Mahdi Beitollahi, Zhimin Mei, Jiale Cai, Bissan Ghaddar, Boyu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dataset distillation (DD) aims to compress a large dataset into a small synthetic one that remains effective for training neural networks. Despite its success in supervised learning, DD remains underexplored in self-supervised learning (SSL), where distilled data must preserve the information needed to learn representations that remain consistent across augmentations. To bridge this gap, we introduce Mutual Information Prototype Alignment (MIPA), an efficient SSL dataset distillation approach that optimizes synthetic images to preserve augmentation-invariant information. Specifically, MIPA treats synthetic images as learnable prototypes and uses a frozen encoder to assign real samples to them based on feature similarity. A synthetic dataset that preserves such augmentation invariance should yield stable prototype assignments across multiple augmented views of the same real image. MIPA encourages this by maximizing mutual information between cross-view assignments while avoiding trivial solutions in which all real samples are assigned to only a few synthetic prototypes. Evaluations on image classification benchmarks show that synthetic data generated by MIPA yield higher-quality representations than existing distillation approaches while reducing time and storage demands during distillation.
  </details>

- **[Analyzing and Improving FlowMap Distillation of Autoregressive Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31164)**  `arXiv:2611.31164`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yong-Hyun Park, Guande He, Joon Hyeok Kim, Hansheng Chen, Kaifan Yu, Chaoqi Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  FlowMap distillation offers a natural route to few-step generative modeling by directly learning a teacher's denoising trajectories. Yet for autoregressive (AR) video generation, existing few-step models still rely on distribution matching distillation (DMD) to remain competitive, sacrificing sample diversity and requiring a large bidirectional teacher. In this work, we analyze what limits standalone AR FlowMap distillation and introduce two complementary guidance mechanisms to close this gap: (1) anti-drifting guidance, which helps the AR teacher correct drifted student rollouts by training an auxiliary model to mimic student drift and using it as a negative guidance branch; and (2) motion-aware guidance, which makes motion easier for a FlowMap student to learn by locating motion patches and selectively applying skip-layer guidance. Empirically, our approach applies to $\pi$-Flow, MeanFlow, and consistency distillation with 1.3B AR teachers, improving generation quality and reducing drift over 30 s rollouts. Notably, our 4-step $\pi$-Flow students match DMD students distilled from 14B teachers on VBench while preserving the sample diversity of their AR teachers, demonstrating that pure FlowMap distillation is effective for few-step autoregressive video generation.
  </details>

- **[SPROUT: Predicting Objective-Grounded Prompt Utility for Efficient Text-to-Image Preference Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31163)**  `arXiv:2611.31163`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Kangping Hu, Akshay Mehra, Stephen Mussmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference tuning of text-to-image diffusion models spends most of its compute generating candidate images, yet training prompts are typically sampled uniformly at random, wasting compute generating images that provide too little signal for model alignment. To best utilize the available compute, we study prompt selection, where at each iteration we identify the most useful prompts for preference tuning. We separate prompt selection into a utility score, an estimator of that utility score, and batch selection. Within this framework, we propose Selection by PRedicting Objective-grounded UTility (SPROUT). The utility score is predicted on every prompt in the pool, including unseen prompts, by a text-only model, refit after every iteration on all utilities observed so far. A randomized acceptance rule then turns these predictions into a batch, so no images are generated beyond those used for training. For direct group preference optimization (DGPO), the objective-grounded utility is the norm of the gradient that the objective produces from a prompt's group of images. Fine-tuning Stable Diffusion 1.5 with DGPO on GenEval, SPROUT matches the final score of uniform sampling in about 44% of the iterations and improves the area under the learning curve with respect to both uniform sampling and CGPO, a recent variance-based prompt sampler. The efficiency gains hold under four held-out reward models never used in training, and the ranking of methods carries over to DPG-Bench and T2I-CompBench++. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[JONES-19: A Cultural Image Dataset Based on The Grammar of Ornament](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30558)**  `arXiv:2611.30558`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Linh Pham, Stuart Shieber, David Alvarez-Melis, Alexandros Haridis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce JONES-19, a high-quality image dataset documenting 1,901 ornament designs belonging to nineteen human cultures. The images and their annotations are based on an open access archive of The Grammar of Ornament (London, 1856), by Owen Jones. The dataset poses numerous challenges as a benchmark for computer vision classification tasks and for research at the intersection of machine learning and art and design: a small sample size, image samples of human-designed artifacts rather than common objects in-context or natural scenes, imbalanced class distribution, and image distinctions based on fine details involving line patterns, reliefs, and colors. As a design-inspired dataset, JONES-19 can serve as a benchmark for various research fields, such as visual recognition, data-efficient learning, art-historical research, architectural style analysis, and cultural heritage. This paper describes the curation of the JONES-19 dataset and reports a baseline classification benchmark that evaluates the suitability of the dataset for training classifiers and exposes insights into inter-class relationships--particularly cultural similarities--by examining patterns in misclassification errors. The dataset and its accompanying documentation are available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[BEND: Spectral Bregman Losses for Multi-Image Unlearning in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30532)**  `arXiv:2611.30532`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohamed-Hicham Leghettas, Markus Püschel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models memorize training images and reproduce them almost verbatim when prompted, creating copyright and privacy liabilities. Data unlearning erases the influence of specific training samples from a trained model without retraining, and is two-sided: it must forget the targets while preserving generation quality on everything else. Prior methods use the squared-L2 diffusion loss, at most with a hand-set frequency mask; however, it is known that every Bregman divergence is a mathematically valid choice and the squared-L2 is only the simplest example. BEND is the first to search this family: it fits a spectral Bregman divergence to the unlearning task through a bilevel loop, so the loss itself is shaped to forget thoroughly while protecting quality. It also redirects a memorized image to the mean of its retain images, where ReTrack, the state of the art, redirects it to a single near-copy. On Stable Diffusion, a single BEND model erases all 48 verifiably memorized benchmark images at once. It forgets at least as much as per-target, sequential, and jointly trained ReTrack (SSCD 0.084 versus 0.094-0.120), at the quality of the model before unlearning (CLIP-IQA 0.81 versus 0.64-0.69). BEND also outperforms the state of the art on CelebA-HQ in all three measures of the benchmark, and matches or exceeds it on MNIST and CIFAR-10.
  </details>

- **[TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240)**  `arXiv:2611.30240`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Qingqiao Hu, Jialin Li, Meilong Xu, Kehan Qi, Yipeng Zhang, Lin Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. Annotation thus becomes a loop of inspecting the prediction and clicking again. Each step of this loop is a visual judgment, which suits general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. With flash-level MLLMs, it outperforms a recent 3D segmentation agent built on a frontier MLLM.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Beyond Imitation: Observation Conditioned Distillation for Video LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29728)**  `arXiv:2611.29728`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Junzhou Chen, Jindong Wang, Gang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation offers a promising approach to transfer temporal reasoning capabilities of powerful Video-LLMs to open-source student models. However, teacher and student Video-LLMs may construct different temporal observations from the same video due to the adoption of distinct video sampling pipelines. A reasoning trajectory valid under the teacher's observations may rely on evidence inaccessible to the student and thus cannot be directly executed based on the student's observations. We formalize this as a conditional target mismatch, thereby revealing the Reasoning Acquisition-Execution Gap. To bridge this gap, we propose an acquisition-to-execution framework that first distills teacher trajectories into a student reasoning prior and then further optimizes the execution capability of this prior across multiple observations. We further introduce Execution-Aware Reference Regularization, which retains the existing prior when it is executable under current observations, while allowing a greater degree of adaptive adjustment when it is not. Experiments across multiple Video-LLM backbones and six video reasoning benchmarks show that explicitly optimizing observation-conditioned execution consistently outperforms supervised distillation and standard RL-based optimization, with gains extending to held-out out-of-domain benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[You Only Think Once: Rescuing Hybrid Vision-Language Models Lost in Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28100)**  `arXiv:2611.28100`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianyi Tan, Wentao Wang, Xiaohan Li, Zining Ren, Siyuan Li, Xutao Mao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thinking at length before answering is expected to strengthen the visual reasoning of vision-language models (VLMs), yet hybrid VLMs often get lost in thought: their thoughts loop or exhaust the budget without an answer, so thinking can score below answering directly. Existing early-exit methods watch a thought for the moment its answer is ready and force the answer out of it, but in a hybrid VLM that moment often never comes. We find that the remedy is not to stop a failing thought earlier, but to stop taking its answer: once a thought is given up, the model's own direct answers decide. Based on this, we propose **YOTO (You Only Think Once)**, a method that answers first and thinks at most once. YOTO draws direct answers until a probe-weighted vote settles; only an unsettled problem receives a single thought, which a failure head on the hidden state monitors and stops as soon as it predicts failure, handing the problem back to direct answers. At a matched 20k-token budget, YOTO is up to 5.9 points more accurate than the strongest early-exit baseline, using one failure-head threshold across model sizes. Keeping the released weights frozen, it adds only two linear probes. On the full MathVision benchmark, YOTO improves Qwen3.5-2B from 40.1% to 57.3% and Qwen3.5-4B from 68.3% to 73.7%, above the 72.8% that Qwen3.5-9B, a model more than twice its size, reaches with thinking. Its gains carry over to five further benchmarks and a second model family. These results suggest that hybrid VLMs reason better by answering first and thinking once than by thinking longer.
  </details>

- **[Self-supervised behavior encoders rarely beat a training-free baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24792)**  `arXiv:2611.24792`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised encoders of animal and human pose are compared with one another, and rarely with simple summaries of the poses they encode. We propose such a summary that needs no training and no knowledge of the species: each body's keypoints and velocities in its own frame, a small block on the nearest other animals, and the mean, spread and histogram of every number over windows from half a second to the whole clip, reduced by PCA. We compare it with seven encoders on five benchmarks under the standard protocol: frozen representations of equal size, read by a linear classifier. It matches or beats hBehaveMAE and BAMS, the encoders pretrained on each benchmark, on all five: behaviors of mouse pairs and of human motion capture, the movements of a simulated basketball player, the strain and lighting of mouse triplets and group-level traits of flies. It leads hBehaveMAE from about a tenth of the labels. A few dozen plain kinematic measurements in place of the keypoints widen the lead over hBehaveMAE to 3.4 to 5.6 points. With all labels only HiDRA, a forecaster built for pairs of mice, beats the baseline, on mouse pairs; it trails on mouse triplets and cannot run on flies or humans. Two choices carry the baseline, the body's own frame and windows of seconds, and readouts of the frozen embeddings show what the encoders miss: they keep where body parts are, but little of how they move over seconds.
  </details>

- **[GRTO: Group Relative Tool Optimization for Referring Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23500)**  `arXiv:2605.23500`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mario Markov, Stefan Maria Ailuro, Mohammad Mahdi, Luc Van Gool, Danda Pani Paudel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring segmentation requires reasoning to connect language with visual evidence and identify the intended target in challenging scenarios. This makes GRPO a natural choice for training VLMs together with a segmentation tool. However, existing approaches optimize only the VLM, assuming that the tool is perfect and leaving it untouched. This assumption is particularly limiting when the tool faces domain shift, as segmentation errors can undermine even correct reasoning. We therefore argue that the segmentation tool should be optimized together with the reasoning policy. However, this is challenging because policy optimization and differentiable tool learning rely on fundamentally different objectives. In this work, we introduce group relative tool optimization (GRTO), a mathematically grounded two-stage framework for jointly optimizing a policy and its differentiable tool. The key idea is to first align the tool with the policy expected after joint training, rather than the initial policy. The prealigned tool then leads to a more robust reward signal and superior policy convergence in subsequent policy training. In the first stage, GRTO bootstraps the tool toward the theoretically optimal policy, capturing readily attainable gains without costly on-policy rollouts. This alignment bootstraps the second stage, where GRTO extends GRPO to jointly refine the policy and tool. Across three challenging referring segmentation benchmarks, GRTO achieves substantial improvements over plain GRPO and alternative tool training procedures, matching or surpassing domain-specific state-of-the-art methods. This demonstrates the value of unifying reinforcement learning with differentiable auxiliary objectives for reasoning-intensive segmentation.
  </details>

- **[CT-VDETR: Semi-supervised 3D Trauma Detection in Computed Tomography (CT) scans using Dense Vertex Relative Position Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12514)**  `arXiv:2603.12514`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shivam Chaudhary, Sheethal Bhat, Andreas Maier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate detection and localization of traumatic injuries in abdominal CT remain challenging because voxel-level annotations are limited and expensive to obtain. We present a label-efficient framework for 3D abdominal trauma detection that combines self-supervised pretraining with semi-supervised transformer-based detection. First, we use Masked Image Modeling (MIM) on 1,098 CT volumes to pretrain a 3D U-Net encoder for anatomical representation learning. Next, we adapt V-DETR to dense volumetric CT through a feature adapter that converts the encoder feature grid into a compact token sequence for transformer decoding. The pretrained encoder is then integrated with V-DETR and 3D Vertex Relative Position Encoding (3DV-RPE) to improve the localization of irregularly shaped injuries. Finally, semi-supervised teacher-student consistency regularization leverages 2,000 additional unlabeled volumes during detector training. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first application of a 3D DETR-style detector to the RSNA abdominal trauma detection task. On this benchmark, the proposed method achieves 31.33% test mAP@0.50 using only 78 labeled training volumes, corresponding to a 1.53x improvement over supervised-only training. These results show that combining medical-domain pretraining with semi-supervised learning is an effective strategy for label-scarce 3D medical detection.
  </details>

- **[UMAMI: A Multimodal Benchmark Dataset for Evidence-Grounded Dietary Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31068)**  `arXiv:2611.31068`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Safal Thapaliya, Jiatan Huang, Debapriya Chatterjee, Yanfang Ye, Chuxu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dietary decisions require identifying a food's nutrient content and considering individual dietary needs, but recognizing food from an image alone does not provide this information. Visually similar foods can have different nutrient profiles, so selecting the wrong food record can lead to incorrect decisions even when nutrient rules are applied correctly. Furthermore, satisfying nutrient constraints does not necessarily establish a food's relevance to a person's intake. Evaluating these challenges together requires connecting food images, nutrient records, and individual intake histories. To support this evaluation, we introduce UMAMI (Unified Multimodal Assessment of Meals and Intake), a multimodal dataset and benchmark linking independently collected food images with nutrient profiles and respondent health and intake records. Using this dataset, we construct three diagnostic tasks covering visual nutrient question answering, personalized food recommendation, and condition-aware food substitution. Paired instances and input controls examine how visual identity and respondent context affect decisions. Our experiments identify errors in both food-record selection and nutrient-rule application, while showing that higher rule compliance does not necessarily improve alignment with subsequent recorded intake. These findings highlight the need to evaluate how multimodal systems select and use structured evidence for dietary decisions. The dataset, code, and dashboard are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auxiliary Clues Aware's Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30617)**  `arXiv:2611.30617`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Qigong Lei, Qingsong Wang, Wang Lin, Qianyi Yang, Chang Yao, Jingyuan Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry Problem Solving (GPS) serves as a rigorous test of the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly when solutions require auxiliary constructions absent from the input diagram. However, explicit diagramming methods suffer from unstable image generation quality and cascading errors, while existing implicit attention mechanisms primarily focus on visible elements, limiting their ability to support reasoning that requires additional geometric constructions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{AuxCA} (Auxiliary-Clues-Aware GRPO), a reinforcement learning framework that helps models learn transferable auxiliary-construction strategies that can be applied beyond the training domain without explicit diagram generation at inference time. Specifically, we introduce Auxiliary Clues Dependency (\textbf{ACD}) to quantify the influence of auxiliary visual clues on token generation and integrate it into GRPO through fine-grained advantage weighting. Furthermore, we incorporate an auxiliary incentive reward to encourage the effective use of auxiliary geometric clues in correct solutions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AuxCA-8B outperforms the explicit-plotting baseline Bagel-Canvas and achieves competitive performance with leading proprietary models on auxiliary-dependent plane geometry problems. Improvements on analytic geometry, solid geometry, and external benchmarks further suggest that the learned strategies can transfer beyond the training domain. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Seeing Is Locating: Spatial Awareness and Geographic Bias in Decision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30564)**  `arXiv:2611.30564`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Wilson Wongso, Hada Melino Muhammad, Xiachong Lin, Hao Xue, Flora D. Salim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models, which output probability distributions over candidate labels instead of generating text, are increasingly used as classifiers and evaluators for inputs associated with real-world locations. However, it remains unclear whether they can leverage spatial context and whether their predictions are geographically biased. We introduce GeoDecisions, a multimodal benchmark for evaluating the spatial awareness and geographic bias of decision models. GeoDecisions comprises two tasks: (1) geo-aware image classification, and (2) location rating on objective and subjective topics. Evaluating six decision models against supervised task-specific models, we find that spatial awareness requires vision: textual coordinates alone provide insufficient context for classifying everyday photos and satellite images. We also find substantial geographic bias. On everyday photos, despite achieving higher accuracy, open-weight decision models exhibit more spatially clustered errors than supervised models. Moreover, across all five subjective topics, every decision model gives lower ratings to locations with lower socioeconomic conditions. Finally, we propose a training-free method that combines decision models with a location prior to improve their spatial awareness without substantially increasing performance bias. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OpenViVE: New Benchmark and Dual-Stream Co-Attention Framework for Vietnamese Visual Entailment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30161)**  `arXiv:2611.30161`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Bao Quoc Trinh, Tin Van Huynh, Kiet Van Nguyen, Ngan Luu-Thuy Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Entailment (VE) determines whether a textual hypothesis is entailed, contradicted, or unverifiable from an image. However, existing VE benchmarks are largely English-centric and do not capture Vietnamese-specific linguistic distinctions or culturally grounded visual contexts. To address this gap, we introduce OpenViVE, the first large-scale native Vietnamese VE benchmark, containing 62,268 balanced image-hypothesis pairs from 10,378 culturally grounded images. OpenViVE is constructed using an Artifact-Aware Hybrid LLM-Human Pipeline that combines constrained generation, human validation, and PMI-guided refinement. The pipeline substantially reduces hypothesis-only shortcuts, with Contradiction accuracy dropping from 96% to 66% after artifact refinement. We further evaluate a dual-stream PhoBERT-ConvNeXt framework with bidirectional co-attention to align visual evidence with Vietnamese hypotheses. The framework achieves 84.84% accuracy and 84.90% macro-F1 on the test set, while PhoBERT consistently outperforms multilingual XLM-R variants. These results highlight the importance of language-specific encoding and explicit cross-modal interaction for Vietnamese VE.
  </details>

- **[Counting Symbols on Technical Drawings: A Benchmark and Verified Rewards for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29309)**  `arXiv:2611.29309`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Junxian Li, Ruixuan Yang, Zhenhua Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today's MLLMs can count objects in natural images. However, when asking them how many bolt holes appear in a mechanical drawing or how many nitrogen atoms are in a chemical structure, they often struggle. Counting in technical drawings remains challenging, as objects can be small, densely packed, or even implied by drawing conventions. To study this problem, we introduce CountDrawBench, a benchmark including floor plans, electrical schematics, mechanical drawings, and molecular structures, with 1,574 counting questions and instance-level annotations where available. Besides evaluation, we introduce Code-Verified RL, a reinforcement learning framework that teaches MLLMs to count with executable feedback. Our method combines three complementary rewards: a count reward that checks the final answer, an instance-matching reward that verifies object locations, and a zoom-based reward that checks counting consistency across local image regions. All three are integrated into a unified GRPO objective. Extensive experiments reveal that even leading MLLMs struggle on CountDrawBench, with the best evaluated model achieving only 59.3% accuracy. Our method improves Qwen3.5-9B from 49.3% to 54.3% accuracy, outperforming all evaluated open-source baselines. These results establish CountDrawBench as a challenging testbed for technical drawing understanding and demonstrate the value of code-verified rewards in these tasks. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[S-Mind: Internalizing Multi-View Geometry for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28649)**  `arXiv:2611.28649`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Muxin Fu, Peike Yu, Guibin Zhang, Wenrui Zhou, Runze Liu, Lang Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence, the ability to reason about 3D space from visual observations, becomes essential as multimodal large language models (MLLMs) move into the physical world. Beyond perceiving each view, it rests on two cornerstones of human cognition: fusing multiple views into one coherent 3D scene and mentally transforming that scene to new viewpoints as reasoning unfolds. Yet our spatial probes reveal that MLLMs largely lack both capabilities, even after spatial fine-tuning. We therefore present S-Mind, which internalizes multi-view geometry within the MLLM through a compact set of spatial tokens. It supervises these tokens to hold the fused 3D scene and re-emits them to re-express this scene from every viewpoint the reasoning adopts. Across four spatial benchmarks, S-Mind improves its Qwen3.5 backbones by up to 22.26 points and surpasses frontier general models such as GPT-6-Astra (+0.45), Kimi-K3.1-Pro (+1.97), and GLM-5.3-Flash (+7.80) in average accuracy. Moreover, the same probes confirm that it acquires both capabilities, reaching fusion and transformation accuracies of 65.11% and 82.17%, against at most 16.02% and 37.28% for the baselines. At inference, S-Mind also runs about 100x faster than a tool-based agent on the same backbone. Instilled through these spatial tokens during training, spatial capability becomes intrinsic to the backbone and persists even when the tokens are withheld, offering a promising path toward spatial intelligence in MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[VidRouteBench: A Benchmark for Budgeted Video-Generation Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28099)**  `arXiv:2611.28099`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Dayu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-video generators differ by two orders of magnitude in price, and a cheap configuration of a strong model is often good enough for a prompt. Routing prompts across such arms (model x sampling configuration) should buy quality at fixed budget, but no dataset supports studying it: public video-preference sets contain only closed models, one configuration each.We release VidRouteBench: 16 arms from 7 open families spanning 121x in measured cost ($0.0025-$0.298 per clip), 27,110 clips (1,421 A800-hours) over 1,625 grouped prompts, a quality score frozen after calibration against human preferences (68.7% CV accuracy vs 66.2% for the best single scorer), per-clip scores on 9 identifiable quality dimensions, repeated seeds, an encrypted test split, and a sealed newest family for cold start. The evaluator compares a router against the best prompt-agnostic mixture of arms, not the best single arm. Per-prompt specialisation here is real and predictable (oracle gap 0.642, 47% reproducible across seeds, prompt-arm interaction F=4.47 at p=1.6e-63), unlike LLM-routing arms, where the same pipeline finds none.We then propose OPMU (one probe, many utilities): label a new arm on K probe prompts, predict its 9 dimensions once, and serve any scenario utility by scalarising afterwards. Over 215 utility directions OPMU Pareto-dominates the prompt-agnostic baseline for 94% of directions versus 90% for per-scenario routers, with no per-scenario retraining. Three baselines fail instructively: bag-of-words features lose almost all routing gain in the low-data regime (0.009 vs 0.168); crediting a new arm with its probe average is harmful (-3.32 recovery) although it is the strong baseline on RewardBench-style arms (0.786); Chebyshev scalarisation does not help, our cost-utility front being convex.
  </details>

- **[NAMESAKES: Probing Identity Memorization in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20155)**  `arXiv:2606.20155`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Morris Alper, Vasudha Varadarajan, Moran Yanuka, Angelina Wang, Hadar Averbuch-Elor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) models generate realistic likenesses of some individuals when prompted with their names, raising privacy concerns. However, distinguishing whether a generated face is memorized or fabricated currently requires ground-truth photos, access to training data, or white-box access to model internals, limiting applicability. We introduce a fully black-box behavioral probe that distinguishes between memorized and unrecognized names, while requiring no reference photos or prior knowledge of training data. To benchmark this task, we present the NAMESAKES dataset of over one thousand names and faces of public figures spanning a wide range of fame levels, along with perturbed, less famous names. Experiments on state-of-the-art T2I models show that our probe substantially predicts identity memorization and separates memorized from unrecognized names, with further insights into differences across model families.
  </details>

- **[Pop-Up Distractions Reveal Bag-of-Events Behavior in Video Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27101)**  `arXiv:2605.27101`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Oscar Chew, Serhii Honcharenko, Qian-Hui Chen, Patricia Lu, Dishant Zaveri, Khoa D. Doan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A key capability for video understanding is reliably linking subjects to events across time, yet whether Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) actually achieve this remains unclear. In this work, we introduce DistractionBench to evaluate whether VideoLLMs can robustly link subjects and events in the presence of unrelated video segments. Through controlled interventions, such as inserting short advertisement clips into longer videos, we show that VideoLLMs frequently hallucinate interactions between entities from different segments, incorrectly attributing actions from injected advertisements to subjects in the main video. We characterize this systematic hallucination as bag-of-events (BoE) behavior, where models process videos as collections of events rather than temporally structured sequences. Evaluating 11 popular VideoLLMs, we find that all models exhibit substantial BoE behavior. Our findings suggest that VideoLLMs lack reliable mechanisms for temporal grounding and motivate the development of models with more robust subject-event association.
  </details>

- **[UniAIDet: A Unified and Universal Benchmark for AI-Generated Image Content Detection and Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23023)**  `arXiv:2510.23023`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Huixuan Zhang, Xiaojun Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid proliferation of image generative models, the authenticity of digital images has become a significant concern. While existing studies have proposed various methods for detecting AI-generated content, current benchmarks are limited in their coverage of diverse generative models and image categories, often overlooking end-to-end image editing and artistic images. To address these limitations, we introduce UniAIDet, a unified and comprehensive benchmark that includes both photographic and artistic images. UniAIDet covers a wide range of generative models, including text-to-image, image-to-image, image inpainting, image editing, and deepfake models. Using UniAIDet, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation of various detection methods and answer three key research questions regarding generalization capability and the relation between detection and localization. Our benchmark and analysis provide a robust foundation for future research.
  </details>

- **[Don't Test Where You Trained: Geographic Overlap Inflates End-to-End Driving Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28177)**  `arXiv:2611.28177`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Willi Poh, Kevin Rösch, Christoph Stiller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In NAVSIMv1, the standard benchmark for end-to-end driving, $89.6\%$ of the test scenes lie within 4m of a training scene. We show that this geographic overlap inflates the reported scores. We propose a geographically disjoint split of NAVSIMv1 and an evaluation that compares models trained on either split on the same scenes, which neither has seen in training. All sensor-based models we test, DiffusionDrive, Latent TransFuser, and DrivoR, lose 3.4 to 6.1 PDMS points on these scenes, mainly by leaving the road and coming close to other objects, against 1.5 points for a baseline without sensors. The loss concentrates where a training drive of the original split passed the same place. At all other places, models trained on the original split are no better than those trained on the geographic split. Models trained on the original split predict road geometry that no camera can see. The gap persists when the auxiliary map loss is removed, so the models memorize locations even without being supervised to. On nuScenes, the geographic split cuts SparseDrive's mapping accuracy by $59\%$ and also degrades its planning. Driving scores on logged scenes thus partly measure how well a model remembers the test locations. We release the split to evaluate generalization to new places.
  </details>

- **[StereoVLM: Binocular Vision-Language Models for Metric 3D Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27915)**  `arXiv:2611.27915`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Chi Yan, Guoyang Zhao, Wentao Yuan, Yixiao Ge, Yanning Zhou, Dan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit strong semantic understanding but remain limited in their ability to reason about metric 3D space in the physical world. Existing VLMs typically infer 3D scene structure from one or more images without known camera calibration or cross-view geometric constraints. This makes metric 3D inference inherently ambiguous and leaves it unclear whether their spatial predictions are grounded in visual evidence or driven primarily by semantic priors. In contrast, calibrated stereo provides explicit cross-view geometry that resolves this ambiguity and offers a more reliable foundation for metric 3D perception and geometric generalization. We investigate how VLMs can learn transferable 3D representations directly from calibrated binocular observations. To this end, we introduce StereoVLM, the first geometry-aware binocular VLM for metric 3D spatial reasoning. StereoVLM follows a hierarchical learning paradigm spanning from cross-view correspondence and disparity estimation to calibration-conditioned metric depth perception and semantic spatial reasoning. We further find that disparity supervision generalizes substantially better than direct binocular depth supervision on unseen environments. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce Stereo3D-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark across three levels, from point-level geometry to object-level spatial reasoning. StereoVLM-8B achieves 95.17% depth accuracy ($\delta_1$) and a 66.98 average object-level score on Stereo3D-Bench. Intervention results further indicate that the model's performance on Stereo3D-Bench benefits from valid cross-view geometry and cannot be explained solely by additional visual tokens or semantic priors. Hierarchical geometric supervision also yields average gains of 3.54 points across five spatial benchmarks and 4.3 percentage points in RoboCasa manipulation success.
  </details>

- **[PhysMoE: Fast Spatial Grounding with Single-Token Coordinate Representation in Modality-Decoupled VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13000)**  `arXiv:2611.13000`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Ruimeng Liu, Denan Liang, Kuan Xu, Ruofei Bai, Fuming Zhang, Shenghai Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are backbones for robotic planning and manipulation. However, for coordinate prediction in grounding tasks, existing architectures suffer from two distinct bottlenecks: discrete tokenization disrupts spatial continuity with high decoding latency, while standard point regression fails on multi-contact affordances and irregularly shaped targets. To resolve these challenges, we propose PhysMoE, introducing three core contributions: (1) sharing lower layers for multimodal context while routing upper layers to specialized spatial (pixel) and linguistic (text) experts by physical modality, eliminating gradient interference; (2) encoding each coordinate as a single token by reusing the visual encoder's learned positional embeddings, injecting 2D spatial priors to natively align coordinates with visual features and ease training; and (3) differentiable Signed Distance Field (SDF) supervision on pointing, accommodating affordances and irregular geometries without costly mask decoders. Compared to base models, PhysMoE achieves a 15.7% average gain and an 8.31x throughput speedup across 9 coordinate-based grounding benchmarks (trajectory, affordance, and localization), while preserving multimodal understanding and reasoning across 11 text-based embodied benchmarks. Our work is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DriveOddsBench: Are Driving Video World Models Aligned with Real-World Odds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29275)**  `arXiv:2611.29275`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Jiaxi Liu, Keshu Wu, Bin Ran, Xiaopeng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models predict how a scene will evolve, and their sampled futures are increasingly used in place of the real one, from planning to simulation. These uses assume that the samples are aligned with real-world odds, with each future appearing among them as often as it would follow the same history in reality, yet current evaluations only score how real each sample looks. Testing this assumption on real driving data is hard for three reasons. Each recorded history has only one real future, so the true probabilities are unknown. Physical quantities must be read from video, where reading errors can look like model errors. Finally, the many possible analyses invite selective reporting. We introduce DriveOddsBench, a pre-registered benchmark that addresses all three. First, if the samples follow the real distribution, the rank of the real outcome among them is uniform in every history, so pooled ranks test them without probability labels. Second, one validated instrument reads the same quantities, such as the lead-vehicle gap and the ego motion, from the generated futures and from the real future, which first passes through the world model's autoencoder so that both are read alike. Third, all confirmatory analyses were frozen before any test clip was measured. We test eleven general and driving world models on 200 held-out Argoverse 2 histories in five scenario families, with 35,200 generated futures. None is aligned, and every model underestimates its uncertainty. The real outcome falls outside the range of all 16 samples in 28-61% of cases instead of the 11.8% expected under the real distribution. Five models are significantly skewed, in opposite directions, and the largest errors are in the ego motion. Nine models beat climatology without being aligned, and among these models neither type nor scale predicts which comes closest.
  </details>

- **[Urban State Representation for Autonomous Mobility: A Survey of Multimodal Perception, Architectures, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28129)**  `arXiv:2611.28129`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Binay Kumar Singh, Niels Da Vitoria Lobo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cities are sensed by many platforms at once: on-board cameras, LiDAR and radar on autonomous vehicles, roadside units at intersections, drones and satellites overhead, and the GNSS traces of millions of trips. Yet most learned representations are built for one platform, one city, and one task. We survey this fragmented landscape through a single lens, the Urban State Representation (USR): a georeferenced, layered, time-varying, uncertainty-aware state that any sensing platform can write to and any downstream task can read from. We (i) formalize USR and five desiderata for it, and relate it to bird's-eye-view (BEV) features, occupancy, vectorized maps, neural fields and 3D Gaussians, scene graphs, road-network graphs, language tokens, and world-model latents; (ii) organize sensing platforms, cross-view (ground-aerial) matching, fusion operators, 3D geometric foundation models, end-to-end and vision-language-action models, and generative world models and data engines in one taxonomy that spans sensors to decisions; (iii) compile 26 urban datasets and simulators across ego, cooperative (V2X), aerial, trajectory, and language settings, with a metric glossary, a cross-benchmark results snapshot, a trend analysis, and a platform-coverage gap analysis; and (iv) review applications and propose, as an outlook, USR-5, a five-test protocol with explicit references and one aggregate score for measuring whether a state is shared, together with a research agenda of six open problems covering cross-city and cross-sensor transfer, uncertainty, efficiency, sim-to-real evaluation, and governance. This survey is an entry point for computer vision, autonomous driving, transportation, and urban computing researchers; the paper list, datasets, and USR-5 code are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Let It Be Simple: One-Step Action Generation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05737)**  `arXiv:2606.05737`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Yitong Chen, Shiduo Zhang, Jingjing Gong, Xipeng Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-step generation from standard diffusion or Flow-Matching training is rarely effective for text-to-image or video generation without distillation or specialized one-step objectives. We find that VLA action generation behaves strikingly differently. Biasing only the training timestep distribution toward the noise endpoint yields strong one-step policies across LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, LIBERO-Pro, and real-world robot tasks. Moreover, for open-source VLA models with different architectures, simply replacing iterative decoding with a single forward pass often performs comparably to the original multi-step decoding on benchmarks such as LIBERO, CALVIN, and SimplerEnv. We attribute this difference to the condition-target structure of VLA generation, which is closer to image-to-text than text-to-image: rich observations strongly constrain a comparatively compact target. We formalize this intuition through the irreducible velocity loss $R_v(t,c)$ of standard Flow Matching, which becomes much smaller toward the noise endpoint for VLA, whereas image-generation models usually exhibit a U-shaped profile. The geometry induced by the learned generative map shows the same contrast: the Jacobian of this map with respect to the initial noise has a substantially smaller largest singular value for action generation than for image generation. Together, these results suggest that VLA's condition-target structure makes one-step generation unusually accessible to standard Flow Matching. Code and training configurations for this paper are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When "A or B" Is Less Likely than A: Coherent Probabilities for Vision Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30096)**  `arXiv:2611.30096`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Seongrae Noh, SeongEun Hong, HyeongYeop Kang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision decision models answer a multiple-choice question about an image with one probability per option, and software uses them directly. We show that the probabilities depend on how the options are grouped: a merged option "A or B" receives on average 11.5 points less probability than the sum of A and B. This under-additivity appears at every model size from 0.85B to 26B and remains after fine-tuning on labels. We propose CORE, which asks each training question with its options regrouped and trains the answers to all these questions toward one target distribution over the original options, summed into each question's groups. On 1,050 items from seven benchmarks, CORE reduces the total variation distance between the answer to a regrouped question and the original answer summed into the same groups from 14.8 to 5.8 points on the 0.85B model, against 9.4 for the best baseline trained on the same questions. At all five model sizes, CORE reduces this distance without lowering accuracy.
  </details>

- **[LexiDet-OVD: Benchmarking Lexical Sensitivity in Open-Vocabulary Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29851)**  `arXiv:2611.29851`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Amit Thakur, Mukesh Singhal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary object detectors localize objects from free-form text prompts, but their reliability depends on how users describe the target. Standard evaluations usually test a fixed class name, leaving prompt-induced failure modes underexplored. We introduce LexiDet-OVD, a benchmark and metric suite for studying lexical sensitivity in open-vocabulary object detection. We organize prompt sets into equivalent, conditional, and broad semantic scopes, and evaluate four detector families on fixed COCO and LVIS samples, each covering 30 classes (800 held-out COCO images and 1,000 LVIS images). Beyond mean and worst-prompt AP50, we report P10, CVaR at 20%, leave-one-prompt-out influence, detection agreement, box consistency, and confidence variance using one-to-one multi-instance matching. Equivalent-prompt best-to-worst gaps range from 5.3 to 23.4 points on COCO1000 and from 5.1 to 29.9 points on LVIS1000-30c, showing that strong average accuracy and lexical stability are distinct properties. We compare maximum-score selection, weighted-box fusion, Prompt Consensus, and an exploratory fusion rule. Aggregation recovers performance in several settings, but no method dominates all model-dataset pairs. A controlled Detic/SHiNe comparison further shows that semantic hierarchy enrichment does not by itself eliminate lexical sensitivity. These results support reporting prompt-distribution robustness alongside conventional open-vocabulary detection accuracy.
  </details>

- **[S1Arena-MM: Which Multimodal Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29773)**  `arXiv:2611.29773`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lang Lin, Tianyu Chen, Yi Gu, Yang Zhao, Xinran Song, Xinyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Perception systems often make discrete decisions: flag a hateful meme, predict whether a pedestrian will cross, detect a spoofed voice, or select the next move in a game. System-1 models, such as OneJev and Jev-Omni, read the media and return probabilities over the options in a single forward pass, while multimodal large language models (MLLMs) generate text. Which multimodal decisions can System-1 models handle, and where do MLLMs perform better? We introduce S1Arena-MM, a benchmark that gives every model the same decision requests and scores the probabilities it returns. It contains 51,060 questions across 94 tasks with image, video, and audio input, drawn from 94 public datasets and three program-generated ones. It also includes 102 closed-loop game environments that a model plays from screenshots; in real-time mode, the game continues while the model decides. We evaluate 33 open models: 12 System-1 models and 21 MLLMs read at the answer token. On static decisions, the best System-1 model leads the best MLLM by 4.9 percentage points on images and is within one point of it on video and audio.
