Post-training for decisions mainly improves the probabilities: on video, a System-1 model has a lower Brier score than the MLLM that it is post-trained from in 10 of 12 pairs. With up to 2,048 thought tokens per question, the most accurate MLLMs pass the best System-1 models only slightly, and thinking raises the Brier score of most MLLMs. Closed loops separate the models: three System-1 models built on a 27B MLLM reach 22.0-33.3% progression in BALROG, and no other model exceeds 9.7% with thinking off. In real-time GameWorld, latencies of up to 1.1 s per decision cause no measurable loss of progress for System-1 models. Code and data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CLASP: Complementary Layer Selection and Profiling for Zero-Shot OOD Detection using Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28986)**  `arXiv:2611.28986`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Saahil Islam, Adarsh Bhandary Panambur, Teodora Spasojevic, Eduardo Castaneda, Andreas Maier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) enable zero-shot out-of-distribution (OOD) detection in medical imaging, yet existing approaches often rely on final-layer confidence or entropy-based layer selection, potentially missing complementary information. We show that low in-distribution (ID) confidence entropy does not reliably predict OOD discrimination. We introduce CLASP (Complementary Layer Selection and Profiling), an ID-only framework that jointly models layer--prompt responses and visual features. CLASP ranks visual encoder layers using Gaussian conditional mutual information and selects a compact subset through anchored spectral selection, without fixing the number of layers. It then combines the selected layer--prompt profiles with PCA-compressed visual embeddings and fits a single shrinkage-regularized Gaussian. OOD scores are computed using squared Mahalanobis distance, without fine-tuning, OOD training data, or test-time adaptation. Across histopathology, chest X-ray, and brain MRI benchmarks with UniMedCLIP and BioMedCLIP, CLASP performs strongly under varied distribution shifts. On MIDOG near-OOD, it achieves 87.54% AUROC with UniMedCLIP, outperforming confidence-based baselines; with BioMedCLIP, it remains competitive but trails visual-only Mahalanobis modeling. Ablations highlight the value of layer--prompt interactions and cross-feature dependencies, while exposing shift-dependent contributions and limitations under semantic shifts.
  </details>

- **[Video-as-Weights: Compiling a Video Once into Parametric and Token Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28221)**  `arXiv:2611.28221`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Ian Miao, Chen Qu, Bill Bi, Donna Schut, Yuntian Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) represent videos as thousands of visual tokens, creating a large key-value cache whose memory grows with video length. At the same time, small VLMs that fit on limited hardware understand video far worse than large models. Existing compression methods shorten visual tokens into a brief prompt to save memory, yet the small model still processes them through fixed weights and cannot close this capability gap.To address both challenges, we introduce Video-as-Weights (VaW), which compiles a video once without seeing any question into the parametric and token memory of a frozen interpreter VLM. Specifically, a compiler VLM watches the video, and a single weight decoder jointly generates the interpreter's LoRA weights and optional prefix tokens from every compiler layer, enabling both same-model compilation and strong-to-weak transfer across model sizes and depths.Across six video QA benchmarks, a 0.8B interpreter beats full in-context reading by 1.7 points when compiled by itself and by 4.3 points with a 9B compiler, reducing context from up to 8,192 visual tokens to 64 prefix tokens while using 5.5x less memory than the video's key-value cache. Generated weights add 3.9 points over token compression alone, and all-layer decoding outperforms layer-wise generation by 2.9 points. Mechanistic analysis shows that with prefix tokens, the weights mainly alter attention queries and MLP down projections to guide retrieval and store a baseline summary, whereas without tokens, they shift to attention output projections to write video content directly into the residual stream.
  </details>

- **[HierSum: A Global and Local Attention Mechanism for Video Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.18689)**  `arXiv:2504.18689`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Apoorva Beedu, Irfan Essa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video summarization creates an abridged version (i.e., a summary) that provides a quick overview of the video while retaining pertinent information. In this work, we focus on summarizing instructional videos and propose a method for breaking down a video into meaningful segments, each corresponding to essential steps in the video. We propose **HierSum**, a hierarchical approach that integrates fine-grained local cues from subtitles with global contextual information provided by video-level instructions. Our approach utilizes the "most replayed" statistic as a supervisory signal to identify critical segments, thereby improving the effectiveness of the summary. We evaluate on benchmark datasets such as TVSum, BLiSS,this http URL, and the WikiHow test set, and show that HierSum consistently outperforms existing methods in key metrics such as F1-score and rank correlation. We also curate a new multi-modal dataset using WikiHow and EHow videos, and associated articles containing step-by-step instructions. Through extensive ablation studies, we demonstrate that training on this dataset significantly enhances summarization on the target datasets. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PaMuk-Bench: A Parallel Multilingual Multitask Benchmark for Vision-Language Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27521)**  `arXiv:2611.27521`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Silviu-Florin Gheorghe, Radu-Stefan Popa, Adriana Valentina Costache, Adrian-Gabriel Chifu, Alessia Amelio, Paul Irofti, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While vision-language models (VLMs) currently obtain notable results for highly-studied languages, e.g. English, they exhibit significant performance drops in certain low-resource languages, especially due to the lack of annotated datasets. To this end, we propose PaMuk-Bench, a novel parallel multilingual multitask benchmark for vision-language tasks. Our benchmark includes a set of over 10K curated English prompts that are translated into six languages: Arabic, Danish, French, Hindi, Italian, Romanian. Parallel translations, which are obtained via a semi-automatic process, along with accompanying generated and retrieved images enable the comparative development and evaluation of VLMs across a wide range of tasks: text-to-image generation, image captioning, content-based image retrieval, image generation difficulty estimation, image retrieval difficulty estimation. We empirically show that zero-shot English and multilingual pre-trained VLMs compare consistently worse than their fine-tuned counterparts, confirming that PaMuk-Bench is a valuable resource that can drastically reduce the language gap across a variety of VLM architectures and tasks. PaMuk-Bench is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HyperCLOVA X 32B Think](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03286)**  `arXiv:2601.03286`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _NAVER Cloud HyperCLOVA X Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this report, we present HyperCLOVA X 32B Think, a vision-language model designed with particular emphasis on reasoning within the Korean linguistic and cultural context, as well as agentic ability. HyperCLOVA X 32B Think is pre-trained with a strong focus on reasoning capabilities and subsequently post-trained to support multimodal understanding, enhanced reasoning, agentic behaviors, and alignment with human preferences. Experimental evaluations against comparably sized models demonstrate that our model achieves strong performance on Korean text-to-text and vision-to-text benchmarks, as well as on agent-oriented evaluation tasks. By open-sourcing HyperCLOVA X 32B Think, we aim to support broader adoption and facilitate further research and innovation across both academic and industrial communities.
  </details>

- **[Parsing Visual Latents for Multimodal Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31021)**  `arXiv:2611.31021`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuoying Yang, Yichen Guo, Kathy Jia, Zichang Su, Ayesha Kashif, Nange Hai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning across multiple views requires inferring the 3D relations between objects and the camera from 2D observations, which depends on cross-view correspondence and scene geometry that vision-language models (VLMs) do not reliably extract. Pretrained vision encoders already capture this information in their latent features, with CLIP linking objects to image regions, DINOv3 matching semantics across views, and VGGT encoding multi-view geometry. However, VLMs do not benefit from these features when we present them directly in context as grayscale feature images. We therefore propose LatentParse, a training-free framework that parses latent features into explicit spatial evidence for VLMs. In LatentParse, an analyst VLM probes the features of all three encoders with predefined tools that locate objects, track them across views, and measure motion between views. It then distills the results into a concise text report, which is provided together with the images to the VLM to answer the question. We evaluate LatentParse on MMSI-Bench, MV-RoboBench and SPAR-Bench, where it improves all five VLMs over the photo-only baseline by 0.7-2.4 percentage points on average. On MMSI-Bench, it raises Qwen3.8-27B from 34.4% to 39.5%, with the largest improvements of 19.2 percentage points on the camera-related questions. These results suggest that latent features become useful for spatial reasoning once they are parsed into relation evidence, without training. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HighwayVerse: A Chain-of-Inspection Benchmark for Highway Infrastructure Anomaly Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28800)**  `arXiv:2611.28800`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junyi Yang, Zheng Si, Yilin Zhai, Pengpeng Ouyang, Hongxi Wang, Kangze Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practical highway infrastructure inspection entails progressive diagnostic reasoning from visual perception to maintenance decisions, yet existing benchmarks remain confined to isolated tasks, leaving Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) insufficiently evaluated across sequential inspection stages. Meanwhile, current datasets focus primarily on highway pavement defects, falling short of real-world inspection across diverse infrastructure components such as highway pavements, guardrails, glare screens, and drainage systems. To address these gaps, we formulate the Highway Infrastructure Inspection Progression Chain (HIIP-Chain), spanning Anomaly Detection, Anomaly Recognition, Condition Analysis, and Maintenance Resolution, and introduce HighwayVerse. HighwayVerse comprises 14,143 real-world Ground and Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) inspection records spanning 100 and 17 anomaly categories, respectively, with 84,858 expert-reviewed question-answer pairs. We evaluate 12 open-source and closed-source MLLMs using the chained metric Final, which credits a correct maintenance decision only when all preceding diagnostic stages are completed correctly. Results show that although models achieve relatively high accuracy when initially judging whether an anomaly is present, their markedly lower Final scores expose struggles across downstream recognition, analysis, and decision-making. This discrepancy indicates that current MLLMs do not fully comprehend specific highway anomaly patterns beyond superficial initial judgments. Furthermore, we propose LaneRAG, a training-free retrieval-augmented framework that provides hierarchical reference guidance achieving consistent performance improvements across diverse MLLMs. Our dataset, evaluation benchmark, and source code will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[CLASP: Deciding Structure and Claims Before Decoding for Whole-Slide Pathology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28576)**  `arXiv:2611.28576`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ziaullah Khan, Rashadul Islam Sumon, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Duc-Son Pham, Moon-Il Joo, Hee-Cheol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole-slide pathology report generation has advanced through stronger slide encoders, retrieved knowledge, and, recently, slide-level multimodal LLMs. However, these generators leave every report-level choice to the decoder: how long the report is, how it is organised, and which findings it states and where. On the standard PathText-BRCA benchmark, we find that these choices dominate n-gram overlap, as reports from the query's hospital selected without learning approach the state of the art, while decoders under-generate and fall back to the most frequent findings. We present CLASP, a decide-then-decode generator that makes these choices explicitly, once per slide. Before decoding, an Exemplar Structure Prior (ESP) takes the sentence plan and length from reports of image-similar cases, a Calibrated Claim Decision (CCD) selects the findings from calibrated slide posteriors, and a Claim-to-Sentence Placement (CSP) assigns each finding to a planned sentence. A Plan-Conditioned Decoder (PCD), refined by structure-anchored sequence training, then writes the report under these decisions. With 35M parameters, CLASP outperforms retrained report generators and slide-level LLMs of up to 7B parameters, matches the strongest published results, and generalises to lung cancer from unseen hospitals. Its decisions change what the reports state, not only their length, transfer to an LLM decoder, and can withhold what H&E cannot show: withholding receptor status leaves 23% fewer contradictions with clinical records than the same network without decisions.
  </details>

- **[When Simpler Is Better: Evaluating Translation Pipelines for Medieval Latin Manuscripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03836)**  `arXiv:2607.03836`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Nguyen Kim Hai Bui, Md. Easin Arafat, Tamás Gábor Orosz, Mufti Mahmud_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite remarkable progress in machine translation, Vision Language Models (VLMs) struggle on historical manuscripts, a domain that stresses core Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities: low-resource transliteration, archaic vocabulary, and noisy input signals. We present a systematic framework for evaluating the full image-to-translation pipeline on medieval Latin manuscripts, a setting in which scribal shorthand, ligatures, and parchment degradation expose failure modes that are invisible in clean-text benchmarks. Benchmarking on the CATMuS Latin dataset reveals a specialization gap: domain-specific Optical Character Recognition (OCR) models reduce character error rate by up to 4.3$\times$ compared to general-purpose VLMs, despite operating at orders of magnitude fewer parameters. We introduce the Interpres-Parallel-Corpus (IPC), a novel dataset comprising 1,383 aligned manuscript image lines, transcriptions, and expert translations, to our knowledge the first of its kind for medieval Latin. Our experiments uncover a complexity paradox: the simplest pipeline, a specialized OCR model feeding directly into a VLM, outperforms all multi-component variants. Adding retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or post-OCR correction introduces prompt saturation and error propagation that degrade aggregate translation quality. These findings offer both a new benchmark and practical guidance for deploying translation systems in low-resource historical settings.
  </details>

- **[Beyond SFT-to-RL: Pre-alignment via Black-Box On-Policy Distillation for Multimodal RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.28123)**  `arXiv:2604.28123`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sudong Wang, Weiquan Huang, Xiaomin Yu, Zuhao Yang, Hehai Lin, Keming Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The standard post-training recipe for large multimodal models (LMMs) applies supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated demonstrations followed by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). However, SFT introduces distributional drift that neither preserves the model's original capabilities nor faithfully matches the supervision distribution. This problem is further amplified in multimodal reasoning, where perception errors and reasoning failures follow distinct drift patterns that compound during subsequent RL. We introduce PRISM, a three-stage pipeline that mitigates this drift by inserting an explicit distribution-alignment stage between SFT and RLVR. Building on the principle of on-policy distillation (OPD), PRISM casts alignment as a black-box, response-level adversarial game between the policy and a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) discriminator with dedicated perception and reasoning experts, providing disentangled corrective signals that steer the policy toward the supervision distribution without requiring access to teacher logits. While 1.26M public demonstrations suffice for broad SFT initialization, distribution alignment demands higher-fidelity supervision; we therefore curate 113K additional demonstrations from Gemini 3 Flash, featuring dense visual grounding and step-by-step reasoning on the hardest unsolved problems. Experiments on Qwen3-VL show that PRISM consistently improves downstream RLVR performance across multiple RL algorithms (GRPO, DAPO, GSPO) and diverse multimodal benchmarks, improving average accuracy by +4.4 and +6.0 points over the SFT-to-RLVR baseline on 4B and 8B, respectively. Our code, data, and model checkpoints are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Replay Before You Reply: Second-Look Prompting for Unified Multimodal Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30909)**  `arXiv:2611.30909`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yicheng Zhang, Xun Zhang, Jingyuan Ma, Tianyu Yu, Yuan Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Solving multimodal tasks requires Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to form a unified understanding of the image and the question text. However, under causal attention in current VLMs, a token can attend only to what precedes it. Whichever modality comes first is therefore encoded under partial observation of the input, uninformed by the rest of the multimodal context. We introduce Second-Look Prompting (SLP), a training-free method that repeats the image-question pair in the input. In the repeated copy, every visual and textual token can attend to the complete input, yielding a unified multimodal encoding of the pair. Moreover, the added visual tokens contribute computation beyond re-exposing the image: even a blank image in their place can improve reasoning. Across 12 benchmarks, SLP improves the overall average score of every evaluated model, spanning nine open-source models (3B-72B) and three proprietary frontier models, with gains reaching 5.6 points for Gemini 3 Flash on MATH-Vision. Our analysis reveals two complementary mechanisms: the question guides where the second image attends, and the added visual tokens act as a prefill scratchpad whose hidden states retain answer-relevant information from the image and question. These findings highlight input presentation as a simple yet effective way to improve VLM performance.
  </details>

- **[Speak When You See It: Proactive Task Assistants Should Wait for Evidence, Not the Clock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30895)**  `arXiv:2611.30895`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Bohe Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive assistants that guide people through physical tasks must decide, every few seconds, whether a step is complete and whether a mistake has occurred. We find that two benchmarks for this task largely reward knowing when rather than seeing what: a timer that never looks at the video, raising an alarm at 25 s and confirming each step at 30 s, reaches a mistake F1 of 0.373 on Ego-MC-Bench, above every published result (at most 0.27). We formalize this as the video-blind frontier, the best trade-off between completion accuracy and mistake F1 that policies reach from the elapsed time, the instruction and training statistics alone: every published per-step result on Ego-MC-Bench lies inside it, and so do all twelve zero-shot models we run on QICD. To move beyond it, we introduce Evidence-Contrastive Training (ECT): fine-tune on the benchmark's own questions, and pair its positives with negatives from the same recording at the same elapsed time, so that elapsed time cannot separate the two sides of a pair. Trained on 209 QICD videos in about 15 GPU-hours, a 2B model with ECT crosses the frontier on both benchmarks in all four seeds; transferred to Ego-MC-Bench without adaptation, it confirms 59% of mistake-free steps in time, against 30% for a published model with the same backbone trained on about 25k synthetic counterfactuals and real recordings. Its decisions follow the video beyond what timing explains: at equal elapsed time it tells a finished step from an unfinished one in 90-92% of test pairs, where the instruction's typical duration reaches 65%, rescaling the time stamps between the frames leaves it essentially unchanged, and swapping in another recording, of the same recipe where one exists, cuts its completion accuracy by two thirds. The code and model weights will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[PixelOne: From Options to Pixels in a Single Forward for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30235)**  `arXiv:2611.30235`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kanghui Tian, Shuai Dong, Yuhan Zhu, Congcong Wang, Ji Zhao, Tianxiang Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.
  </details>

- **[SCRA-CLIP: Spherical-Contextual Representation Adaptation for Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29545)**  `arXiv:2611.29545`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Peng Chen, Chao Huang, Wenqiang Wang, Mingbo Yang, Yannan Chen, Yunkang Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language models have facilitated zero-shot anomaly detection (ZSAD) through transferable visual-semantic representations. However, detecting fine-grained anomalies remains challenging due to inadequate modeling of diverse anomaly patterns and their spatial context. To address these challenges, we propose SCRA-CLIP, a Spherical-Contextual Representation Adaptation framework that jointly enhances anomaly-aware textual semantics and context-sensitive visual representations. Specifically, we introduce Spherical Prototype-Conditioned Prompting (SPCP), which employs von Mises--Fisher mixtures to model the directional distributions of normal and abnormal patch features. These state-specific prototypes are incorporated into textual contexts to guide the learning of more discriminative textual representations. Furthermore, we develop Context-Deviation Guided Visual Adaptation (CGVA), which captures local feature deviations relative to multi-scale neighborhood contexts and refines patch representations through adaptive gating. By integrating distribution-aware semantic modeling with context-sensitive visual adaptation, SCRA-CLIP facilitates more discriminative cross-modal matching for fine-grained anomaly detection and localization. Extensive experiments on 11 industrial and medical benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization capability of SCRA-CLIP across diverse unseen categories and domains. The code will be publicly available upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Seeing vs. Knowing: Counterfactual Supervision and Correction for 3D-Aware Object Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29542)**  `arXiv:2611.29542`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jian Zahng, Zhijun Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rotating, translating, or rescaling an object in a photograph requires revealing unseen surfaces, preserving visible appearance, and updating shadows and reflections. Reconstructed 3D assets, rendered at the target pose as a 3D state, provide explicit geometric guidance, but their imperfect textures may conflict with the photograph, while standard paired training does not explicitly teach the editor which source to trust. To address this, we propose counterfactual state supervision, which constructs controlled photo--state conflicts through hidden-surface repainting, hidden geometry with re-rendered shadows and reflections, and visible-surface texture replacement. This teaches the editor to follow the 3D state for unseen content while preserving photograph-visible appearance. However, learning this distinction does not ensure that it is maintained during sampling. We therefore introduce symmetric counterfactual correction, which uses state-side and photo-side twins, which share the photograph and the state respectively, to construct directed latent perturbations toward condition-inconsistent targets and train recovery toward the target specified by the current condition. Both strategies operate only during training, requiring no architectural changes or additional inference steps. Experiments on OBJect and SpatialEdit-Bench demonstrate strong spatial editing performance and content preservation, while controlled ablations show improved source selection and sustained responsiveness to conditioning during sampling.
  </details>

- **[Can an Agent Trust Its Own Zoom? Cross-Magnification Provenance Verification for Whole-Slide Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29150)**  `arXiv:2611.29150`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yan Wang, Yiran Song, Zizhuo Gong, Jianxiang Qi, Zhongxing Xu, Lingfei Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents navigate whole-slide images through magnified observations associated with claimed locations. However, a visually plausible observation does not necessarily originate from its claimed location, particularly across magnifications and in regions with similar tissue structures. We formulate cross-magnification provenance verification (CMPV) to evaluate whether visual evidence supports a claimed spatial association, recover the source when possible, and abstain when evidence is insufficient. We introduce PyraFault, a benchmark of 3.06 million coordinate-supervised examples from 2,200 cases across five TCGA cohorts, covering within-context substitutions, absent-source faults, and geometric perturbations. We further propose ZoomTrace, a claim-independent verifier that estimates the observation's source before evaluating its agreement with the claimed location. It combines physical-scale alignment, visual retrieval, geometric refinement, and reliability estimation using spatially disjoint competing matches. On 503,320 test examples, ZoomTrace achieves 0.969 AUPRC and 0.987 AUROC, compared with 0.789 and 0.903 for Efficient LoFTR. Further evaluations reveal trade-offs in reliability estimation and limitations in vision-language models, cross-dataset retrieval, and calibration. Integrating ZoomTrace into visual agent tool paths reduces exposure to corrupted observations and silent failures, while downstream accuracy improvements depend on the agent and verification threshold.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Is Not Planning: Spatiotemporal Planning and Plan Repair in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28726)**  `arXiv:2611.28726`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Siyuan Li, Ethan Lin, Xiang Li, Tianming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning in a visual world means reading the layout, foreseeing how the state will change, and following the task rules all at once. When a plan fails, a model should also use what went wrong to fix it. We call these two abilities spatiotemporal planning and plan repair, and test them with Railbound-Bench. A model sees a railway puzzle, adds tracks under a budget, and submits one complete layout; a simulator then moves all cars at once and decides whether the layout works. After a failure, the model sees what happened and may submit a new layout, up to five attempts in total. On the 200-puzzle main set, the best of nine models solves 59.5% within five attempts, the next 39.5%, and the other seven at most 10.0%; a 1,000-puzzle extended set with a wider range of difficulty is far easier, at 90.9% for the best model. Controlled studies on 180 fixed puzzles show that knowing the task is not the same as using it. GPT-6.1 Terra copies 97.2% of boards exactly and, given a correct layout as text, predicts all four requested future states in 91.3% of cases, yet only 22.8% of its first layouts work. Planning fails more often as the cars' routes interact, even among puzzles that a search solver finds equally easy. Visual feedback removes the reported fault in 175 of 417 first revisions, but only 43 of these revisions solve the puzzle, and at an equal budget of 40k tokens it does no better than fresh retries. The code and data are open-sourced on GitHub atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LoopSeek: Decoupling System One Search from System Two Answering for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28635)**  `arXiv:2611.28635`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengzhong Wang, Lang Lin, Xinyang Liu, YueQin Yin, Mingyuan Zhou, Shengwei An_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video question answering requires finding relevant evidence before reasoning over it. With a limited frame budget, single-pass frame selection can miss critical evidence, while adaptive search often relies on repeated text generation to decide where to look. We introduce LoopSeek, a framework that decouples evidence search from answer generation through a division of labor between a non-thinking System I and a generative System II. Our key insight is that search can be expressed as a sequence of small, typed decisions, allowing System I to find the evidence that System II needs. Four System I role agents navigate a tree of video windows and coordinate through probability outputs to select frames, assess evidence, and revise the search without generating text. To align search with System II, System I learns from annotated evidence on questions that System II answers correctly only from that evidence, and the verifier's evidence estimates steer the search without calling System II, which stays frozen and answers once from the selected frames. On five long-video benchmarks, with a frozen Qwen3-VL-8B reading 32 frames, LoopSeek improves average accuracy from 53.5 to 63.9 over the uniform baseline and takes 32--80\% less GPU time per question than agentic methods.
  </details>

- **[Learning a Rule Bank to Revise 3D CT Reports with Independent Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28281)**  `arXiv:2611.28281`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Renjie Liang, Mengxian Lyu, Zijian Xu, Boxin Zhao, Yang Yang, Jinqian Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--language models can generate fluent radiology reports from 3D chest CT scans, yet they may still omit abnormalities or misstate findings. Independent image analysis using models separate from the report model provides additional observations, but acting on uncertain evidence may introduce new errors. We propose an agent system that learns a revision policy represented as an executable rule bank for each report model. Inspired by experience banks, the agent proposes and refines rules using revision outcomes across development cases. During inference, the retained rules guide local edits using independent classifier predictions and quantitative image measurements without retraining the report model. Unedited sentences are preserved, and each change is linked to the rule that triggered it. On CT-RATE, revision increases CE-F1 by 11.5 points for SelfCue-KD and 0.9 points for NV-Reason-CT, with gains across five draft-sampling seeds. SelfCue-KD's CT-FineBench score also increases by 15.2 points. Draft-only rule learning yields limited gains, while the two image-evidence sources provide complementary benefits for abnormality detection. Measurements also supply numerical details missing from the drafts. These results support learning model-specific rules to control how independent image evidence informs report revision. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Output Suppression: Mitigating Privacy Risks in LVLMs via Visual Intrinsic Privacy Erasure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28135)**  `arXiv:2611.28135`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangkui Cao, Jie Zhang, Meina Kan, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models can extract personally identifiable information from images, making privacy protection essential for their deployment in perception-oriented tasks. Existing targeted fine-tuning and model-editing methods usually suppress sensitive answers by aligning the model toward safe responses. In this paper, we show that such protection can be illusory: although the fine-tuned model refuses to disclose private information under normal decoding, privacy leakage still occurs in sentence completion scenarios. This indicates that existing algorithms only change the model's output distribution, but the model still retains the ability to perceive private information. Private information therefore still remains inside the model. To address this problem, we propose Visual Intrinsic Privacy Erasure (VIPE), which targets privacy-related visual representations to weaken the model's ability to perceive sensitive information. VIPE constructs paired privacy-sensitive and privacy-masked images, uses their internal feature residuals to identify privacy-related visual components, and suppresses these components before they are perceived by the model. To preserve benign visual representations, the erasure objective projects feature differences onto a relaxed benign subspace and adaptively controls the erasure strength. VIPE compels the model to ignore the extraction of sensitive information in the visual representation space, thereby blocking the source of privacy leakage more fundamentally rather than merely altering the model's output distribution. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL and LLaVA-OneVision show that VIPE substantially alleviates prefix-triggered privacy leakage while maintaining competitive utility on general visual-language benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Seeing the Invisible: How Vision-Language Models Read, Predict and Trace Radio Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28123)**  `arXiv:2611.28123`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yiyang Zhu, Xin Li, Guangyuan Liu, Ziheng Liu, Jiayi Zhang, Chau Yuen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Operating in the physical world requires reasoning beyond what cameras directly capture. In a city, radio signals are invisible to the lens, yet their strength is governed by the buildings and streets an image does show. Given only these visible structures, can vision-language models (VLMs) infer the invisible radio field they shape? Probing true physical reasoning requires exact fields in real cities, alongside controlled interventions where a single physical factor changes. We build a label-free engine that turns any coordinate on Earth into a ray-traceable city, simulates matched single-factor counterfactuals, and computes every answer exactly. This yields RadioVISTA, a benchmark and training dataset for visual reasoning about invisible fields. Physically, a radio field is the sum of its propagation paths, yet across six continents we uncover a striking field-path dissociation: frontier VLMs accurately judge where a field is strong, but fail to tell whether a single direct or one-wall path exists. Open models fail even earlier at reading the field itself, and are systematically wrong, scoring below random guessing even when credited for any of four attempts. On unseen cities, fine-tuning brings a 9B open model close to frontier proprietary models on fields, yet neither supervised nor reinforcement fine-tuning improves path tracing. Supervising only final answers teaches models to approximate the field, yet leaves single-path geometry out of reach.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Memory-Constrained Video Object Segmentation via Subspace Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28087)**  `arXiv:2611.28087`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hai-Anh Le, Le-Trung Nguyen, Enzo Tartaglione_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory-based architectures for video object segmentation (VOS) have achieved remarkable performance by maintaining dense feature representations from past frames in a streaming Memory Bank. However, storing these features over time incurs substantial memory overhead and computational cost. In this work, we identify significant temporal redundancy in memory representations, where consecutive frames exhibit highly overlapping low-rank subspaces that remain stable over time. Building on this observation, we propose Memory Subspace Iteration (MSI), a training-free, model-agnostic plugin that exploits temporal subspace stability to efficiently compress streaming memory features. Extensive experiments on standard VOS benchmarks demonstrate that MSI preserves segmentation accuracy across multiple architectures while reducing the memory bank footprint by up to 25%. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Violence Understanding and Temporal Retrieval in Movies with Adaptive Audio-Visual Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28080)**  `arXiv:2611.28080`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaqian Li, Rohitash Chandra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Violence in films and streaming media remains a concern for audience protection and creates substantial workload for manual content classification. Existing violence-detection methods often rely on limited modalities or short video clips, making it difficult to capture complementary evidence and localise violent events in long, untrimmed videos. In this study, we develop a framework for multimodal video understanding that classifies violence severity, predicts multiple violence types, and locates violent events in time. We combine temporally aligned visual and audio features extracted using pretrained encoders through attention-based adaptive fusion, followed by Transformer-based temporal modelling. Our framework supports three complementary tasks: video-level violence-severity classification, multi-label violence-type prediction, and temporal localisation of violent events. We evaluate the framework on the XD-Violence benchmark and examine its cross-domain performance using AIRTLab. On XD-Violence, our multimodal model achieves 93.27\% severity-classification accuracy and a micro-F1 score of 88.60\% for multi-label violence-type prediction, outperforming the corresponding unimodal configurations. We achieve a mean temporal intersection-over-union (tIoU) of 0.5075 for temporal localisation. Our cross-domain experiments reveal performance degradation under different recording conditions, while our movie case studies demonstrate the framework's potential to retrieve potentially violent scenes for further review. Our results highlight the practical value of multimodal intelligent systems in supporting human reviewers with the identification, classification, and temporal retrieval of violent content in films and streaming media.
  </details>

- **[Search-RSI: Expanding Search Worlds Along Multimodal Agents' Capability Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27780)**  `arXiv:2611.27780`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengchao Lv, Yin Wang, Chunzheng Zhu, Wanli Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal search agents answer knowledge intensive visual questions by grounding them in retrieved evidence, and recent recipes train them on multi hop tasks that are synthesized from knowledge graphs and grown online to keep pace with the agent. These recipes, however, treat the searchable world as fixed: a new question can be well formed and verifiable while its evidence is absent from the retrieval backend, so the agent issues the right query, retrieves nothing, and is penalized as if its policy were at fault. Such unreachable negatives accumulate with every round of growth. We propose Search-RSI, a recursive self improvement framework that expands the search world along the agent's capability frontier. In each round, the current policy diagnoses its partially mastered regions of a task space spanned by hop depth, relation rarity, and distractor density; new tasks there are grown together with their evidence; and the same policy decides how they are used: candidates on which its probes disagree feed reinforcement learning, while those beyond its reach become hint guided demonstrations of its own. Keeping the policy inside this loop matters: an improver frozen at the start of training loses 3.2 points. On nine multimodal search benchmarks, Search-RSI keeps improving where static task growth saturates, raising the average accuracy of the recipe it builds on by 9.8 and 9.5 points at 9B and 27B, of which 7.0 points are out of reach for a world that is merely made consistent. The loop uses no external model and only privileged task structure that is removed before training, at 1.27x the compute of static growth.
  </details>

- **[DuoJEPA: Cross-Modal Translation Between Unaligned Self-Supervised Embedding Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15968)**  `arXiv:2611.15968`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mahmut S. Gokmen, Mitchell A. Klusty, Evan W. Damron, Caroline N. Leach, Samuel E. Armstrong, V.K. Cody Bumgardner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from paired modalities is commonly equated with aligning them: two encoders are trained until the embeddings of a pair coincide in a shared space. We study whether cross-modal retrieval and classification can instead be obtained while the two encoders' own embedding spaces remain directly unaligned. DuoJEPA trains one encoder per modality with the self-supervised LeJEPA objective and appends to each encoder a single transformer layer that translates its embedding into the other encoder's space, supervised by a contrastive objective computed entirely within that target space. No loss term compares the two own spaces directly, although the encoders are optimised jointly through the translation objective. In our experiments, the translator's residual connections determine where the cross-modal pressure is absorbed: with a residual translator the two encoders become directly aligned, whereas with a skipless (residual-free) translator a paired-cosine test detects no alignment in the final models, and at most a weak one in the ablations, at comparable cross-modal performance. The learned class-token translation is well approximated by a single scaled rotation of the source space. We evaluate DuoJEPA on two modality pairs that differ in nearly every respect. On chest radiographs and reports from CheXpert Plus, it attains a zero-shot AUC of 86.6 and a cross-modal retrieval score of 78.5, compared with 60.0 and 64.2 for our reimplementation of LeVLJEPA with the same encoders and data. On Sentinel-2 optical and Sentinel-1 radar imagery, the frozen encoders fall within the range of published Sentinel foundation models under our reproduction of the Copernicus-Bench linear-probe protocol, the translator retrieves the paired image of the other sensor among 4,000 candidates in 77-79% of cases, and a patch-level extension transfers the within-image similarity structure of the scene.
  </details>

- **[MoT3DVG: A Benchmark for Outdoor 3D Visual Grounding with Motion-Aware Descriptions and Temporal Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11334)**  `arXiv:2611.11334`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shijia Zhao, Xusheng Guo, Qiming Xia, Xun Huang, Kezheng Xiong, Zhihong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D visual grounding (3DVG) localizes language-referred objects in 3D scenes, with broad applicability in both indoor and outdoor environments. However, existing 3DVG research focuses on static indoor objects, limiting its use in dynamic outdoor autonomous driving. Motion descriptions, which capture the temporal dynamics of moving objects, offer a promising way to address this challenge. We therefore introduce MoT3DVG, a large-scale dataset for dynamic-aware outdoor 3DVG with temporally evolving motion descriptions. It contains 850 scenarios with 31,128 frames from nuScenes dataset, and provides 144,568 language prompts with motion-aware descriptions for dynamic objects across time. We further propose DynaVG, a novel framework that effectively leverages temporal cues for outdoor 3DVG. In addition to the standard modality-specific encoders, DynaVG introduces a Short-Long Integrated Dynamic Encoding (SLIDE) module before the language-point cloud alignment. SLIDE models both local motion cues and global temporal context via short-term window encoding and long-term window shifting. Extensive experiments demonstrate its state-of-the-art performance on MoT3DVG, validating the effectiveness of motion-aware encoding. Our work reveals open challenges and promising directions for future research in outdoor 3DVG.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge-Guided Floodwater Level Reasoning for Cloud-Edge Collaborative Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01970)**  `arXiv:2611.01970`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Ziyi Geng, Lu Tao, Qian Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frequent urban floodwater poses a serious threat to the safety and efficiency of intelligent transportation systems. Existing vision-based methods for floodwater level detection face two main limitations. First, the lack of explicit reasoning leads to poor detection performance and little interpretability. Second, these methods struggle to generalize to the complex and varied real-world road scenes seen from different viewpoints. To address this, we reformulate the task as an explicit visual reasoning problem and propose HydroReasoner, a floodwater level reasoning framework designed for cloud-edge collaboration. HydroReasoner builds on Qwen2.5-VL and is trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization. To address the scarcity of reasoning process annotations, we build a floodwater domain knowledge graph HydroKG and design a novel KG path reward. Through deterministic graph traversal over HydroKG, this reward supervises the model's reasoning process at a fine granularity, which improves both reasoning and generalization. For edge deployment, we apply 4-bit quantization to the model, cutting GPU memory usage by 57% and inference latency by 43% while out-of-domain accuracy drops by only 0.2 percentage points. We also propose a confidence-based cloud-edge routing mechanism, which improves system safety and eases the computational load on the cloud. In addition, we build HydroLevel, a floodwater benchmark dataset of 3,000 images, for training and evaluating the model. Experimental results show that HydroReasoner achieves detection accuracy 4.0 and 13.6 percentage points higher than conventional vision baselines in the in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations respectively, with performance dropping by only 4.4 percentage points out of domain.
  </details>

- **[ARCH-B: Architectural Representation, Comprehension and Hierarchy Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34047)**  `arXiv:2609.34047`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kieran Sagar Parikh, Jose Luis Garcia del Castillo y Lopez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal models increasingly interpret visual environments, but their ability to recognize the same building across photographs, floor plans, elevations, sections, and renderings remains poorly characterized. We introduce ARCH-B, a benchmark of 354 four-choice questions across 11 cross-representational archetypes, constructed from a building-linked corpus of 3.9 million architectural images using visually similar distractors, model-guided difficulty screening, and manual validation. We evaluate 25 multimodal models and collect 5,830 responses from non-expert human participants. Model accuracy ranges from 10.45% to 83.90%, compared with a human baseline of 35.35%. Models perform comparatively well on mixed-representation outlier detection and photograph matching, but remain weaker on floorplan-to-photograph correspondence. Human and model difficulty across archetypes is only weakly correlated (Spearman's (\rho=0.33)). Held-out evaluation confirms that the difficulty identified during screening generalizes beyond the curation models. ARCH-B provides a diagnostic evaluation of visual correspondence and representation transfer across architectural media.
  </details>

- **[CoRE: Weakly Supervised Coarse-to-Fine Risk Evidence Learning in Driving Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25344)**  `arXiv:2608.25344`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kaiser Hamid, Can Cui, Nade Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driver perceived risk evolves in dynamic environments and is supported by specific scene entities, yet supervision is typically limited to coarse video-level judgments. Learning when supporting evidence emerges and which entities support a risk predictor would require costly temporal- and entity-level annotations. We introduce CoRE, a weakly supervised coarse-to-fine framework that learns fine-grained prediction support from coarse video supervision. CoRE trains and freezes a video-level predictor. Structured interventions over candidate temporal regions or entity tracks measure how each candidate changes the coarse prediction, producing graded prediction-effect targets. These targets are distilled into a student that directly predicts temporal and entity support from the original video, without interventions at inference. We evaluate this learning principle across three complementary settings. RISEE tests perceived-risk support from subjective clip-level judgments without temporal or entity-level risk annotations. DoTA provides independent temporal event annotations for evaluating weakly supervised traffic-anomaly localization. UCF-Crime tests whether the same coarse-to-fine mechanism extends to a standard non-driving anomaly-detection benchmark. Across these settings, CoRE learns informative fine-grained support from coarse supervision, with strong temporal localization on DoTA and competitive performance on UCF-Crime. These results establish coarse video predictions as supervision for recovering the fine-grained evidence supporting them, without corresponding fine-grained labels.
  </details>

- **[Which Modality Decides? Counterfactual Modality Attribution for Multimodal Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00076)**  `arXiv:2608.00076`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Vahidin Hasic, Chao Wang, Luis C. Garcia-Peraza-Herrera, David Watson, Senka Krivic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal classifiers can achieve high accuracy while exploiting modality-specific shortcuts. Modality attribution quantifies the contributions of image and text, but does not by itself identify which input changes would alter a suspicious prediction. We introduce Counterfactual Modality Attribution (CMA), which connects modality-level contributions to explicit prediction-changing edits. CMA uses image and text diffusion priors to generate counterfactual references and constructs a two-player reference game whose Shapley--Taylor decomposition separates image, text, and interaction contributions. The method requires class-score queries but no model gradients. Attribution profiles are compared with a reference population to prioritize predictions with atypical modality reliance for review, while counterfactual edits expose localized evidence for inspection. On controlled image- and text-biased classifiers, CMA identifies the ground truth dominant modality for all evaluated inputs. On planted-shortcut benchmarks, it achieves shortcut-reliance detection AUROCs of 0.987 on Synthetic MNIST and 1.000 on OpenI, with image-shortcut localization IoUs of 0.97 and 0.84, respectively. An API experiment demonstrates applicability to a native multimodal language model with scored answer choices. CMA enables predictions to be screened by modality reliance and investigated through explicit counterfactual edits.
  </details>

- **[MuseBench: Benchmarking Intent-Level Audiovisual Arts Understanding in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30026)**  `arXiv:2606.30026`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Fan, Gyusik Seo, Jing Hao, Jaemin Cho, Mohit Bansal, Jaehong Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audiovisual arts encompass diverse creative disciplines, including cinema, visual arts, stage performance, and game design, where artistic meaning arises from deliberate combinations of visual, auditory, and narrative elements (e.g., fear amplified through claustrophobic framing, or grief conveyed through silence and lingering close-ups). Artistic understanding extends beyond recognizing what is depicted to reasoning about why it is expressed through particular creative choices. Despite progress in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), reasoning about artistic intent remains underexplored, as existing benchmarks largely measure perceptual recognition. To address this gap, we introduce MuseBench, a benchmark for evaluating intent-level artistic understanding in MLLMs. It comprises 4,016 questions derived from video essays spanning cinematic arts, static visual arts, stage performing arts, and game arts. To capture the open-ended nature of artistic analysis at scale, the benchmark combines single-select and variable-option multi-select questions. All questions are generated and refined through a four-phase iterative pipeline combining shortcut filtering, adversarial distractors, and expert validation. We evaluate 34 MLLM versions using either video and audio transcripts (V+T) or video, native audio, and text (V+A+T). The highest observed accuracy is 64.62% (V+A+T), trailing the 87.18% human expert baseline. Further analysis reveals that native-audio models score lowest on game arts, models often recover partial interpretations but struggle to identify complete correct option sets on multi-select items, and adaptive frame-selection models trail the strongest video-specific model. These results identify artistic knowledge and complete interpretation as challenges for audiovisual models.
  </details>

- **[AVIS: Adaptive Test-Time Scaling for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11576)**  `arXiv:2606.11576`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmadreza Jeddi, Minh Ngoc Le, Amirhossein Kazerouni, Hakki Can Karaimer, Hue Nguyen, Iqbal Mohomed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern Vision-Language Models (VLMs) benefit from chain-of-thought prompting and test-time scaling, but these gains often come with prohibitive inference cost due to large visual contexts and long decoding chains. We view this cost through two coupled axes: Visual Context Scaling (VCS), which controls how much visual evidence is passed to the language model, and Visual Reasoning Scaling (VRS), which controls how much inference-time reasoning search is performed. Existing methods typically optimize one axis at a time, leaving the joint allocation of compute across these axes underexplored. We introduce Adaptive Visual Inference Scaling (AVIS), a lightweight policy that adapts both VCS and VRS per query. AVIS realizes VCS through Key Diversity Visual (KDV) pruning, a training-free $O(N)$ key-based rule for removing redundant visual tokens before prefilling, and realizes VRS through adaptive self-consistency, using a learned difficulty predictor to select the number of reasoning rollouts. AVIS is deployment-friendly and compatible with shared-prefill inference, where all rollouts reuse a single prefilling pass and KV cache. Across diverse image and video reasoning benchmarks, AVIS improves the accuracy--compute trade-off relative to VCS-only and VRS-only baselines, and remains effective on top of RL post-trained VLMs while keeping compute and latency low.
  </details>

- **[MemOVCD: Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Change Detection via Cross-Temporal Memory Reasoning and Global-Local Adaptive Rectification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26774)**  `arXiv:2604.26774`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zuzheng Kuang, Honghao Chang, Boqiang Liang, Haoqian Wang, Lijun He, Fan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary change detection (OVCD) seeks to identify semantic changes in bi-temporal remote sensing imagery with text-specified target categories. Representative foundation-model pipelines decode the two timestamps independently before comparing their predictions, leaving cross-temporal evidence unavailable to semantic decoding. Moreover, current patch-wise inference can undermine global semantic continuity, yielding fragmented predictions. To address these limitations, we put forward MemOVCD, a training-free framework which reformulates OVCD as cross-temporal memory-conditioned inference. Specifically, MemOVCD casts bi-temporal analysis as a bidirectional two-frame tracking problem and exploits the memory mechanism of video SAM to propagate semantic candidate regions across timestamps. Their features are pooled and averaged into a local exemplar token. Subsequently, a shared memory, whose number of prototypes is determined by local-exemplar availability, aggregates regional features from the unlabeled target collection. The local and shared tokens are then jointly used to condition the subsequent semantic segmentation. We further devise a global-local adaptive rectification strategy, which adaptively fuses local and global predictions to compensate for incomplete spatial support when processing large images while preserving fine-grained details. MemOVCD and its inductive variant L-MemOVCD show favorable performance on five benchmarks, and ablations and analyses further validate its effectiveness under evaluated settings. Our code will be publicly available.
  </details>

- **[EraseAnything++: Enabling Concept Erasure in Rectified Flow Transformers Leveraging Multi-Object Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00978)**  `arXiv:2603.00978`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaoxin Fan, Nanxiang Jiang, Daiheng Gao, Shiji Zhou, Wenjun Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Removing undesired concepts from large-scale text-to-image (T2I) and text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models while preserving overall generative quality remains a major challenge, particularly as modern models such as Stable Diffusion v3, Flux, and OpenSora employ flow-matching and transformer-based architectures and extend to long-horizon video generation. Existing concept erasure methods, designed for earlier T2I/T2V models, often fail to generalize to these paradigms. To address this issue, we propose EraseAnything++, a unified framework for concept erasure in both image and video diffusion models with flow-matching objectives. Central to our approach is formulating concept erasure as a constrained multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly balances concept removal with preservation of generative utility. To solve the resulting conflicting objectives, we introduce an efficient utility-preserving unlearning strategy based on implicit gradient surgery. Furthermore, by integrating LoRA-based parameter tuning with attention-level regularization, our method anchors erasure on key visual representations and propagates it consistently across spatial and temporal dimensions. In the video setting, we further enhance consistency through an anchor-and-propagate mechanism that initializes erasure on reference frames and enforces it throughout subsequent transformer layers, thereby mitigating temporal drift. Extensive experiments on both image and video benchmarks demonstrate that EraseAnything++ substantially outperforms prior methods in erasure effectiveness, generative fidelity, and temporal consistency, establishing a new state of the art for concept erasure in next-generation diffusion models.
  </details>

- **[Toward Cognitive Supersensing in Multimodal Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01541)**  `arXiv:2602.01541`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Boyi Li, Yifan Shen, Yuanzhe Liu, Yifan Xu, Jiateng Liu, Xinzhuo Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in open-vocabulary visual understanding, yet they often struggle with cognitive visual reasoning tasks that require maintaining, transforming, and simulating abstract visual states. Existing approaches primarily scale Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in language, forcing inherently spatial or visual computations into discrete text tokens. We introduce Cognitive Supersensing, a training paradigm that equips MLLMs with latent visual reasoning by integrating a Latent Visual Imagery Prediction (LVIP) head that progressively learns refined visual latent states aligned with the evolving reasoning context and target answer, thereby providing a structured visual grounding signal for internal reasoning. We further introduce a reinforcement learning stage that optimizes rationale trajectories using both answer evidence and LVIP-based latent grounding, encouraging reasoning paths that remain visually consistent. To evaluate the cognitive capabilities of MLLMs, we present CogSense-Bench, a comprehensive visual question answering (VQA) benchmark assessing five cognitive dimensions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MLLMs trained with Cognitive Supersensing significantly outperform state-of-the-art baselines on CogSense-Bench and exhibit superior generalization on out-of-domain mathematics and science VQA benchmarks, suggesting that internal visual imagery may be key to bridging the gap between perceptual recognition and cognitive understanding. Causal analyses through noise injection, latent patching, and linear probing show that LVIP states progressively encode task-relevant visual structure and influence downstream answer probabilities, supporting their role as active reasoning signals.
  </details>

- **[WorldLatent: A Unified 4D Latent Space for World Perception and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31209)**  `arXiv:2611.31209`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingxi Chen, Yujia Chen, Cornelia Fermuller, Yiannis Aloimonos, Wen-Sheng Chu, Du Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative Pre-Training (GPT) represents a profound milestone in natural language processing, serving as the foundation for most of today's state-of-the-art large language models. By casting diverse understanding and generation problems into a shared token representation, GPT enables a single architecture and unified optimization loop to master heterogeneous tasks across massive corpora, giving rise to powerful foundation models for natural language. In contrast, 4D spatiotemporal vision lacks a common representational protocol: perception (such as depth and camera estimation) and generation are either handled by separate one-way pipelines or bolted together from unaligned single-modality spaces. In this work, we introduce WorldLatent, a framework that formulates a unified 4D latent space as a common protocol for both world perception and generation. Rather than relying on fragmented, task-specific models or stacked single-modality representations, our framework proposes a multimodal VAE architecture that compresses and fuses complex 4D visual appearance, geometry, and dynamics into a single structured latent representation. Within this formulation, perception and generation operate as complementary denoising processes over the same latent space, enabling one generative model to learn both capabilities simultaneously within a single training loop. Experiments across representative 4D benchmarks verify that our fused latent protocol achieves specialist-level zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing from a single model. By demonstrating that diverse 4D tasks can be seamlessly unified under a shared representational protocol, our work provides a scalable foundation for general-purpose 4D spatial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Déjà VAE: When Do Autoencoder Traces Reveal Generated Images?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31036)**  `arXiv:2611.31036`  `cs.CV`  
  _Saugat Pandey_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A latent diffusion model's output has already passed through its autoencoder, so a second round trip changes it very little. This "déjà vu" underlies reconstruction-based detectors of generated images; we analyse when it reveals a generated image and when it does not. Read by a frozen vision foundation model without training, the trace is strongest in intermediate layers, and, unlike pixel-level reconstruction error, it treats blur as a sign of a real image. It belongs to the generator's exact decoder: on Synthbuster, the exact decoders of Stable Diffusion 1.3, 1.4, 2 and XL are the strongest of eight autoencoders and catch 94-100% of their generators' images at a 5% false-alarm rate (SDXL's version identified post hoc), while near-identical versions catch 7-42%. A detector trained only on real photographs and their autoencoder copies matches one trained on 162k generated images on GenImage (+2.3 points, 95% CI [0.9, 3.7]) and complements it in fusion, but one resize with JPEG re-save, or a mild blur, costs it 19-27 points. Two lessons concern benchmarks. The in-the-wild benchmark Chameleon can be solved from image width alone. And on Synthbuster, how the real photographs are matched to the generated images' size decides whether our detector leads or trails (+4.4 or -9.7 points): the matching protocol alone can reverse a detector comparison. Code and per-image scores:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WildAnySplat: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31023)**  `arXiv:2611.31023`  `cs.CV`  
  _Richard Shaw, Arthur Moreau, Athanasios Papaioannou, Thomas Tanay, Zhensong Zhang, Eduardo Pérez-Pellitero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present WildAnySplat, a feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that enables robust novel-view synthesis from in-the-wild multi-view images. Unlike controlled captures, real-world images often contain multi-view inconsistencies caused by transient occluders, illumination changes, and camera ISP, which degrade the quality of existing feed-forward reconstruction methods.To address this challenge, we introduce a student-teacher framework that learns transient-robust and appearance-consistent 3D representations. A teacher network operates on clean images, while a student processes the corresponding in-the-wild inputs. The teacher provides clean supervision to guide the student toward a consistent canonical representation. Since paired clean and in-the-wild data are scarce, we use a hybrid data strategy, combining real paired captures, synthetic augmentations, and pseudo-clean labels generated from optimization-based reconstructions, bridging the domain gap between synthetic and real-world data.We adopt a transformer architecture with dedicated DPT heads for camera pose, depth, and Gaussian prediction. We additionally predict per-view transient masks to reweight photometric supervision and suppress unreliable Gaussians. To account for cross-view appearance variation, we incorporate a bilateral grid module that models per-image color transformations and enables recovery of a canonical radiance representation.Experiments on challenging in-the-wild benchmarks show that WildAnySplat improves robustness to transient occlusions and appearance variation while retaining the fast inference of feed-forward Gaussian Splatting.
  </details>

- **[OmniV-Bench: Can MLLMs Comprehend Immersive Spatio-temporal Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31022)**  `arXiv:2611.31022`  `cs.CV`  
  _Liu Yang, Huiyu Duan, Yixuan Gao, Sirui Liu, Lu Liu, Zhaolin Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omnidirectional videos (ODVs) capture dynamic environments with a full $360^\circ$ field of view, providing rich spatial context and temporal dynamics for applications such as virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), and embodied intelligence. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made great progress in spatial and temporal understanding, yet their ability to comprehend the immersive 360 spatio-temporal information conveyed by ODVs remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we first introduce OmniV-Bench, a Benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the capabilities of MLLMs on spatio-temporal Omnidirectional Video understanding. OmniV-Bench contains 4,123 high-quality ODVs collected from diverse scenes, along with 8,923 human-annotated question-answer pairs spanning 11 fine-grained tasks across three categories, i.e., general, spatial, and spatio-temporal understanding. Extensive benchmark experiments reveal that the spatio-temporal complexity of ODVs poses significant challenges for current MLLMs. To address these limitations, we further propose OmniV-Harness, an agentic harness that enhances ODV understanding through viewpoint-guided evidence initialization and task-relevant evidence acquisition with adaptive tool use. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that OmniV-Harness consistently improves immersive spatio-temporal reasoning ability of MLLMs. Both OmniV-Bench and OmniV-Harness will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Graph2Scene: Relation-Aware Text-to-Image Generation with Graph-Guided Diffusion Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30964)**  `arXiv:2611.30964`  `cs.CV`  
  _Peizheng Li, Xin Ai, Guanghui Cheng, Zikang Dong, Qiange Wang, Yanfeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image diffusion models still struggle with complex prompts containing many elements and interactions. Existing attention-control methods mainly focus on controlling where elements appear, but often ignore how objects interact with each other. As a result, relation tokens describing interactions between elements may assign high attention to irrelevant regions, leading to missing or incorrect interactions. In this paper, we propose Graph2Scene, a training-free framework that treats scene graphs as a core control signal for diffusion generation. It represents elements as nodes and their relations as edges, and translates these element-relation dependencies into selective attention guidance. It grounds each element to its corresponding region and guides relational interactions among the involved elements. To achieve appropriate relation control without over-constraining the generation process, Graph2Scene further adapts the control duration to scene complexity and progressively relaxes graph guidance during denoising. Experiments on T2I-CompBench and ConceptMix show clear improvements in compositional generation, especially for object relations. On ConceptMix, Graph2Scene improves relationship accuracy from 0.59 to 0.84 over the strongest FLUX.1-based regional-control baseline. Results on LongBench-T2I further show its advantage on long and complex prompts.
  </details>

- **[SIAMBA: A Pseudo-Siamese Mamba-based Deep Learning Network for Multimodal Flood Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30938)**  `arXiv:2611.30938`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Fasih Ishfaq, Giulio Weikmann, Lorenzo Bruzzone_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flood change detection from bi-temporal multispectral and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) images is essential for rapid disaster response. In this context, Sentinel-2 multispectral imagery provides discriminative spectral observations, whereas Sentinel-1 SAR provides weather-independent structural information. Using these modalities jointly requires modelling temporal evidence while accounting for cross-modal heterogeneity. In particular, change detection is complicated by cloud contamination, illumination variability, acquisition geometry and speckle, and heterogeneous land-cover responses. To address these challenges, we propose SIAMBA, a pseudo-siamese Mamba-based network for bi-temporal flood change detection from paired Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 observations. SIAMBA uses dedicated modality-specific encoders with dual-pooling squeeze-and-excitation recalibration and selective state-space processing for long-range spatial context. A gated cross-modal change module derives absolute, signed, and relative log-ratio differences at multiple semantic levels, and attention-refined skip connections support progressive multi-scale decoding. Experiments are carried out on OMBRIA benchmark, which includes an independent test set and geographically diverse subsets in France, Albania, Guyana, and Timor. SIAMBA achieves 81.98% IoU, and 93.57% OA on the independent test set, reaching the highest values in the considered comparison. It also provides the highest unweighted average IoU and OA across four regional subsets spatially disjuncted. These results indicate that combining selective state-space contextual modelling with gated multiscale discrepancy learning effectively improves flood change mapping across the evaluated geographic settings.
  </details>

- **[A High-Resolution UAV Benchmark Dataset and Baseline Evaluation for Teak Crown Segmentation in Western Ghats Tropical Forests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30888)**  `arXiv:2611.30888`  `cs.CV`  
  _Anand S Sahadevan, Sunita Arya, Anup Kumar Das, Bimal Kumar Bhattacharya, S Manthira Moorthi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate teak-tree segmentation from high-resolution drone imagery is critical for sustainable plantation management, yet lacks dedicated benchmarks. We introduce TeakTreeNet, a comprehensive dataset and evaluation protocol for binary teak-tree segmentation at native 2cm resolution. Four state-of-the-art architectures: U-Net, PSPNet, DeepLabV3, and SegFormer were rigorously benchmarked across scratch and ImageNet-pretrained settings with three-seed reproducibility. SegFormer establishes new SOTA performance with F1 0.938 +/- 0.002 (Teak), IoU 0.968 +/- 0.001 (Teak), and Accuracy 0.986 +/- 0.001, outperforming all pretrained CNNs. Domain-adaptive training suffices, as the scratch SegFormer outperforms ImageNet pretrained baselines, avoiding large-scale pretraining overhead. Comprehensive analysis reveals Transformer's superior boundary delineation for dense plantation scenarios. TeakTreeNet provides researchers with 6391 expertly annotated drone patches (224x224) and establishes reproducible evaluation protocols, enabling direct comparison of future methods. Our benchmark demonstrates SegFormer's optimal balance of accuracy, efficiency, and deployability for operational forestry monitoring of teak plantations for India's western tropical forests.
  </details>

- **[BridgeMVS: Bridging Multi-View Stereo and Monodepth via Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30787)**  `arXiv:2611.30787`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jianfei Jiang, Haochen Yu, Qiankun Liu, Rui Yang, Hongyuan Liu, Xianglong Meng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-View Stereo (MVS) and Monocular Depth Estimation (MDE) provide complementary cues for dense 3D reconstruction. While MVS leverages explicit geometry for metric depth, it struggles in challenging regions where feature matching is unreliable. Conversely, MDE provides robust structural priors but typically yields scale-ambiguous results. Existing hybrid methods mainly exploit monocular cues in a unidirectional manner, leaving the mutual enhancement between MDE and MVS insufficiently explored. To address this limitation, we propose BridgeMVS, a unified cascaded multi-stage MVS framework that bridges monocular depth features and multi-view cost volume representations through Bidirectional Dynamic Fusion (BDF). At each stage, BDF performs dynamic mono-to-cost and cost-to-mono updates, where monocular structural features guide cost volume refinement for more robust MVS depth estimation, while multi-view geometric evidence recalibrates monocular representations to improve their metric-scale consistency. Furthermore, the fused monocular and multi-view representations are progressively propagated from coarse to fine stages and constrained by stage-wise relative-scale MDE supervision and metric-scale MVS supervision, enabling cross-branch interactions to benefit subsequent high-resolution depth estimation. In addition, BDF is designed as a plug-and-play module that can be conveniently integrated into existing cascaded MVS frameworks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BridgeMVS achieves state-of-the-art performance on the DTU, Tanks-and-Temples, and ETH3D benchmarks, while simultaneously boosting the accuracy of monocular depth estimation. Notably, BridgeMVS ranks first on both the Tanks-and-Temples Intermediate and Advanced benchmarks. The source code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Optimising VLM Harnesses to Localise Errors in Generated Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30673)**  `arXiv:2611.30673`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ankit Yadav, Nikhil Cherian Kurian, Arpit Garg, Lingqiao Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying subtle errors in generated videos is an important step toward automated video generation, where errors must be detected to assess and refine generated clips. Recent work shows that pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) can localise such errors, but their effectiveness depends strongly on the workflow used: choices about which frames to inspect, which tools and model calls to make, and how to combine predictions can lead to substantially different results. We study VLM harnesses for video error localisation. Instead of relying on manually designed harnesses, we propose Harness Search and Merge (HSM), an algorithm that automatically constructs them by searching over executable evaluation programs. An LLM orchestrator refines candidate harnesses independently on random training subsets, records findings in a memory, and constructively merges complementary candidates. We evaluate HSM on Spotlight, a recently proposed benchmark for localising errors in generated videos.
