HSM achieves 0.234 +- 0.012 F1, exceeding the strongest benchmark strategy (0.173 +- 0.016), and retains its lead on an unseen video generator (0.213 +- 0.008 versus 0.140 +- 0.003). On IPV-Bench, it achieves strong localisation performance with lower false-alarm rates than existing optimisation baselines. Together, these results show that searching over complete VLM harnesses can outperform existing evaluation strategies, transfer to an unseen video generator, and reduce false alarms on real videos.
  </details>

- **[Zhengrong: World Models with Pole-Based Action-Response Factorization for Direct Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30666)**  `arXiv:2611.30666`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Zhang, Jingrui Li, Congyu Wang, Yuhang Cheng, Wenbo Chen, Xiaobo Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct visual world models predict the effects of candidate action sequences without recursive latent rollout, yet they leave the temporal structure of action effects unspecified. We introduce Zhengrong (Zhengrong-PoleWM), a world model that decomposes a latent trajectory into an initial-condition anchor, a free response, and delayed responses to action innovations. Nonlinear networks map visual history and causal action prefixes to response coefficients. A shared bank of stable conjugate poles determines how each response evolves with elapsed time. This factorization preserves nonlinear interactions across actions, anchors predictions exactly at the initial state, prevents future actions from affecting earlier predictions, and yields bounded responses to finite control plans. We derive an exact re-anchoring identity that identifies the additional basis direction $\Psi_\kappa(s)$ needed to represent shifted trajectory tails. Across four offline image-goal control benchmarks (Cube, TwoRoom, PushT, and Reacher), Zhengrong-PoleWM achieves $90.83\% \pm 0.47\%$ macro-average success over two training seeds at epoch 20, exceeding the strongest baseline (QQWorld) by 6.08 percentage points. Selecting checkpoints by validation loss yields $91.21\% \pm 1.00\%$. In a controlled first-epoch Cube study without restart regularization, neural and pole response heads outperform a generic direct predictor by 20.67 and 23.34 percentage points, respectively. On an NVIDIA A100 GPU, median latency for a full 30-iteration cross-entropy method search ranges from 74 to 87 ms across the four tasks. The mean of these medians is 77.5 ms, a 15.8% latency reduction relative to Fast-LeWM and a $7.1\times$ speedup over autoregressive LeWM.
  </details>

- **[CineAct: Interaction-State Agents for Playable, Branching Film Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30613)**  `arXiv:2611.30613`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyan Lin, Jianqi Ma, Shujie Duan, Hanxin Zhu, Zhihao Hu, Jinming Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open video generators such as MiniMax-H3 produce short clips of only several to a dozen seconds per run. To synthesize longer sequences, existing methods either rely on agentic systems following preset scripts, or roll out autoregressively from the previous clip's last frame, losing off-screen states and accumulating hallucinations across turns. Consequently, users still cannot take an explicit goal, choose how to accomplish it, and see that choice persist across subsequent clips. We study goal-grounded interactive video generation, where one goal admits multiple valid approaches, the user's chosen approach must be executed on screen, and its consequences must persist across clips. To this end, we propose CineAct, a state-first agentic framework in which language-model agents handle reasoning while frozen image and video generators serve purely as renderers. Before interaction, the Architect initializes the world as a spatial layout with cameras, an asset library, and a scene graph; at each turn, the system deduces the planned state change, generates a target end frame with an image generator and rigorously verifies it, and synthesizes intermediate motion with an FL2V model. We further introduce a multi-space benchmark covering two suites: long-horizon step execution and open-intent branching admissibility. Experiments show that explicit world-state modeling boosts multi-step rollout consistency over the pixel-only baseline (+11.4 automated, +23.1 human points) and raises paired branching success toward a shared goal by +30.8 points.
  </details>

- **[SVUBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Proactive Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30541)**  `arXiv:2611.30541`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuxuan Liang, Bohan Zhang, Haiyang Yu, Bocheng Hu, Ke Niu, Xu Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive streaming video understanding requires models to interpret unfolding events, respond timely, and retrieve relevant historical evidence. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce SVUBench, a comprehensive proactive streaming video benchmark comprising 12,131 questions and 62,881 answer points across ten proactive tasks spanning Perception, Understanding, and Reasoning. Constructed through model-assisted annotation and human verification, SVUBench introduces two complementary interaction modes. Basic evaluates sustained proactive responses from video onset, while Trajectory requires historical recovery and continuous response updates following task activation during streaming. A Duration-Scaled Challenge further evaluates these capabilities across four video duration tiers. We develop the Proactive Response Score (PRS) with task-adaptive answer windows to jointly assess answer correctness, response timeliness, historical consistency, and content quality. Extensive experiments and analysis reveal the strengths and limitations of existing models, highlighting directions for advancing proactive streaming video understanding.
  </details>

- **[Where Compositionality Hides: Shared-Content Interference in Vision-Language Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30538)**  `arXiv:2611.30538`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chimata Anudeep, Harsh Vardhan Poddar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Paired compositional benchmarks such as Winoground ask each image to prefer its matching caption and each caption to prefer its matching image. A failed Group decision can come from a wrong caption preference or from the cross-image comparison alone, and Group Score merges the two. Choosing between retraining the encoder and changing the scoring rule therefore requires separating them. We study mean-erasure, which projects each frozen image embedding away from the mean of the two normalized candidate captions. We prove that the projection preserves every within-image caption ranking and makes Group Score equal baseline Text Score. On Winoground, mean-erasure raises CLIP ViT-B/32 Group Score from 9% to 31%, and five CLIP and SigLIP checkpoints gain 21 to 27 percentage points. ColorSwap shows a similar gain for color-object binding. A score decomposition shows that about 60% of Image failures already score the correct joint assignment above the swapped one and fail only on an offset between the two images. Fixed corrections and a learned bilinear scorer gain under one point. A direction estimated from retrieved captions gains about five points with two captions and falls below baseline with sixteen. Before retraining an encoder for paired retrieval, measure the Group loss that mean-erasure recovers: gains beyond baseline Text Score require changing the caption decisions themselves.
  </details>

- **[Do learned image captioning metrics generalize? Auditing duplicated samples in human-judgment benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30488)**  `arXiv:2611.30488`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaochen Zhang, Jin Li, Chunlong Xia, Longjiang Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned image captioning metrics are trained and tested on human judgments, so their reported correlation is a claim about generalization. We audit this claim for the three most recent collections of such judgments, Polaris, Nebula, and Spica, matching images at the pixel level. First, test samples reappear in training sets: the Spica training set contains about two-thirds of the Polaris test set, with identical human ratings. Second, controlled retraining shows that duplicates can inflate a metric: for Pearl, the gain is confined to the duplicated samples and grows with every epoch; for DENEB, it stays below one point. The gain depends on the learner and on how often a training set repeats a sample. Third, Kendall's $\tau$ over all samples is the sum of two terms, detecting captions with the lowest human score and ranking the others; the first supplies most of the reported correlation and is nearly saturated. The recent test sets cannot order Polos, DENEB and Pearl consistently, and a ridge regression on frozen features matches or exceeds all three. Fourth, we propose a protocol that ranks a learned metric only with a model trained without test samples, reports both terms, and compares metrics by paired tests. Under it, zero-shot vision--language judges rank first on the three recent test sets, and a fine-tuned judge ranks first on the three earlier benchmarks. The same analysis applies to any evaluator learned from human judgments; filter lists and code are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spatial-Zero: Self-Evolving Spatial Reasoning in Vision-Language Models from Zero Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30424)**  `arXiv:2611.30424`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yutong Li, Haoyu Zhao, Daoguo Dong, Ziyi Ye, Yugang Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning requires a vision-language model~(VLM) to understand metric distances, three-dimensional relationships, and viewpoint-dependent geometry. Learning these capabilities typically requires externally curated training data. This paper introduces Spatial-Zero, a VLMs' training framework that can self-evolve through autonomous spatial exploration with zero data. Spatial-Zero addresses two key challenges in autonomous spatial exploration: reliably generating valid spatial interaction tasks and providing accurate supervision for learning from these tasks. Specifically, a proposer specifies object configurations and questions, and a Coder turns them into executable scene programs that produce multi-view images and explicit scene geometry. A Geometric Oracle checks spatial constraints, visibility, and answer uniqueness and derives ground-truth labels from executed geometry; these labels supervise the Solver and ground difficulty feedback, while validation outcomes guide subsequent task generation. Across six spatial reasoning benchmarks and three VLM backbones, Spatial-Zero yields consistent gains through iterative self-evolution. After three iterations, Qwen3.5-9B improves average accuracy by 3.23 percentage points; with Qwen3.5-4B, Spatial-Zero also outperforms existing VLM self-evolution methods. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Shift by Analogy for Compositional Zero-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30342)**  `arXiv:2611.30342`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zanwu Liu, Bo Li, Guanglin Niu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compositional zero-shot learning recognizes attribute-object pairs absent from training. An unseen pair has a semantic description but lacks an observed visual reference. We propose Shift by Analogy (SBA) to make unseen compositions visually seen through a shift-then-match process. Its core add-subtract-add rule transfers an attribute change between two seen image-text residuals on a source object to a reference residual on the target object. This shifts the target's own text feature toward its visual center; learned refinement produces a proxy for matching query images. During training, each target is excluded from its supports, and recognition is learned using references constructed from other pairs. An analogy library and same-object attention extend this construction across candidates, while joint learning integrates proxy matching with semantic and primitive evidence. Visual-center analysis confirms substantially improved correspondence for unseen compositions. Experiments across three standard benchmarks show that SBA consistently outperforms strong CZSL baselines under both closed- and open-world evaluation.
  </details>

- **[AirVT-15k: A Query-Diverse UAV Video-Text Dataset and Query-Adaptive Evidence Alignment Network for Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30339)**  `arXiv:2611.30339`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xi Chen, Xu Chen, Xiangyang Jia, Ting Gan, Xu Zhang, Shuquan Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unmanned aerial vehicle video-text retrieval (UAV-TVR) requires matching language to both scene context and subtle object changes. A harbor-layout query relies on global scene context, whereas a departing-vessel query requires local functional cues and motion states. Existing benchmarks provide diverse aerial descriptions but give limited attention to distinct evidence needs within the same video. We introduce AirVT-15k, a benchmark of 15,504 UAV videos and 155,040 captions, with ten complementary bilingual captions per video describing scenes, object states, and temporal changes. We also propose AirFocus-CST, which selects query-relevant frames and patches, encodes temporal feature differences, and combines coarse-to-fine interaction (CFI) with optimal-transport alignment (OTA). Average mean recall reaches 63.67% on AirVT-15k and 24.41% on CapERA, improving over the strongest baselines by 2.96 and 1.91 percentage points, respectively. The dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Draft Forcing: Target-guided Speculative Drafting and Learned Verifier for Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30328)**  `arXiv:2611.30328`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenkai Xu, Yuji Ren, Zihui Liu, Yijie Jin, Yuhao Shen, Xin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive video diffusion models enable streaming synthesis chunk by chunk, yet invoking the large model for every chunk remains costly. While chunk-level speculative decoding can offload chunks to a lightweight draft model, existing approaches suffer from two limitations: (1) an off-the-shelf draft diverges from the target's trajectory, compounding errors in history; and (2) they either omit verification or rely on costly post-decode aesthetic scores rather than verifying consistency against the target. We address both with Draft Forcing, a framework coupling target-guided drafting with an on-policy trained verifier. Specifically, to align the draft for high acceptance, we opt for feature-level drafting, conditioning it on multi-layer target hidden states persisted from committed chunks, whose outputs are pushed to match target trajectories with multi-aspect losses. For verification, we propose an on-policy trained feature-space verifier predicting CDF-calibrated frame-wise relative RMSE against the target. It is trained on speculative rollouts as contexts anneal from random to inference-aligned mixtures, and gates proposals without VAE decoding. On the 14B Self-Forcing target, Draft Forcing achieves $1.63\times$ DiT and $1.57\times$ end-to-end speedups on VBench with minimal quality loss, and up to $1.93\times$ DiT ($1.82\times$ end-to-end) when combined with orthogonal KV compression. It generalizes to accelerate a streaming variant of MiniMax-H3, generating over $50\%$ draft frames with competitive quality.
  </details>

- **[Video Generalized Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30316)**  `arXiv:2611.30316`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bardia Safaei, Vishal M. Patel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) aims to categorize unlabeled data from both known and unknown classes using labeled data from only the known classes. While recent GCD methods have shown promise on static images, their performance degrades in videos due to temporal dynamics, larger intra-class variation, and the lack of temporal consistency in existing approaches. We introduce VideoGCD, a framework tailored for video action recognition under the GCD setting. Our method integrates (i) a partitioned Mixture-of-Experts module that encourages specialization for known and novel categories, (ii) a novel-class balancing loss to reduce bias toward known classes, and (iii) a temporal optimal transport loss that promotes consistent routing across frames. Experiments on three video benchmarks demonstrate that VideoGCD significantly improves performance over existing GCD methods, particularly on novel categories. Our findings highlight the importance of combining temporal modeling and expert specialization for video GCD.
  </details>

- **[SafeRank-ReID: Candidate-Conditioned Re-Ranking under Identity Conflicts for Multimodal Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30314)**  `arXiv:2611.30314`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yizhi Wang, Zixuan Li, Zhuwen Chen, Feiwei Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Person Re-Identification (ReID) exploits complementary identity cues from different sensing modalities to mitigate the performance degradation caused by challenging imaging conditions. Existing methods typically estimate Modality Reliability at the query level, making it difficult to identify conflicting inputs that retain normal visual quality but correspond to an incorrect identity. Such inputs may receive high confidence, promote irrelevant gallery candidates, and lead to negative fusion. To address this issue, we propose SafeRank-ReID, a candidate-conditioned safe re-ranking framework for Multimodal Person Re-Identification (ReID). To capture the varying support of the same modality for different candidates, we introduce a candidate-conditioned evidence arbitration mechanism that integrates unimodal retrieval evidence, input quality, and cross-modal consistency to estimate trust and conflict logits for each modality-candidate pair. Together, these logits contribute to the normalized modality weights, allowing support strength to vary across modalities and candidates while reducing the contribution of potentially unreliable evidence. A candidate-set refinement module subsequently applies self-attention to jointly model candidate representations and predicts residual scores from their competitive relations, thereby refining the initial fused ranking. For fusion results that remain uncertain after refinement, a query-level fallback gate estimates an interpolation coefficient from the overall conflict level and cross-modal uncertainty, adaptively combining the fused scores with those of the most reliable unimodal branch. Experiments on RGBNT201 and MSVR310 show that SafeRank-ReID maintains competitive clean-input retrieval while improving robustness to Identity Conflicts across person and vehicle benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[PinGround: Pinning Queries to Points for Parallel Grounding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30304)**  `arXiv:2611.30304`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can localize through their text interface, grounding open-vocabulary objects, complex referring expressions, text lines and layout regions with strong semantic understanding. They write the boxes one after another as a single sequence, although the instances of a query, unlike the words of a sentence, form an unordered set with no sequential dependence. The number of decoding steps therefore grows with the number of instances, which makes dense scenes the most expensive. Decoding the instances in parallel avoids this growth, but streams that cannot see each other duplicate some instances and miss others. We propose \method, which writes a single point into each query as a spatial anchor and asks for the matching instance nearest to that point within a local radius, or \none. Given the image, the anchored queries are independent, so they are decoded in parallel as leaves of a prefix tree that shares the image and the query, and the model is trained on the same tree. Coarse-to-fine anchors refine only the regions where instances are found, duplicate suppression keeps one box per instance, and set-level reinforcement learning optimizes the set F1. Only the prompt and the attention mask change, not the architecture. On three different VLMs and 12 benchmarks covering detection, OCR, layout and referring, \method is at most 5.5 F1 below sequential decoding trained on the same data, while its speedup grows with instance density and reaches 21.9$\times$ on dense scenes.
  </details>

- **[AIGI-26: A Benchmark for AI-Generated Image Detection Across Diverse Modern Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30236)**  `arXiv:2611.30236`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qishuai Ding, Yifeng Wang, Shichuang Xie, Yanli Ren, Guorui Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing AI-generated image (AIGI) detection benchmarks predominantly rely on earlier synthesis systems with limited content diversity and conspicuous artifacts, failing to fully reflect the detection challenges posed by high-fidelity images from contemporary models. We introduce AIGI-26, a benchmark of 253,200 images covering 26 recent open- and closed-source generators. Its novel, diverse, and heterogeneous sources provide a basis for systematically evaluating detection methods on modern generated imagery. Additionally, to capture subtle statistical discrepancies across complementary forensic views, we propose the multi-aspect forensic detection network (MAFDNet). Its fixed forensic operators expose complementary texture, local prediction, resampling, and chromatic responses, summarized by a hierarchical encoder using multiscale means and standard deviations. In parallel, a spectral branch captures Fourier log-magnitude and neighboring-frequency phase variation across radial bands and directional axes. Fusing these descriptors combines local inconsistencies with global spectral organization for authenticity detection. Trained on 12,660 AIGI-26 images, MAFDNet achieves 88.5% accuracy and 94.9% average precision on the 240,540-image test set, with only 1.04M parameters and 1.39 GFLOPs per $256\times256$ input. MAFDNet also demonstrates effective cross-dataset generalization on DailyBench and Treasure-37.
  </details>

- **[EditGRN: Inversion-Free Image Editing by Source-Target Generative Synchronization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30224)**  `arXiv:2611.30224`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiahuan Wang, Jianan Chen, Jian Han, Rui Li, Guangming Lu, Wenjie Pei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-guided image editing faces a persistent trade-off between preserving source content and realizing coherent target semantics. We explore this challenge through training- and inversion-free image editing based on discrete generative models. Existing inversion-free discrete editors primarily rely on post-hoc probability correction, creating a disconnect between local token-level adjustments and globally coherent semantic generation. To bridge this gap, we introduce EditGRN, a training- and inversion-free framework with trajectory-wide source-target generative synchronization in discrete space. EditGRN adopts a dual-branch design---the source branch constructs source references and the target branch generates the target image-enabling trajectory-wide synchronization between the two. Exploiting GRN's ability to recover clean tokens from arbitrary binary corruptions, the source branch directly constructs corrupted source states at each refinement step to extract useful editing features, without recovering a specific source-generation trajectory. The target branch synchronizes with the source branch through the same corruption schedule, fusing the corresponding source context with editable representations intra-model. By synchronizing source context with target generation along the entire trajectory, our approach faithfully preserves the background while producing high-quality edits that blend seamlessly with the surrounding scene through intra-model fusion. PIE-Bench show state-of-the-art performance on background preservation and text alignment across diverse editing tasks against continuous and discrete baselines.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Geospatial Foundation Models under Calibrated Label Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30215)**  `arXiv:2611.30215`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mollah Md Saif, Showrin Rahman, Anindya Majumder, Sowad Rahman, Md. Golam Rabiul Alam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Earth-observation labels from maps, older products or hurried annotation arrive misaligned, coarse, loosely outlined or incomplete. Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) are nevertheless benchmarked with labels taken as correct. A practitioner therefore cannot tell which encoder to choose when the labels are imperfect. We present a benchmark protocol for fine-tuning under such labels. Four spatially structured error types are calibrated to equal label mIoU and applied identically to every model and seed. Every contrast is paired and read against an equivalence margin of $\pm 3$ points and a minimum detectable effect. We apply it to nine encoders, six decoder heads and ten datasets. In an exploratory reading at a matched learning rate the satellite-pretrained and web-pretrained DINOv3 encoders are equivalent within the margin on 8 of 9 datasets. Multispectral GFMs and an ImageNet ResNet-50 are equivalent within the margin on about half of them. Labels with random offsets cost the least at equal label quality. Under other decoder heads the verdicts mostly hold on FLAIR-2 and are decoder dependent on HLS Burn Scars. On four of five real label pairs the real label costs more than its matched simulated type. No choice of pretraining data makes an encoder consistently more robust, so the encoder is better chosen by its accuracy under imperfect labels.
  </details>

- **[Visual-RFT++: Extending Visual Reinforcement Fine-Tuning to Multimodal Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30193)**  `arXiv:2611.30193`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziyu Liu, Zeyi Sun, Xiaoyi Dong, Yuhang Cao, Haodong Duan, Dahua Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT) has recently become a central paradigm for advancing Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), as demonstrated by systems such as OpenAI's o3 and the R1 series. Despite significant progress in LLMs, how to leverage RFT to unify visual perception, step-by-step reasoning, and agentic tool use into one coherent training paradigm remains an open challenge. In this work, we present Visual Reinforcement Fine-Tuning++ (Visual-RFT++), a unified framework that extends RFT to both visual perception tasks and multimodal agentic workflows, enabling LVLMs to reason with images, act upon images, and evaluate their own outputs using verifiable reward. With Visual-RFT++, open-source LVLMs not only show significant gains on diverse visual perception tasks, but also acquire agentic abilities such as web searching for real-time information and code-based image manipulation (e.g., cropping, rotation, and analysis). Across visual perception tasks, including fine-grained classification, few-shot detection, reasoning grounding, and open-vocabulary detection, Visual-RFT++ delivers strong gains over SFT, including a +24.3 percentage points improvement in one-shot fine-grained classification and +21.0 percentage points on COCO's 4-shot setting and +15.4 percentage points on LVIS with around several hundred samples. To evaluate agentic behaviors, we introduce the Multimodal Agentic Tool Bench (MAT) with MAT-Search and MAT-Coding. Visual-RFT++ surpasses its baseline by +18.6 F1 / +13.0 EM percentage points on MAT-Coding and +10.3 F1 / +8.7 EM percentage points on MAT-Search. It also shows gains of 29.3 F1 and 25.9 EM percentage points on multi-hop QA benchmarks such as 2Wiki and HotpotQA, demonstrating strong cross-modal generalization. Visual-RFT++ introduces a data-efficient and reward-driven framework that strengthens both visual reasoning and agentic adaptability.
  </details>

- **[PathStream: Towards Streaming Diagnostic Copilots for Whole-Slide Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30167)**  `arXiv:2611.30167`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dankai Liao, YuFeng Wu, Ziyue Wang, Guanxi Liu, Tianyi Zhang, Zeyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathologists reach a diagnosis by integrating morphological evidence across regions and magnifications along viewing trajectories. Existing pathology AI rarely reflects this process: models predict from selected image regions, answer questions within fixed visual contexts, or navigate slides autonomously. We formalize pathologist-led streaming diagnosis, in which the pathologist controls navigation while a copilot selectively describes incoming views and synthesizes accumulated evidence into a final diagnosis. We record five pathologists' viewing trajectories on 1,791 TCGA whole-slide images (WSIs) spanning ten organs, with response labels, morphological descriptions, and session-level findings and diagnoses. We construct PathStreamBench to assess response timing, morphological description, and session-level diagnosis, alongside auxiliary video understanding. We introduce PathStream, which jointly learns streaming responses and diagnosis through two-stage training, with a diagnosis-attention loss guiding attention during diagnosis to relevant visual and textual evidence accumulated as the examination unfolds. PathStream outperforms 14 vision-language models and three domain-fine-tuned baselines on the core tasks, achieving a trajectory WSI Relevance score of 77.87, surpassing the strongest domain-fine-tuned baseline's 62.76. Without additional training, its diagnostic capabilities generalize to new educational pathology videos, three external centers, and an unseen organ.
  </details>

- **[WaDiS: Boundary-Preserving Diffusion Segmentation via Multi-Scale Wavelet Conditioning and Frequency-Aware Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30164)**  `arXiv:2611.30164`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiang Gao, Yi Wang, Yong Zhang, Bin Fang, Yong Li, Lan Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have shown strong potential in medical image segmentation due to their generative capabilities and robustness. However, existing methods often struggle to preserve fine anatomical boundaries and to retain multi-scale structural information due to the loss of spatial and frequency details during feature encoding, particularly in complex clinical imaging conditions. This work presents WaDiS, a wavelet-powered conditional latent diffusion framework for medical image segmentation that addresses these limitations through multi-scale wavelet conditioning and frequency-aware information filtering for boundary preservation. The model adopts a multi-scale wavelet conditioning encoder to retain rich structural features, leveraging its lossless nature to better preserve semantic priors critical to the diffusion process. In addition, a Frequency-Aware Gating Block (FAGB) is introduced into the skip connections of the diffusion U-Net, enabling adaptive spectral filtering and residual fusion to better emphasize boundary structures while reducing irrelevant content. To improve generalization across clinical domains, we adopt a structure-preserving color augmentation strategy during training. Specifically, global color statistics are transferred between samples in the LAB color space, allowing the model to decouple appearance from structure and focus more on anatomical shape rather than color bias. Experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that WaDiS consistently outperforms existing diffusion-based and conventional segmentation methods, with notable improvements in boundary accuracy, robustness to noise, and generalization under color variation. The code for this work will be made publicly available upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[UUBench: Understanding the Unspoken toward Full-Duplex Multimodal Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30157)**  `arXiv:2611.30157`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ye Pan, Chenfei Liao, Bin Ren, Hanqian Li, Haocong He, Jialei Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective real-time, full-duplex multimodal interaction requires systems to understand not only what users say, but also the communicative needs conveyed through prosody, facial expressions, gestures, and context, and to generate audiovisual responses that appropriately address those needs. Existing evaluations offer limited insight into whether systems translate an understanding of such unspoken needs into effective responses. We introduce UUBench, a benchmark for assessing how well multimodal interactive systems Understand the Unspoken intent and address users' implicit communicative needs. The inherent subjectivity of implicit intent inference poses a key challenge for evaluation. To address this, we introduce Process-Oriented Scoring, which grounds the assessment of intent fulfillment in observable auditory and visual cues, such as prosody and gestures. Rather than relying solely on a single overall score, it evaluates how well a response addresses the communicative needs conveyed by each applicable primitive dimension. We aggregate the dimension-level scores according to each dimension's relevance to the annotated evidence. Beyond intent fulfillment, Outcome-Oriented Scoring evaluates the naturalness, appropriateness, and human-likeness of the reply itself under audio-only, visual-only, and audiovisual conditions. To make these judgments traceable to observable evidence, we propose a Fact-Grounded Scoring JEV module, which uses structured, timestamped audiovisual observations of the user and the digital human's reply to assess one dimension or criterion at a time. UUBench provides a foundation for assessing whether multimodal interactive systems respond effectively to meaning beyond literal wording, supporting progress toward context-aware, real-time, full-duplex interaction.
  </details>

- **[AxE: Benchmarking Agent-Environment Generalization of Video World Models via Grounded Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30120)**  `arXiv:2611.30120`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haiyu Xu, Yiyang Chen, Jiayi Zhou, Jiaming Ji, Juntao Dai, Yaodong Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge cases are not the long tail; they are the norm. An embodied system deployed across changing environments, bodies, objects, and tasks will routinely encounter combinations that were absent from its training data. General-purpose physical intelligence therefore requires world models that reuse prior knowledge, predict consequences, and adapt with little or no additional training. Yet current evaluations of conditional video generation emphasize visual quality, prompt alignment, or isolated physical plausibility, leaving systematic generalization difficult to measure. We introduce AxE Bench, a benchmark that organizes condition changes along eight factors of the acting agent and of its environment, and pairs every original condition with controlled variants that change one side or both. WBench-plus applies the same structure to an existing multi-turn benchmark, pairing each WBench scenario with variants on the environment side, through its world setting or camera behavior, or on the agent side, through its interaction instructions. Additionally, to score generated futures, we develop an evaluation harness that combines vision-language reasoning with targeted computer-vision evidence, converting each video into temporally localized events and comparing generated events with reference events in both time and semantics. We summarize each model with the AxE Score, event Fidelity weighted by its Stability across original-variant pairs. Across twenty models, generated events match reference events far more often in timing than in content: no model exceeds a Fidelity of 13.01, the highest Stability is 0.55, and the highest AxE Score is 7.10. Together, these components turn world-model generalization from an informal aspiration into a falsifiable evaluation program for real and simulated interactions. Our project website can be found at:this http URL
  </details>

- **[Thermal-Guided Low-Light UAV Image Enhancement: A Reliability-Aware Guidance Network and a Unified Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30114)**  `arXiv:2611.30114`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhicheng Zhao, Yu Wang, Jiangong Xu, Lixiang Xu, Chenglong Li, Jin Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thermal-guided low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is promising for nighttime unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) perception because thermal imagery remains informative under severe visible degradation. However, the degraded visible image serves as both the alignment reference and the enhancement target, making thermal guidance uncertain in its spatial correspondence and local utility under low illumination, sensor differences, and UAV motion. Misaligned or unsuitable thermal cues may introduce artifacts, while spatially varying degradation requires region-specific restoration rather than uniform enhancement. To address these challenges, we propose a Reliability-Aware Multi-Strategy Guidance Network (RMGNet) for thermal-guided UAV LLIE under weak optical-thermal alignment. Its Reliability-Aware Guidance Calibration Module (RAGCM) performs coarse-to-fine displacement estimation and jointly evaluates the reliability of aligned thermal guidance and the usability of degraded visible evidence. Based on calibrated guidance, degradation, and reliability cues, the Multi-Strategy Enhancement Routing Module (MSERM) generates candidates for detail preservation, highlight-protected relighting, and thermal compensation, then spatially routes their contributions. We further construct the UAV weakly aligned Optical-Thermal dataset (UWOT), the largest UAV-oriented benchmark to date for weakly aligned optical-thermal low-light enhancement, containing 5,000 triplets. Extensive experiments show that RMGNet outperforms representative single-modal and multimodal state-of-the-art methods in enhancement quality while retaining competitive computational efficiency and greater robustness to practical cross-modal misalignment. The dataset and code will be publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SalivaryHSI-Seg: A New Challenging Benchmark for Ex Vivo Salivary Gland Hyperspectral Tissue Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30071)**  `arXiv:2611.30071`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoqi Guo, Xianran Wang, Xinquan Yang, Qinyi Lu, Fangqian Zhang, Jiangyi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Salivary gland tumors are common lesions in oral and maxillofacial practice, and accurate identification of tumor regions and surrounding salivary gland tissues is important for tissue assessment, pathological sampling, and clinical decision support. Hyperspectral imaging (HSI) records continuous spectral responses together with spatial imaging information, providing a promising way to capture fine-grained tissue-discriminative information. Nevertheless, the development of salivary gland HSI tissue segmentation remains limited due to the lack of standardized benchmarks with pixel-level annotations and unified evaluation protocols. To address this issue, we introduce SalivaryHSI-Seg, a hyperspectral tissue segmentation benchmark for ex vivo salivary gland specimens, containing 119 HSI samples from 31 patients with multi-class pixel-level annotations. We establish a patient-level five-fold cross-validation protocol and evaluate different input representations, model architectures, and challenging subsets with small tumor and complex boundary. Based on this benchmark, we further propose HyBSD, a hyperspectral boundary-guided spectral-difference network for full-HSI salivary gland tissue segmentation. HyBSD exploits boundary-guided spectral-difference cues to improve tissue delineation in ambiguous transition regions. Extensive experiments show that full-HSI input provides more discriminative information than sampled spectrums, and that HyBSD achieves 74.97% mDice and 87.76% Tumor Dice, with further advantages on the challenging subsets. Dataset and code will be released.
  </details>

- **[FireDesmoke: Fire Smoke Removal via Real Smoke Transfer and Semantic Rectification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30057)**  `arXiv:2611.30057`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chih-Chung Hsu, Rong-Lin Jian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fire smoke removal must restore dense and medium smoke regions while preserving the clear (smoke-free) content. Existing methods either leave the smoke or alter the visible content, and generative models may hallucinate people or remove flames. Real fire smoke is spatially local: the degradation and its corruption of semantic features are both concentrated in the smoke regions. We propose FireDesmoke, built on this locality: Real Smoke Transfer calibrates smoke fields on real-fire recordings and transfers their spatial layout onto clean images to synthesize training pairs, and Semantic Rectification uses pixel features to rectify the tokens of a frozen foundation encoder where smoke corrupts them. With matched training budgets, replacing the transferred pairs with SmokeBench pairs lowers dense-smoke PSNR by 0.74 dB, and removing Semantic Rectification leaves clear regions 1.31 dB below the input. On what is, to the best of our knowledge, the first paired dataset of real fire smoke for image restoration, with 1,500 test frames from a held-out viewpoint, FireDesmoke improves dense- and medium-smoke PSNR by 3.73 and 2.63 dB while keeping clear regions at the input level (+0.41 dB); no other compared method gains at least 1 dB in dense smoke without lowering clear-region PSNR, and no hallucinated person is found in its outputs. It achieves state-of-the-art overall results on this benchmark and, trained on SmokeBench, the highest PSNR with the second-best SSIM and LPIPS.
  </details>

- **[Bi-Refine: Semantic Verification-Guided Progressive Refinement for Moment Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30055)**  `arXiv:2611.30055`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mengnan Liu, Sanping Zhou, Kun Xia, Jiaxuan Lin, Yuxuan Wu, Gang Hua_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Moment Retrieval (MR) aims to identify the temporal segment in an untrimmed video that corresponds to a natural language query. Existing approaches commonly refine temporal predictions within the localization pathway, without explicitly verifying candidate-level semantic consistency. Moreover, directly reusing semantic scores as feedback can cause over-correction when the verification signal is unreliable. To address these issues, we propose Bi-Refine, a semantic verification-guided progressive refinement framework that formulates MR as a closed-loop verification-and-refinement process. A Heuristic Localization Branch first generates candidate moments, while a Semantic Verification Branch evaluates their query consistency in a semantically enriched latent space. Query supervision further trains the verifier with the same candidate moment but different queries, encouraging it to distinguish the matched query from semantically similar mismatched queries. The resulting verification features are incorporated into a Progressive Refinement Loop that updates both query representations and multimodal features. To suppress potentially harmful updates, a lightweight reliability estimator gates query updates and reweights candidate contributions in feature feedback. A convergence-based adaptive stopping strategy additionally terminates unnecessary refinement during inference. Experiments on three standard MR benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and state-of-the-art performance of our method.
  </details>

- **[Teaching How to Reason, Beyond What to Answer: Evidence-Faithful On-Policy Distillation for streaming video understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30040)**  `arXiv:2611.30040`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mengnan Liu, Sanping Zhou, Kun Xia, Jiaxuan Lin, Gang Hua, Le Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Moment Retrieval (MR) aims to localize the temporal segment in a video that corresponds to a given textual description. However, a textual query often provides only a sparse semantic description of the target event, whereas the video contains rich and fine-grained visual variations. This mismatch creates an inherent gap between textual semantics and visual content, making a single textual input insufficient for precise cross-modal alignment. As a result, when multiple temporally adjacent or semantically similar segments exist, the model may struggle to distinguish the target moment from confusing candidates. To address this challenge, we propose a Multi-Perspective Auxiliary Representation (MPAR) framework, which aims to bridge this alignment gap by enriching a single textual input with complementary visual cues from multiple perspectives. Specifically, MPAR consists of three key components: (1) Auxiliary Representation Initialization (ARI) constructs multiple learnable representation slots from the original query, preserving the query intent while preparing flexible carriers for subsequent visual cue injection; (2) Visual-Induced Semantic Structuring (VISS) injects target-specific visual attributes into each representation while preserving semantic coherence with the original query; and (3) Multi-Perspective Semantic Aligner (MPSA) aligns each auxiliary representation with corresponding video regions to enhance discriminative localization. By constructing multi-perspective auxiliary representations and guiding the model to attend to relevant visual regions across these representations, our model achieves more accurate moment localization. Extensive experiments on MR benchmarks demonstrate that our MPAR outperforms state-of-the-art methods, validating the effectiveness of MPAR.
  </details>

- **[3AGameFactory-Motion: Agentic Rigging and Reliable Complex Motion Generation for Diverse 3D Assets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30013)**  `arXiv:2611.30013`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bohan Zeng, Kaixin Zhu, Yifan Yang, Dongge Yu, Zihao Wu, Yifan Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Animating arbitrary 3D assets from instructions requires both structural inference and motion control, yet existing motion benchmarks mostly cover conventional human or animal skeletons with simple instructions. We introduce DivDynBench, which evaluates rigging and motion generation across six morphology categories, with instructions requiring multi-phase part coordination and explicit timing, angle, repetition, and trajectory constraints. With large language models, we systematically compare three common paradigms: specialized motion generation, video generation followed by motion recovery, and direct agent-driven generation. We analyze their control capabilities, cross-morphology adaptability, and generation costs. Guided by this comparison, we propose an Agentic Motion Framework in which an agent writes compact parametric skeleton and motion programs, and a deterministic executor performs rigging, kinematic solving, and geometric correction. Compact parameter control reduces generation overhead and improves rigging reliability and motion-control accuracy, providing practical guidance for deploying complex 3D motion generation.
  </details>

- **[FlexGaze: Weakly Supervised Gaze Estimation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30012)**  `arXiv:2611.30012`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zongjian Li, Mohammad Soleymani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most gaze estimation models are trained on data collected using specialized equipment. The limited diversity of subjects, scenes, and head poses in these datasets hinders generalization to new conditions. We present FlexGaze, a family of 3D gaze estimators pretrained with weak supervision from gaze labels (pseudo-labels) automatically generated from in-the-wild images. We use off-the-shelf models to predict 2D gaze targets and scene depth, then combine these predictions to generate 3D gaze labels for approximately 3.24 million head instances. At inference, FlexGaze predicts gaze from a head crop alone and can handle profile and rear views without requiring face detection or head pose estimation. We use data augmentation during training to improve robustness to variations in crop position and scale. Pretrained using only these generated gaze labels, FlexGaze generalizes to unseen benchmarks. After supervised fine-tuning on public gaze datasets, we evaluate FlexGaze on eight benchmarks under a unified evaluation protocol. Our models match or outperform publicly available gaze estimators with several times as many parameters on most benchmarks. We release our dataset of generated gaze labels, all trained models, and code viathis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MORSE: Optimizing Streaming Memory Design via Open-World Auto-Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30011)**  `arXiv:2611.30011`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ce Zhang, Jianshu Zhang, Jinxi He, Xiyuan Yang, Pan Wang, Han Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A streaming video system must decide in a single pass what evidence to retain under bounded frame storage, before it knows which details a future question will require. Recent recursive self-improvement methods automate the design of the harness that stores and retrieves this evidence. They revise the harness in response to failures, but the editor must still devise each repair from its prior knowledge and a predefined set of tools. A failure that recurs across videos, however, may point to a problem that prior work has already addressed. We introduce MORSE (Memory Optimization with Recursive Self-Evolvement), an open-world auto-research framework that uses recurring failures to guide an online search for solutions. Its editor groups failures in training traces by cause, then searches papers, model cards, and code for methods it can adapt or pretrained perception models it can use. When the editor introduces a new perceptual capability, it can also revise how the harness stores and retrieves the resulting evidence for a frozen video-language model. On held-out validation streams, MORSE first selects revisions that improve answer quality, then seeks faster revisions that preserve those gains within a specified tolerance. Across three streaming video benchmarks, MORSE consistently achieves the highest answer accuracy within each frozen-backbone comparison. On OVO-Bench, our Qwen3-VL-8B harness answers queries in 0.30 seconds on average while achieving 75.1% accuracy, 3.1 percentage points ahead of the next-best evaluated harness using the same frozen backbone.
  </details>

- **[When Should We Trust Medical Image Models? Evaluating Confidence Reliability under Cross-Dataset Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29955)**  `arXiv:2611.29955`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoran Zhang, Sarah Lam, Yuting Lin, Nuo Chen, Mingyang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for chest X-ray analysis often achieve strong performance on benchmark datasets, but their reliability under cross-dataset deployment remains unclear. In clinical practice, models trained in one hospital system may be applied to data from another, where distributional and labeling differences can affect both prediction accuracy and model confidence.We conduct a controlled empirical study of model confidence under cross-dataset transfer. We train ResNet-50 and DenseNet-121 on CheXpert and evaluate them on MIMIC-CXR across five experimental settings, focusing on Pneumothorax and Pleural Effusion. We measure predictive performance using AUC and confidence reliability using Expected Calibration Error (ECE), Brier score, and average confidence on incorrect predictions, all reported with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals. We further evaluate post-hoc temperature scaling fit on the source validation set and use Grad-CAM to examine the mechanism behind cross-domain performance differences.Cross-domain AUC drops 7-13 percentage points, and the best in-domain model is not the best cross-domain model. Source-domain temperature scaling produces marginal ECE changes but systematically increases confidence on incorrect predictions across all four model-pathology combinations. Grad-CAM reveals that DenseNet relies on a concentrated spatial prior that fails to transfer, while ResNet's more distributed attention generalizes better. These findings argue for cross-site validation and safety-specific calibration metrics in medical AI evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking VLM Medical Hallucination across 5 Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29934)**  `arXiv:2611.29934`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhixing Cao, Yusen Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucinations in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) within the medical domain remain a difficult and not well-understood problem, and it is still unclear where these models break down. Although several benchmarks targeting medical hallucination have been introduced, they often lack broad coverage across different medical imaging modalities. We introduce Multi-Med, a medical hallucination benchmark containing 1,000 image-question pairs spanning five distinct modalities. Using this benchmark, we perform an in-depth experimental study of 10 widely used VLMs across five modalities and four question categories. Our findings indicate that model performance differs substantially depending on the imaging modality and question type, and that the differences among the best-performing models are not statistically significant.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Gaussian Scene Reconstruction by Integrating Events and Structural Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29920)**  `arXiv:2611.29920`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihao Yu, Guozhen Liu, Jie Yan, Jialiang Liu, Panlong Tan, Qinglin Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing approaches to event-based dynamic Gaussian reconstruction rely on two assumptions for support augmentation and reliability estimation. First, existing Gaussians can absorb reconstruction errors. Second, photometric optimization has converged sufficiently to estimate reliability using analytical variance. Both assumptions can fail under fast motion. When the first fails, regions without Gaussian support cannot generate densification candidates. When the second fails, Gaussians in moving regions where optimization has not converged may be excluded as motion references. We propose Event-Structure Collaborative Dynamic Gaussian Reconstruction (ESC-DGR), using event observations independent of both conditions. Events first identify changes that require additional support, placed only where rendered depth is valid. The augmented representation still needs reliable motion references. Online rendering statistics and historical event evidence therefore propagate displacement estimates from reliable Gaussians to weakly observed neighborhoods. These references describe dominant motion at the frame-interval timescale. Target-time events therefore further condition bounded position residuals for active Gaussians. Support placement, reference selection, and residual prediction thus operate on the same evolving representation within one optimization run. For event-driven monocular dynamic scene reconstruction, our method achieves the best reconstruction quality among the evaluated methods on the E-D3DGS benchmark. Relative to E-D3DGS, dynamic-region PSNR (dPSNR) improves by 1.02 and 1.45 dB on synthetic and real scenes, respectively. The total dynamic Gaussian count across eight synthetic scenes decreases by 8.5%. Although mean frame rate decreases by about 21%-25% relative to E-D3DGS, rendering remains above 80 FPS in all twelve scenes. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SpectralRoute: Adaptive Spectral Routing for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Hyperspectral Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29919)**  `arXiv:2611.29919`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qingmei Li, Xiaoya Fan, Juepeng Zheng, Jianxi Huang, Haohuan Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation enables the recognition of arbitrary categories through natural language descriptions and has recently been extended from natural images to remote sensing. However, existing approaches primarily focus on three-channel optical imagery or limited-band multispectral data, while pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) struggle to directly exploit the rich spectral information of hyperspectral imagery (HSI) without additional adaptation. More importantly, different land-cover categories rely on distinct spectral cues, which may be weakened by uniform fusion. To address these challenges, we propose SpectralRoute, a training-free framework for open-vocabulary hyperspectral segmentation. Specifically, we construct complementary spectral views from HSI and enrich category descriptions with external spectral knowledge, enabling frozen vision-language models to extract diverse spectral-semantic responses. We then introduce a class-adaptive spectral routing mechanism that selectively amplifies discriminative spectral responses for each category, overcoming the limitations of uniform fusion. Furthermore, physical spectral priors are incorporated to refine predictions for visually similar land-cover categories. Experiments on three hyperspectral benchmarks demonstrate that SpectralRoute consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods without additional training.
  </details>

- **[Segment Anything In-Context via Visual Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29896)**  `arXiv:2611.29896`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuzhu Wang, Manni Duan, Hongliang Ye, Shu Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Segment Anything Model via Visual Demonstrations (SAYD), a foundation model for in-context segmentation. Given visual demos comprising prompt images and their segmentation masks, SAYD segments coherent regions (e.g., objects or their parts) in a query image by establishing visual correspondences between the prompts and the query. SAYD generalizes to previously-unseen segmentation tasks, including those requiring tacit visual knowledge or involving concepts that are difficult to articulate unambiguously in language. We train SAYD on 12 public datasets originally curated for panoptic segmentation using our proposed techniques, including a lightweight mask decoder for prompt-agnostic mask generation and a learning mechanism for recognizing novel query classes absent in the prompts. These innovations enhance both computational efficiency and out-of-domain generalization. Consequently, SAYD significantly outperforms existing methods of in-context segmentation and open-vocabulary segmentation across 24 out-of-domain benchmarks, spanning natural, biological, ecological, cosmological, medical, pathological, and satellite imagery.
  </details>

- **[Towards Agentic Video Corpus Moment Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29877)**  `arXiv:2611.29877`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenwei Xu, Shang Wu, Zhenyu Pan, Jianshu Zhang, Pranav Maneriker, Lie Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video corpus moment retrieval (VCMR) requires finding the video and time interval that match a text query in a corpus of videos. We present RELAY, a training-free framework for VCMR that shares memory across queries. RELAY processes a batch of queries in three stages: search with a query bank, ranking with video notes, and localization with video prompt caching. In the search stage, an embedding model scores each video by its best-matching frame. The query bank penalizes videos that score highly for many other queries in the batch. In the ranking stage, a vision-language model (VLM) writes notes on each candidate video's scenes, people, objects, and actions once. Then, a large language model (LLM) reranks the candidates from these notes. In the localization stage, a VLM predicts the time interval of the moment in the top-ranked video. When multiple queries select the same video, prompt caching lets the VLM reuse its frames. On the three VERIFIED benchmarks, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in video retrieval R@1 without training. Without any labeled moment, its predicted intervals are shorter than the annotated ones. In VCMR, it achieves similar performance to VideoSearch-R1 on one benchmark and trails it at IoU 0.5 on the other two. Human annotators also mark shorter moments than the original annotations of the benchmarks. A single margin, calibrated on a few labeled queries, widens the predicted interval to the annotation convention of the benchmarks. With it, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in VCMR at IoU 0.5.
  </details>

- **[CivicBench: Robust Detection of AI-Generated Political Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29766)**  `arXiv:2611.29766`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mert Onur Cakiroglu, Elham Buxton, Mehmet Dalkilic, Hasan Kurban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated video of politicians can pass for real footage, and once such fakes are common, real footage can be dismissed as fake. Platforms address both problems with detectors, but whoever uploads a clip controls its pixels. Existing datasets of political video are small, show real politicians, and test no attacks. We introduce CivicBench, with 10,800 generated clips of invented politicians from eight open generators and 2,632 public-domain or openly licensed real clips. Each clip can be attacked, to hide a fake or to frame real footage. Published detectors trained on a general benchmark reach at most .781 AUC on CivicBench. Adversarial changes of 1 gray level per pixel hide nearly every generated clip from the detectors not built to resist attacks. An attack's reach is how far it can move a clip's score. A clip keeps its label when its margin from the threshold exceeds this reach, and we find that the reach grows almost linearly with the budget. On this basis, we propose Tau-Anchor, which fine-tunes an encoder pretrained on attacked images. It attacks real and generated clips during training and adds a margin term at its deployed threshold. It reaches .941 AUC on CivicBench. It also keeps .668 of generated clips flagged at 2 gray levels, against at most .289 for published detectors. When real clips are framed at the same 2 gray levels, Tau-Anchor's false alarms rise only from 8% to 14%, whereas four published detectors flag nearly every framed real clip at just 1 gray level. External political footage and two general benchmarks show the same pattern under attack. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DictaRashi: A State-of-the-Art OCR Model For Rashi Script](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29760)**  `arXiv:2611.29760`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ometz Shmidman, Yonah Taurog, Aryeh Sanders, Shlomo Bluth, Moshe Koppel, Joshua Geudalia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We release DictaRashi, a Tesseract OCR model for scanned Hebrew books printed in the so-called "Rashi" font. We trained DictaRashi on over one million manually corrected lines of text from over 100 different books of Rabbinic and post-Rabbinic literature. On a held-out set of 1,120 manually transcribed lines from scanned Rashi-script pages, DictaRashi reaches a character error rate (CER) of 0.86% and a word error rate (WER) of 3.38%, setting a new state-of-the-art for publicly-available Tesseract models. We provide a full comparison of our results to those of the 31 other publicly available Hebrew Tesseract models; the best of those models achieves a 2.98% CER and 11.86% WER. We further demonstrate that DictaRashi outperforms the current frontier LLMs on this OCR task. We release the test set of Rashi-script documents so that it may be used as a benchmark for future work in this area. We are pleased to release the model and test set to the community under a CC BY NC 4.0 license.
  </details>

- **[DREAM: Dual-Memory Reasoning for Anticipatory 3D Perception in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29753)**  `arXiv:2611.29753`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sriram Mandalika, Athira Nambiar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous driving systems must not only interpret the current scene but also anticipate perceptual challenges such as occlusions, sparse observations, and recurring failure patterns. However, most existing LiDAR semantic segmentation methods operate in a frame-centric manner, processing each scan independently and discarding past contextual experience, which limits robustness under dynamic scene changes. We propose DREAM, a Dual-memory REAsoning framework for continual LiDAR seMantic segmentation that reframes perception as an experience-driven and anticipatory process. DREAM maintains two complementary memory banks in a strictly online setting: a latent memory that stores compact semantic abstractions of previously observed scenes, and an error memory that records representations associated with uncertain predictions. At each timestep, relevant memory entries are retrieved via cosine similarity and integrated into the feature space through a lightweight modulation mechanism, enabling the model to reinforce consistent semantic patterns while suppressing recurring failure modes. The backbone remains frozen and no past scans are replayed, ensuring computational efficiency and bounded memory growth. Extensive experiments on multiple large-scale LiDAR benchmarks demonstrate that DREAM achieves state-of-the-art performance, with consistent improvements on dynamic and small-scale objects, highlighting the effectiveness of persistent and error-aware memory for robust long-horizon perception.
  </details>

- **[MISeg-Agent: A Human-in-the-Loop Agentic Framework for Error Diagnosis and Executable Prompt Review in 3D Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29741)**  `arXiv:2611.29741`  `cs.CV`  
  _Delin An, Mingqi Xu, Jian-Xun Wang, Pengfei Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the strong performance of modern medical image segmentation models, their predictions can still contain localized errors that compromise segmentation quality. Identifying and correcting these errors is therefore essential for improving the reliability of existing segmentation results. While interactive segmentation models enable targeted corrections, identifying potential errors, determining where to intervene, and evaluating corrective actions remain challenging. Moreover, each correction changes the current mask, requiring subsequent error diagnosis and recommendations to adapt accordingly. To address these challenges, we propose MISeg-Agent, a human-in-the-loop agentic framework that diagnoses segmentation errors and provides evidence-based correction recommendations. MISeg-Agent first selects a suitable pretrained segmentation model based on benchmark performance and computational requirements. Given its predicted mask, the framework identifies potential missing and excess foreground regions and recommends suspicious slices and corrective points for review. The framework then evaluates candidate point prompts using an interactive segmentation model and presents the resulting corrections for human verification before applying them. By operating directly on images and predicted masks, the error diagnosis process remains independent of the underlying segmentation model architecture. Evaluations on CT datasets demonstrate that MISeg-Agent improves initial segmentation results and produces final masks closely aligned with reference annotations. By integrating adaptive error diagnosis with validated correction recommendations, MISeg-Agent provides an interpretable approach to identifying and reducing residual errors in existing medical image segmentations.
  </details>

- **[PROPS: Can Visual Generators Keep the Current Look of a Prop?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29665)**  `arXiv:2611.29665`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangzhao Hao, Dianyi Wang, Keyan Zhou, Yiqian Zhang, Zefeng Zhang, Bailin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many generation tasks draw the next image from a visual history, such as storyboards, illustrated stories and multi-turn editing. A new request often states what happens next without restating how earlier objects look. Instead of recovering an object's current look from the history, generators often redraw it from the request alone or bring back an outdated look, and the error can carry into later steps. We call this failure prop drift. Existing evaluations rarely isolate it, because similarity to the history rewards copying and a judge that sees a single history cannot tell a look recovered from that history from one the generator happens to prefer. We introduce PROPS, a benchmark of 2,257 pairs of twin histories that end on the same request but show one prop in two different looks. A pair passes only if the generator draws the matching look on each side and carries out the request, so a look preferred by the generator or the judge passes at most one side. All 34 open and closed systems we test show prop drift, and even systems that carry out the requested action on most sides often lose the current look of the prop. Swapping the planners and editors of agents further shows that both history understanding and image generation limit agent performance. We also build PROPS-Train, 80,250 training chains in four tracks whose targets recover an earlier state or carry scripted changes forward, and fine-tuning on it clearly improves all four tested models. Both datasets will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Mass Forcing: On-Policy Mass-Aware Distillation for Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29618)**  `arXiv:2611.29618`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiyu Li, Fei Wei, Yong Wang, Zaiquan Yang, Gerhard Petrus Hancke, Xiangxiang Chu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) has emerged as a promising paradigm for real-time video synthesis, distilling pretrained diffusion models into few-step autoregressive generators. However, its reverse-KL objective is mode-seeking and can collapse the student's conditional video distribution onto a narrow subset of modes, leading to reduced sample diversity, diminished temporal dynamics, and color oversaturation. Existing methods mitigate this issue mainly via coverage-oriented student initialization, but we observe that such coverage is transient: across three initializations, the fraction of dynamic videos drops sharply during subsequent DMD training, indicating that the mode-seeking objective, not the initialization, drives this degradation. We propose Mass Forcing, a probability-mass-aware framework that moves on-policy DMD supervision from whole videos to individual states. Each update branches several continuations from a shared student-generated prefix, making the student's probability mass at that state measurable against the teacher. First, Mass Forcing estimates relative teacher-to-student density ratios among these continuations through latent-space score integration and uses them to rebalance conditional DMD updates, amplifying rollouts underrepresented relative to the teacher. Second, since mismatched mode probabilities do not necessarily induce strong local score corrections, it aligns generated rollout sets with a reweighted reference distribution defined by the same mass estimates, providing cross-rollout supervision. Experiments on MovieGen and VBench show that Mass Forcing improves video fidelity and sustains temporal dynamics throughout training, achieving the highest Total scores among compared methods and a Dynamic Degree of 83% after 1,000 generator updates, versus 12-32% for the baselines, with no additional inference-time overhead.
  </details>

- **[HorizonFlow: Few-Step Video Distillation in 600 Iterations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29431)**  `arXiv:2611.29431`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xianglong Guo, Haobo Xu, Zanyi Wang, Zhen Lei, Chang Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distribution matching distillation (DMD) enables fast video generation by distilling a multi-step teacher into a few-step student. However, DMD-based methods often suffer from unnatural motion and costly training. We trace these limitations to the DMD gradient, which jointly optimizes appearance, layout, and motion without distinguishing their learning dynamics. Its static component, which carries appearance and layout, has a much higher signal-to-noise ratio than its motion component. As a result, per-frame quality converges within a few hundred iterations, whereas motion lags far behind. Beyond being noisy, the gradient also lacks two signals. (1) Video VAEs encode several frames into one latent frame (four in Wan2.1), and the gradient provides little guidance for spreading motion over these frames, causing periodic stutter. (2) DMD does not constrain the final sampling step to preserve the layout set by earlier steps, so objects merge and drift once the video moves. We propose HorizonFlow, which decouples the correction of motion from that of layout. It keeps the DMD loss and only shifts its regression target, with one training-only correction in the motion component and one in the orthogonal layout component. Chunk Evenness addresses (1) by spreading the motion of each latent chunk evenly over its frames, and Semantic Commitment addresses (2) by anchoring the coarse layout to the teacher's completion of the student's last step. In only 600 iterations, HorizonFlow distills Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B into a 4-step model using 8.4k training samples, 4.6-304$\times$ fewer than prior work. On VBench, HorizonFlow outperforms all released few-step Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B models. The same recipe transfers to Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B, where our student outperforms NVIDIA's distilled model. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[MSBench: Benchmarking Script-Controlled Multi-Shot Text-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29417)**  `arXiv:2611.29417`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tao Huang, ZiHao Liang, Junhao Liu, Junzhe Li, Yuancheng Wei, Sifan Zuo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-video (T2V) models can now generate visually plausible short clips, but multi-shot generation requires a different set of skills: composing multiple shots with intended cut timing, transition style, camera language, and cross-shot continuity. How far this multi-shot output can be controlled from text remains unmeasured: existing benchmarks either evaluate single shots or, when they provide shot-level scripts, typically check whether the requested shots appear but not when they change or how they are joined. We introduce MSBench, a benchmark for controllable text-driven multi-shot video generation built around structured Scene-Entity-Shot mini-scripts that specify each shot's time span, camera state, content, and outgoing transition. MSBench defines a four-dimensional hybrid evaluation framework spanning Visual Quality, Dynamic Quality, Semantic Alignment, and Cross-Shot Consistency with 11 sub-metrics. Experiments on 14 commercial and open-source systems show that visual quality does little to separate the systems, whereas multi-shot control does: many models cut mainly on scene changes and render requested gradual transitions as hard cuts. Human evaluations further validate MSBench, with its system ranking reaching a Spearman correlation of 0.87 with human preferences. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ClothCast: Generative Cloth World Modeling under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29369)**  `arXiv:2611.29369`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yushen Hu, Yilong Song, Zhen Zhou, Apurv Kushwaha, Harshvardhan Chandirasekar, Zhu-Tian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models support robotic manipulation by predicting how the scene evolves under a planned action. For garments this prediction is notoriously difficult: deformations are complex, and self-occlusion leaves much of the cloth unobserved. Planned gripper trajectories provide a useful motion prior because cloth held by a gripper moves with it, while the remaining deformation must be predicted. We introduce ClothCast, a world model that applies this prior to the cloth's points and learns the residual motion. ClothCast takes one RGB-D observation, the camera pose, the robot's own model and the planned gripper trajectories, with no cloth mesh or material parameters. It lifts the observation to a layered point set that includes hypothesized occluded layers, carries the points with a blended rigid prior derived from the gripper poses, and predicts the residual motion with a video-diffusion point-flow model. Over entire 4- to 11-second episodes at 10 Hz, the moved points are rendered into depth, from which a geometry-conditioned completion model synthesizes RGB; after release, a hand-designed settling step replaces the learned motion. We further release ClothCastBench, 10,000 simulated dual-arm cloth-manipulation episodes with the ground-truth cloth mesh, including occluded layers, at every frame. On 492 held-out episodes, ClothCast achieves 2.5x lower sequence-averaged visible-surface Chamfer distance than Flex-{\pi}, the strongest baseline in geometry (14.0 mm vs. 35.6 mm), and 3.7 dB higher cloth-region PSNR than Cosmos-Predict2.5, the strongest baseline in cloth PSNR. On a 50-episode ablation subset, the rigid prior alone, applied to ClothCast's learned initial geometry with the same post-processing, already outperforms both geometry-capable baselines, and the learned residual lowers its error by about a third.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Attention Maps: Understanding and Improving Medical MLLMs via Visual Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29361)**  `arXiv:2611.29361`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yichen Zhao, Fenghe Tang, Wenxin Ma, Rongsheng Wang, Zihao Wan, Shaohua Kevin Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current MLLM methods largely focus on whether models attend to the right visual regions, implicitly treating spatial attention map as the primary key of effective visual perception. To examine this assumption, we introduce a medical VQA dataset with paired non-contrast (NC) and contrast-enhanced (CE) images that share corresponding anatomy while providing different cues. We show that this view is incomplete: across paired NC and CE medical images, MLLMs often exhibit similar attention patterns despite producing contrasting answers, while controlled bidirectional interventions induce substantially larger prediction changes on visual values than attention weights. These results identify the visual content retrieved through values as a critical factor in improving perception beyond spatial attention alone. Guided by this finding, we propose VIVID (VIsual Value Intervention Distillation), which uses enhanced visual values to identify and refine teacher corrections aligned with useful visual evidence to improve on-policy distillation from CE to NC images. Across six internal and external test sets, VIVID consistently outperforms existing on-policy distillation methods across different model scales and architectures, requires only NC images at inference, and maintains strong zero-shot performance on general-domain VQA benchmarks. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Polytropos: What Do Vision-Language Model Descriptions Make of a Cultural Object?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29355)**  `arXiv:2611.29355`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuechen Jiang, Enze Zhang, Yuyan Wang, Mohsinul Kabir, Chenying Lin, Sijia Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
