  Vision-language models have become a new kind of describer, writing about almost anything people see, and their descriptions now shape how cultures are known by people and by other models. Descriptions of cultures tend to turn particular things into types, and what a description loses shows what its describer lacks. We introduce Polytropos, a benchmark that measures what model descriptions of cultural objects keep of the particular object by turning each description back into an image and comparing it with the original photograph. On 374 objects from the Metropolitan Museum of Art and the Getty, with 1,870 descriptions from five models and 230 written by curators, reconstructions are recognised as the recorded culture more often than the photographs, at 54.55% to 59.09% against 52.14%, yet 16% to 21% of objects change outcome and only 0.49% of reconstructions are identified as the specific object. On the same objects, models lose recorded cultures at a rate similar to curators but gain more. Model descriptions give each object many faces, each typical of its culture and almost none the object itself.
  </details>

- **[A Multi-Criteria Evaluation of Color Normalization Methods in Histopathology: Nuclei Preservation, Artifact Sensitivity, Background Fidelity, and Downstream Performanc](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29348)**  `arXiv:2611.29348`  `cs.CV`  
  _Falah Jabar, Aria Torkpour, Pasquale Lombardi, Masoud Tafavvoghi, Per Niklas Benzler Waaler, Sigve Andersen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for nuclei detection and classification may generalize poorly across medical centers because of variations in staining and scanning. Although color normalization aims to reduce these variations, its effects on nuclear structure, background preservation, artifact sensitivity, and nuclei detection and classification performance have not been systematically evaluated. We present a procedure to benchmark 11 classical and deep learning normalization methods using 7 public and private nuclei datasets. Additionally, we investigate the effect of 7 input color spaces. The normalization methods differed in their effects on nuclear structure, background, and sensitivity to artifacts. However, detection and classification performance varied more across datasets than across normalization methods, and the input color space had limited influence. No single method performed best on all criteria, and image-level metrics did not reliably predict downstream performance, highlighting the need for nuclei- and structure-aware normalization methods. To support this work, we introduce WSI Quality Control and Normalization (WSI-QCN), a publicly available pipeline for robust whole-slide image quality assessment and color normalization; the source code is available on GitHub.
  </details>

- **[SKIA: Spatial-Knowledge Informed Attention for Robust Remote Photoplethysmography under Lower-Face Motion Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29330)**  `arXiv:2611.29330`  `cs.CV`  
  _Van-Truong Le, Viet-Tham Huynh, Minh-Triet Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables contactless physiological monitoring from facial videos, but facial motion, particularly mouth and jaw movements during cognitive and communicative tasks, can severely degrade signal reconstruction. We present SKIA, a lightweight, mask-guided framework that suppresses motion-prone lower-face regions while preserving physiological cues from the forehead and cheeks. Using the UBFC-rPPG benchmark, we first investigate static lower-face suppression in the classical CHROM pipeline. Static masking improves waveform Pearson correlation from 0.3507 to 0.4166 (p < 0.001) and reduces heart-rate mean absolute error (MAE) from 33.50 to 23.44 bpm. Building on this finding, we introduce SKIA-Net, a compact spatiotemporal architecture that combines raw facial frames, statically masked representations, and temporal motion differences to reconstruct blood volume pulse (BVP) waveforms. The full model achieves a Pearson correlation of 0.7522 and a heart-rate MAE of 3.73 bpm on UBFC-rPPG, outperforming single-stream variants. Ablation studies show that the static mask provides complementary information to raw appearance and that static anatomical priors outperform the tested dynamic optical-flow-based masking strategy. These results suggest that spatially informed, temporally stable masking can improve motion-robust rPPG estimation. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Prompt-Consistent Cross-View Object Geo-Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29312)**  `arXiv:2611.29312`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lingyun Xing, Wenning Hao, Kai Jiang, Dawei Jin, Xiaohan Zhang, Xiaoxiong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-view object geo-localization (CVOGL) aims to locate an object indicated by a click point in a ground or drone query image within a satellite reference image. Through a quantitative prompt-sensitivity evaluation, we find that existing CVOGL methods suffer from a prompt-disobeying overfitting problem: when the click point is moved or removed, their predictions barely respond, indicating that the models rely on spurious feature correlations rather than genuinely following the prompt semantics. We trace this behavior to the limited scale of the benchmark dataset, which contains only 4,343 prompt-object pairs per task, so that the models simply memorize the object associated with each scene instead of learning the true prompt-to-object correspondence. To address this, we propose two plug-and-play components. First, we construct the CVOGL-MO dataset by expanding the existing training and test sets with diverse multi-object annotations, mitigating the memorization that induces shortcut learning. Second, we propose the Cross-View Co-Scene Multi-Object Contrastive Loss (CVMCL), which enforces distinct feature representations for different click points within the same scene, compelling the model to follow prompt instructions. We further design a prompt-sensitivity evaluation protocol with prediction-shift metrics, providing the first quantitative measurement of prompt-following capability for CVOGL. Both components are architecture-agnostic and can be integrated into existing methods without any architectural modification. Experiments on three representative backbones show that our components substantially improve prompt-following accuracy on the new multi-object test sets and markedly increase prompt sensitivity. Our dataset and code will be publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CT-Navigator: Towards Interpretable and Generalizable CT Understanding through Agentic Perception and Tool-Invocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29305)**  `arXiv:2611.29305`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yibing Fu, Huidong Xie, Yihao Niu, Wei Ji, Heming Dong, Ziyue Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical computed tomography (CT) interpretation inherently requires progressive visual exploration, where radiologists navigate from whole scans to individual slices and regions of interest and interact with external tools when necessary. However, existing 3D medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) typically connect a specialized 3D visual encoder to a language model, without fully leveraging the native 2D visual and agentic capabilities of generalist MLLMs. Trained on large-scale one-hop CT instruction-following data, these models also have limited ability to perform iterative reasoning and explicitly ground their conclusions in visual evidence, limiting their support for traceable CT interpretation. To solve these problems, we propose \textbf{agentic perception} as an alternative paradigm inspired by radiologists' interpretation workflows. We introduce \textbf{CT-Navigator}, a framework that harnesses generalist MLLMs' 2D visual and agentic capabilities to progressively ground CT interpretation in explicit, traceable visual evidence through hierarchical visual exploration and radiology tool invocation. To build these capabilities, we curate 8,904 visual chain-of-thought (V-CoT) trajectories for cold-start agentic supervised fine-tuning. We then optimize the policy through reinforcement learning on 33,219 questions, with data selection and dynamic rollout sampling prioritizing informative learning signals. Finally, we introduce CTNavi-Bench, a 3D CT visual question answering benchmark comprising 7,000 questions spanning anatomical regions throughout the human body. CT-Navigator achieves 79.50\% accuracy on CTNavi-Bench, surpassing the strongest baseline by 17.59\%, and demonstrates strong generalization across three external CT benchmarks. The code, model, and benchmark will be released on \href{this https URL}{GitHub}.
  </details>

- **[ContinuLoc: Continuous Pose Inference over Neural Fields for UAV Geo-Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29276)**  `arXiv:2611.29276`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weikun Zhou, Jinliang Lin, Yu Zang, Lin Xie, Haiqing Fu, Sheng Ao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-view UAV geo-localization involves three design choices: how to represent the UAV observation, how to represent the geo-referenced map, and how to infer pose from their interaction. The dominant paradigm organizes the map as a discretized 2DoF candidate library, coupling map representation to pose inference and thus bounding spatial localization precision by candidate sampling resolution. We decouple map representation from pose inference by encoding the geo-referenced map as a continuously queryable semantic neural field, enabling localization to be formulated as explicit optimization over a continuous 4DoF pose space parameterized by 2D ground position, in-plane heading, and viewing scale. Pose hypotheses are evaluated on demand using global descriptors in a shared semantic feature space, bypassing the sampling bottleneck of retrieval. We learn 4DoF pose-sensitive visual representations under full 4DoF supervision provided by ContinuScenes, a multi-city benchmark with dense near-nadir UAV imagery and complete pose annotations. Inference proceeds hierarchically, progressing from broad pose-space coverage to adaptive concentration on high-compatibility regions. Experiments demonstrate substantial gains over representative localization paradigms under progressively stricter 4DoF accuracy criteria.Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[MM-DataLoop: Closing the Data-Side Loop for Recursive Self-Improvement in MLLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29254)**  `arXiv:2611.29254`  `cs.CV`  
  _Likang Peng, Chen Ju, Xu Chen, Xiangyang Wu, Jinsong Lan, Haihong Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a path toward improving models through repeated evaluation and revision. We study its data side in multimodal language model (MLLM) post-training, where heterogeneous operations and cross-round evidence must be coordinated. MMDataLoop organizes data interventions into five operation families and connects them through an Observe-Diagnose-Plan-Execute-Verify-Reflect cycle with shared rule memory. Across 34 candidate rounds on Qwen3.5-0.8B and five benchmarks, we hold the base initialization, shared training schedule and evaluation protocol fixed while varying data recipes. The five-benchmark mean reaches 63.04 at R33, up 5.32 points from the zero-shot baseline; R29, R32, and R33 form a close top cluster within 0.09 points. In eightcandidate comparisons, guided search reaches the preselected target in six candidates and identifies the top observed candidate, while random arms show higher means and lower dispersion. The selected R29 recipe also scores above two full-pool baselines under the same epoch schedule. Together, these findings establish MM-DataLoop as a traceable workflow and provide an empirical basis for studying how data-side decisions shape outcomes across MLLM post-training recipes.
  </details>

- **[Complementary Geometry in Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29236)**  `arXiv:2611.29236`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingle Jiang, Jikui Zhou, Chenghan Lyu, Rui Xu, Yunke Wang, Chang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gaussian Splatting has achieved high visual quality in novel view synthesis, but the accuracy of its rendered geometry remains a challenge. In particular, the rendered depth is computed as an opacity-weighted average along each ray, which may not correspond to the actual scene surface. In this paper, we observe that depth predictions from different views of the same frozen Gaussian representation exhibit complementary errors, allowing us to improve novel-view geometry using information from other views without modifying the original representation. Based on this observation, we propose REVEAL, a general framework that selectively refines novel-view depth using complementary geometric information. Specifically, we first reproject depth predictions from source views to the target view and combine them using robust multi-view consensus. We then introduce a learned selection mechanism to determine whether the candidate depth is more accurate than the original prediction, based on the expected reduction in depth error. In addition, we introduce RIG, a real-world benchmark captured with synchronized RGB-D cameras, which provides ground-truth depth for evaluating both static and dynamic reconstruction models. Experiments on 6 Gaussian Splatting backbones across 3 benchmarks show that REVEAL consistently improves depth accuracy while maintaining comparable rendering quality.
  </details>

- **[Unleashing the Value of Raw Point Clouds for 3D Understanding with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29194)**  `arXiv:2611.29194`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tailai Chen, Jiawei Yu, Yiming Cheng, Ping Nie, Yongyong Chen, Tao Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard 3D point cloud language models typically rely on pretrained point encoders to extract 3D features and project them into representations processable by large language models (LLMs). However, these encoders are often originally optimized for pre-defined tasks, resulting in a representation bottleneck that may lead to a substantial mismatch with downstream 3D understanding. Moreover, existing models largely follow a single pass closed-book generation, limiting their ability to actively inspect task-relevant 3D regions or retrieve necessary external knowledge. In this work, we present PointTool, an encoder-free and tool-augmented framework for 3D reasoning. PointTool introduces Alignment-Free Patch Tokenization (AFPT) to locally aggregate raw point clouds while preserving fine-grained geometric structure, and Decoupled Point-Text LoRA (DPL) to facilitate global aggregation and 3D knowledge internalization within a frozen LLM. Building upon these designs, Program-Supervised Tool Reasoning (PSTR) enables the model to explicitly operate on raw 3D data and external resources through executable tools, supporting interactive 3D reasoning grounded in spatial evidence. We further introduce PointCloud-TR, a benchmark comprising 13K 3D objects, 39.8K questions, and 35.6K tool trajectories to facilitate training and evaluation of complex 3D reasoning. Experiments demonstrate that PointTool achieves strong performance on both standard and complex 3D understanding tasks, validating the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
  </details>

- **[PhyCoVE: Benchmarking Physical Counterfactual Video Editing across Diverse Material Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29184)**  `arXiv:2611.29184`  `cs.CV`  
  _Conghan Yue, Yuanjie Chen, Yunyan Xiao, Zeyu Peng, Xingsong Ye, Ya Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical counterfactual video editing turns a source video and a language-specified physical edit at a given execution frame into a counterfactual video that shows how the scene would evolve after the edit. Existing evaluations with counterfactual target videos mainly cover object removal or specific rigid-body motions, although real scenes also involve elastic deformation, fluid flow, and interactions among rigid bodies, elastic solids, and fluids. We introduce PhyCoVE, a benchmark of 533 cases on 110 source videos that covers rigid, elastic, and fluid dynamics in five interaction patterns, with edits that remove an object, change a physical quantity, or insert a new object. For the simulated cases, a language model proposes scene designs, coding agents build them with solvers suited to each material and physically based rendering and then simulate each edit from the source scene state, and humans check every stage. For the real cases, we record continuously from one camera position before and after each edit. We also propose counterfactual fidelity, a metric that applies across materials and their interactions and measures how much of the physical change from the source video to the target video a generated video reproduces. We evaluate seven open-source and commercial video editing models and find that counterfactual fidelity ranks them nearly as human preference does, but even the best model reproduces only about one seventh of the physical change. The models change how a scene looks but rarely how it evolves, whereas a physical edit requires understanding the dynamics that it changes. Physical counterfactual video editing thus remains far from solved, and PhyCoVE provides a foundation for developing and evaluating methods for it.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free 3D Editing via Feature-Divergence Localization and Trajectory Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29165)**  `arXiv:2611.29165`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tri Ton, Siwoo Lim, Joshua Tian Jin Tee, Chang D. Yoo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modifying 3D assets requires enacting targeted structural transformations while strictly preserving the geometric identity of unedited regions. While recent probability flow models enable training-free editing, bounding these modifications within a high-dimensional latent space presents severe mathematical challenges. Current inversion-free trajectories rely on global velocity updates, which often cause spatial bleed into preserved structures and induce trajectory under-commitment, causing edits to stall mid-path. To resolve these dual failures, we introduce FocusFlow, a segmentation-free framework that structures the integration trajectory into two disjoint phases. During early coarse structure formation, FocusFlow applies Feature-Divergence Localization (FDL) to spatially gate the velocity field using intrinsic cross-attention. Once coarse structure has formed, FocusFlow applies Reference Generation Guidance (RGG), a trajectory-correction term that drives convergence to the target shape by pulling the trajectory toward an explicit clean-latent target. Evaluated on the joint Google Scanned Objects and PartObjaverse-Tiny benchmarks, FocusFlow obtains the best preservation scores and the largest edit magnitude among compared methods, suggesting that it can reduce the traditional preservation-modification trade-off. By providing stable, localized geometric control without manual spatial annotations, this phase-scheduled approach lowers technical barriers to the modification of synthetic 3D media.
  </details>

- **[RVLM: Fewer Tokens, Better Resolution Scaling, Fine-Grained Spatial Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29146)**  `arXiv:2611.29146`  `cs.CV`  
  _Savya Khosla, Chris Dongjoo Kim, Alex Schwing, Derek Hoiem, Christopher Clark, Sangho Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) tokenize images using fixed spatial grids, so visual token count grows with pixel area rather than scene content. We propose to group neighboring patches based on their visual appearance, allowing tokens to represent coherent image regions rather than fixed spatial windows. We introduce RVLM, a VLM trained to use regions rather than patches as its visual units. Analyzing region construction for general-purpose VLMs, we find that regions must cover the full image, stay spatially connected during end-to-end training, and preserve fine structure such as small text, while semantic grouping adds little. RVLM therefore partitions images using training-free Felzenszwalb-Huttenlocher segmentation, assigns each ViT patch to a region, and pools patches within each region into a variable-length sequence of visual tokens. The patch-to-region assignment is retained to support spatially precise prediction through coarse-to-fine decoding. Across tasks spanning VQA, pointing, counting, segmentation, and depth estimation, RVLM matches or improves example-weighted task performance while using 21.5% fewer visual tokens than an identically trained patch baseline. The advantage grows with image resolution, reaching a 5.3-point gain while using 51.8% fewer visual tokens at the highest-resolution setting. The gains are largest on spatially demanding tasks, where prior token-reduction methods degrade most sharply. RVLM adds no model parameters and improves inference throughput by up to 35.9%. On unseen pathology and satellite images and on video benchmarks without any video training, RVLM performs on par with the patch baseline while using 2-3$\times$ fewer visual tokens. These results establish regions as a scalable alternative to patch-based visual representations while preserving the spatial precision required for grounded prediction.
  </details>

- **[RAVEC: Reward-Aligned Visual Evidence Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29142)**  `arXiv:2611.29142`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junjie Yang, Rui Qian, Xutao Mao, Yuhe Wu, Mingda Zhang, Jiaxuan Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards improves multimodal reasoning, but response-level feedback provides limited guidance on which intermediate decisions to correct. Visual interventions reveal sensitivity to image content, yet this sensitivity alone does not establish reward alignment or support across questions. We introduce RAVEC (Reward-Aligned Visual Evidence Correction), which selects visual corrections using local and cross-question reward support and jointly determines their strengths within each response. Paired Visual Evidence Contrast (PVEC) compares predictions from an exponential moving average teacher at the same sampled response prefix under the original image and a version with its annotated evidence region suppressed, yielding signed correction directions. Credit-Risk Allocation (CRA) screens these directions using local reward alignment and reward support from other questions, excluding all responses to the source question. It then solves a response-level budgeted allocation problem, assigning additional cost to directions that locally reduce predictive entropy. The resulting signed visual loss augments Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) during training. Inference uses only the original image and question. Across ten multimodal benchmarks, RAVEC achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared methods with both 3B and 7B Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct models, outperforming GRPO by 2.67 and 1.39 percentage points, respectively. Component ablations support the combined design of evidence alignment, credit qualification, and risk-aware allocation. The project is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recurrent Point Transformer via Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29059)**  `arXiv:2611.29059`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wentao Zhou, Xuweiyi Chen, Charles Saunders, Aruni RoyChowdhury, Zezhou Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world point clouds are not naturally bounded: 3D scans grow as sensors move through rooms, LiDAR surveys extend across forest plots, and 4D captures can span thousands of frames. Yet most point-cloud encoders are designed for fixed-size inputs, forcing inference-time cropping or tiling that loses long-range context and requires ad-hoc output stitching. We formulate arbitrary-extent point-cloud segmentation as recurrent sequence-of-chunks processing and introduce PoinTTT, a recurrent point-cloud encoder built on Point Transformer V3 (PTv3). PoinTTT augments PTv3 with residual Large Chunk Test-Time Training (LaCT) layers in both the encoder and decoder. Each LaCT layer maintains compact fast weights as recurrent state: tokens from each chunk update the fast weights, which are then applied to produce chunk features and passed to the next chunk. This design allows PoinTTT to train on fixed-size chunks while processing scenes of arbitrary spatial or temporal extent, with activation memory bounded by the chunk size. A pilot study on ScanNet and nuScenes shows that recurrent TTT improves segmentation over PTv3 baselines. On FOR-instanceV2, PoinTTT achieves state-of-the-art forest semantic segmentation of 87.9 mIoU with nearly constant memory. On HOI4D, online full-sequence recurrent inference carries state across clips rather than resetting at benchmark boundaries, reaching 42.6 mIoU; a matched full-sequence Mamba control reaches approximately 39.9. These results suggest that test-time training is a practical primitive for scalable spatial and temporal point-cloud understanding.
  </details>

- **[T2ISpec: Benchmarking Speculative Decoding for Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29029)**  `arXiv:2611.29029`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yifan Zhang, Shatong Zhu, Hui Shen, Yunta Hsieh, Jianing Ma, Muxin Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) generators produce detailed images but remain slow at inference. Speculative decoding can reduce this cost, yet its behavior across the rendering requirements of T2I prompts remains insufficiently understood. We introduce T2ISpec, a benchmark of 1,400 prompts balanced across seven rendering topics and two difficulty levels. It evaluates autoregressive and diffusion/flow accelerators by latency, work performed by the target model, and image quality. Our study reveals three key findings: (1) aggregate results hide large differences between topics, (2) alignment and preference scores can disagree with prompt constraint satisfaction, and (3) large reductions in target work can yield limited latency gains. Motivated by these findings, we propose TopicRoute, a plugin that combines prompt routing with speculative and cache controls without retraining the generator. Across six model--method pairs, it achieves a geometric mean speedup of 1.0308x over the native methods, and the speedup carries over to external prompts with the same settings, while quality gains and losses vary across configurations.
  </details>

- **[What the Background Hides: Thresholds, Vocabularies and Collisions in Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28966)**  `arXiv:2611.28966`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wendong Yao, Binhua Huang, Soumyabrata Dev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) built on CLIP has advanced rapidly, with methods typically ranked by mean intersection-over-union (mIoU) across standard benchmarks. On datasets with a background class, however, implementations rely on per-dataset thresholds and background word lists that are rarely documented or unified (11 of 13 surveyed methods configure per-dataset background decisions). We audit configurations and papers of 13 training-free OVSS methods, re-run seven from official codebases, and compare official settings against a strict unified protocol under pre-registered contracts. The protocol gap depends strongly on the benchmark---ranging from 22.8 mIoU points on PASCAL VOC (VOC21) to 0.1 points (indistinguishable from zero) on ADE20K---and alters pairwise method rankings. Despite large shifts in absolute mIoU, most of the reported improvement of later CLIP-only methods over SCLIP remains under the unified protocol (roughly 79% of the published gain). Decomposing official background handling shows that it combines numeric settings and class-name files whose dominant component reverses across benchmarks, while no tested tuning-free rule transfers reliably across all datasets. Moreover, exact collisions between background words and target names---present in six of seven official COCO-Object configurations through "bed"---make affected classes unpredictable under official post-processing, while the main per-class drops under language-model-generated vocabularies on VOC21 are largely attributable to a few generated terms. We offer reporting recommendations and release our evaluation code, frozen configurations, contracts, and a collision-check tool.
  </details>

- **[PIAE: Physics-Informed Continuous Autoencoding for Compact 3D Human Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28922)**  `arXiv:2611.28922`  `cs.CV`  
  _Osama Alshammari, Omar Mohammed, Kerven Durdymyradov, Naeemullah Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal compression reduces the sequence length that motion generators must model, but can damage articulated detail and global trajectory. These errors have unequal consequences: root-velocity and heading errors propagate through trajectory recovery and affect the placement of the entire body. We propose PIAE, a physics-informed continuous autoencoder that allocates a fixed token budget between explicitly retained kinematic signals and complementary learned features. At 8 x temporal compression, each 256-dimensional token contains 224 learned values and a 32-dimensional physical carrier. The complete token conditions neural decoding, while a parallel recovery path constrains selected outputs. On the official HumanML3D benchmark, PIAE achieves 8.936 mm global MPJPE, an 82.0% reduction relative to a same-width learned-only model. The benefit extends beyond trajectory restoration: facing-local neural-body error before analytical correction decreases by 76.6% in a frozen-checkpoint validation diagnostic, and supplying the same true trajectory to both models preserves the body-quality advantage. Targeted root-only and explicit-root hybrid controls establish strong trajectory-aware comparisons; alternative carriers reveal that torso references favor position accuracy, whereas stance anchors favor support quality. With the original 8 x AEs frozen, matched text-to-motion generators predict every carrier value and improve FID from 0.506 to 0.163. Frozen reconstruction also transfers to AIST++ without adaptation. The compressed carrier interface reduces measured generation time by 27.8% relative to its 1 x counterpart. Together, these findings demonstrate that selective preservation can use a fixed representation budget more effectively, improving neural articulated reconstruction and downstream generation in compact human motion.
  </details>

- **[Gaze Graphs: A Scene-Level Representation of Visual Attention in the Operating Room](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28901)**  `arXiv:2611.28901`  `cs.CV`  
  _Séraphin Baributsa, Keqi Chen, Vinkle Srivastav, Nicolas Padoy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: Analyzing clinicians' visual attention from exocentric camera views can support surgical scene understanding without requiring wearable eye trackers. However, existing gaze-following research in the operating room (OR) estimates only 2D gaze locations without identifying attended scene entities or localizing gaze targets in 3D space. In this work, we investigate gaze graphs as a scene-level representation of visual attention that connects clinicians to attended scene entities, and evaluate their utility for fine-grained surgical action recognition and semantic scene graph (SSG) generation. Methods: We extend the public EgoExOR dataset with gaze annotations to create EgoExOR-Gaze, the first benchmark for multi-view gaze graph generation in the OR. We propose a novel architecture that jointly estimates 3D gaze targets and predicts attended entities using only exocentric camera views. Specifically, our approach exploits 3D gaze geometry to sample local visual features across multiple views for attended entity prediction. We further utilize predicted gaze graphs for fine-grained surgical action recognition and SSG generation. Results: For gaze graph generation, our model outperforms both exocentric-only and combined exocentric-egocentric baselines. For surgical action recognition, our model incorporating predicted gaze graphs outperforms both exocentric and egocentric baselines across all evaluated metrics, with particularly strong gains in temporal metrics. For SSG generation, incorporating gaze graphs improves macro-F1 from 0.42 to 0.52 compared to exocentric inputs alone, although performance remains below that achieved with egocentric inputs. Conclusion: Our results demonstrate the value of gaze graphs for surgical action recognition and SSG generation, highlighting the importance of explicitly modeling scene-level visual attention even when egocentric views are unavailable.
  </details>

- **[Controlled One-Click Benchmarking and Post-Fusion Recalibration for Support-Conditioned Medical Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28890)**  `arXiv:2611.28890`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wang-Chi-Shiun, Wei-Huan Hu, Liu-En-Chi, Liu Zhi Shun, ChengYi Peng, Shin-Jye Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse-prompt medical segmentation is hard to compare when prompts, supports, thresholds, and splits differ. This paper uses a controlled one-click protocol: each test image receives one positive foreground click, training-only support, validation-only checkpoint selection, and no test-time threshold tuning. We propose {Support-Aware Recalibration (SAR)}, a late post-fusion recalibration block for MultiverSeg that combines large-kernel spatial gating with squeeze-and-excitation channel recalibration after support--query fusion. BUSI, NerveUS-BP-present, and Kvasir-SEG are evaluated with matched 697 foreground-present sample budgets, shared splits/clicks, and threshold-free Dice-AUC/IoU-AUC. Compared with a protocol-matched raw-support MultiverSeg baseline, the main SAR configuration shows favorable mean Dice-AUC/IoU-AUC performance on BUSI, NerveUS-BP-present, and Kvasir-SEG, while reducing logged training time in this implementation. These controlled results indicate that late post-fusion recalibration is useful under a leakage-controlled one-click protocol.
  </details>

- **[Eidetic Code World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28878)**  `arXiv:2611.28878`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenqing Tian, Zijian Zhang, Qiang Liu, Liang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Executable code can preserve a world's structural layout, yet video renderers frequently alter the appearance of previously visited places once they leave the camera view. We present Paintress, a code-conditioned world model that couples an executable geometric proxy with an independently parameterized 3D Gaussian appearance memory in a shared metric coordinate frame. While the proxy enforces structural and visibility constraints, 3D Gaussians capture the visual realization synthesized by emitted observations. To balance stable incremental fusion with consistent readout, Paintress separates writing from reading: newly emitted frames update a persistent raw Gaussian map under confidence-gated selection, whereas a cloned reader copy is globally refined against the observation archive and rendered with coverage masks to condition generation. We also introduce Expedition-33, a benchmark for generated-appearance retention across 11 static scenes, five route families, and 15, 30, and 60 s target absences (165 settings per system). Excluded from the opening image, targets are synthesized during a shared prefix and scored against each system's own first visit at matched return cameras. Across ten systems (1,650 videos), Paintress achieves 0.1220 ROI-LPIPS, 0.0613 MAE, and 52.12% detail retention (vs. 0.2731, 0.1401, and 10.91% for CWM), with lower discrepancy at 60 s (0.1630) than CWM, AgentGarten, and Lyra 2.0 achieve at 15 s.
  </details>

- **[MQCODet: Modality Quality-Guided Collaborative Optimization for UAV-Based RGB-T Tiny Pedestrian Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28850)**  `arXiv:2611.28850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hui Li, Jianbo Ma, Hui Luo, Guanglin Qi, Zhixing Wang, Haorui Zuo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-based RGB-thermal (RGB-T) remote sensing provides complementary visual and thermal information for pedestrian perception under complex aerial observation conditions. However, detecting tiny pedestrians in UAV-based RGB-T imagery remains challenging due to cross-modal spatial heterogeneity, manifested as modality quality imbalance and spatial position misalignment. Existing RGB-T detectors primarily address these challenges at the feature fusion stage, without establishing an effective coupling between feature fusion and object localization, potentially resulting in suboptimal detection performance. To address this limitation, we introduce a Modality Quality-Guided Collaborative Optimization Detector (MQCODet), which extends modality quality from a fusion prior to a localization supervision cue. Specifically, we propose a Dynamic Calibration and Region-Aware Fusion (DCRF) module that progressively refines cross-modal spatial alignment and performs region-aware fusion to generate reliable multi-scale features and a scene-level modality quality prior. We further design an Efficient Contextual Interaction (ECI) encoder to enhance multi-scale feature interaction while preserving fine-grained spatial details. Moreover, we propose a Modality-Aware Adaptive (MAA) loss that incorporates the modality prior and object scale to adaptively balance spatial consistency and geometric similarity for localization supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MQCODet surpasses state-of-the-art methods on two UAV-based RGB-T tiny pedestrian detection benchmarks while achieving real-time inference on the edge platform.
  </details>

- **[Omni-H3-Image: Constrained Foundation Priors Usher in a New Era of All-in-One Blind Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28836)**  `arXiv:2611.28836`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihao He, Songhua Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-the-shelf unified image models such as 33B MiniMax-H3-Image, FLUX.1-Kontext, and Qwen-Image-Edit-2511 synthesize crisp textures yet collapse to 11.33-16.74 dB PSNR across 429 degraded public restoration scenes due to spatial drift, chromatic hallucination, and semantic fabrication. We present Omni-H3-Image (Omni-H3), an approximate three-stage constrained posterior projection framework over unmodified MiniMax-H3-Image and Qwen3-VL-32B using only a 0.92M-parameter auxiliary weather pre-conditioner (<0.003% of backbone weights). Omni-H3 unifies blind physics-VLM sensing with exact-32 px lattice anchoring, joint affine-Wiener-Butterworth projection in opponent YCbCr space, and closed-loop Z=4 text verification with sub-Nyquist abstention. Across 925 blind tasks against 11 external SOTA baselines -- five public benchmarks (N=647), Dev-160, and a held-out locked_test suite (N=118) -- Omni-H3 recovers +4.73 dB degraded-scene PSNR over Base H3 (21.47 vs. 16.74 dB; +6.27-10.14 dB over other omni backbones) while winning overall #1 DISTS (0.207), #1 NIQE (4.45), and #1 Edge-F1 (0.807) on all 429 degraded public scenes (MUSIQ 66.8 vs. 40-44 for discriminative SOTA), 76.59 dB on CBSD68 Clean, -91.6% Urban100 chroma drift, #1 2K Dev Non-Clean DISTS (0.202), and #1 locked_test 2K DISTS (0.173) and 132-ROI SubCER, cutting pre-annotated 132-ROI SubCER from 0.456 to 0.319 under non-Qwen RapidOCR (PP-OCRv4) (with recognizer-dependent tradeoffs under TrOCR-large-str).
  </details>

- **[3DCodeFactory: Scaling Training Data for 3D Code Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28783)**  `arXiv:2611.28783`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoyu Wang, Xueru Wen, XingYu Li, Jie Lou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As code agents evolve, their scope is expanding from generating simple static 3D meshes to constructing structurally complete, complex, interactive 3D scenes. However, existing datasets mainly rely on converting or captioning pre-existing 3D assets, consisting mostly of single-turn code data for static single objects and struggling to scale task diversity and scene complexity, leaving such training data scarce. We present 3DCodeFactory, an automated framework that directly constructs diverse, complex 3D training data from abundant raw text (e.g., diffusion prompts and product descriptions). The framework first leverages an image generator to synthesize 3D reference images from raw text as visual anchors, guiding the generation of concrete modeling specifications and interactive functional requirements; meanwhile, it jointly constructs visual, specification, and functional rubrics from the reference images and requirements to automatically score and filter multi-turn agent rollouts. Based on this framework, we release 3DCodeFactory-Data, comprising 5,045 high-quality task instances and multi-turn interaction trajectories. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.8-27B on 3DCodeFactory-Data significantly improves performance on BlenderWorld, P3D-Bench, and 3DCodeBench, consistently outperforming models trained on existing open-source 3D code datasets, with our 27B model further surpassing the same-series 2.4T flagship Qwen3.8-Max in overall performance.
  </details>

- **[Frozen by the Mean: Early Temporal Guidance Unfreezes Motion in Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28762)**  `arXiv:2611.28762`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuanhua Yin, Boyu Wei, Chengkun Yue, Shunqi Mao, Chuanzhi Xu, Wei Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video diffusion models can render convincing frames while failing to produce the requested action. Increasing classifier-free guidance (CFG) can strengthen motion, but also saturates and shifts colors. Scalar CFG assigns the same coefficient to the averaged and frame-varying components of its prediction difference, limiting independent control of temporal variation. THAW, Temporal Harmonic Amplification during Warm-up, retains stock CFG and adds a separately weighted residual during an initial denoising window. Its dose specifies the added norm relative to the current CFG difference, giving the extra guidance a consistent relative budget across sampling steps. THAW is training-free and reuses CFG's two model predictions with an unchanged network evaluation count. It improves cost-matched motion over scalar and scheduled CFG on five video models, with positive point estimates against existing guidance methods on three of them. On VBench, THAW raises dynamic degree by about 14 to 15 points over stock CFG while increasing total scores on a different set of three models.
  </details>

- **[PSC-Gen: Physical Sequence Conditioning for Video Generation via Agentic Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28756)**  `arXiv:2611.28756`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wonsik Shin, Jinju Jang, Junhoo Lee, Yeonjin Chang, Jimyeong Kim, Nojun Kwak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in video diffusion models have enabled the synthesis of high-fidelity, visually compelling videos. However, despite being trained on internet-scale video data, understanding underlying physical laws and generating physically plausible dynamics remain challenging. To mitigate this, conditioning video generators on explicit physical reasoning has emerged as a promising direction. Nevertheless, existing conditioning approaches often fail to incorporate detailed physical guidance into generation, resulting in physically implausible motion or visual artifacts. We propose PSC-Gen, a training-free framework that employs an agentic approach to synthesize visual conditions and inject their core dynamics via low-frequency filtering. By iteratively planning physical states and verifying generated keyframes, our Physics Agent constructs visually and physically consistent keyframe sequences. We then interpolate these keyframes into a continuous physical sequence and inject only its low-frequency components, steering the video model to preserve physical dynamics while synthesizing fine visual details. Qualitative and quantitative evaluations across physics-informed and robotic manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that PSC-Gen faithfully follows instructions and generates physically plausible videos.
  </details>

- **[DynFlux: Learning to Dynamically Balance Consensus and Conflict in Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28716)**  `arXiv:2611.28716`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeheng Wang, Hao Zhang, Yige Wang, Xi Xiao, Hao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal emotion recognition remains challenging due to the intrinsic heterogeneity of different modalities. Effectively leveraging both cross-modal consensus (consistency) and conflict (inconsistency) is essential, as they jointly provide complementary cues that enhance emotion understanding from diverse perspectives. Traditional multimodal methods often suffer from semantic ambiguity and rarely explicitly model the interplay between cross-modal consensus and conflict. Moreover, existing approaches struggle to capture and exploit the dynamic interactions between these two complementary factors. To overcome these limitations, we propose DynFlux, a model guided by a temporal heterogeneous graph that encodes both consensus and conflict relationships across modalities. DynFlux introduces a dedicated graph branch that explicitly captures cross-modal dependencies through sparse temporal graphs, and modulates the conventional multimodal emotion recognition pipeline at three hierarchical stages: adaptive feature modulation during feature extraction, guided aggregation modulation during feature integration, and fusion strategy modulation at the multimodal fusion stage. Through this design, DynFlux dynamically balances cross-modal consensus and conflict, enabling richer and more robust emotion understanding. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets confirm that DynFlux consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, and its generality is further validated on multimodal intent recognition and multimodal sarcasm detection tasks.
  </details>

- **[Domain-Specific Self-Supervised Learning for Cuneiform Sign Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28712)**  `arXiv:2611.28712`  `cs.CV`  
  _Asim Niaz, Morris Alper, Shai Gordin, Enrique Jiménez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing cuneiform signs is challenging: sign shapes vary across historical periods, scribal practices, and preservation conditions, while expert annotations are scarce. We study whether domain-specific self-supervised pretraining yields better cuneiform sign representations than generic natural-image pretraining. We pretrain a masked autoencoder, CuneiMAE, on over 900K unlabeled sign crops and compare it with ImageNet-pretrained MAE, CLIP, and DINOv2 on a 218-class sign recognition benchmark. CuneiMAE learns substantially stronger frozen representations (43.7% linear-probe accuracy vs. 25.2% for ImageNet-MAE), with gains over ImageNet-MAE on cross-period transfer. Under fine-tuning, CuneiMAE outperforms ImageNet-pretrained baselines on our internal benchmark, with the largest gains when labels are scarce (+20.4 pp over ImageNet-MAE with 1% of labels). On an external benchmark, CuneiMAE reaches 69.9% top-1 accuracy after fine-tuning, about 8 pp above the ResNet-18 baseline from prior work. Explicit structural priors, such as edge-guided masking and part bottlenecks, do not improve over standard masked reconstruction. Overall, domain-specific self-supervision shows strong performance on cuneiform recognition tasks, particularly when labels are limited.
  </details>

- **[When Local Semantics Disagree: Learning to Detect Fake News through Weakly Supervised Cross-Modal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28711)**  `arXiv:2611.28711`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeheng Wang, Hao Zhang, Yige Wang, Xi Xiao, Hao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of multimodal social media content has made fake news detection a pressing challenge for the vision-language community. Most existing approaches rely on coarse global similarity between text and images, which overlooks subtle, uneven cross-modal inconsistencies -- such as sentiment-scene mismatches, object-level manipulations, or semantic dissonance between visual and textual cues. Moreover, these models treat all inconsistencies as equally informative, ignoring domain-specific variation in how such conflicts manifest, which limits both generalization and interpretability. To address these challenges, we propose DMIL, a dual-branch multiple instance learning framework that formulates multimodal fake news detection as a weakly supervised learning problem. Each news article is treated as a bag of visual and textual instances processed through two complementary branches: an embedding-space branch that aggregates global semantics via expert-guided modality fusion, and an instance-space branch that models cross-modal inconsistencies across local-to-global granularities. A retrieval-augmented, domain-adaptive router further contextualizes inconsistencies using retrieved domain exemplars and dynamically fuses multi-level cues. Extensive experiments on public misinformation benchmarks demonstrate that DMIL achieves competitive state-of-the-art performance and demonstrates strong discriminative capability against fake news across different domains.
  </details>

- **[CoopOcc: Cooperative 3D Semantic Occupancy Perception via Multi-Agent and Cross-Modal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28710)**  `arXiv:2611.28710`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zengle Zhu, Zhen Li, Tianyi Huai, Xinhu Zheng, Liuqing Yang, Rongqing Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic occupancy perception has emerged as a promising paradigm for jointly modeling 3D scene geometry and semantic information. However, this unified representation cannot compensate for missing information caused by occlusion and sensor coverage. Multi-agent cooperation alleviates this limitation, but information from different agents is not equally useful across spatial regions. Meanwhile, feature representations from each agent form the basis of reliable multi-agent fusion. Cross?modal fusion constructs such representations by integrating complementary semantic and geometric information. Despite this complementarity, jointly establishing reliable 3D correspondence across agents and preserving modality-specific structures remains challenging. To address these limitations, we propose CoopOcc, which performs cooperative 3D semantic occupancy perception through multi-agent and cross-modal fusion. CoopOcc uses ego?query cross-agent attention (EQCA) to selectively aggregate aligned features across agents, and anchor-based bidirectional feature interaction (ABFI) for cross-modal exchange at matched coordinates. We further construct SemanticVVSim, a simulation dataset, and establish a unified benchmark. For simulation-to-real transfer, we propose confidence-aware hierarchical occupancy and relational distillation (CHORD). CoopOcc performs best overall among ten evaluated methods on SemanticVVSim. On Occ3D-nuScenes, CHORD improves performance using knowledge from SemanticVVSim. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Visual Token Retention in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28684)**  `arXiv:2611.28684`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fawaz Sammani, Nikos Deligiannis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive Large Vision-Language Models solve multimodal reasoning tasks by retaining hundreds or thousands of visual tokens, allowing every generated token to attend to the visual tokens at any time during the generation process. However, whether continuous access to these visual tokens is necessary remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate how visual token influence evolves along two dimensions: generation time and transformer depth. Using long-form visual reasoning benchmarks and multiple model scales, we first analyze the attention dynamics across generation time and transformer depth. We identify a temporal and depth-wise structure in the use of visual information. Visual attention peaks during the initial generation steps and then rapidly declines as attention shifts toward previously generated reasoning tokens. Across depth, visual influence is concentrated in intermediate layers and becomes very weak in the final layers. We further verify these observations with causal intervention that removes all visual tokens across generation time and model depth. These interventions confirm that visual-token dependence in multimodal reasoning is neither temporally persistent nor uniformly distributed across transformer depth. Thus, retaining the complete visual context at every layer and decoding step may be unnecessary. Our experiments demonstrate that we can remove up to 59\% of tokens from the full input prompt across generation time and 11\% across all transformer layers, while incurring only a very small performance drop. On video benchmarks, up to 65\% of the input prompt tokens can be removed during generation without affecting performance.
  </details>

- **[Learning Continuous Scene Depth Representations via Cross-Modal Gaussian Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28670)**  `arXiv:2611.28670`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiwei Zhong, Lingyu Zhu, Baoliang Chen, Peilin Chen, Shiqi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depth super-resolution with color guidance is a fundamental problem in geometric scene understanding that aims to reconstruct high-resolution depth using structural cues from color images. In practical RGB-D systems, the resolution gap between depth and color images often varies, requiring flexible upsampling factors. However, many existing methods are designed for predefined integer scales. Although implicit methods support arbitrary-scale reconstruction, they typically rely on computationally demanding per-pixel neural decoding and may struggle to recover sharp depth boundaries and fine structures. To this end, we propose a cross-modal Gaussian rendering framework that learns continuous depth representations for arbitrary-scale depth super-resolution. Specifically, we first construct hierarchical Gaussian representations for progressive depth reconstruction, with explicit spatial support defined by learnable Gaussian primitives. Building on this representation, we design a Structure-Aware Gaussian Aggregation module that combines spatial weights with cross-modal feature affinities to reduce aggregation across depth discontinuities. We then develop a Scale-Adaptive Gaussian Rendering module that adapts Gaussian spatial kernels to the target pixel footprint. To further facilitate cross-modal learning, we propose a training strategy that gradually introduces higher-frequency guidance information during training. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method at both in-distribution and out-of-distribution upsampling scales.
  </details>

- **[Dual-Stream Event-to-Video Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28656)**  `arXiv:2611.28656`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lei Sun, Zhendong Li, Chenxin Shao, Weilun Li, Ruibo Ming, Kaiwei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event cameras capture brightness changes with high temporal resolution and dynamic range, but converting event streams into videos remains fundamentally ill-posed due to the lack of absolute intensity information. Resolving this ambiguity calls for a brightness reference that complements the intensity changes recorded by events. Inspired by the complementary spatial and temporal processing of the parvocellular and magnocellular pathways in human vision, we propose EvDualDiff, a dual-stream framework that combines a brightness reference from temporal-mapping events with motion-event cues for video reconstruction. Conditioned on both streams, our video diffusion model autoregressively reconstructs long sequences from a single temporal map. Photometric constraints and finetuning on self-generated rollouts reduce accumulated drift. At the hardware level, our approach requires only a minimal light-modulation mechanism on the lens of an event camera. We build this setup and collect a dataset comprising temporal-mapping events, motion events and synchronised ground-truth RGB frames. Experiments on a public benchmark and on our collected EvDual dataset demonstrate that EvDualDiff outperforms existing event-to-video reconstruction methods in fidelity, perceptual quality and video-level realism. The code and dataset will be released.
  </details>

- **[OrthoLatentCAD: Orthographic Spatial Latents for Hand-Drawn CAD Program Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28646)**  `arXiv:2611.28646`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaolei Zhou, Xinjie Zhu, Chuangjie Fang, Chenyu Wang, Jianwei Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Freehand orthographic drawings capture early design intent, yet recovering executable sketch--extrude CAD programs from raster front, right, and top views remains challenging: noisy, dimension-free 2D layouts must be reconciled into an ordered construction history. We introduce Hand2CAD, a benchmark of 215K ABC-derived synthetic orthographic-drawing--CAD program pairs and 1,000 real three-view sets drawn by CAD-trained participants. Recovering CAD programs from orthographic drawings requires both cross-view geometric reasoning and alignment between visual features and program representations. We propose OrthoLatentCAD to address these two challenges. It fuses local geometry with global context on canonical planes, exchanges evidence between paired plane lines indexed by the same shared-axis coordinate, and maps the spatial representation to ordered program latents through queries inherited from a frozen CAD autoencoder. Across the evaluated one- to three-view configurations, OrthoLatentCAD outperforms protocol-matched baselines in procedural accuracy, compiled geometry, and CAD-kernel validity while benefiting from additional views. Without real-data fine-tuning, it also outperforms the baselines on held-out real drawings. We will release the dataset, code, and evaluation scripts.
  </details>

- **[CrossPanel: Exposing the Answer-Evidence Gap in Scientific Figure Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28641)**  `arXiv:2611.28641`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengyang Lu, QianhongWen, Chenpeng Wang, Qiang Huang, Ziyang Ling, Huan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A correct answer to a scientific-figure question can conceal an incorrect account of the experiment. Reading evidence across panels requires keeping each observation attached to the object and conditions that give it meaning. We introduce CrossPanel, a benchmark of 2,650 human-reviewed questions that exposes this answer-evidence gap. Its tasks combine parallel observations or follow dependent queries, while a response audit separates object selection, experimental conditions, cross-panel binding, and local readout. Across 15 vision-language models on 251 diagnostic questions, 8.42%-56.64% of correct answers contain explicit factual errors. The audit distinguishes coherent readings of the wrong target from incorrect readings of the right one. Controlled interventions then test how these failures can be repaired: verified object-condition hints, which withhold the requested measurement, raise Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's target-query success by 22.81 percentage points on 57 questions, while additional image tiles alone yield little improvement. Other models benefit most from combining both forms of assistance. These findings identify experimental attribution as a distinct requirement alongside local perception, and make both measurable beyond final-answer accuracy.
  </details>

- **[VUOD: A Versatile Unsupervised Outlier Detection Framework for Natural, Industrial, Medical Images and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28608)**  `arXiv:2611.28608`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhonghang Liu, Siyuan Chen, Jingwen Yu, Changshuo Wang, Kunyang Li, Jiangbo Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unsupervised outlier detection that automatically identifies whether visual systems involve anomalous images is a significant research topic. However, most approaches are limited to natural images because they rely on frozen and pre-trained feature extractors. So that their performance cannot be maintained in practical scenarios, especially for industrial inspection and medical imaging. In this work, we first revisit this task and then introduce a versatile unsupervised outlier detection framework to enrich the application domains. The core idea of this framework is to improve feature discriminativess via exploiting intrinsic distribution priors. Evaluated on 3 domains and 14 benchmark datasets, our proposed solution achieves state-of-the-art performance and significantly outperforms existing methods. More importantly, we show its plug-and-play property that can be integrated into diverse visual applications to improve their robustness, such as image classification and 3D reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[WorldWeave: Rethinking Embodied Navigation through Cross-Task World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28553)**  `arXiv:2611.28553`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuan Yao, Jiahao Zhang, Rongjunchen Zhang, Kaixuan Huang, Junyu Gao, Changsheng Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile embodied agents continuously alter their observations through their own motion, while external dynamics further shape future interactions. Different mobile embodied tasks therefore capture complementary patterns of state evolution, yet jointly learning world dynamics across such interactions remains less explored. We investigate this problem through Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) and Embodied Visual Tracking (EVT), which respectively involve navigation toward a stationary goal and interaction with a moving target. We propose WorldWeave, a cross-task world model that couples shared predictive dynamics with task-specific adaptation. Instead of conditioning state transitions on heterogeneous physical actions, WorldWeave learns latent actions from observed state changes via inverse-forward dynamics, establishing a shared transition representation across tasks. The resulting future-state predictions are adaptively integrated into task-specific representations for downstream decision making. Experiments across multiple VLN and EVT benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over specialized baselines.
  </details>

- **[VICA: Video Contextual Agreement for AIGC Video Detection and Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28485)**  `arXiv:2611.28485`  `cs.CV`  
  _Runheng Lin, Jialiang Shen, Jiyang Zheng, Ruiqi Liu, Zonglin He, Yu Yao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid advancement of AI-generated content (AIGC) video generation and editing technologies has raised growing societal concerns about the authenticity and reliability of digital videos. Existing video detection methods primarily exploit temporal inconsistencies through holistic frame-level representations, whereas manipulation localization methods focus on artifacts within individual spatial regions. However, holistic representations may obscure localized artifacts through global feature aggregation, while region-centric approaches may overlook disagreements between individual regions and their surrounding spatiotemporal context. To address these limitations, we propose VIdeo Contextual Agreement (VICA), a unified framework that masks individual video regions and leverages a frozen V-JEPA model to predict their representations from the surrounding spatiotemporal context. Complementary masking ensures complete regional coverage, while context-conditioned predictions establish references for assessing regional agreement with the broader video context. By comparing predicted and observed representations, VICA derives spatial residuals that characterize intra-frame discrepancies and bidirectional temporal residuals that capture inconsistencies across preceding and subsequent frames. These residuals characterize contextual disagreements, providing a shared forensic representation for both tasks and shifting the focus from identifying isolated manipulation artifacts to assessing regional agreement with expectations inferred from the broader video context. Extensive experiments demonstrate that VICA achieves state-of-the-art performance on both tasks across multiple benchmarks, with strong generalization to unseen video generators and editing methods.
  </details>

- **[CAMA: Calibrated Anti-Collapse Marginal Alignment for Test-Time Adaptation of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28461)**  `arXiv:2611.28461`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinho Kang, Yunhui Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time adaptation (TTA) updates a model using unlabeled test data to help it handle distribution shifts. However, prior work has found that image corruption causes a vision-language model (VLM) to produce more similar features for different images, a phenomenon known as feature compression. Under compression, entropy minimization, the most common TTA objective, can cause the model to favor a few classes and perform worse than before adaptation. Despite these observations, a detailed analysis of how these failures are connected and an effective strategy for preventing them are still lacking.In this paper, we first analyze why entropy minimization fails for VLMs. We then propose CAMA, a new test-time adaptation objective that maximizes batch mutual information to encourage confident predictions for individual images while maintaining diverse predictions across the batch. CAMA also estimates a reference class distribution early in the test stream and aligns each batch's average prediction with this reference during adaptation. We prove that feature compression biases the predictions and that entropy minimization amplifies this bias into collapse, whereas CAMA is guaranteed to preserve every class. Across standard corruption benchmarks, we observe collapse on nearly every VLM backbone evaluated. CAMA prevents collapse in every observed case and, in turn, achieves the best accuracy on every backbone. Quantitative and qualitative analyses confirm that CAMA reverses the feature compression behind the collapse, and ablations show that each of its components contributes.
  </details>

- **[Plan Big, Paint Small: Semantic-Aware Computation Allocation for Efficient Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28402)**  `arXiv:2611.28402`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yixuan Luo, Yanjing Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion streams a video chunk by chunk, but large AR models are too slow for real-time use. We ask where in an AR rollout a large model is actually needed. Pairing Krea-Realtime-14B with Self-Forcing-1.3B, which share their latent space and denoising schedule, we find that the order of the two models matters: when the 14B model generates only the first chunk and the 1.3B model continues, the rollout keeps 95\% of the 14B model's semantic advantage, whereas the reverse allocation keeps 16\% at about ten times the cost. The large model decides what the video shows; the small model only has to carry it forward. We turn this into BigSmall, a training-free planner--renderer sampler. The 14B model plans the opening chunk and sketches the chunks in which content changes, which preserves motion, and the 1.3B model renders the rest; a faster variant, BigSmall, plans only the opening chunk. A cross-model KV cache that is approximate, windowed and lazy lets the planner leave and re-enter the rollout at low cost. On VBench, BigSmall is $3.02\times$ faster than the 14B model at an unchanged semantic score and motion, and $1.55\times$ faster than SDVG, which lets the small model draft first; BigSmall reaches $3.77\times$ at the same semantic score, trading away 10\% of the motion. On minute-long videos (LoCoT2V-Bench), where the 14B model degrades when it reads its own long history, BigSmall is $3.14\times$ faster than the 14B model and scores higher ($+1.78$).`
  </details>

- **[TOGO: Take One Image Editor, Get One Video Editor for Free](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28397)**  `arXiv:2611.28397`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yixuan Luo, Jose Caballero, Yanjing Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-based image editors are trained on millions of edit pairs and follow instructions far more reliably than current video editors. We show that this ability can be transferred to video editing without any training. Our key observation is that a growing number of image editors and video generators share the same latent space. Their velocity fields can therefore be combined at sampling time: the difference between the editor's velocity under the instruction and under a neutral prompt, which we call the \emph{edit velocity}, can be added to the sampler of a video model. Added naively, however, it blurs the video, alters the background and flickers. An analysis of the edit velocity explains why. It is small and reliable only at coarse scales and high noise levels, not concentrated on the edited object, and uncorrelated across frames. From these properties, we derive a simple guidance operator that amplifies the edit velocity and restricts it in noise level, space, and time. The video model then turns the incoherent per-frame signal into a temporally coherent edit. Our method, TOGO, combines Qwen-Image-Edit with the Wan2.1 video model without training or fine-tuning. On FiVE-Bench, it raises the edit success rate from 47.0\% (the same video model without the editor) to 65.6\%, above the best published method (59.2\%). At the same time, it maintains the background PSNR and obtains the best LPIPS and SSIM of all methods. Because the video editor is assembled at inference time, it can be upgraded to any newer image editor that shares the latent space with no cost.
  </details>

- **[Task-Agnostic Incremental Vision-Language Object Detection via Prompt Augmentation and Distribution-Aware Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28291)**  `arXiv:2611.28291`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yonghan Jiang, Zhengyuan Xie, Wenchu Liu, Linlan Huang, Fei Yang, Xialei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Incremental Vision-Language Object Detection (IVLOD) empowers pre-trained open-vocabulary detectors to continuously learn new visual concepts from sequential tasks without forgetting foundational knowledge. However, existing methods predominantly rely on oracle task identities during inference, severely limiting their open-world practicality. In this paper, we formalize Task-Agnostic IVLOD (TA-IVLOD), a more realistic setting where such priors are unavailable, requiring the model to simultaneously recognize classes from all learned tasks. This unconstrained setting exposes severe performance degradation due to cross-task semantic interference and parameter conflicts. To tackle these challenges, we propose TADA, a novel modular framework. Specifically, TADA introduces Stochastic Prompt Augmentation to mitigate semantic interference via training-time noise injection, and Test-Time Distribution-Aware Fusion to dynamically weight class-specific LoRA experts, effectively resolving parameter conflicts. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms baselines in standard IVLOD and yields substantial improvements in the rigorous TA-IVLOD setting on the ODinW-13 benchmark, while effectively preserving zero-shot generalization on the MS COCO dataset. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DeFlowCritic: Dense Latent Reward Alignment for Text-to-Image Flow Matching Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28265)**  `arXiv:2611.28265`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeeshan Khan, Xin Yu, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for aligning text-to-image flow matching models with complex user intent. However, existing methods typically rely on sparse rewards computed only from final decoded images. This delays credit assignment across the denoising trajectory and makes training computationally expensive. Moreover, simply combining multiple rewards does not reliably produce a Pareto trade-off, where gains in compositional accuracy often degrade aesthetic quality. In this work, we propose DeFlowCritic, an efficient online RL framework based on Dense Latent Reward Alignment. Unlike prior work, our method provides dense supervision on intermediate latents, enabling early-stage guidance for the denoising trajectory. To achieve this, we introduce an internal critic that evaluates noisy latents using features extracted directly from the diffusion model, a representation inherently better suited for latent-space reward estimation than standard image encoders. We construct a high-quality reward-modeling dataset with continuous scores for critic training, capturing compositional correctness on high-quality reference images. The resulting dense reward enables efficient online RL training and inference-time scaling. Experiments show that our method achieves a superior balance between prompt faithfulness and visual quality on GenEval2 and T2I-CompBench++ benchmarks. The approach also generalizes across text-to-image flow matching models, including SD3.5-Medium, SD3.5-Large, and FLUX.1-dev.
  </details>

- **[VideoJSON: A Minimal Harness Beats Purpose-Built Memory for Ultra-Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28167)**  `arXiv:2611.28167`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenqi Marshall Guo, Kuan-Hao Huang, Junda Chen, Hao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering questions over days or weeks of video requires searching for scattered clues across long timelines. Existing video agents rely on complex memory graphs and fixed retrieval pipelines, which restrict how the agent can search. We propose VideoJSON, a minimal harness for ultra-long video understanding that keeps the memory simple and lets a coding agent write its own search programs. During indexing, a vision-language model turns each minute of video into a single JSON record containing a dense caption and structured fields such as events, entities, places, and speech transcripts. At query time, the agent writes Python or Bash programs in a sandboxed container to filter, link, and read these records over multiple steps, without task-specific training or raw video access. On four ultra-long video benchmarks, VideoJSON with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash outperforms MAGIC-Video by 7.0, 20.0, and 24.75 points on EgoLifeQA, Ego-R1-Bench, and MM-Lifelong Week, and beats the best prior EgoMemReason score by 12.6 points. Across four policy models of increasing capability, VideoJSON's average score rises by up to 11.58 points over the weakest model, compared with at most 5.50 points for MAGIC-Video. Ablations show that both agentic code search and the structured JSON records drive these gains, while adding hourly summaries, extra tools, or video access changes the average score by less than 1.5 points. In addition, fine-tuning Qwen-3.5-Flash on 64 VideoJSON trajectories from an unrelated 100-hour livestream improves its MM-Lifelong scores by up to 8.25 points. Code will be open-sourced after publication.
  </details>

- **[ReMiC: Representation-Driven Token Pruning for 3D VLMs via Minimax Hierarchical Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28124)**  `arXiv:2611.28124`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bangfeng Yan, Chaoning Zhang, Yuanhao Liu, Pengcheng Zheng, Mengyuan Li, Wei Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D vision-language models (3D VLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning. However, their reliance on multi-view inputs produces long visual token sequences, imposing substantial computational overhead during inference. Existing pruning methods often rely on token salience or feature diversity as proxies for representativeness, without explicitly modeling the representation gap between the original token set and the retained subset, thereby leading to suboptimal representation of the original scene. In this paper, we propose ReMiC, a training-free, representation-driven token pruning framework based on minimax hierarchical clustering. ReMiC treats retained tokens as prototypes for the original observations, formulating pruning as minimizing the maximum representation error under a fixed token budget. Local prototype initialization allocates tokens according to regional representation needs, while global prototype refinement updates the selected prototypes using cross-region information to further reduce the maximum representation error. Extensive experiments conducted on three representative 3D VLMs across five 3D scene understanding benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of our method. Notably, by retaining only 10% of visual tokens, ReMiC preserves 93.2% of the performance of Video-3D LLM and achieves a $2.04\times$ faster inference speed.
  </details>

- **[PhoneQC: Benchmarking Multimodal LLMs for Order-Level Smartphone Trade-in Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28117)**  `arXiv:2611.28117`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihan Deng, Xinpei Lin, Du Tiantian, Lequan Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As vision-language models enter remote smartphone trade-in, sellers already upload six views and a diagnostic screen, which makes it easy to treat the human quote as a step a model can take over. A model may mark a crack or read the screen, yet the quote is a staff form: appearance fill stays near 0.25, and the closed models that score a little higher do so only on the orders they answer, so human appraisal remains necessary. Two obstacles stand in the way. (1) Single-view defect sets and attribute benchmarks do not score an order-level form under a fixed input contract, so a box or a leaked field can be mistaken for a finished quote. (2) The unread half of the form is the staff fields, and a model left to emit boxes or captions does not learn them. We present PhoneQC, a 335-order iPhone-heavy cut that ranks systems by a 0 to 100 form score (Score100) only inside matched protocols (images alone; images plus a detector sidecar), with a declared upper bound that is not ranked as a peer. As a case study on that board, field-only LoRA freezes the backbone, adapts attention projections, and supervises staff fields while leaving the defect list empty; the adapter sees the same images as the zero-shot model. On image-only T1 the 3B model rises from 26.6 to 33.3 at coverage 0.928; without the empty-blocker item the open ranking still favors the adapter, while the remaining gap to a person stays large on this cut.
  </details>

- **[Is It the Agent or the Retry? Separating Selection from Agency in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28116)**  `arXiv:2611.28116`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jian Xu, Delu Zeng, Qibin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic text-to-image systems wrap generators in loops of planning, routing, verification and refinement, and report large gains on compositional benchmarks, almost always relative to a single sample. We ask how much of the gain survives against Single-tool Rejection Retry (\ours): the strongest generator already in the system's pool, an off-the-shelf verifier, and retries that stop at the first accepted image under the system's own budget.
