Auditing four recent systems with official evaluators, we find that on GenEval SRR matches the accuracy of OctoT2I (0.962 vs.\ 0.96), recovers 63 to 90% of the gain of GEMS depending on the verifier, and exceeds GenPilot by 12 points with 2.3 generations per image; the effect persists with generators not tuned to the benchmark and on T2I-CompBench++. A closed-form analysis predicts the accuracy of SRR at every budget and shows that its gain is capped by the verifier's likelihood ratio. The same analysis also exposes genuine agentic gains: on GenEval 2, even the best of five samples chosen by the evaluator stays 15 points below the score GEMS reports (12 at the upper end of its 95% interval). We prove a ceiling on the accuracy that routing across generators can add, bracket it between one and three points on OctoT2I's own pool, and show that what OctoT2I's routing buys is compute (about 2.5less than SRR at equal accuracy), not accuracy. In our offline distillation experiments selected samples teach the generator no more than random ones, which a short information argument explains. We end with a reporting checklist for agentic generation papers.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Foundation Models for Bone Scaffold Pore Detection: A Comparative Analysis of Few-Shot Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28113)**  `arXiv:2611.28113`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tri Tin Vo, Vu Thien An Ngo, Nhi Kieu, Cuong Hung Luu, Giang V. H. Phan, Kien Nguyen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating the mechanical properties of bone scaffolds conventionally requires destructive testing or costly Finite Element Analysis, while deep learning alternatives are constrained by the scarcity of labeled biomaterial imaging data. This study introduces the first expert-annotated, pixel-dense dataset of biopolymer bone-scaffold SEM images, spanning five magnification series, and uses it to benchmark two state-of-the-art pre-trained foundation models under a 12-shot fine-tuning regime. SAM3, an instance segmenter, and YOLOv26, a bounding-box detector, feed the predicted pore geometry to regression heads that estimate Young's modulus and compressive yield strength. Our central finding is that both models fail by pore density rather than magnification: YOLOv26's predicted pore counts saturate and porosity estimation collapses on the most crowded frames, whereas SAM3 over-fragments sparse pore fields and merges adjacent pores in dense ones, producing eight catastrophic failures against three for YOLOv26. Averaged across the 44-image evaluation set, YOLOv26 achieves lower porosity error (19.9% vs. 23.1% MAE), higher precision, and a higher F1 score, whereas SAM3 offers pixel-level instance segmentation that YOLOv26's bounding boxes cannot reproduce. We conclude that model selection should be driven by pore density rather than SEM magnification alone.
  </details>

- **[ROSE-SAM3: Response-Oriented Prompt Selection for Open-Vocabulary Remote Sensing Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28112)**  `arXiv:2611.28112`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yanghui Song, Chengfu Yang, Yongpeng Wen, Zhanxuan Hu, Wei Lu, Qi Ming, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) of remote-sensing imagery, class-name prompts can miss objects with varied appearances, while richer descriptions can activate competing classes. We propose Response-Oriented Prompt Selection for SAM 3 (ROSE-SAM3), a training-free framework that selects descriptions using frozen SAM 3 responses on unlabeled target-domain images. We screen candidate descriptions for coverage beyond the native prompts while protecting reliable native class responses, retaining candidates that pass these checks on at least four of five image folds. An offline directed acyclic graph (DAG) search then evaluates candidate combinations and builds a compact PromptBank for each class. Each bank retains the native prompts and adds selected descriptions, which we call residual prompts. At inference, the bank is fixed, and Semantic-Instance Cooperative Head (SICH) uses instance evidence to refine semantic responses before assigning pixel labels. We also introduce RS-OVSS-C to evaluate robustness under six image corruption families across eight remote-sensing datasets, preserving their class vocabularies and reference labels. ROSE-SAM3 achieves 57.3% mean mIoU across eight clean benchmarks and 54.7% on RS-OVSS-C, achieving state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ORDO: Fine-Grained Motion Understanding in Video LLMs via Order-Dependent Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28097)**  `arXiv:2611.28097`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaye Luan, Chaoning Zhang, Pengcheng Zheng, Dongshen Han, Sheng Zheng, Ping Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video large language models (video LLMs) answer a wide range of questions about real videos, yet they remain weak at fine-grained motion such as whether an object speeds up or slows down. Existing methods train these models on synthetic motion clips, on order pretext tasks such as reordering shuffled clips, or with rewards that compare ordered and perturbed frames. None of them requires that the label be unrecoverable from the frames once their order is discarded, so a model can fit such data from static cues without learning to use frame order. We propose ORDO, a framework that builds this requirement into the training data. We define order-necessary supervision as data in which the unordered set of frames has the same distribution under competing labels, and we realise it for motion attributes with three constructions, time-reversal pairing, matched spatial extent and uniform interval coverage. A procedural curriculum of six motion primitives instantiates these constructions, and low-rank adapters of the language model are trained on it with an answer-level objective and self-distillation replay on real videos. With 4,000 synthetic clips, ORDO raises the mean accuracy over four motion benchmarks by +7.6, +4.9 and +5.1 points on Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3-VL-8B and Molmo2-8B, while the same training with shuffled frames changes it by less than one point. Code and generator will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[When Recognition Meets Discovery: Learning Need Not Be Entangled in One Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28092)**  `arXiv:2611.28092`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenglin Liu, An Tian, Mingxuan Du, Shang Rui Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) aims to recognize known classes while discovering novel categories from unlabeled data containing both old and novel samples. Existing methods typically optimize recognition and discovery within a shared representation space, implicitly assuming that the same representation geometry can serve both objectives. Through controlled spectral analysis, we uncover a striking asymmetry: old-class discrimination is increasingly concentrated in the dominant singular directions, while novel-class structure is better preserved in the complementary residual space. More importantly, strengthening the former can even degrade the latter, revealing that recognition and discovery compete for representation capacity rather than merely for optimization weight. Motivated by this observation, we propose Dual-Space Spectral Routing, which dynamically decomposes the representation via online SVD and routes supervised recognition and unsupervised discovery to complementary discriminative and general subspaces. We further exploit their distinct responses to old and novel samples to construct a dual-space novelty signal, which guides Subspace Relation Aggregation (SRA) for organizing candidate novel categories. Extensive experiments across multiple GCD baselines, backbones, and benchmarks demonstrate a consistently improved recognition-discovery balance without introducing additional inference parameters. Code will be open source.
  </details>

- **[Leakage-Free Is Not Confounder-Free: What a Patient-Level Split Cannot Certify About a Medical Imaging Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27585)**  `arXiv:2611.27585`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ignazio Gallo, Mattia Gatti, Leonardo Sgroi, Darwin Castillo, Yuliana Jiménez-Gaona, Nicola Landro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patient-level splitting is the standard safeguard against subject leakage in medical imaging evaluation, but it cannot remove a confounder that every fold inherits: an acquisition variable aligned with the diagnosis across the whole cohort. We present a five-question audit of a candidate confounder: how well it predicts the diagnosis, whether it can be decoded from the images, whether diagnostic scores depend on it, whether the image adds predictive value beyond it and how much discrimination survives once it is controlled. We apply it to DMR-IR, the public dataset on which most machine-learning results in breast thermography rest. On 236 patients under a strict patient-disjoint protocol, the acquisition date alone predicts malignancy at AUROC 0.980, above every imaging arm we evaluated (best 0.949), because benign and malignant patients were recruited in different years. The acquisition year is decodable from the pixels among patients who share a diagnosis, largely through a global thermal offset outside the breast, yet only one arm, the one pooling every frame of a visit, demonstrably carries it into its diagnostic score. No arm improves on the date on paired folds, and once the date is controlled, discrimination either collapses or survives only on comparisons the benchmark cannot attribute. The dataset contains only eight cross-label same-date pairs, from ten patients, which limits reliable evaluation within recorded acquisition-date groups. A second, independently acquired cohort, whose dominant channel is patient age, reproduces every finding except legibility, from a confounder that is clinically legitimate. Mitigation may suppress such a confounder, but without cross-label acquisition support no evaluation on the benchmark can certify that what remains is pathology.
  </details>

- **[Zing-1.0: A Playable World System Built on Controllable Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27336)**  `arXiv:2611.27336`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenbo Zhang, Guoli Zhang, Xuewen Yu, Xueliang Yang, Jiangqiao Yan, Teng Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Zing-1.0, a Playable World System built on Controllable Rendering. An executable engine resolves player actions under the world's rules and produces proxy video from the updated state. A streaming video model synthesizes the player's view from this proxy, and the runtime delivers it to inform subsequent actions. (1) Principle and system. We formulate Controllable Rendering and define the interfaces among world construction, world execution, observation synthesis, and the interaction runtime. To our knowledge, this is the first implementation-independent specification of a Playable World System. (2) AI-native engine. WorldKit provides structured authoring tools and reusable character, camera, and control components for coding agents to construct executable proxy worlds. It supports extensible interactions and precise geometric motion control while separating executable behavior from final visual appearance. (3) Streaming renderer. We adapt MiniMax-H3 for block-causal autoregressive generation and incremental decoding. Proxy video constrains structure and motion, while text and separate scene and subject references specify appearance. We train the generator on diverse tasks and distill it to generate each block in a few denoising steps. The renderer leads the compared streaming editors on OpenVE (3.90) and LongV2VBench (3.44). On StreamEval, Zing-1.0 scores 57.86 on instruction editing and 54.06 on reference editing, exceeding the published baselines on both tracks. (4) Interaction infrastructure. We build an Interaction Runtime that routes player actions to WorldKit while supporting in-play prompt and reference updates. Cross-block joint execution and sparse attention with compressed K/V reduce inference overhead. With the DiT on a single H200 and encoding and decoding on one H100 each, the serving system achieves a steady-state throughput of 23.82 FPS at $832\times480$.
  </details>

- **[EgoCaptioner: Towards Comprehensive and Fine-Grained Egocentric Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26380)**  `arXiv:2611.26380`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqi Tang, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Tengfei Liu, Rundong Wang, Zongyang Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Egocentric video captioning requires fine-grained detail and accurate timing, yet current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often miss brief actions, confuse their order, or misinterpret the wearer's motion. Existing caption formats also offer limited support for combining detailed narration with precise timing. We present EgoCaptioner, which pairs a video-level overview of the setting, participants, and the wearer's overall activity with timed steps whose narration adapts to varying perceptual demands and marks key moments at specific timestamps. We develop a coarse-to-fine annotation pipeline that establishes event structure before expanding step descriptions, yielding 50,000 training examples from eight egocentric video sources. Complementary rewards assess factual accuracy and coverage, measure step-level temporal alignment, and penalize fragmented or redundant narration, guiding caption refinement through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). To improve the reliability of factual supervision, we supplement caption references with pose-derived motion facts when sensor data are available. Across five egocentric benchmarks, EgoCaptioner achieves leading performance among evaluated open-source models in caption quality, caption-conditioned question answering, and temporal alignment. It also surpasses Gemini 3.1 Pro on PhysCapBench and all evaluated proprietary baselines on Minerva-Ego under the caption-to-QA protocol. Ablations support the complementary roles of factual and temporal rewards and show that coarse-to-fine annotation provides more effective supervision than single-pass annotation.
  </details>

- **[HandWorldBench: A Unified Benchmark for Hand-Centric World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24731)**  `arXiv:2611.24731`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaolu Wang, Jiacheng Xu, Lihong Wang, Shuqing Wang, Jianjie Fang, Ziyou Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in world models are opening new possibilities for fine-grained embodied tasks, with hand-object interaction offering a particularly demanding setting. Modeling these interactions involves capturing how hand actions change objects and how the resulting states persist over time. Yet systematic evaluation tailored to these hand-centric capabilities remains underdeveloped. To address this gap, we introduce HandWorldBench, a unified benchmark that systematically evaluates hand-centric world models across human hands, simulated dexterous hands, and real dexterous hands along six dimensions: visual quality, hand fidelity, appearance consistency, instruction following, physical consistency, and temporal coherence. To construct the benchmark, we develop a data curation pipeline that extracts hand-object interaction clips and screens them for quality, yielding 20,500 high-quality egocentric clips from over 600 hours of footage. We then manually select 500 clips covering seven task families for evaluation. Benchmarking eleven models under a unified protocol reveals that stronger hand-pose control does not consistently translate into coherent object-state evolution and persistence or robust cross-embodiment performance, with several specialized hand-control models underperforming general-purpose text/image-to-video generators on dexterous-hand tasks.
  </details>

- **[SIGNTOK: A Text-Aligned Continuous Pose Tokenizer for Sign Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23944)**  `arXiv:2611.23944`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nabeela Khan, Gaurav Rai, Bowen Wu, Sihan Tan, Benjamin Yen, Takeshi Ashizawa, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-conditioned sign language generation (SLG) depends not only on the generator but also on the space in which signing is generated. Existing approaches generate directly in pose space, in quantized code spaces, or in learned latent spaces trained for reconstruction. We find that these spaces are organized largely by signer identity rather than linguistic content: on CSL-Daily, a test clip's nearest neighbor in raw pose space is the same sentence signed by another person in only 2% of cases. To address this, we propose SIGNTOK, a continuous pose tokenizer whose latent space is shaped by three training-only heads: a kinematics head that predicts motion dynamics, a sentence-level head that aligns signing with the overall text, and a word-level head that aligns short motion windows with content words. Together, these objectives raise the same-sentence cross-signer nearest-neighbor rate to 82%. On three sign language benchmarks, the same generator trained in the SIGNTOK latent instead of pose space raises back-translation BLEU-4 in five of six benchmark-scorer settings at comparable joint accuracy, and word-level alignment gives higher back-translation scores than sentence-level alignment on every benchmark.
  </details>

- **[PhyVGGT: Feed-Forward Articulated Object Reconstruction from Monocular Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22244)**  `arXiv:2611.22244`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Shao, Zhihao Cao, Shuhao Zhai, Anh Nguyen, Hong Liang, Liang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing an articulated object with its 3D movable parts, joint parameters and unseen articulation states from an uncalibrated monocular video is essential for robot manipulation and interactive digital twins. Existing per-scene optimisation methods require minutes to hours and external depth or camera poses, whereas feed-forward 3D foundation models reconstruct dense point maps in one pass without segmenting movable parts or inferring joints. Even on these monocular point maps, regressing or fitting continuous joints directly fails on casual videos, since cross-frame attention blurs moving part boundaries in deep layers, underestimates motion along the viewing ray and leaves continuous axes ill-conditioned under small opening angles. To address these challenges, we propose PhyVGGT, a single-pass framework on a frozen 3D foundation model that anchors moving-part matching and closed-form joint estimation to the undistorted geometry of the stationary base. First, our Part and Motion Decoder fuses intermediate per-frame tokens with global tokens and references part queries to a static-base query to segment 3D parts, align point maps on the static base and predict learned Bingham priors. Second, our Kinematic Inference Layer fits an orthogonal Manhattan frame to static surface normals, selects the joint axis in closed form under a Bingham posterior combining pairwise motion scatter with the learned priors, and solves a linear system for the hinge pivot to drive unseen states. Trained solely on synthetic data, PhyVGGT achieves state-of-the-art monocular performance across five benchmarks, outperforming FAMOS and monocular ArtiPoint, surpassing posed RGB-D ArtiPoint in median error, and running in 6.8 s per 48-frame video against hours of per-scene optimisation in Articulat3D. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CoSphere-SLAM: Multi-Agent Panoramic Gaussian Splatting SLAM with Spherical Loop Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22235)**  `arXiv:2611.22235`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Shao, Zhihao Cao, Shuhao Zhai, Taoyu Wu, Zhuoxiao Li, Anh Nguyen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense collaborative mapping is essential for search and rescue, building inspection, and warehouse automation, where panoramic cameras capture the entire surroundings in a single frame. Unlike pinhole agents that share views only at similar headings, panoramic agents observe overlapping scenes at any 3D orientation, yet searching full rotations also produces mutually consistent false loop closures across distinct symmetric rooms. Moreover, continuous monocular scale drift splits rigidly merged maps at revisited surfaces, while depth noise at short baselines distorts loop translation directions. To address these challenges, we propose CoSphere-SLAM, the first multi-agent Gaussian splatting SLAM framework for panoramic cameras, whose spherical loop closure aligns spherical features over $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ via fast Fourier correlation across arbitrary orientations and rejects symmetric false closures through global $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ consensus. Building on these closures, directional global optimization solves a per-keyframe $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ pose graph with log-scale regularization to correct scale drift while separating optical-flow translation directions from depth-based magnitudes. Finally, omnidirectional Gaussian fusion transforms each Gaussian with its keyframe similarity and renders Gaussians at their maximum ray response to jointly refine poses and maps. Experiments on indoor and cross-session multi-agent panoramic benchmarks demonstrate that CoSphere-SLAM achieves state-of-the-art trajectory accuracy and rendering quality among single-agent and multi-agent methods. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Source-Free Domain Adaptation for Multi-Label Image Classification: A Framework, Baselines, and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21107)**  `arXiv:2611.21107`  `cs.CV`  
  _Salih Numan Büyükbaş, Erchan Aptoula, Mamatha Thota, Petra Bosilj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a model trained on a labelled source domain to an unlabelled target domain without access to the source data. Almost the entire SFDA literature assumes mutually exclusive classes, yet many deployed settings are multi-label. This paper formulates SFDA for multi-label classification and establishes the first benchmark for it: five adaptation tasks over six datasets spanning remote sensing, natural, clipart-style and driving imagery, with four single-label SFDA methods extended to provide the first baselines and a published multi-label method run alongside them, over three seeds. The benchmark shows that extending a single-label method is unreliable rather than merely suboptimal: half of the extended baselines fail to improve on the unadapted source model at all, and on two of the five tasks none of them does. The failures are not incidental. Nearest-centroid pseudo-labelling, softmax-based confidence, the diversity term of information maximization and confidence-weighted prototype construction each rely on mutual exclusivity, and each fails differently without it, so replacing the softmax with per-class sigmoids is a valid but insufficient extension. Multi-label Prototype Alignment with Contrastive Teaching (MPACT) is proposed in response, a mean-teacher framework whose objective, prototype construction and alignment term are each reconsidered for overlapping label sets. It attains the best average on every reported metric and is the only method that improves on the unadapted source model on all five tasks. The analysis further introduces a prototype-agreement diagnostic separating feature-space alignment from self-reinforcing agreement with the classifier, and reports a negative result for confidence-based pseudo-label filtering.
  </details>

- **[AcrossPlace: Do Vision--Language Models Recognize Places Beyond Their Source Views? A Comprehensive Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15314)**  `arXiv:2611.15314`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenyu Li, Shuhan Dong, Ruotong Xu, Qingyu Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-guided place recognition requires recovering geographic identity across visual observations beyond the image from which a description originates. However, aggregate retrieval scores often entangle observation transfer, gallery competition, and linguistic evidence, obscuring the underlying recognition capability. We introduce AcrossPlace, a provenance-aware benchmark establishing a systematic evaluation standard for bidirectional language--vision place recognition. Built upon GSV-Cities, AcrossPlace comprises 56,726 places across 23 cities, 156,800 automatically generated, rule-filtered descriptions, and 113,404 target images. Each description retains its source-image provenance, while retrieval operates on disjoint target observations. Text-to-image (T2I) and image-to-text (I2T) retrieval share a unified place-identity relevance criterion supporting multiple valid matches. Two geographic protocols evaluate unseen places within represented cities (P0) and generalization to held-out cities (P1). Paired observation comparisons, gallery-controlled evaluations, fixed-query scaling, and blinded annotation auditing systematically characterize retrieval performance. Across three training seeds, place-supervised SigLIP2 T2I Recall@1 decreases from 19.69% on source images to 12.31% on independent targets under P0. For fixed P1 queries, expanding the gallery from 500 to 8,425 places reduces Recall@1 from 14.67% to 2.87%. A 240-description audit further reveals discrepancies between automated acceptance and factual evidence. These findings establish AcrossPlace as a reproducible evaluation standard for assessing transferable place identity across visual observations and geographic environments.
  </details>

- **[WordEval: Evaluating Word-Native Operation Fidelity in Document Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10475)**  `arXiv:2611.10475`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinyoung Park, Yupan Huang, Shimin Zhang, Jiayu Ding, Yilin Jia, Yuzhong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Microsoft Word is a central environment for office automation, and the faithful execution of its editing operations is an important capability for document agents. Unlike plain-text editing, editing in Word modifies a structured object model, where the requested effect is realized in object properties. Existing benchmarks, however, evaluate office workflows or desktop automation, treating word-processor documents as one task domain among many and verifying outcomes with keyword matching, per-task property checks, or screenshot comparison. We introduce WordEval, an operation-fidelity benchmark that instead centers evaluation on Word editing operations, testing whether each requested edit is realized as intended changes to Word objects and properties. It contains 642 cases across 15 Word feature areas, ranging from text and lists to charts, shape geometry, and SmartArt. 546 atomic cases cover 231 commands, 96 compositional cases combine multiple edits in a single document, and a Word-native oracle checks task-specific object properties and preservation conditions. Cases are constructed from editing-unit specifications, yielding controlled input documents, reference documents, and aligned oracle checks. Each compositional case is linked to corresponding atomic cases, supporting fine-grained diagnosis of editing failures. We evaluate eight agent systems and find that many struggle on the Atomic track, with performance varying across feature areas. Moreover, every system scores lower on Compositional than on Atomic, with gaps that vary substantially by system. Exact atomic-intent links and recorded failure diagnoses provide complementary views of this variation.
  </details>

- **[SleuthVLM: Do We Really Need Audio-in-Video for Omni Video Understanding?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06083)**  `arXiv:2611.06083`  `cs.CV`  
  _Peiran Wu, Fangfei Gou, Zhaoxi Mu, Changhao Zhang, Jian Liu, Weiqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni models answer questions about videos by jointly processing sampled frames and audio in one turn, while VLMs have become strong agents that can search a video over many turns. We ask how far a model can go without hearing the audio, and present \method, a simple multi-turn harness built on such a VLM that never hears the audio directly: speech is transcribed into text, and the VLM sleuths the video without a predefined workflow. On four omni benchmarks with videos up to 90 minutes, \method achieves the best average accuracy: \textbf{75.0\%}, 2.6 points above Gemini 3.8 Flash, and it tops the Video-MME-v2 leaderboard with \textbf{77.8\%}; with Qwen3.8-27B, it is comparable to open-source omni models on short videos and better on long ones. We draw three insights: searching outperforms passive observation; agentic ability matters as much as direct audio access; and small agentic models are highly competitive. So we provide a simple but effective starting point for omni video understanding.
  </details>

- **[Robust Reconstruction of Power Lines from Incomplete Mobile and Aerial LiDAR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05197)**  `arXiv:2611.05197`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mohamed Gaha, Damos Ayobo Abongo, Philippe Massicotte, Eya Ben Kacem, Wael Jaafar, Rami Langar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate vegetation-encroachment assessment and risk mitigation require electric utilities to accurately locate and characterize distribution-network assets, including poles and power lines, and distinguish among medium-voltage, low-voltage, neutral, and telecommunication lines. Mobile and aerial Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) point clouds provide detailed 3D representations of these assets and their surroundings. However, power-line detection and reconstruction remain challenging because conductor returns are often sparse, incomplete, noisy, or occluded by vegetation. These challenges are especially significant in distribution networks, where lower pole heights and complex line configurations place conductors close to trees. In addition, mobile and aerial LiDAR datasets differ substantially in point density, viewing geometry, spatial coverage, and occlusion patterns. We propose a robust and automated reconstruction method combining scaled density-based clustering, robust polynomial fitting, and iterative fragment merging to recover individual conductors under severe data incompleteness and occlusion. Following semantic classification, the same reconstruction pipeline processes both mobile and aerial LiDAR data without modality-specific code or parameter tuning. The method is evaluated through degradation experiments, benchmarking, parameter sensitivity analysis, and large-scale validation. On the aerial dataset, it achieves 96.87% precision, 96.66% recall, a 96.76% F$_1$-score, and a geometric RMSE of 0.127 m. On the mobile dataset, where conductor-level reference annotations were unavailable, it achieves a point-to-model RMSE of 0.045 m and a reconstruction coverage of 95.27%. Source code, documentation, and evaluation data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BGD-SF PolySegNet: boundary-guided dynamic selective fusion network for robust polyp segmentation in colonoscopy images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02603)**  `arXiv:2611.02603`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nishi Kanta Paul, Shamia Maherin, Morsheda Akter, Moinul Hossain, Md Kishor Morol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective polyp segmentation in colonoscopy images is crucial for early detection of colorectal cancer, a leading cause of cancer-related death worldwide. While current deep learning models perform well on traditional Dice benchmarks, border segmentation remains difficult. Most methods use boundary information only as an additional loss signal during training, which prevents the network from explicitly learning border-guided feature selection or mask refinement. This paper introduces BGD-SF PolySegNet, a boundary-guided encoder-decoder architecture that controls three phases of the segmentation pipeline: dynamic multi-scale context aggregation, selective skip-feature fusion, and final mask refinement. The architecture comprises a pretrained EfficientNet-B4 backbone and three proposed modules: Boundary-Guided Dynamic Cascaded Multi-Scale Context Aggregation (BGD-CMSCA), Boundary-Guided Selective Attention Fusion (BG-SAGF), and a Multi-Level Boundary Guidance Head (MBGH). The model is trained on 3,535 annotated images from Kvasir-SEG, BKAI-IGH NeoPolyp-Small, and PolypGen, and evaluated on four held-out benchmark datasets. On the internal test set, BGD-SF achieves a Dice of 0.887, IoU of 0.839, mask-derived Boundary F1 of 0.531, HD95 of 30.32, and ASSD of 18.10, improving Boundary F1, HD95, and ASSD by 15.7%, 27.5%, and 34.6% over the strongest CNN baselines. External validation on CVC-ClinicDB, CVC-ColonDB, ETIS-Larib, and CVC-300 yielded Dice scores of 0.891, 0.776, 0.863, and 0.885, respectively. The model runs at 35 FPS with 22.51M parameters. Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm statistically significant mask-derived Boundary F1 improvements over six of seven ablation variants (p < 0.001; small-to-moderate effect sizes, r = 0.215-0.434), demonstrating that integrating boundary guidance as an active architectural component measurably advances polyp boundary delineation.
  </details>

- **[What Helps Denoising Can Hurt Memory: Rethinking History in Long Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00937)**  `arXiv:2611.00937`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yubin Jing, Ziyang Zhang, Meng Chen, Yuyao Li, Haofan Wang, Yichen Gong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive video generators produce videos block by block from historical context. They are trained within a bounded horizon, whereas inference can extend far beyond it, causing temporal consistency to gradually decrease. Existing methods focus on eliminating this mismatch, but we instead ask whether the historical context used in training remains suitable for inference. Each block reads historical context in two stages, when denoising generates it and when committing writes it into the KV cache for later blocks. We find that older historical context such as the sink helps denoising but can hurt later blocks when read during committing, even in generators trained to read it there. We also show that as committing reads more preceding blocks, clip-to-clip Subject Consistency rises, plateaus, and then declines. We therefore propose Commit Forcing, a training-free rule that organizes committing into a chain. Each committing pass reads only the preceding block's stored KV, while denoising keeps the base historical context. Commit Forcing improves 60-second VBench Quality and Total on all 6 generators, by up to 1.27 and 1.10 points. Its consistency gains extend to 240-second videos, and it achieves an 82.9% win rate in overall human preference on 120-second LongLive videos.
  </details>

- **[Queryable Gaussian View Index: Retrieval, Localization, and Agentic View Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41373)**  `arXiv:2610.41373`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tianyu Xiong, Rui Li, Jiaqi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given an existing 3D Gaussian scene and calibrated references, we seek camera poses and views that answer text, RGB, or image-and-instruction queries. RGB localization recovers a source camera; a target-view instruction can require a different camera while preserving the referenced scene content. Queryable Gaussian View Index (QGVI) supports these requests through a semantic view index that binds structured captions and observations to calibrated camera poses. QGVI-T retrieves indexed candidates with BM25. QGVI-A uses this interface and geometric source localization (QGVI-I) as tools, sharing candidate poses, observations, and query conditions marked satisfied, violated, or unknown. Unmet conditions guide camera proposals and rendered checks. Supported answers exit early; unresolved queries return insufficient evidence within a shared search budget. We construct a nine-scene benchmark, including city-scale environments, covering semantic view retrieval, RGB localization with out-of-map rejection, and image-conditioned target-view search. Under GPT assessment on the development cohort, QGVI-A returns confirmed correct views for 90.4\% of target-view queries, compared with 26.8\% for one-shot Retrieve + Select. This comparison evaluates complete policies with different information and call budgets; QGVI-A achieves higher coverage at lower answer precision. The benchmark separates semantic satisfaction, source-camera accuracy, and acceptance decisions.
  </details>

- **[Accuracy Is Not Enough: An Efficiency- and Statistics-Aware Benchmark of ResNet50 and Vision Transformer for Explainable Multi-Class Gastrointestinal Diseases Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33965)**  `arXiv:2610.33965`  `cs.CV`  
  _Akwasi Asare_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated classification of gastrointestinal (GI) findings in endoscopic images could support early diagnosis, but architectures are often chosen on accuracy alone. We present a controlled, reproducible benchmark of ResNet50 and ViT-B/16 for eight-class GI classification on the public Kvasir dataset (4,000 images), using a stratified split (400 test images) and stratified group 5-fold cross-validation over all images. In cross-validation, ResNet50 and ViT-B/16 reached 92.12% and 91.70% accuracy, and a two one-sided tests (TOST) procedure showed equivalence within a +/-3 percentage-point margin (difference +0.43 points, 90% CI -0.33 to +1.18; p < 0.001). The architectures differed mainly in cost: ResNet50 had 3.6 times fewer parameters and 4.3 times fewer operations, 2.5-fold lower single-image CPU latency (69 vs. 177 ms) and 3.8-fold higher GPU throughput. Cross-validated calibration was similar (expected calibration error 0.021 vs. 0.023). An edge-oriented transformer, MobileViT-S, was most accurate on the test split (95.5%) but lost 9.4 points when an acquisition artefact present in some classes (the ScopeGuide inset) was occluded, whereas ResNet50 was not significantly affected. On 500 unseen polyp images with expert masks, both models' Grad-CAM maps focused on the lesion (3.6 times chance). On a de-duplicated external subset of GastroVision (1,998 images), accuracy fell to 78-80% for all three models and differences between them remained small. Accuracy differences between architectures were thus small relative to the effects of shortcut learning and domain shift, whereas efficiency differences were large. An efficient CNN is a defensible default for resource-constrained screening, but no model is deployment-ready without local validation.
  </details>

- **[WildAIGC-Bench: Can AI Image Detectors Handle the Wild?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28087)**  `arXiv:2610.28087`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zijian Yu, Hangwei Zhang, Shunxing Chen, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An AI-generated scene or a subtle edit to a photograph can make a false claim about a public event appear credible. Existing benchmarks test detectors across generators and platforms, but offer limited insight into how well they recognize fabrications that circulate as evidence for such claims. We introduce WildAIGC-Bench, an event-driven benchmark designed for broad coverage, timely updates, and human-verified labels. A recurring search across news outlets, fact-checking sites, and social posts identifies emerging cases, and reviewers verify the evidence for each image. The benchmark contains 600 AI-generated or edited images and 638 genuine photographs of related subjects. It preserves posts alongside crops of the depicted images and links documented edits to their original photographs where available. All ten visual detectors achieve lower balanced accuracy on WildAIGC-Bench than their respective averages across eight external benchmarks with both classes. On 37\% of the AI images, at least three quarters of the evaluated detectors predict real. Cropping improves recall for most visual detectors but reduces it for most general-purpose multimodal models. Comparisons with editing originals further reveal local alterations that most detectors miss even when they correctly accept the original photograph.
  </details>

- **[Learning Representations to Guide Pixel Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12978)**  `arXiv:2610.12978`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihao Pan, Mengting Chen, Cheng Shi, Taihang Hu, Zhengrong Yue, Zikang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual representations are largely developed for understanding, yet what makes them useful for generation remains unclear. We study this question in pixel diffusion transformers and uncover two key insights. First, better representation quality does not necessarily translate into greater generative utility, as externally aligned semantics can be progressively lost during pixel synthesis. Second, architectural separation with specialized pixel decoders mainly redistributes semantic and spatial modeling, without necessarily yielding representations better suited to generation. These insights suggest that a useful generative representation must be both properly formed and actively exploited. Motivated by these insights, we introduce Representation Guidance (RG), which directly forms and exploits internal representations while retaining a plain Transformer architecture. A low-dimensional bottleneck forces generation-relevant information into a compact representation, which is then explicitly used to guide decoder generation by modifying the flow-matching target according to its induced prediction offset. RG learns clearer semantic and spatial structure while scaling efficiently in both parameters and computation. On ImageNet 256x256, RG-L/16 with only 459M parameters outperforms substantially larger pixel diffusion transformers, while RG-XL/16 achieves an FID of 1.55. RG further reaches an FID of 1.74 at 512x512 and extends to text-to-image generation, achieving 0.89 on GenEval and 87.1 on DPG-Bench.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Through the Glare: A Multi-Source Benchmark and Ocular-Adaptive Pixel MeanFlow for Eyeglass Reflection Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10703)**  `arXiv:2610.10703`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tao Liu, Youwei Pang, Kailai Zhou, Jiaming Zuo, Hanqi Liu, Wei Ji, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Eyeglass reflection removal is important across smartphone imaging, video conferencing, and other face-centric visual applications. The task is challenging because reflections range from mild photometric contamination to severe ocular occlusion, requiring selective correction and plausible reconstruction without altering identity or natural appearance. Existing datasets cover limited reflection conditions, constraining generalization to complex real-world scenes and systematic evaluation. We introduce OcuBench, a multi-source benchmark comprising 10,280 controllable synthetic pairs, 732 real-input pseudo-pairs, and 458 independent real-world test images, supporting both paired evaluation and assessment beyond generated supervision. We further propose OcuFlow, an ocular-adaptive pixel MeanFlow (pMF) framework for efficient, detail-preserving restoration. It combines geometry-adaptive representation with one-step pMF to focus reconstruction on reflection-obscured ocular regions, together with native-resolution frequency-preserving synthesis to retain reliable observed details. Experiments across diverse reflection conditions demonstrate that OcuFlow achieves consistent advantages in reflection removal quality, ocular fidelity, and efficiency. In a blind user study, it receives $67.32\%$ of selections, $6.2\times$ the next-best share. The code and dataset will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Argos: Adapt Rich Geometric Priors for Generalizable Online Scene-Change-Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10181)**  `arXiv:2610.10181`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruihan Xu, Jiae Yoon, Kaichen Zhou, Ue-Hwan Kim, Luca Carlone_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots operating in dynamic environments require reliable detection of how their surroundings change over time. Existing learning-based methods largely rely on pairwise 2D image features, which struggle under large viewpoint changes and occlusions, are sensitive to noise, and show limited generalization across domains, while explicit 3D approaches typically require costly offline optimization. We show that the implicit 3D knowledge of Geometric Foundation Models (GFMs) provides a strong basis for addressing these limitations. We introduce Argos, which adapts GFM features for joint scene change detection and 3D reconstruction. To address data scarcity and take a step toward a foundation model for scene change detection, we introduce a large-scale benchmark comprising two synthetic datasets and one real-world dataset, and train jointly across diverse datasets to improve cross-domain generalization. We further introduce Argos-SLAM, a real-time system designed for robotics, which performs online change detection and change-aware 4D mapping. Across benchmarks, our framework substantially outperforms existing baselines, with gains of up to 42.01% in change IoU and 27.91% in F1, while supporting scalable deployment in changing real-world environments.
  </details>

- **[Long Time No See: Benchmarking VLMs for Out-of-Sight Spatiotemporal Reasoning in Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34630)**  `arXiv:2609.34630`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fangzhou Ma, Ivo Alexander Ban, Eren Homburg, Gabriele Goletto, Rémi Pautrat, Mahdi Rad, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world AI systems must reason about objects that are no longer visible: an AR assistant guiding a user back to an object used earlier, a household robot retrieving an item someone put away. This requires not just recalling where an object was last seen, but updating its state when it is moved and retaining that update once it leaves view. We refer to this as out-of-sight spatiotemporal reasoning. We introduce Beyond3D, the first VQA benchmark to isolate this ability in dynamic egocentric video: every query targets an object that has been relocated and has since left the field of view. We create our questions from HD-EPIC annotations, building a visibility track for each dynamic object from its 3D position, the camera pose, and the scene geometry to understand at each moment whether it is visible, occluded, or out of view. Beyond3D comprises 9,000 questions in eight types over 135 videos from nine participants, organized as one reasoning chain: visual grounding (is the target observable now), temporal grounding (when it was last visible and last placed), scene localization (which fixture anchors that location), and 3D spatial perception (where it lies relative to the current viewpoint or another object in the scene). We benchmark nine general-purpose and spatially specialized VLMs. The best model reaches 42.2% against 29.7% chance and text-only baselines reaching 31.9%, with the largest failures in recovering when an object was last visible, showing that tracking object movement out of sight remains far from solved for current VLMs.
  </details>

- **[DecFlowEdit: Self-Localized Flow-based Image Editing via Guidance Decoupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34237)**  `arXiv:2609.34237`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zheyuan Zhan, Can Wang, Jiawei Chen, Chun Chen, Siwei Lyu, Zeyu Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based image editing (FlowEdit) enables inversion-free semantic changes through the difference between source and target velocities. In this paper, we observe that FlowEdit's default classifier-free guidance (CFG) configuration, with asymmetric source and target scales, causes substantial background leakage. Matching these guidance scales, for example by removing CFG, improves edit-relevant localization but severely degrades editability. To get the best of both worlds, we propose DecFlowEdit, which decouples the optimal guidance scales for localization and for editing in flow-based generative models. In particular, DecFlowEdit first extracts an edit-relevant prior by temporally aggregating velocity differences evaluated without CFG, and then uses this prior to reweight the original updates under default CFG. Our method remains training-free and inversion-free, requiring neither external spatial masks nor attention manipulation. Experiments on PIE-Bench across FLUX.1-dev, SD3, and SD3.5 show that DecFlowEdit improves background preservation, reducing structure distance by approximately 61-73% and background LPIPS by 68-80% relative to FlowEdit at comparable editing fidelity.
  </details>

- **[When Do Frozen VLMs Respond to Image-Free Object-Token Edits?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03429)**  `arXiv:2609.03429`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wonbin Son, Gyumun Choi, Junil Seo, Seungmin Rho, Mi Young Lee, Hyungjoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering what-if queries about a scene with a vision-language model (VLM) usually involves stating the assumption in text or editing the scene with a generative model. We instead move the edit to the representation level, before the model input. The image is represented as a set of object tokens, and the model receives edited tokens without accompanying patch tokens from the original image. This design rests on an open question: when do frozen VLMs actually respond to image-free object-token edits? We study this question in a simple testbed, keeping the visual encoder and language backbone frozen and training only a projector. Our analysis reveals three findings. Responsiveness depends on training: unlike standard VQA training, explicit edit training produces responsiveness in dense scenes and strengthens it in sparse scenes, across deletion, movement, and addition. Token construction and context token count shape responsiveness: the observed differences between token constructions appear to reflect more than token count alone, and responsiveness tends to weaken as surrounding context grows. Standard VQA performance and edit responsiveness are distinct: the object-token route preserves 92-96% of a matched patch-token baseline's free-form VQA accuracy, and responses depend on the input tokens. The observed trends in responsiveness, token construction, and VQA retain their direction across two remote-sensing datasets (iSAID, VRSBench) and three frozen language backbones. We release the evaluation-question generator, measurement records, scoring logs, and code.
  </details>

- **[Unleashing the Potential of Vision-Language Models for Generalizable AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04935)**  `arXiv:2608.04935`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weihan Cai, Hao Tan, Zichang Tan, Jun Wan, Xinping Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A linear classifier trained on frozen DINOv3 features can outperform specialized detectors of AI-generated images in the wild. This success has motivated efforts to further adapt DINOv3 for detection, but we find that the same evaluation protocol underestimates the potential of a vision-language model. When linear probes are trained on images from the same generator, Perception Encoder (PE) performs worse than DINOv3 on in-the-wild benchmarks. Yet real and generated images form more distinct local groups in PE's frozen feature space. Image-text pretraining gives PE a source of supervision directly relevant to this distinction: captions can identify an image as AI-generated, allowing PE to learn high-level semantics about image provenance. Conventional linear probing uses only the image encoder. We introduce Semantic Prototype Calibration (SPC), which instead initializes the linear head of the frozen image encoder with text embeddings that describe real and AI-generated images. Across nine in-the-wild subsets, SPC raises mean accuracy from 84.8\% to 92.9\% for PE and from 81.4\% to 98.5\% for MoEViE. Both models also improve on controlled benchmarks. MoEViE-SPC outperforms specialized detectors with more elaborate task-specific designs. These results establish SPC as a simple and effective baseline for generalizable AI-generated image detection.
  </details>

- **[PhysAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Reliable Remote Heart Rate Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00066)**  `arXiv:2608.00066`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yehui Yang, Bo Zhao, Junzhe Cao, Hui Ma, Yue Sun, Wenjin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables non-contact heart-rate estimation from facial videos, but its weak physiological signal is easily corrupted by motion, illumination changes, occlusion, skin-appearance variation, and device noise. Existing rPPG methods typically rely on a single model to directly predict heart rate or recover pulse waveforms, while different strong estimators may produce conflicting yet individually plausible candidates for the same video. To resolve these conflicts, we propose PhysAgent, an inference-time multi-agent candidate-verification framework. Unlike direct prediction approaches, PhysAgent neither trains a new base rPPG model nor asks Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to output heart rate directly. In contrast, it treats outputs from multiple base estimators as physiological hypotheses to be verified and uses a lightweight 4B MLLM, Qwen3-VL-4B, to drive multi-agent reasoning over video conditions, signal reliability, and candidate disagreement. A deterministic physiological verifier checks the fusion proposal, and a reproducible numerical fusion process produces the final heart rate. Experimental results on multiple public rPPG benchmarks show that PhysAgent improves fusion stability and reliability across different datasets and source-domain settings, while avoiding the irreproducibility and physiological inconsistency of direct MLLM prediction or unconstrained ensemble fusion. The code will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[The generator is the tracker: Multi-object tracking by painting persistent identity colours](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17120)**  `arXiv:2607.17120`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haiyu Yang, Miel Hostens_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-object tracking (MOT) is usually split into detection and association, with identity kept as external state: track buffers, motion models, appearance embeddings. We ask whether a video generator can keep that state in pixels. We fine-tune a 22B text-to-video diffusion model (LTX-2.3) with an in-context LoRA to translate an RGB clip into an ID-map clip, where every person is painted a flat, distinct colour that persists over time. Long videos are generated as chained windows, each conditioned on the cleaned tail of the previous one; a brief continuation fine-tune teaches the model to extend a given colouring, after which identity flows through the chain with no separately trained detector, motion model or association module. On the DanceTrack test server the system reaches 40.3 HOTA, to our knowledge the first entry read off a pretrained video generator. This is well below today's specialists (66-76 HOTA), but its association (AssA 44.1) exceeds every tracker of the 2020-22 benchmark suite while detection is the deficit. A 2x2 factorial on validation shows where identity comes from: neither chaining nor continuation training helps alone (16.8 and 15.9 HOTA against 18.2 for independent windows linked post hoc), but together they reach 34.8; re-linking the chain's own windows changes nothing (34.7), and motion- and appearance-based associators on the same boxes reach only 22-24 AssA against 38.3 for the colours, an ordering that holds in three sampling seeds and on SportsMOT. On 383 mined occlusion and exit events the chain re-acquires 42% of the identities it held on both sides of the gap, including gaps longer than its 49-frame context; the pre-gap colour is painted during the gap in every recovery but also in 110 of 119 failures, so carry-over is necessary but not sufficient. We offer the system as a proof of concept, not a competitive tracker. We release code and checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Single Expert: Harmonizing Diverse Visual Priors in MLLMs for Spatial Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15054)**  `arXiv:2607.15054`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Lin, Xiaohu Huang, Kai Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated substantial promise in spatial understanding. Existing works typically incorporate prior knowledge extracted from a pre-trained foundation model to further enhance the spatial awareness of MLLMs. In this paper, we first reveal that when integrating diverse foundation models into MLLMs, different models provide complementary spatial priors that benefit different tasks. Motivated by this, we propose $\textbf{ViPS}$, a novel multi-model prior framework designed to fully unleash the potential of incorporating multiple $\textbf{Vi}$sual $\textbf{P}$riors from diverse models into MLLMs for $\textbf{S}$patial understanding. Specifically, ViPS introduces an Efficient Prior Proxy to generate multiple foundational priors with minimal inference overhead, and a Dynamic Prior Fusion mechanism to achieve harmonious and context-aware prior fusion and injection from the prior proxies. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ViPS successfully harmonizes diverse visual priors, establishing new state-of-the-art performance across multiple complex spatial reasoning and 3D spatial understanding benchmarks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[T-VSS: Test-Time Visual Subspace Steering for Adversarial Robustness of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23132)**  `arXiv:2606.23132`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jaehyuk Jang, Minseok Seo, Seungju Cho, Kangwook Ko, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive vision-language models (VLMs), exemplified by CLIP, enable strong zero-shot visual recognition but remain highly vulnerable to adversarial perturbations. Recent test-time adaptation methods improve robustness without retraining. Prompt-based methods adapt learnable text prompts, while input-space methods optimize pixels or padding at test time. These approaches can improve predictions, but they do so through an indirect and expensive optimization path. We propose \textbf{Test-time Visual Subspace Steering (T-VSS)}, a lightweight defense that performs test-time adaptation directly in the visual feature space. T-VSS first builds a sample-specific low-rank subspace from multi-view feature residuals anchored at the attacked image. It then learns a shared feature correction within this subspace using reliability-weighted entropy minimization. By constraining adaptation to a compact visual geometry, T-VSS steers attacked features toward more stable and discriminative predictions while avoiding noisy full-space updates. Experiments on fine-grained, ImageNet, and ImageNet-OOD benchmarks show that T-VSS improves adversarial robustness while maintaining competitive clean accuracy and better efficiency than prior test-time adaptation methods. Code is available at \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[From Context to Canvas: Benchmark Dataset and Verify-then-Route Framework for Multi-Subject Reference-Based Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15867)**  `arXiv:2606.15867`  `cs.CV`  
  _Long-Bao Nguyen, Quang-Khai Le, Tam V. Nguyen, Minh-Triet Tran, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized image generation should compose several people, each with the right objects and outfits, in a specified scene, yet current generators collapse as references are added and existing benchmarks do not capture this degradation. We introduce CogCanvas, a benchmark of 1,361 compositional prompts with 2--5 subjects, 1,952 curated references (100 people, 115 objects and fashion items, 29 scenes), and graphs specifying interactions and spatial relations. It adds two metrics: BG-Sim for background fidelity outside foreground regions, and Attr-VQA for person--item binding, with 76.5% agreement with human majority judgments. We also propose Context2Canvas, a training-free multi-agent framework built on a verify-then-route principle: agents verify a layout against a hierarchical scene graph, and its masks then route each reference to its own region in a two-phase renderer that grounds references independently before refining them jointly. Context2Canvas achieves 2.5x the attribute-binding score of the strongest published baseline, with larger gains as the subject count increases. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ChartArena: A Unified Benchmark with Atomic-Primitive Reasoning for Chart Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01348)**  `arXiv:2606.01348`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shangpin Peng, Gengluo Li, Xingyu Wan, Chengquan Zhang, Hao Feng, Binghong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Charts convey dense quantitative and relational information, yet current general chart parsing remains challenging due to fragmented evaluation and limited structural reasoning. Existing benchmarks focus on narrow sub-tasks with inconsistent output formats (e.g., Markdown, CSV, JSON, SVG, code), hindering fair comparison and often overlooking real-world scenarios such as printed or hand-drawn photos. Meanwhile, end-to-end multimodal models tend to learn shallow pixel-to-text mappings without explicit structure understanding, making them fragile under visual variations. To address these issues, we present a unified framework that connects evaluation and modeling. On the evaluation side, we introduce ChartArena, a comprehensive bilingual benchmark covering eight chart families, including both numeric charts (bar, line, pie, radar, box plot, combination) and diagrammatic structures (flowchart, mind map). The dataset is constructed via a human-agent collaborative annotation pipeline, where model-assisted drafts are refined through multi-stage human verification to ensure structural consistency and annotation reliability. Furthermore, we design a format-agnostic evaluation protocol that maps different outputs into two canonical semantic spaces: a normalized triple view and a directed graph view, and evaluates them with structure-aware metrics. On the modeling side, we propose Chart Atomic Primitives (CAP), a lightweight semantic scaffold that introduces structural inductive bias during supervised fine-tuning. By guiding the model to infer structure before generating outputs, CAP leads to more consistent and robust parsing behavior. Extensive experiments show that models trained with CAP mechanism achieve reliable performance on ChartArena. ChartArena is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PARE: Pruning and Adaptive Routing for Efficient Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27336)**  `arXiv:2605.27336`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yutong Wang, Yunke Wang, Tianfan Xue, Yu Qiao, Yaohui Wang, Xinyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) generate high-quality videos but demand substantial compute due to wide blocks, deep architectures, and iterative sampling. Recent methods reduce cost by compressing width, depth, or sampling steps, but typically commit to a fixed architecture that cannot adapt to individual inputs or denoising stages. We propose PARE (Pruning and Adaptive Routing for Efficient video generation), which jointly compresses width and depth with structure-aware pruning and input-adaptive routing. For width, we observe that attention heads specialize into spatial and temporal roles, and design importance scoring that accounts for this distinction to prevent motion-critical temporal heads from being pruned prematurely. For depth, we train a lightweight router conditioned on denoising timestep and visual content to dynamically select which blocks to execute at each step, enabling per-input compute adaptation rather than static block removal. A progressive pipeline first recovers width-pruned quality via distillation, then jointly optimizes the student and router to decouple the two learning objectives. Experiments on Wan2.1-14B for both image-to-video and text-to-video generation show that PARE substantially reduces per-step computation while preserving quality across VBench dimensions, and composes with step distillation for further acceleration.
  </details>

- **[LaMo: Self-Supervised Latent Motion Priors for Physical Realism in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23878)**  `arXiv:2605.23878`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bo Jiang, Depu Meng, Yihan Hu, Yichen Xie, Tianshuo Xu, Wei Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern video generators produce visually compelling clips but still struggle with physical and motion consistency, limiting their use as reliable world simulators. Existing remedies often rely on external simulators, teacher models, or curated physics-focused data. We explore a complementary self-supervised direction: extracting motion cues from the unlabeled videos already used to train video diffusion models. We propose LaMo, which formulates a latent motion prior over frame-to-frame latent changes conditioned on the current latent and prompt. This prior is exposed through two lightweight readouts: a macro motion drift used during training as a Motion Drift Loss, and a learned micro motion field used during sampling as Motion Prior Guidance. Both components are plug-and-play with existing video diffusion backbones, requiring no architectural or I/O changes. On VideoPhy, VideoPhy2, and PisaBench, LaMo improves CogVideoX backbones and outperforms recent physics-aware baselines that use external supervision. On VBench, it preserves overall generation quality while improving motion-related dimensions. These results suggest that unlabeled video contains useful motion supervision for improving physical fidelity in modern video diffusion models.
  </details>

- **[Common Principal Component Networks for Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05136)**  `arXiv:2605.05136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu-Hsi Chen, Abd-Krim Seghouane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain Generalization (DG) aims to learn representations that generalize to unseen target domains under out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts. Despite recent advances in invariant representation learning, explicitly capturing shared structures across source-domain distributions remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we integrate Common Principal Component Analysis (CPCA) into deep learning to identify a common orthogonal basis across multiple domains, proposing two complementary end-to-end differentiable frameworks. First, we introduce Common Principal Component Network (CPCNet), which unrolls the iterative Flury-Gautschi (FG) algorithm into fully differentiable neural layers, enabling covariance-based joint diagonalization within end-to-end representation learning. Second, to address rank deficiency arising from mini-batch covariance estimation in small-sample regimes, we develop Projection Pursuit-based Common Principal Component Network (PP-CPCNet), which directly optimizes a common orthogonal basis without explicit covariance estimation. Experiments validate the joint diagonalization capabilities of both methods, while evaluations on four standard DG benchmarks demonstrate competitive zero-shot generalization performance with favorable computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[NucEval: Revisiting and Extending Evaluation Metrics for Nuclear Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03144)**  `arXiv:2605.03144`  `cs.CV`  
  _Amirreza Mahbod, Ramona Woitek, Jeanne Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In computational pathology, nuclear instance segmentation is a fundamental task with many downstream clinical applications. With the advent of deep learning, many approaches, including convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs), have been proposed for this task, along with both machine learning-based and non-machine learning-based pre- and post-processing techniques to further boost performance. However, one fundamental aspect that has received less attention is the evaluation pipeline. In this study, we identify four key issues associated with nuclear instance segmentation evaluation and propose corresponding solutions. Our proposed modifications, namely handling vague regions, score normalization, overlapping instances, and border uncertainty, are integrated into a unified framework called NucEval, which extends commonly used metrics for evaluating nuclear instance segmentation. We evaluate this framework on the NuInsSeg dataset, which provides unique characteristics that make it particularly suitable for this study, as well as two additional external datasets, using three CNN- and ViT-based nuclear instance segmentation models. Beyond its effect on the metrics, we show that NucEval increases inter- and intra-observer agreement between expert annotations and can change the ranking of closely competing methods on an external benchmark, indicating that the choice of evaluation protocol can affect model selection. The code, along with complete guidelines and illustrative examples, is publicly available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Early-Fusion Cue Analysis for Thin-Obstacle Semantic Segmentation in UAV Navigation: A Systematic Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09694)**  `arXiv:2604.09694`  `cs.CV`  
  _Negar Fathi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thin and ultra-thin obstacles such as wires, poles, and branches remain challenging for UAV semantic segmentation because they occupy few pixels, often exhibit weak visual contrast, and are affected by severe class imbalance. Depth and edge information can complement RGB appearance with geometric and boundary cues, but their individual and combined contributions are difficult to isolate when their use is coupled with specialized fusion architectures. We present EDFNet, a modular early-fusion framework that enables controlled evaluation of these complementary inputs under a fixed input-level fusion strategy. We study RGB, RGB-D, RGB-E, and RGB-D-E on the Drone Depth and Obstacle Segmentation (DDOS) benchmark across four segmentation architectures under pretrained and non-pretrained settings, examining their performance through quantitative, class-wise, qualitative, and efficiency analyses. Results identify depth as the most consistently beneficial auxiliary input, whereas explicit edge information produces smaller and more heterogeneous effects. Incorporating edges alongside depth yields limited additional benefit, with the effect varying across architectures and obstacle categories. Architecture choice also has a larger influence on inference throughput than the addition of depth or edge channels.
  </details>

- **[AGuide: Guiding Air-Ground Feature Adaptation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13919)**  `arXiv:2603.13919`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xianke Wu, Xinyi Li, Minxue Li, Jian Di, Guiyang Luo, Yonglin Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Air--ground collaborative perception uses aerial observations to extend vehicle sensing coverage and alleviate occlusion. However, viewpoint differences create a cross-platform representation gap that limits the effective use of aerial information. Existing methods focus on depth estimation, feature interaction, and agent association, with less attention to explicit UAV-to-vehicle feature adaptation. UAV features can be adapted before fusion to bridge this gap, but adapters are typically trained with detection loss alone, which supervises adaptation indirectly through the final predictions. To provide explicit cross-platform supervision, we propose AGuide, a dual-granularity Flow Matching framework that provides explicit cross-platform auxiliary supervision through paired velocity prediction. Object-level Flow Matching captures foreground semantics, while token-level Flow Matching accommodates local cross-view differences. Both branches learn to predict UAV-to-vehicle feature displacements along interpolation paths, providing directional guidance for UAV adaptation alongside detection supervision. We further introduce OPV2V-Air, a camera-based benchmark for air--ground collaborative perception. AGuide improves AP@0.7 by 2.5 percentage points on OPV2V-Air and 4.0 percentage points on V2U4Real, demonstrating more accurate detection in both camera-based simulation and real-world LiDAR-based collaboration.
  </details>

- **[StereoAdapter-2: Globally Structure-Consistent Underwater Stereo Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16915)**  `arXiv:2602.16915`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeyu Ren, Xiang Li, Zeyu Zhang, Hao Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stereo depth estimation is fundamental to underwater robotic perception, yet suffers from severe domain shifts caused by wavelength-dependent light attenuation, scattering, and refraction. Recent approaches leverage monocular foundation models with GRU-based iterative refinement for underwater adaptation; however, the sequential gating and local convolutional kernels in GRUs necessitate multiple iterations for long-range disparity propagation, limiting performance in large-disparity and textureless underwater regions. In this paper, we propose StereoAdapter-2, which replaces the conventional ConvGRU updater with a novel ConvSS2D operator based on selective state space models. The proposed operator employs a four-directional scanning strategy that naturally aligns with epipolar geometry while capturing vertical structural consistency, enabling efficient long-range spatial propagation within a single update step at linear computational complexity. Furthermore, we construct UW-StereoDepth-80K, a large-scale synthetic underwater stereo dataset featuring diverse baselines, attenuation coefficients, and scattering parameters through a two-stage generative pipeline combining semantic-aware style transfer and geometry-consistent novel view synthesis. Combined with dynamic LoRA adaptation inherited from StereoAdapter, our framework achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on underwater benchmarks with 17% improvement on TartanAir-UW and 7.2% improvment on SQUID, with real-world validation on the BlueROV2 platform demonstrates the robustness of our approach. Code:this https URL. Website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[VLM-UQBench: A Benchmark for Modality-Specific and Cross-Modality Uncertainties in Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09214)**  `arXiv:2602.09214`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenyu Wang, Tianle Chen, Yuxin Wu, Qijia He, Mingyu Chen, H. M. Sabbir Ahmad, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Uncertainty quantification (UQ) is essential for ensuring the safety and reliability of vision-language models (VLMs). A central challenge is to identify the source of uncertainty, whether it arises from the image, the text, or their interaction. We introduce VLM-UQBench, a benchmark for modality-specific and cross-modal data uncertainty in VLMs. It combines human-curated VizWiz subsets covering clean, image-, text-, and cross-modal uncertainty with a scalable perturbation pipeline comprising visual, textual, and cross-modal perturbations. We further introduce two metrics to assess how UQ scores respond to these perturbations and how their changes correlate with hallucinations. Using this framework, we systematically benchmark both LLM-based and VLM-specific UQ methods across nine VLMs and three datasets. Empirically, we find that (i) UQ performance varies substantially across uncertainty sources and VLMs; (ii) modality-specific uncertainty frequently co-occurs with hallucinations, yet changes in UQ scores only weakly track these failures; and (iii) we uncover response-length confounding in UQ evaluation, where seemingly effective uncertainty detectors may rely on simple answer-length cues. These findings highlight limitations in both current UQ methods and their evaluation, calling for more robust, modality-aware UQ approaches and more rigorous evaluation protocols that go beyond detection performance to assess the reliability of uncertainty signals.
  </details>

- **[TOM-GS: Robust Thermal Odometry and Mapping with Depth-Guided Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07493)**  `arXiv:2602.07493`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tianhao Zhou, Shengqian Wang, Zhihao Zhan, Yuhang Ming, Jianzhu Huai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thermal infrared cameras offer robustness to darkness, smoke, and illumination changes, making them attractive for robotic deployment in visually degraded environments. However, most existing thermal SLAM systems are geometric and produce sparse maps, while recent learning-based odometry and neural mapping methods are primarily developed for RGB imagery.We present TOM-GS, a monocular thermal odometry and mapping system that integrates learning-based dense tracking with depth-guided 3D Gaussian Splatting for dense reconstruction. Monocular depth priors are used to stabilize thermal odometry and supervise Gaussian geometry, improving scale consistency and robustness in low-texture scenes.Experiments on the RRXIO and VIVID benchmarks show that learned thermal odometry outperforms classical geometric pipelines and that thermal sensing provides improved robustness over RGB under degraded illumination. Furthermore, Gaussian Splatting enables high-quality dense thermal reconstruction in challenging environments.
  </details>

- **[Lingua-SafetyBench: A Benchmark for Safety Evaluation of Multilingual Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22737)**  `arXiv:2601.22737`  `cs.CV`  
  _Enyi Shi, Pengyang Shao, Yanxin Zhang, Chenhang Cui, Jiayi Lyu, Xiaobo Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The robust safety of Vision-Language Large Models (VLLMs) against joint multilingual and multimodal threats remains severely underexplored. Current benchmarks typically isolate these dimensions, being either multilingual but text-only, or multimodal but monolingual. While recent red-teaming efforts attempt to bridge this gap by rendering harmful prompts as images, their overreliance on typography-style visuals and lack of semantically grounded image-text pairs fail to capture realistic cross-modal interactions under multilingual and multimodal conditions. To address this, we introduce Lingua-SafetyBench, a comprehensive benchmark of 100,440 harmful image-text pairs spanning 10 languages. Crucially, Lingua-SafetyBench explicitly partitions data into Image-Dominant and Text-Dominant subsets to precisely disentangle sources of risk. Extensive evaluations reveal that current VLLMs retain non-negligible vulnerabilities under these joint inputs. Furthermore, analyzing modality-language interactions uncovers a striking asymmetry: on average, Image-Dominant Risk poses greater threats in High-Resource Languages (HRLs), whereas models are more vulnerable in Non-High-Resource Languages (Non-HRLs) under Text-Dominant Risk. Finally, a controlled study on the Qwen series demonstrates that while model scaling and iterative upgrades improve overall safety, they disproportionately benefit HRLs. This exacerbates the safety disparity between HRLs and Non-HRLs under Text-Dominant Risk, highlighting that achieving robust safety requires dedicated language- and modality-aware alignment strategies beyond mere scaling. Our data and code are available atthis https URL. Warning: This paper contains examples with unsafe content.
  </details>

- **[TPGDiff: Hierarchical Triple-Prior Guided Diffusion for Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20306)**  `arXiv:2601.20306`  `cs.CV`
