  _Yanjie Tu, Qingsen Yan, Axi Niu, Jiacong Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  All-in-one image restoration aims to address diverse degradation types using a single unified model. Existing methods typically rely on degradation priors to guide restoration, yet often struggle to reconstruct content in severely degraded regions. Although recent works leverage semantic information to facilitate content generation, integrating it into the shallow layers of diffusion models often disrupts spatial structures (\emph{e.g.}, blurring artifacts). To address this issue, we propose a Triple-Prior Guided Diffusion (TPGDiff) network for unified image restoration. TPGDiff incorporates degradation priors throughout the diffusion trajectory, while introducing structural priors into shallow layers and semantic priors into deep layers, enabling hierarchical and complementary prior guidance for image reconstruction. Specifically, we leverage multi-source structural cues as structural priors to capture fine-grained details and guide shallow layers representations. To complement this design, we further develop a distillation-driven semantic extractor that yields robust semantic priors, ensuring reliable high-level guidance at deep layers even under severe degradations. Furthermore, a degradation extractor is employed to learn degradation-aware priors, enabling stage-adaptive control of the diffusion process across all timesteps. Extensive experiments on both single- and multi-degradation benchmarks demonstrate that TPGDiff achieves superior performance and generalization across diverse restoration scenarios. Our project page is:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SLGNet: Unifying Structural and Language Guidance for Multimodal Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02249)**  `arXiv:2601.02249`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiantai Xiang, Jiacheng Deng, Kai Liu, Zixiao Wen, Wenshuai Li, Ben Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RGB--infrared object detection combines complementary appearance and thermal cues to improve detection under challenging imaging conditions. However, adapting a pretrained backbone does not by itself ensure that useful geometry is preserved during fusion or that the resulting features are effectively used across scenes. Without explicit geometric guidance, weakened boundaries can make object regions harder to separate and compromise localization. Even when useful geometry is retained, changes in scene context may leave target responses weak or spatially incomplete, limiting the use of available evidence. We propose SLGNet, a parameter-efficient adaptation architecture that addresses both feature construction and use through structure-guided fusion and scene-conditioned recalibration. The Structure-Aware Adapter uses a complementary edge reference to weight multi-scale RGB--infrared fusion and injects the resulting features into a frozen vision transformer. Language-Guided Modulation converts structured scene descriptions from a pretrained vision-language model into channel-wise affine parameters that rescale and shift the final structure-enriched backbone features according to scene context. We evaluate SLGNet on LLVIP, FLIR, KAIST, and DroneVehicle. With 12.1M trainable parameters, SLGNet achieves 66.1 mAP on LLVIP and 45.1 mAP on FLIR, the highest mAP scores among the compared methods on both benchmarks. These results improve over the reported UniRGB-IR scores by 2.9 and 1.0 points, respectively. On FLIR, SLGNet also outperforms our full fine-tuning baseline by 1.5 mAP while using approximately 87\% fewer trainable parameters.
  </details>

- **[PhyGDPO: Physics-Aware Groupwise Direct Preference Optimization for Physically Consistent Text-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24551)**  `arXiv:2512.24551`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Kunpeng Li, Menglin Jia, Jialiang Wang, Junzhe Sun, Feng Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in text-to-video (T2V) generation have achieved good visual quality, yet synthesizing videos that faithfully follow physical laws remains an open challenge. Existing methods mainly based on graphics or prompt extension struggle to generalize beyond simple simulated environments or learn implicit physical reasoning. The scarcity of training data with rich physics interactions and phenomena is also a problem. In this paper, we first introduce a Physics-Augmented video data construction Pipeline, PhyAugPipe, that leverages a vision-language model (VLM) with chain-of-thought reasoning to collect a large-scale training dataset, PhyVidGen-135K. Then we formulate a principled Physics-aware Groupwise Direct Preference Optimization, PhyGDPO, framework that uses real-world video as winning case to guarantee correct physics learning and builds upon the groupwise Plackett-Luce probabilistic model to capture holistic preferences beyond pairwise comparisons. In PhyGDPO, we design a Physics-Guided Rewarding (PGR) scheme that leverages VLM-based physical rewards to direct the optimization to focus on challenging physics cases. In addition, we propose a LoRA-Switch Reference (LoRA-SR) scheme that avoids full-model duplication as reference for efficient DPO training. Experiments show that our method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art open-source methods on PhyGenBench and VideoPhy2. Please check our project page atthis https URLfor more video results. Our code, data, and models are publicly available atthis https URL. An online Gradio demo is publicly available on HuggingFace atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[EditThinker: Enhancing Image Editors via Iterative Reasoning with a Plug-and-Play VLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05965)**  `arXiv:2512.05965`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongyu Li, Manyuan Zhang, Dian Zheng, Ziyu Guo, Yimeng Jia, Kaituo Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image editors can produce realistic images while still failing to satisfy the requested changes. Improving their instruction following requires identifying these failures and translating visual feedback into instructions that the editor can execute. We introduce EditThinker, an 8B vision-language model (VLM) that enhances existing image editors through iterative reasoning while keeping their weights fixed. EditThinker jointly critiques semantic consistency and visual quality, reasons about editing failures, and refines the instruction in a structured response. It interacts with the editor through a Critique-Refine-Repeat loop, providing a plug-and-play interface for feedback-driven editing. Learning effective refinements requires more than imitating plausible expert suggestions: the instructions must work with the capabilities of the underlying editor. We therefore combine supervised fine-tuning on expert demonstrations with reinforcement learning from executed editing outcomes, using format, critic, and edit rewards. To support this training, we construct ThinkEdit-167k, a dataset of 167k step-wise samples with unified supervision for critique, reasoning, and instruction refinement. Across four tested editors, EditThinker improves overall scores on GEdit-Bench-EN, ImgEdit-Bench, RISE-Bench, and KRIS-Bench. Ablations examine the joint model design, data allocation, and training across VLM backbones. In our GEdit round-budget study, refinement achieves higher scores with fewer editor calls than best-of-N selection. We will release our data construction framework, datasets, and models.
  </details>

- **[6 Fingers, 1 Kidney: Natural Adversarial Medical Images Reveal Critical Weaknesses of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04238)**  `arXiv:2512.04238`  `cs.CV`  
  _Leon Mayer, Piotr Kalinowski, Caroline Ebersbach, Marcel Knopp, Tim Rädsch, Evangelia Christodoulou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly integrated into clinical workflows. However, existing benchmarks primarily assess performance on common anatomical presentations and fail to capture the challenges posed by rare variants. To address this gap, we introduce AdversarialAnatomyBench, the first benchmark comprising naturally occurring rare anatomical variants across diverse imaging modalities and anatomical regions. We call such variants that violate learned priors about "typical" human anatomy natural adversarial anatomy. Benchmarking 25 state-of-the-art VLMs with AdversarialAnatomyBench yielded three key insights. First, when queried with basic medical perception tasks, mean accuracy dropped from 71% on typical to 28% on atypical anatomy. Even the best-performing models, GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, and Llama 4 Maverick, showed performance drops of 39-51%. Second, model errors closely mirrored expected anatomical biases. Third, neither model scaling nor interventions, including bias-aware prompting and test-time reasoning, resolved these issues. These findings highlight a critical limitation in current VLMs: their poor generalization to rare anatomical presentations. AdversarialAnatomyBench provides a foundation for systematically measuring and mitigating anatomical bias in multimodal medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. The code and dataset are available atthis https URLandthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Geospatial Foundation Models Against a Supervised Baseline for Fine-Grained Alpine Habitat Change Detection with Very High-Resolution Imagery and LiDAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.00073)**  `arXiv:2511.00073`  `cs.CV`  
  _Harald Kristen, Daniel Kulmer, Manuela Hirschmugl_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) are proposed for operational Earth observation but benchmarked mostly on urban and agricultural data, so their value and limitations for fine-grained ecological monitoring, with fuzzy class boundaries and severe class imbalance, are largely untested. We benchmark five GFMs (Clay v1.0, Clay v1.5, Prithvi-EO-2.0, DOFA, TerraMind) against two supervised U-Net baselines, one with a transformer (MiT-B2) and one with a convolutional (ResNet-50) encoder, for post-classification habitat change detection in Gesäuse National Park, Austria. All models are trained with three seeds on 2013 expert-interpreted labels (23 habitat classes), using very high-resolution RGB and near-infrared aerial imagery and a normalised digital surface model (nDSM) derived from airborne light detection and ranging (LiDAR). Applied to 2019 imagery and 2020 LiDAR, their predictions are compared with the 2013 reference to yield change maps with eight transition classes plus no change. All five GFMs are robustly ahead of the convolutional U-Net, but so is the transformer U-Net, which only Clay v1.0 exceeds, by a small margin (multi-class change macro IoU 0.233 vs. 0.209) whose paired 95% confidence interval (+0.001 to +0.045) nearly includes zero. Adding the nDSM helps every model, but mostly in the canopy-cover classes, whose reference labels are themselves LiDAR-derived. Rare or heterogeneous transitions remain poorly detected by every model. Rule-based post-processing defined with the Gesäuse National Park administration converts the predictions into polygon-structured, ecologically plausible change maps that meet the park's data standards, with little effect on pixel accuracy (multi-class macro IoU -0.03 to +0.04) but lower recall for forest density loss and setback; the maps are therefore suited to flagging candidate changes for expert verification rather than to unsupervised use.
  </details>

- **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713)**  `arXiv:2506.04713`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hanxin Wang, Tian Liu, Shu Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. Contemporary methods mitigate this challenge by exploiting pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for their learned transferable features. Some recent ones augment the scarce data by retrieving relevant yet out-of-distribution (OOD) examples from external data sources. However, they largely overlook an equally fundamental challenge: the absence of validation data for model selection and hyperparameter tuning. As a result, the adapted VLMs overfit the few-shot training data and generalize poorly to OOD test data. We show that retrieved data provide a natural source of validation, but their direct use creates a fundamental paradox. Since the retrieved samples are inherently OOD w.r.t the few-shot ID training data, adapting a VLM to the ID data constantly degrades its performance on the retrieved data. Consequently, conventional validation relying on the accuracy of retrieved data favors no adaptation and prevents improved generalization. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. This strategy enables hyperparameter tuning, which had been unrealistically done by using test data, thereby mitigating overfitting and improving generalization. We integrate it into a stage-wise finetuning pipeline, termed Validation-Enabled Stage-wise Tuning (VEST). Extensive experiments on five established benchmarks show that VEST consistently outperforms existing approaches on both ID and OOD test data.
  </details>

- **[ComposeAnything: Composite Object Priors for Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24086)**  `arXiv:2505.24086`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeeshan Khan, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layout-based compositional methods improve text-to-image generation by specifying where objects should appear, but most rely on sparse 2D constraints with limited appearance, occlusion, and depth-aware cues. We show that compositional generation can be substantially improved by replacing purely geometric guidance with an editable visual prior for diffusion initialization. We introduce ComposeAnything, an inference-time framework built around a 2.5D Composite Object Prior, an editable RGB image that specifies object appearance, layout, scale, and depth-aware ordering. We transfer this prior through Prior-Guided Diffusion, which combines Object Prior Reinforcement (OPR) to preserve object structure and appearance with Spatial-Controlled Denoising (SCD) to improve region-text alignment and reduce semantic leakage. Early guidance preserves the prior structure, after which standard denoising refines the image coherently. Extensive evaluations on T2I-CompBench, NSR-1K, and GenEval show strong gains in spatial reasoning, 3D-spatial relations, counting, and surreal compositions while maintaining visual quality and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Learning Delicate Local Representations for Multi-Person Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.04030)**  `arXiv:2003.04030`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Zhicheng Wang, Zhengxiong Luo, Binyi Yin, Angang Du, Haoqian Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a novel method called Residual Steps Network (RSN). RSN aggregates features with the same spatial size (Intra-level features) efficiently to obtain delicate local representations, which retain rich low-level spatial information and result in precise keypoint localization. Additionally, we observe the output features contribute differently to final performance. To tackle this problem, we propose an efficient attention mechanism - Pose Refine Machine (PRM) to make a trade-off between local and global representations in output features and further refine the keypoint locations. Our approach won the 1st place of COCO Keypoint Challenge 2019 and achieves state-of-the-art results on both COCO and MPII benchmarks, without using extra training data and pretrained model. Our single model achieves 78.6 on COCO test-dev, 93.0 on MPII test dataset. Ensembled models achieve 79.2 on COCO test-dev, 77.1 on COCO test-challenge dataset. Code and model are publicly available for further research atthis https URL. Our project page atthis https URLshows more video results. An online Gradio demo is available on HuggingFace atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Unlocking the Power of Loops in Pretrained LLMs with Loop Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31094)**  `arXiv:2611.31094`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xuanlei Zhao, Hailun Xu, Kai Wang, Yang Luo, Yingxuan Ren, Zirui Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.
  </details>

- **[Learn the Effects, Not the Examples: Implicit ICL via Attention Patching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30690)**  `arXiv:2611.30690`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Seungwon Woo, Hyunseo Shin, Wonseok Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) enables language models to adapt to new tasks from demonstrations, but repeatedly including multiple examples increases context length and inference cost. Many existing implicit ICL methods encode demonstrations as fixed vectors, limiting adaptation of the intervention to individual queries and token positions. We propose Attention-Patching, an implicit ICL method that models contextual changes in self-attention outputs through task-specific updates to attention output projections. Using query-answer pairs processed with and without a fixed demonstration set, Attention-Patching fits layer-wise affine transformations from non-contextual attention representations to the demonstration-induced output changes. Across 12 classification benchmarks and four model families, Attention-Patching achieves higher average accuracy than the evaluated implicit ICL baselines while maintaining performance comparable to explicit ICL at near zero-shot inference cost. Further experiments on diverse generation tasks demonstrate its applicability to autoregressive multi-token generation.
  </details>

- **[Reading the Tea Leaves, Again: Can Large Language Models Predict Language Model Training Runs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30141)**  `arXiv:2611.30141`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinran Zhao, Xiang Li, Tong Chen, Jinchuan Tian, Zichun Yu, Tongshuang Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents now propose research ideas and run the experiments that test them, but they train every run to its end, whereas a researcher reads a run's configuration and the beginning of its curve and stops the runs that show no promise. Reading a run this way takes more than describing or implementing a training method: it takes knowing what a design choice does to a run. We build Tea Leaves to measure this knowledge on 682 real training runs across pretraining, fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning (RL): a forecaster sees the first 20% of a run with its configuration, description, and code, and predicts the rest. Across 11 LLMs and a range of forecasting baselines, the strongest LLMs match or exceed the best curve fitter on smooth loss curves (skill 0.66 against 0.62) and lead on noisy RL reward curves (0.43 against 0.13). What bounds every forecaster is what the log carries: on sparsely logged community runs all methods lose most of their skill, and none reliably flags a failing run in advance. Beyond the curve, information helps and effort does not: related runs in the prompt cut the error on loss curves by 39%, and knowledge of training dynamics lets the strongest LLMs order all 36 pairs of runs that differ by a described code change, which the early curves do not separate, whereas tools add little. This knowledge can be learned from logged runs: a small open model trained on public runs matches the best LLM on loss curves. Applied to the progress curves of research agents, the same reading ranks tasks by their remaining headroom, although for a single agent run it does not improve on the last value. We release the benchmark, the trained readers, and their training data to test and train LLMs' understanding of training and to help research agents decide which runs deserve their compute.
  </details>

- **[Parameter-Efficient Vocabulary Expansion via Cross-Lingual Embedding Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30123)**  `arXiv:2611.30123`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Andrew Ivan Soegeng, Muhammad Reza Qorib, Weishi Wang, Daniel Dahlmeier, Hwee Tou Ng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual pretraining with vocabulary expansion is often used to make large language models (LLMs) more effective and efficient at handling new languages. However, when LLMs are extended to many languages simultaneously, the number of model parameters increases significantly. In addition, the newly added tokens do not receive the same number of training steps as the tokens in the original vocabulary, which sometimes causes vocabulary expansion to yield only limited performance improvements. We propose leveraging the semantic equivalence between words in the model's primary language and those in the extended vocabulary to transfer learned representations. In this paper, we introduce MATE, a parameter-efficient vocabulary expansion method that enables effective cross-lingual transfer with 8 times fewer additional parameters per token. MATE reduces the average tokenization compression ratio and delivers improvements on multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[FOLD: Position-Free Far Memory with Local Order for Long-Context Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20392)**  `arXiv:2610.20392`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM generation through lightweight drafting and parallel verification. It is particularly promising for long inputs, where KV-cache access costs can be amortized over multiple accepted tokens. Yet most drafters are trained on short sequences and degrade on long inputs, while long-context training is costly. Existing training-free approaches restrict the context window, rescale positions, or augment recent context with retrieved distant entries. Drafters, however, read target hidden states that already encode contextual and positional information, and their lightweight architecture makes full-history attention affordable. We propose FOLD, a training-free method that preserves exact local positions and treats all distant entries as far memory at a shared position. To counter attention dilution as the memory grows, we sharpen attention within it to emphasize relevant entries while separately controlling its total weight relative to the local window. Across six target-drafter pairs and diverse benchmarks of up to 256K tokens, FOLD predominantly delivers the highest speedup, with the best case more than doubling the speedup over autoregressive decoding, from 2.08$\times$ to 4.22$\times$.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Evaluation of Contrastive Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460)**  `arXiv:2611.30460`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Ting Chen, Geng Li, Guohao Chen, Hanjia Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). CGM also enables analysis of directional consistency, composition, and reliability of contrastive corrections. Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Don't Sleep, Keep Watch: Proactive Perception for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28947)**  `arXiv:2611.28947`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaqi Su, Jie-Jing Shao, Yichen Liu, Long-Kai Huang, Haiyan Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-use agents (CUAs) built on vision-language models are increasingly entrusted with long-horizon tasks in live desktop, web, and mobile environments, where the screen keeps changing on its own. However, current CUAs lack real-time situational awareness: they perceive the environment through discrete snapshots, and the substantial inference latency of VLMs, compounded by highly dynamic interfaces, opens a perception-action gap in which the observed state frequently becomes stale before the corresponding action is executed. To bridge this gap, we present NightWatch, a plug-and-play framework that endows any CUA with continuous, temporally grounded perception without changing the agent's model, prompt, or action space. NightWatch couples the VLM agent with NightWatch-0.8B, a lightweight System-1 model that performs streaming monitoring of the interface state and keeps the agent synchronized with the evolving environment at low computational cost and latency, thereby grounding each decision in the current state rather than an outdated snapshot and reducing both stale actions and idle waiting while preserving task success. Experiments on desktop and dynamic web environments (OSWorld-Verified and DynaCU-Bench) show that NightWatch, driven by a watcher of only 0.8B parameters, reduces non-reasoning wall-clock time by 33% and wasted time by 53% on OSWorld while keeping task success on par, raises success on DynaCU-Bench from 39.5% to 44.2% with the large VLM agent, and is more efficient than passive-watching baselines such as AOI and PUSV under the same screenshot-only input, as well as a System-1 decision model more than $30\times$ its size. Overall, NightWatch demonstrates that the timing of perception is as critical as its content, marking a step toward agents that maintain continuous awareness of their environment rather than perceiving it turn by turn.
  </details>

- **[NepOOC-M: Bilingual Nepali-English Benchmark and Comparative Analysis of Multimodal Architectures for OOC Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19212)**  `arXiv:2608.19212`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Sanjeev Khatiwada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-context (OOC) misinformation pairs authentic images with misleading captions to construct false narratives without image manipulation, making detection a problem of multimodal alignment rather than image forensics. Despite the prevalence and consequences of OOC misinformation in Nepal, no public benchmark exists for Nepali. We introduce NepOOC, the first publicly available Nepali-dominant multilingual OOC benchmark, comprising 1,090 image-caption pairs (545 pristine, 545 OOC) annotated across five typologies (fabricated, miscaptioned, temporal mismatch, geographic mismatch, identity mismatch) with inter-annotator agreement kappa = 0.84. Systematic evaluation of five multimodal architectures alongside text-only and image-only baselines shows that text-only models perform strongly on the current benchmark: a text-only mBERT model reaches 94.65 +/- 0.20% Macro-F1, with no statistically detectable difference from the best multimodal system (ResNet-50+mBERT, 94.56 +/- 0.24%). Image-only models perform near chance, as expected because the two captions of each item share one image. Caption-only baselines show that terminal punctuation and caption length separate the classes to a large degree, and retraining the neural models on captions without punctuation lowers Macro-F1 by about 21 points. We therefore interpret the reported scores as caption discrimination on this paired benchmark, and recommend text-only and caption-style baselines as a standard check for OOC benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When 1+1 $\neq$ 2: Benchmarking Civil-Time Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29972)**  `arXiv:2611.29972`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Melika Shirian, Kianoosh Vadaei, Arshia Hemmat, Mohammad Hossein Shaker Ardakani, Ali Mamanpoosh, Matin Moqadas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Calendar arithmetic is non-linear. When 1:59 AM + 1 minute occurs during daylight saving spring-forward, the result is 3:00 AM, not 2:00 AM, as the hour is skipped. We introduce CivilTime-Bench, a benchmark for civil-time reasoning covering daylight saving transitions, non-hour offsets, date-line crossings, and ISO week boundaries. With 60,011 training and 10,500 evaluation examples under strict output-contract evaluation, we benchmark seven LLMs (six instruction-tuned models and Llama-3.1-8B base) and find: zero-shot exact match varies substantially across models, contract-aware prompting often improves format compliance but yields inconsistent accuracy gains, few-shot in-context learning provides model-dependent improvements, and fine-tuning achieves 74.6--86.6% EM on synthetic evaluation but only 36.6--45.2% on HumanTime-500 for models with valid results on both splits, highlighting substantial limitations in generalization. We release the benchmark and code.
  </details>

- **[Population-Based Post-Training for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29014)**  `arXiv:2611.29014`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixuan Even Xu, Aleksei Petrenko, Raja Giryes, Jackson Hamburger, Kevin Chen, Fei Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is one of the most popular post-training paradigms for large language models. RLVR typically trains a single model under hyperparameters chosen before training begins, while researchers tune hyperparameters across consecutive runs. We introduce Population-Based Post-Training (PBPT), which trains several models in parallel and lets the training process do the tuning through population-based training. PBPT periodically evaluates models on a validation set. High-performers are duplicated and their hyperparameters perturbed to explore new configurations. Low-performers stop training and are replaced by these copies. On reasoning benchmarks (Enigmata, Nemotron) and interactive tool-use environments (Gaia2, AppWorld), PBPT produces stronger agents than compute-matched groups of independent RLVR learners. Furthermore, PBPT is able to recover from poor initial hyperparameters, automatically discovering better settings over the course of training.
  </details>

- **[When Rubrics Do Not Decide: Silent Value Completion in LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28940)**  `arXiv:2611.28940`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuangzhuang Pan, Yan Xia, Kaiwen Lei, Chee Seng Chan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-criterion LLM judges often evaluate responses without specifying how conflicting criteria resolve, leaving the winner undetermined even under exact criterion scores. Issuing a strict verdict without declaring the required trade-off premise constitutes silent value completion. We introduce TradeoffBench to isolate this failure by separating rule execution from response scoring. On authoritative criterion vectors, six open-weight non-reasoning judges pass all dominance and equality controls yet pick unsupported winners on 9%-81% of 1,345 conflicts. All 577 order-stable choices follow an explicitly banned majority rule. Reasoning-enabled configurations drop anonymous-vector violations to 2 in 9,720 judgments under an explicit rule, yet cannot bridge omitted trade-offs or divergent component scoring. On raw TradeoffBench text with unspecified trade-offs, GPT-6.1-sol omits its completion premise in 29.8% of 400 verified conflicts. Even under an explicit rule on four-rater-consensus MultiPref conflicts, four reasoning judges still issue strict verdicts on 16/45 (GPT-6.1-sol) up to 35/46 and 25/29 (open-weight judges) of valid presentations. We propose identified-first evaluation, decoupling specification support, explicit completion, and target agreement. In MultiPref human-score audits, peer-mean substitution raises directional disagreement from 0.6% to 28.8% on 2,707 shared targets. A single-peer ablation confirms a 29.38-point gap on 2,175 targets, ruling out averaging as the cause. In HUMAINE, declared partial trade-offs add 2.5 percentage points of coverage, yet newly decided pairs incur 3.9% disagreement versus 0.9% for Pareto. Reliable LLM judging, therefore, requires auditing, specification support, declared trade-offs, and human target agreement separately.
  </details>

- **[Post-training is (Massive) Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07527)**  `arXiv:2606.07527`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Michael Hassid, Yossi Adi, Roy Schwartz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The prevailing paradigm for training LLMs has evolved to rely on a massive post-training phase consisting of SFT and RL. In this position paper, we argue that this methodology effectively marks a reversion to the ``pre-train then fine-tune'' approach of the BERT era, explicitly tailoring models to the desired behaviors and specific benchmarks on which they are evaluated. We begin with a historical overview of LLMs, describing the different phases of the LLM evolution. We argue that the current landscape is remarkably similar to the early days of LLMs, where task performance heavily relied on fitting the models to in-distribution datasets. To empirically demonstrate this, we compare pre-trained models to randomly initialized ones, by fine-tuning both variants on modern reasoning datasets and evaluating them on competitive math and code benchmarks. We show that models post-trained from scratch yield highly non-trivial performance. Our findings suggest that current post-training methodologies function primarily as a distribution-fitting mechanism. We finish by positing that developing generally capable models and systems requires moving beyond extensive post-training for predefined behaviors, shifting instead toward training procedures where models ``learn how to learn''.
  </details>

- **[ToolSmith: An Automatic Tool-Generation Framework for Multimodal Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31198)**  `arXiv:2611.31198`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Rui Sun, Junhua Huang, Luca Zancato, Md Mofijul Islam, Kai-Wei Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal search agents use reasoning, vision, and live web search across many turns to answer questions beyond parametric knowledge by orchestrating a small library of hand-crafted search and visual tools. However, the library is laborious to create and maintain, and frozen when new query types, evidence formats, and failure modes are encountered in the wild. Human practitioners, by contrast, cope with insufficient tools by fashioning new ones from familiar parts and reusing them across tasks. Inspired by this, we propose ToolSmith, an automatic tool-generation framework that grows and reuses modules on top of a lean base tool library. We evaluate ToolSmith on three in-domain multimodal search benchmarks and ten out-of-domain benchmarks under a VLM-as-a-Judge protocol. ToolSmith consistently improves over the base library across deployed agents of varying scales, with larger backbones reaping greater gains, demonstrating the scalability of the approach. Our code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRIMEAPE: A Multilingual Benchmark for Fine-Grained Meta-Evaluation of Judge Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30925)**  `arXiv:2611.30925`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Vladislav Mikhailov, Sarfraz Ahmad, Momina Ahsan, Zoia Butenko, Vladimir Dobrovolskii, Preslav Nakov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly serve as judges of other models, shaping benchmark rankings and model development. Yet, a judge whose rationale consistently supports its verdict may still disagree with human preferences. To study this, we introduce PRIMEAPE, a multilingual benchmark for evaluating judge verdicts and rationales. PRIMEAPE provides 2,205 response pairs covering 243 instructions in English, French, Spanish, Urdu, Hindi, Norwegian Bokmål, and Yoruba, with 11k human annotations of preferences and rationales across relevance, naturalness, truthfulness, safety, and overall quality. Our evaluation of twelve open- and closed-weight judges reveals that aggregate performance conceals differences across languages and criteria, while requesting rationales does not consistently improve agreement with human preferences. Judges also favor the first position, longer responses, and responses from related models, with effects that vary across criteria and languages. Crucially, judges can justify verdicts that disagree with human preferences while maintaining consistency between their verdicts and rationales. These findings show that consistency with a judge's own verdict does not establish alignment with human judgment. PRIMEAPE enables assessment of both, providing a foundation for more reliable multilingual judges. We release our benchmark and all annotation materials under the CC BY-NC 4.0 license atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[CocoonBench: Can Multilingual Search Agents Escape Language Information Cocoons?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30727)**  `arXiv:2611.30727`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hongchuan Zeng, Bowen Liu, Changjiang Gao, Senyu Han, Zhipei Xu, Peisong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual search agents can access the global Web, yet may remain trapped in a language environment with insufficient evidence; we refer to this failure as a language information cocoon. However, existing search benchmarks largely operate within a single language, especially English, overlooking entity connections across multiple languages. We introduce CocoonBench, a benchmark that evaluates whether multilingual search agents can escape such language information cocoons. CocoonBench mines answer-centered relational paths from a frozen Wikidata-derived graph and selects clue sets that are individually necessary and jointly identify a unique answer. Using language-specific entity names, it records each task's minimum language cover, removes direct identifiers, and renders each task as matched prompts in three languages together with its construction certificate and provenance. Across 10 state-of-the-art models, mean accuracy on CocoonBench ranges from 33.8% (DeepSeek-V4-Flash) to 69.7% (Claude-Opus-4.8), leaving even the strongest system below 70%. The same construction certificates support path-aligned trajectory analysis, enabling diagnosis of model failures in language routing, evidence-path recovery, and answer use. The result shows that escaping a language cocoon requires more than switching languages: successful cross-lingual search depends on both covering the correct languages and connecting the evidence found across them.
  </details>

- **[Voyag: Joint Multi-Intent Detection and Execution-Relation Classification for In-Vehicle Voice Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30253)**  `arXiv:2611.30253`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sen Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An in-vehicle voice assistant must decide not only which intents an utterance contains but also how co-active intents may be executed: concurrently, in a fixed order, or not at all without arbitration. We present Voyag, a single-forward decision model that jointly predicts ten controlled vehicle-intent slots and a three-way execution relation (parallel, sequential, mutually exclusive) over each co-active intent pair. Voyag combines a multilingual ModernBERT-style encoder (mmBERT-base, 306.9M parameters), a typed per-slot choice head, and a lightweight GroupCalibration relation head (1.61M parameters) that consumes marker hidden states, pooled context, explicit resource-requirement profiles, and symmetrized outer-product conflict features. All objectives are optimized in one joint loss: soft cross-entropy over slot choices, focal cross-entropy over relations, and a mutex-consistency penalty. On a synthetic Chinese vehicle-life benchmark with semantic scene-level splits and a leakage audit (30,017 / 3,473 / 5,000 groups), validation-only selection yields 0.9893 slot accuracy and 0.0526 expected calibration error over 50,000 slot decisions. Conditioned on gold intents, the relation head reaches 0.8548 accuracy, 0.8473 macro-F1, and 0.6062 mutually-exclusive recall over 4,000 pairs; end to end with one encoder forward it reaches 0.8530 accuracy and 0.8464 macro-F1. Against a two-stage rule pipeline, the aggregate end-to-end gain is small and borderline, so we claim no aggregate advantage; the learned head's advantage lies in sequential and parallel recall where surface rules are blind. We report rule baselines with a tautology disclosure and a held-out thermal conflict failure.
  </details>

- **[FiRE: Fine-grained Ranking Evaluation for Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30168)**  `arXiv:2611.30168`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenyang Gao, Yinghao Yang, Xi Jin, Jing Li, Yue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing reliable machine translation (MT) systems hinges on our ability to distinguish superior translations from inferior ones. However, existing evaluation paradigms, whether limited to coarse overall rankings or misaligned with human preferences, fail to deliver interpretable, fine-grained feedback in reference-free settings. We present a Fine-Grained Ranking Evaluation method (FiRE) that leverages off-the-shelf large language models to perform criterion-driven pairwise comparison across three complementary dimensions: faithfulness, fluency, and consistency of style, instead of producing a single holistic judgment. To enable rigorous meta-evaluation of evaluation paradigms in the absence of any suitable testbed, we construct the first human-annotated, reference-free benchmark for fine-grained ranking evaluation, achieving substantial inter-annotator agreement. Through meta-evaluation on this benchmark and existing MQM datasets, FiRE demonstrably outperforms regression-based and error-analysis metrics in aligning with human comparative judgments, while providing more informative insights into translation quality. Finally, our examination of LLM evaluator biases (position and self-enhancement) and their handling of tied cases offers guidance for more nuanced MT evaluation. Code and benchmark resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ESGPT: A Data-Centric ESG Language Model with Unified Instruction and Benchmark Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30052)**  `arXiv:2611.30052`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jaeyoung Lee, Misuk Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ESG analysis requires complex language understanding across diverse sources, including corporate reports, news, and disclosures, for tasks such as ESG relevance detection, action interpretation, impact analysis, and question answering. However, existing ESG datasets are fragmented by task and source, with heterogeneous formats and annotation schemes, limiting their use for training general-purpose ESG LLMs and unified evaluation. In this work, we construct ESG-Instruct, a 30,000-instance ESG instruction dataset built from public ESG resources, and ESGU-Bench, an ESG understanding benchmark covering six representative ESG tasks. Based on these resources, we further develop ESGPT, an 8B ESG-specialized LLM, and evaluate it against diverse open-source and API-based LLMs. Experimental results show that general-purpose LLMs exhibit substantial performance variation across ESG tasks, while ESGPT, despite its relatively small scale, achieves the best performance on multiple ESG tasks. This work provides unified data and benchmark resources for training and evaluating ESG-specialized LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Refine, Don't Reinvent: RASA-Bench for Source-Grounded Hindi Prose Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29750)**  `arXiv:2611.29750`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Abdur Rahman, Pratham Singla, Mohammad Saifullah, Vasu Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For a producer of Hindi creative prose, drafting is cheap and editing is not: a machine-written episode, story, or essay reads as under-written and flat, laced with translationese, and it sometimes quietly alters what the source says. RASA-Bench targets that editing task across three Hindi prose forms, measuring how well a model refines an under-written AI draft, a task distinct from writing from scratch. Across 2,050 drafts and eight refiners (16,400 rewrites), a four-layer protocol credits a rewrite only after it passes a faithfulness check against its own source, then ranks the survivors by holistic editorial preference through per-form Bradley-Terry fits whose centered coefficients are standardized before equal-weight averaging. Staying faithful proves to be a decisive test that behaves differently by form: gate-failure rates range from 9.1% on short stories to 29.5% on audio fiction, and the drift that disqualifies a rewrite shifts from events and identity in narrative to claims and evidence in essays. A 600-case human audit puts gate agreement at 0.925 (0.91 to 0.94 across forms), whereas subjective preference is form-sensitive and least settled for essays, among human readers no less than the model judge. The benchmark, evaluation protocol, and Hindi prose diagnostics are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Is Not Acting: WARD Diagnoses the Evidence-to-State-to-Action Gap in Longitudinal Clinical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29243)**  `arXiv:2611.29243`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Ding, Yidong Jiang, Junzhi Ning, Juncheng Wu, Yuhan Wang, Renjie Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models for longitudinal clinical care must revise their assessments and actions as patient information accumulates. However, benchmarks centered on isolated responses or final task outcomes provide limited insight into intermediate failures in evidence use, state updating, and action selection. We introduce WARD, a benchmark for evaluating how clinical agents translate accumulating evidence into patient-state judgments and timely, safe actions. WARD comprises 4,560 clinical tasks across seven clinical output domains, organized into 240 checkpoints from 40 longitudinal clinical trajectories spanning 95 hospitalizations. Its Longitudinal Track evaluates these tasks under controlled cumulative evidence. Its Interactive Track evaluates end-to-end, tool-mediated clinical management across 271 verifiable evidence items and 141 reference transitions. Across 12 models with scorable checkpoint outputs, evidence recognition consistently outpaces downstream state and action decisions, with linked-evidence nonresponse reaching 74.47--90.90\%. Models also struggle to retain and update information over time, while in the Interactive Track only 11.46\% of executed actions advance a reference transition. These results reveal a persistent evidence-to-state-to-action gap and highlight the need for process-level evaluation of longitudinal clinicalthis http URLand data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Group-Balanced Supervised Fine-Tuning for Knowledge-Intensive Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29127)**  `arXiv:2611.29127`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mark Heimann, Bruno Jacob_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) weights every supervised token equally, so on knowledge-dense corpora the loss budget follows token count rather than importance: on a legal QA benchmark, citation strings absorb 81% of the supervised loss weight budget while the answers being taught absorb 5%. We present Group-Balanced SFT (GB-SFT), which equalizes the loss budget across the semantic roles of tokens within a target sequence. With one tunable dial and one structural switch, GB-SFT contains standard SFT, class- and span-level balancing as corners whose geometry we characterize exactly. On benchmarks in law, medicine, code, and mathematics, we show across model families that: (i) GB- SFT injects new knowledge better than uniform SFT, pointwise token weighting, memory editing, and matched-budget data mixing; (ii) it matches the injection of masking out everything but the knowledge payload while retaining the citation recall that masking destroys (0.98 vs. 0.38); and (iii) role-aware grouping carries the gain: label-free partitions recover at most a third of it, an LLM tagger nearly all where the groups are lexically distinctive; and and (iv) the budget governs what fine-tuning breaks as well as what it learns, acting as a monotone lever on the safety-guardrail erosion SFT causes, at matched task accuracy. We release two new contamination-controlled knowledge-injection benchmarks with validated ground truth.
  </details>

- **[RES: Accelerating Diffusion Language Models with Rolling Entropy Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29066)**  `arXiv:2611.29066`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Patrick Pynadath, Nicolas Perez-Nieves, Bobak Shahriari, Jean Tarbouriech, Ruqi Zhang, Ivan Lobov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A diffusion language sampler makes two choices for every position in its denoising window: where the next token comes from, and when the position is committed to the KV cache. Using the entropy-bound sampler in DiffusionGemma, we study two extremes of the second choice. Committing each token as soon as it is accepted removes the chance to revise it, which hurts when the entropy bound is relaxed to trade accuracy for speed. When accepted tokens are frozen, macro accuracy falls from 73% to 20% as the bound is relaxed, while the unfrozen sampler stays near 70%. Committing a whole block at once wastes computation, since tokens converge in a largely left-to-right order and more than half of the window holds already-final tokens by twenty denoising steps. These findings motivate the Rolling Entropy Sampler (RES), which commits a contiguous accepted prefix once it reaches a minimum length and keeps positions farther ahead open to revision. Unlike earlier rolling samplers, RES runs on uniform diffusion, where accepted tokens can still change, and it needs no training. Across seven benchmarks, RES increases tokens per forward pass from 19.3 to 25.5, while macro accuracy goes from 80.7% to 78.8%, and it extends the speed-quality Pareto frontier beyond what tuning the entropy bound alone reaches.
  </details>

- **[KMed-31B Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28719)**  `arXiv:2611.28719`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _KMed Team at NAVER_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present KMed-31B, a medical large language model post-trained from an open-weight model for on-premises deployment with limited hardware. Post-training a model for diverse medical tasks remains difficult, because improving one kind of task can lower performance on another. We attribute this to differences between tasks in expected output and available feedback. Medical question answering and diagnosis prediction have reference answers, whereas clinical inquiry has no single correct response and is judged with rubrics. We therefore divide medical training tasks into four categories by these two properties, train one specialist per category with a reward that its feedback supports, and integrate the specialists into a single student with Multi-teacher On-policy Delta Distillation (MOPD$^2$). For clinical inquiry, we build Synthetic Health Dialogue, a simulated corpus of consultations with lay users and clinicians whose patient cases span common clinical presentations in primary care. Relative to its base model, KMed-31B raises HealthBench Hard from 22.95 to 41.87 and HealthBench Professional from 37.92 to 50.20 in non-thinking mode, and from 23.80 to 51.48 and from 41.97 to 59.18 in thinking mode, while its scores on four multiple-choice medical benchmarks are maintained or slightly improved. KMed-31B has the highest unadjusted HealthBench Hard score among the compared models, including proprietary ones, and scores higher on HealthBench Professional than every open-weight model we compare, including models with more than ten times as many total parameters. Applied to another base model, Qwen3.8-27B, the same training recipe also raises both HealthBench scores. We describe the training recipe, data construction, and settings in detail so that institutions requiring on-premises deployment can develop medical models from open-weight foundations.
  </details>

- **[MedCoast: Semantic Abstraction of Reasoning Trajectories for Test-Time Biomedical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28586)**  `arXiv:2611.28586`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yishan Zhong, Jieru Li, Xuanzhe Ding, Shaunna Wang, Yanxin Yang, Micky C. Nnamdi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical question answering often requires multi-step reasoning under limited inference budgets. While test-time search methods such as Tree-of-Thought can improve reasoning performance, directly searching over free-form language is computationally expensive and highly redundant, as many reasoning trajectories contain semantically equivalent intermediate steps expressed in different surface forms. We introduce MedCoast, an offline-to-online reasoning framework that converts sampled chain-of-thought trajectories into a compact space for structured process supervision. Offline, we recursively cluster semantically similar reasoning steps under shared prefixes to construct an abstract reasoning tree that captures recurring clinical reasoning patterns. We then derive node-level supervision from progress toward correct solutions, and train a lightweight process reward model (PRM) to evaluate partial reasoning states. At inference time, the learned critic guides test-time exploration by prioritizing promising reasoning prefixes. Across seven biomedical QA benchmarks, MedCoast often improves over self-consistency under fixed inference budgets, with gains of up to 15 points. Despite using a reward model more than 50 times smaller, MedCoast achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art reward models in several settings, highlighting its potential for resource-constrained biomedical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Tool-to-Self: Internalizing Visual Tool Capabilities via Scaffolded Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28564)**  `arXiv:2611.28564`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jin Cui, Hao Dong, Xinyue Long, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly rely on external visual tools to access fine-grained visual evidence, but repeated tool execution incurs substantial inference overhead and deployment dependencies. Moreover, conventional tool-use RL can improve visual-task performance, yet the resulting tool gains are often unstable. Motivated by this, we propose Tool-to-Self (T2S), a tool-scaffolded reinforcement learning framework that turns external visual tools into temporary training scaffolds. T2S cold-starts tool-use ability and observation prediction from tool-repairable examples, then progressively anneals tool execution on a per-tool basis during RL. By combining answer-correctness and observation-consistency rewards with privileged on-policy self-distillation from actual tool observations, T2S effectively teaches the model both to reproduce tool outputs and to reason from them. Experiments across diverse visual perception and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over both explicit tool-use models and implicit zooming models while enabling tool-free inference with reduced latency.
  </details>

- **[From Reward Engineering to Data-Centric Reinforcement Learning for Effective Agentic Visual Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28558)**  `arXiv:2611.28558`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jin Cui, Xinyue Long, Jiepeng Zhou, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren, Hao Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly use external visual tools to acquire evidence during reasoning, yet learning when to invoke these tools remains challenging. Existing tool-use reinforcement learning (RL) methods often rely on call-contingent rewards to encourage beneficial tool use, but such reward engineering can distort the task objective and lead to degenerate calling behaviors. We argue that effective tool-use learning requires identifying not merely which questions are difficult, but which failures are attributable to missing visual evidence and can therefore be corrected through tool observations. We propose AOVO, a data-centric framework that enables MLLMs to learn effective tool use through perception attribution and policy-adaptive data sampling (PADS), without introducing tool-specific rewards. It counterfactually probes the model's reasoning states to identify observation-sensitive questions, distinguishing them from reasoning-dominated ones. PADS then periodically estimates category-level observation effects from recent policy rollouts and dynamically reallocates the training distribution toward questions where visual observations provide informative learning signals, while optimizing solely for final-answer correctness. Experiments across diverse visual perception and multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over competitive tool-use MLLMs, with causal analysis further validating the data-centric perspective for learning effective visual tool use.
  </details>

- **[Grammar-as-a-Draft: More Tokens per Speculative Decoding Step on Grammar-Constrained Structured Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28349)**  `arXiv:2611.28349`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _John Zhang, Silky Arora_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under structured outputs (JSON schemas, regexes, tool calls), speculative drafters do not see the grammar constraint and repeatedly propose invalid keys, delimiters, and indentation, while the grammar engine already knows which tokens must come next.We present Grammar-as-a-Draft (GaaD), which treats the grammar as a second drafter. Before each verify step, the scheduler merges the drafter's proposal with grammar-forced tokens into one draft row: matching tokens stay as GRAMMAR, the proposal is cut where it leaves the forced path, and forced tokens fill the rest of a row wider than the drafter's proposal. The verify step accepts GRAMMAR drafts without computing their logits, composing with any drafter with no new kernels or compilation on the critical path. Because grammars fix text rather than tokens, committing canonical forced tokens changes the output distribution relative to grammar-masked target decoding.We implement GaaD in vllm-torchtpu and an independent Rust serving runtime, evaluating on TPU v7x with MTP, DFlash, and n-gram drafters on a JSON character-profile benchmark. GaaD raises average acceptance length from 2.06 to 5.43 (MTP, Qwen3.5-0.8B), 1.99 to 5.31 (MTP, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B), 1.57 to 4.13 (DFlash, Qwen3-4B), and 1.30 to 3.64 (n-gram, Qwen3-4B), decoding 2.66-2.81x faster at batch size 1 and 1.14-1.70x at batch size 64. In Rust, MTP+GaaD is 2.23x faster on Qwen3.5-0.8B, 2.02x on Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8, and 1.22x at 128 concurrent requests. Under mixed traffic (25-50% structured), GaaD finishes the workload 1.03-1.23x sooner (structured requests 2.2-3.2x sooner) while plain-text TPOT rises 7-42%. We also show grammar-masked greedy decoding can pick non-canonical tokenizations, so GaaD can yield different valid outputs that an fp32 reference finds equally likely.
  </details>

- **[Language Model Harnesses can be Compositional Generalizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28298)**  `arXiv:2611.28298`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alex L. Zhang, Souradip Chakraborty, Omar Khattab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern language models (LMs) are post-trained and deployed as agents inside a "harness", but standard harness designs accumulate context that the neural network is forced to generalize to, making training for long-horizon tasks expensive and time-consuming. We study an alternative emerging path: training inherently neurosymbolic LM harnesses, which externalize and manage context programmatically to compose existing LM capabilities. We hypothesize that training around these harnesses can offer a better return on compute by enabling compositional generalization, as they can learn to decompose complex problems so that every observation their neural LM receives is locally in-distribution, even when the full task is novel. We find that harnesses like the Recursive Language Model (RLM) learn such decompositions readily under reinforcement learning post-training: (1) we demonstrate that training only on short tasks can generalize to tasks 2-64$\times$ longer while the base neural LM improves less, and (2) training on one domain transfers to novel domains that share latent structure. On messier, complex tasks, we find that we can target individual LLM calls within a harness by generating and training on easier, synthetically generated surrogate environments with fake tool / sub-calls, a technique we call surrogate harness training. From this finding, we train RLM-27B on synthetic data, and show it improves Qwen3.8-27B in the same RLM harness on a hard suite of long-context tasks like CTC-Bench and ExpertLongBench by 26.9% on average while using $\sim$2$\times$ fewer tokens at inference time. Finally, we show that harnesses can compose over these newly learned decompositions using recursive sub-harness calls.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Adapting LLMs for English-Swedish Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28050)**  `arXiv:2611.28050`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Seyed Alireza Molavi, Zhan Su, Stefan Byttner, Yan Hu, Peyman Sheikholharam Mashhadi, Prayag Tiwari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models do well on English medical exams, but less is known about Swedish. This matters most for small open models that hospitals can run locally. To reduce this gap, we first build an item-aligned English-Swedish benchmark from ten medical multiple-choice datasets; each Swedish item keeps the options and answer of its English source. A conservative refinement loop repairs the translations: open models from three families find errors, ground them in a Swedish medical term base, and keep a model-written edit only if blind judges vote for it. On this benchmark, small models lose much more accuracy in Swedish than large ones, especially when they answer without reasoning. Second, we ask whether English data and open teacher models can close this gap. We translate the training data, let teachers write reasoning traces, and fine-tune two small students on gold answers or traces in English, Swedish, or both. Training on reasoning traces that include Swedish closes the gap and lifts the 1.2B student from 23.5\% to 52.0\% in Swedish, slightly above a 20B open model in its fast mode, with a sixth of its memory. We release the benchmark, its edit history, and the code.
  </details>

- **[TUSI: A Citation-Grounded Graph-Based Benchmark for Retrieval-Augmented Generation in Persian Legal Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21823)**  `arXiv:2611.21823`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Marzieh Karimi, Mehran Safayani, Alireza Basiri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in Persian law remains challenging because benchmarks lack realism, grounding, and coverage of legal reasoning. Existing resources are either synthetically generated from statutory texts, lacking natural information needs, or derived from informal legal question--answer sources without explicit linkage to authoritative legal documents, making them unsuitable for rigorous evaluation of grounded legal reasoning. To address this gap, we introduce TUSI-Bench, a structured Persian legal benchmark designed for the evaluation of RAG-based systems. TUSI-Bench is built upon a multi-layered legal knowledge framework containing 7,261 legal articles and integrating codified statutes, legal commentaries, doctrinal writings, and Supreme Court unification rulings. We also construct a legal knowledge graph linking articles and concepts for structure-aware retrieval. Based on this foundation, TUSI-Bench provides two complementary evaluation tasks: (i) TUSI-QA, an open-ended question answering benchmark containing 1,999 questions that requires grounded responses with explicit legal citations, and (ii) TUSI-Bar, a multiple-choice benchmark containing approximately 500 four-option questions derived from Iranian Bar examination questions. Furthermore, we propose a graph-based retrieval baseline that leverages relational structure within the legal knowledge graph. Experimental results demonstrate that incorporating graph-based relations improves retrieval recall by up to 8 percentage points compared to standard baselines, highlighting the importance of structured legal representations for RAG systems. The proposed benchmark provides a standardized resource for evaluating grounded legal reasoning in Persian and enables future research on structured legal intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Closing the Loop: Practical Training Recipes for Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00673)**  `arXiv:2610.00673`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Andrei Marchenko, Viacheslav Bezrukov, Oleg Kashurin, Inessa Fedorova, Dmitry Bocharov, Yuliana Shakhvalieva, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block of layers, but existing large-scale recipes require multi-stage training over trillions of tokens, while the benefits of recurrence remain difficult to separate from differences in data and training. In this work, we establish practical training recipes for looped language models, with three main results. (1) We develop a compute-efficient from-scratch pipeline that reduces the training budget from 7.7T tokens in Ouro to 310B tokens while retaining strong reasoning performance. Pretraining followed by high-quality mid-training, together with learning-rate warmup and stronger exit-gate regularization, enables stable recurrent training without prior multi-stage schedules. (2) Under controlled comparisons, our 1.4B LoopLM outperforms a parameter-matched dense model trained on the same data and token budget on all 12 evaluated benchmarks, including +14 points on GSM8K, +10 on MATH, and +22 on DROP. At matched inference compute, it approaches a 3.9B dense model on mathematical reasoning and reading comprehension while using only 36\% as many parameters. (3) We introduce a minimal recipe for converting pretrained dense models into looped ones: a single learned input-mixing scalar and a smoothed exit loss, with no step-specific parameters. Applied to Qwen3-1.7B-Base, Looped Qwen improves over an identically continued dense baseline on every evaluated benchmark across two data regimes, with statistically clear gains on GSM8K, MATH, and MMLU-Pro on the curated mixture. Together, these results make looped language models substantially cheaper to train from scratch and practical to introduce into existing pretrained checkpoints, while isolating the gains due to recurrence itself.
  </details>

- **[SocialMemBench: Are AI Memory Systems Ready for Social Group Settings?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17789)**  `arXiv:2605.17789`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Olukunle Owolabi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for AI assistants were built for single-user dialogue, a design mismatched to multi-party social group settings. This matters for the social assistants being built today: group-acting agents in chat platforms, and proactive personal assistants whose model of a user must include their social context. Existing memory benchmarks evaluate dyadic or workplace dialogue; none targets social groups, where memory must anchor facts in shared history rather than professional roles, separate group norms from individual exceptions, and attribute correctly even after a member leaves.We introduce SocialMemBench, a benchmark of human-verified synthetic social group networks across five archetypes and three size tiers (4-30 members), with 430 personas and 1,031 QA pairs across nine question categories. Our evaluation exposes five failure modes, framed as testable hypotheses, and we provide evidence on two through the research probes Subject-Mem and SMG.Our results support three connected takeaways. First, social-group memory must preserve distinctions that ordinary fact recall misses, such as who dissented from a decision and what one member knows about another. Second, across 43 networks the four evaluated frameworks (Mem0, LangMem, Graphiti, Cognee) score 0.12-0.18, well below uncompressed retrieval (0.346) and full context (0.370) with the same answerer, and changing the answerer or retrieval budget does not close the gap. Third, explicitly representing social information mitigates specific failure modes but leaves others open: Subject-Mem targets single-stream conflation, lifting attribution from 0.25-0.46 to 0.78, and SMG targets norm-individual conflation, lifting group-decision accuracy to 0.70, while the other three modes remain open. We release the dataset, the evaluation pipeline with both research probes, and the audit viewer as a testbed for social-memory research.
  </details>

- **[BAGEL: Benchmarking Animal Knowledge Expertise in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16241)**  `arXiv:2604.16241`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiacheng Shen, Masato Hagiwara, Milad Alizadeh, Ellen Gilsenan-McMahon, Marius Miron, David Robinson, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models perform well on broad knowledge benchmarks, yet their animal-domain competence remains poorly characterized under a consistent protocol. We introduce BAGEL (\textbf{B}enchmarking \textbf{A}nimal knowled\textbf{G}e \textbf{E}xpertise in \textbf{L}anguage models), a 6,822-question multiple-choice benchmark drawn from Wikipedia, Global Biotic Interactions (GloBI), and bioRxiv. It spans encyclopedic species knowledge, ecological interaction inference, and interpretation of animal-focused preprints. Questions are generated automatically and screened by GPT-5.5 using a rubric calibrated on 150 expert-reviewed items; this process is not exhaustive human adjudication. We evaluate 14 models without external retrieval, using model parameters and question-local evidence. Accuracy varies sharply by source and question type: frontier models that excel on Wikipedia and bioRxiv remain comparatively weak on GloBI participant identification, where pooled errors reveal ecological role confusion. A 100-item post-release bioRxiv probe provides preliminary evidence that GPT-5.4's literature performance is not entirely due to verbatim recall, without ruling out broader training exposure. Proprietary checkpoints lead the best open-weight systems overall, but aggregate scores incompletely characterize animal-domain competence.
  </details>

- **[The Token Tax: Systematic Bias in Multilingual Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.05486)**  `arXiv:2509.05486`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jessica M. Lundin, Ada Zhang, Nihal Karim, Hamza Louzan, Victor Wei, David Adelani, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization inefficiency is associated with structural disadvantages for morphologically complex, low-resource languages, inflating compute resources and reducing accuracy. We evaluate 11 large language models (LLMs) on AfriMMLU (5 subjects; 16 African languages); for the 10 models whose fertility we could compute, higher token fertility is associated with lower accuracy: all 50 model--subject slopes are negative, 24 are significant at p<0.05, and 12 remain significant after false-discovery-rate correction. We further find that reasoning models (e.g., DeepSeek R1, o1) have the highest average accuracy and the smallest English-African gaps among the models evaluated. In terms of economics, a doubling in tokens results in quadrupled training cost and time, underscoring the "token tax" faced by many languages. These results motivate morphologically aware tokenization, fair pricing, and multilingual benchmarks for equitable natural language processing (NLP).
  </details>

- **[Look Back, Draft Ahead: Corrective Prefix Attention for Parallel Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31112)**  `arXiv:2611.31112`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mohammad Mahdi Moradi, Ali Pourranjbar, Zongzuo Wang, Mingming Zhu, Ben Feng, Boxing Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel speculative decoders propose an entire token block at once, yet one early inconsistency can cause the target to reject its remaining suffix. We introduce Corrective Prefix Attention (CPA), which combines prefix-aware selection with autoregressive supervision to improve these parallel drafts. CPA lets each candidate attend to earlier choices within the block. Fixed candidate shortlists allow all pairwise interactions to be computed before selection, reducing the sequential walk to table lookups, accumulation and lightweight scoring rather than repeated neural evaluations. An auxiliary autoregressive loss trains the shared backbone on clean prefixes without adding an inference pass. Across eight math, code and dialogue benchmarks with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B targets on Ascend NPUs, CPA commits 13--15\% more tokens per verification round than DSpark and 25--26\% more than DFlash. Its mean speedups reach $3.35\times$ and $3.37\times$ under greedy decoding and $2.86\times$ under sampling for both targets. Ablations support both components, while capacity sweeps demonstrate that greater acceptance alone does not guarantee faster decoding. These results motivate correcting parallel proposals with causal information while keeping sequential computation inexpensive.
  </details>

- **[Empirical Margin Sensitivity: An Interventional Neuron-Level Probe of Post-Trained Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30675)**  `arXiv:2611.30675`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aniruddh Shreyas, Aarnav Kembhavi, Minal Moharir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical post-training raises benchmark accuracy, but accuracy does not show which internal components a model's decisions depend on, or whether components that support one benchmark also support another. We apply the weight-space intervention paradigm of ROME and MEMIT at the granularity of one MLP down-projection column. Our measurement, Empirical Margin Sensitivity (EMS), replaces each coordinate magnitude of a column by its mean while preserving the column's sign pattern and L1 scale, and records the resulting change in the logit margin between the correct option and its hardest-wrong competitor. This isolates the margin's dependence on a column's fine-grained coordinate structure while the column stays in place. We discover high-EMS columns (anchors) on MedQA in ten public checkpoints (four general-purpose bases and six medical post-trained variants) across the Gemma, Llama, Qwen, and AFM families, and measure the same columns on MedMCQA and PubMedQA. Mean-ablating each discovered anchor set lowers MedQA accuracy in all six post-trained models, by 6 to 28 points. In the two pure-SFT checkpoints, crushing anchors raises the PubMedQA margin on correctly answered items (negative EMS), and anchors with this property sit significantly deeper than the same model's other anchors; the four checkpoints trained with RL or mixed recipes show positive PubMedQA EMS and no significant depth shift. The depth association recurs in each of three held-out checkpoints. Because the checkpoints differ in data and pipeline as well as objective, we report these as per-checkpoint observations.
  </details>

- **[DUET: Dual-MDL Unsupervised Entropy-Trie Segmentation for Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30273)**  `arXiv:2611.30273`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaotian Wang, Youyuan Lin, Zhan Shen, Hitomi Yanaka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Morphological segmentation, the task of decomposing words into constituent morphemes, becomes particularly challenging under extreme data scarcity, a regime that can arise in language documentation and linguistic reasoning. This difficulty stems from existing approaches' reliance on annotated data, external resources, or recurring patterns that emerge only in larger corpora. To address this limitation, we propose Dual-MDL Unsupervised Entropy-Trie Segmentation (DUET), a deterministic unsupervised algorithm that decouples structural candidate generation from statistical candidate ranking. DUET combines branching entropy with recursive paradigm discovery to generate linguistically plausible candidate segmentations, then ranks them using a dual minimum description length (MDL) criterion: a corpus-level objective rewards morpheme reuse, while a construction-tree objective guards against over-segmentation. On the SIGMORPHON 2023 interlinear glossing shared task, DUET outperforms unsupervised baselines in Morpheme F1 under both the standard and simulated extremely low-resource settings, while providing broader candidate coverage.
