Augmenting prompts with DUET segmentations improves performance on a linguistic reasoning benchmark, yielding the best overall results among the evaluated segmentation methods.
  </details>

- **[The Answer Is Not the State: Trajectory-Level Corrigibility in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29937)**  `arXiv:2611.29937`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junchi Liao, Wentao Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models generate tokens at many positions on a shared canvas, making unfinished responses appear revisable. We find a counterexample: after the final answer is reopened, previously generated rationale can reconstruct the old decision despite newly available evidence. We isolate this effect by comparing reopening the answer alone with reopening both rationale and answer, and by exchanging rationales between paired trajectories. On a 150-question source-disjoint benchmark, reopening both regions makes decisions follow the evidence currently present--whether correct or deliberately corrupted--across Dream, LLaDA, and iLLaDA. On a new 100-question free-form short-answer evaluation, the same effect remains strong in LLaDA and iLLaDA but is negligible in Dream. Coherent transplanted rationales steer rebuilt decisions, while token-level attribution improves which positions to reopen without producing a compact repair. Corrigibility therefore depends on retained rationale state, not answer openness alone.
  </details>

- **[GlobalHRBench: Does Changing the Question Language Change the Legal Answer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29852)**  `arXiv:2611.29852`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuding Wang, Yuhang Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language matters when deploying LLM agents across borders, especially in law, where the governing rules must hold across working languages. Does changing the language of a legal question change answer accuracy? We introduce GlobalHRBench, pairing 240 employment-law problems across eight jurisdictions and eight languages while fixing facts, applicable law and required conclusions. We evaluate nine models on all 1,920 prompts in independent, closed-book, single-answer trials, scoring legal correctness, completeness and answer-language compliance. GPT-6.1 Sol leads at 76.2% strict passes, with a 5.8% relative best-to-worst language decline, compared with 42.2% for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. Kimi K3.1 Pro and Claude Sonnet 5.5 reverse order between English and Chinese. A four-model study on 16 paired families compares closed-book answers with governing evidence, added cross-jurisdiction distractors and removal of a governing passage. Governing evidence corrects legal errors and shortens reasoning on recovered answers, yet recognizing an evidence gap does not ensure correct application of the rules that remain. These findings motivate multilingual training for accurate, efficient and evidence-supported answers, and deployment evaluation in the user's working language.
  </details>

- **[ComplexIF: A Challenging Multi-Turn, Multi-Constraint Instruction-Following Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29775)**  `arXiv:2611.29775`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhichao Xu, Bingsen Chen, Yawei Wang, Tao Li, Haoyu Wang, Dan Roth, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As frontier LLMs saturate existing instruction-following benchmarks, measuring further progress requires harder tests that remain faithful to real use. In practice, users often combine several constraints, add them at different turns of a conversation, and expect all of them to hold in the assistant's response. We introduce ComplexIF, a challenging benchmark for this setting, together with the automatic pipeline that generates it. The pipeline expands seed requests into multi-turn conversations, weaves constraints into their turns, and keeps only tasks that a strong model can solve, so difficulty can be raised by adding constraints or turns while every task stays solvable. ComplexIF's two splits test complementary sides of instruction following: a verifiable split of format constraints, checked by programs, and a rubric split of semantic instructions, such as a tone or an audience, checked with an LLM judge. Across 14 proprietary and open-weight models, even the strongest satisfies every constraint on fewer than half of the verifiable tasks, and no model leads on both splits.
  </details>

- **[Diversity in Unity, Theory in Practice: Hierarchical Multitask Benchmarks for Chinese Minority Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29635)**  `arXiv:2611.29635`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yijie Li, Xi Cao, Yuan Sun, Quulgan Minggad, Abdulla Ablikim, JiaQingCaiWang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the rapid advancement of LLMs, their performance on linguistically and culturally diverse minority languages within a unified national context remains underexplored. We present CMiLBench, a collection of hierarchical multitask benchmarks designed to translate theoretical notions of "diversity in unity" into practical evaluation for three representative Chinese minority languages: Tibetan, Mongolian, and Uyghur. CMiLBench comprises 24,663 instances across 5 difficulty levels and 17 tasks spanning foundational ability, cultural specificity, and safety alignment. We adopt existing dataset adaptation, minority knowledge construction, and high-resource benchmark translation to construct CMiLBench. We assess 14 state-of-the-art commercial and open-source LLMs with a hybrid framework that integrates automatic metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge scoring. The comparative experimental results reveal the gap between theoretical capability and practical utility. CMiLBench serves as a foundational and scalable evaluation resource to bridge the digital language divide and promote the informatization and intelligentization of low-resource Chinese minority languages.
  </details>

- **[CompareDR: Benchmarking Deep Research Reports from Individual Quality to Comparative Merit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29549)**  `arXiv:2611.29549`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wanli Wu, He Zhu, Yue Xu, Haolin Ren, Yan Chen, XunLiang Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research systems can produce reports with different evidence, analyses, and conclusions for the same query. A reader deciding which findings to rely on needs to know how well each report meets the task requirements and which differences matter to the answer. A score for a single report can summarize its quality, but it does not identify which contributions make one report more useful than another. We introduce CompareDR, a benchmark that combines task-specific pointwise audits with guideline-based pairwise comparisons. We use passage-linked report representations to locate relevant differences and selective evidence verification to check claims that could affect the judgment. The benchmark comprises 40 Chinese and English tasks from three existing benchmarks, 360 reports from nine systems, 1,099 human-reviewed evaluation criteria, and human preferences elicited through blinded comparisons of 80 report pairs. The hybrid evaluator matches 69 of these 80 preferences (86.25%, Cohen's $\kappa=0.746$). The benchmark supports both comparisons of research systems and examination of the evidence and analyses behind those judgments.
  </details>

- **[Meddies-PII: A Multilingual Framework for Personally Identifiable Information Extraction in Clinical De-identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29491)**  `arXiv:2611.29491`  `cs.CL`  
  _Le Linh Uyen, Christian Hoang, Ho Dinh Tri, Huy Hoang Ha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual PII extraction in clinical text is limited by scarce governed data and by synthetic corpora whose construction is thinly documented. We present a large multilingual dataset covering seventeen languages, including seven Southeast Asian languages, and nine PII labels. We also document a detailed generation pipeline and release the complete prompts, catalogues, checks and label maps. A compact 350M-parameter BIOES token classifier trained on the resource achieves the highest score on all fifteen public benchmarks, significantly ahead of the strongest of four public systems on fourteen of them, with a mean exact-match F1 of 0.827 against 0.672 for the strongest system. We also tested it, without adaptation, on multilingual benchmarks, including two human-annotated German and Spanish clinical-style corpora, where it is competitive with the best public systems once annotation conventions are aligned, and is best in four of ten MultiGraSCCo languages; the results show that performance transfers across public benchmarks. We also analyse the data and find almost no near-duplicates and no text overlap between training and any evaluation set. The dataset, benchmark, model, generation framework and evaluation code are planned for public release upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[GraphSeer: Multimodal Knowledge Graph Relational Image Retrieval with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29470)**  `arXiv:2611.29470`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linyu Li, Zhi Jin, Yuanpeng He, Haoran Duan, Dongming Jin, Wenchun Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal knowledge graphs (MMKGs) store entities with photos and names and link them by facts. MMKG methods predict links between known entity IDs, and image retrievers, from CLIP to MLLM embedders, return entities that look like a query photo or match its description. Neither answers a common question about a new photo: which graph entity is related to the one shown, such as the team of a pictured player or the city of a landmark. We call this knowledge graph relational image retrieval (KRIR): given a photo and a natural-language relation, retrieve the related MMKG entity, which rarely resembles the photo, whether the depicted entity is in the graph (known) or not (unseen). We build three benchmarks from YAGO, Wikidata and in-the-wild OVEN photos. The task hides two hops: recognising which graph entity is shown, and recalling what it is connected to. GraphSeer bridges them inside one multimodal LLM: it links the photo to the graph, reads the facts of the best match, and retrieves with the hidden state at which the model is about to say the answer. An unseen entity has no facts of its own, but every candidate answer does, so a listwise verifier reads the other end of the graph: it re-ranks the top candidates by their facts and photos in one forward pass, and the facts matter far more than the photos. With Qwen3.5-27B, GraphSeer improves MRR on known entities by 24.2-70.0% over an MLLM embedder fine-tuned on the same data with the same budget. When it also fuses and verifies the embedder's candidates, it improves MRR on unseen entities by 12.5-25.6%, and on known entities it beats KG-completion models given the ground-truth entity ID.
  </details>

- **[Can Speech LLMs Recognize When Audio Evidence Is Insufficient? A Diagnostic Study and a Pre-Generation Probing Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29393)**  `arXiv:2611.29393`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vladislav Nesterenko, Polina Doronicheva, Olga Golubeva, Polina Sheviakova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speech large language models are usually evaluated on questions that have answers. We ask what they do when a recording establishes none, and whether the standard way of finding out is sound. Diagnosed on synthesized speech the answer looks clear: Qwen2-Audio fabricates an answer to 92.5% of unanswerable questions, while an ASR cascade reading the same recordings reaches 2.5% hallucination at 6.7% over-refusal, placing the failure in reasoning rather than in perception. The identical protocol on 376 native SQuAD 2.0 questions over human-read recordings removes that conclusion: the cascade hallucinates on 67.7% against the end-to-end model's 69.8%, at a higher over-refusal cost. A paired subset asking the same questions of a synthesized and a human recording of each passage localises the effect in the ASR stage -- the end-to-end model's abstention behaviour does not move with the speaker, the cascade's goes from 11.1% to 65.9%. Reliance on synthesized speech has been named as a limitation of audio benchmarks but not quantified; we quantify it, and show it large enough to reverse a finding. Since no prompt we tested repairs the residual failure, we transfer pre-generation attention probing to the multimodal setting. A probe over frozen prompt representations reaches 0.773 AUROC at predicting which unanswerable questions the model will answer anyway, where semantic entropy over ten sampled generations is at chance: the model fabricates the same answer every time, so sample disagreement cannot see a failure it is confident about.
  </details>

- **[HyperHarness: Learning to Generate Personalized Agent Harnesses from User Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29062)**  `arXiv:2611.29062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhaoxuan Tan, Jiachen Zhao, Jingbo Yang, Zheyuan Liu, Chuhan Wang, Pei Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized LLM agents rely on an agent harness, the surrounding memory and control code, to connect user history with downstream decisions. Existing methods typically employ either a single shared memory workflow across all users, which is efficient to deploy but cannot adapt how evidence is retrieved and interpreted across diverse histories, or per-user harness optimization, which customizes behavior for each user but requires costly repeated candidate evaluation at deployment and suffers from sparse, noisy supervision in raw logs. To address this trade-off, we present HyperHarness, a framework that learns a shared history-to-harness generator to synthesize executable, user-specific retrieval and answer-guidance code directly from user histories. Given a user's permitted history, HyperHarness prompts a construction LLM to write personalized retrieval and guidance functions, while downstream task scores across training users supervise iterative refinements to the shared generator without reference harnesses or answering-model updates. At deployment, the frozen generator constructs a harness for each unseen user in a single pass from history alone, eliminating per-user task-scored search. Across three benchmarks (PersonaMemv3, Mem-PAL, and MemoryCD) and three answering models (Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.8-27B, and GPT-5.6-Luna), HyperHarness achieves the highest aggregate score in seven of nine settings and ranks second in the remaining two, improving over the strongest baselines by up to 5.95 points while cutting matched answer-time tokens by 90.32% over long-context prompting. Analyses show that optimization tailors context allocation to each executor, transfers to unseen user clusters, and amortizes adaptation costs.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Collaborative Agents in Group Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29036)**  `arXiv:2611.29036`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhaoxuan Tan, Mengting Wan, Jingbo Yang, Kwei-Herng Lai, Renyi Qu, Lorenzo Jaime Flores, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaborative agents joining an ongoing team rely on shared context to connect multi-party interaction histories with downstream coordination decisions. Existing work typically focuses either on retrieving historical facts from conversation logs, which surfaces isolated evidence without capturing who knows or owns what, or on evaluating end-to-end interactions, which conflates preparation with execution and lacks direct supervision because a team's history has no single ground-truth summary. To bridge this gap, we formulate group briefing, the task of reconstructing task-relevant group cognition across participants' expressed beliefs, group dynamics, and grounding gaps, and introduce a four-domain benchmark of 1,351 requests and 9,975 verified reference points paired with a two-stage evaluation pipeline combining reference-point coverage and downstream collaboration simulation. Using this pipeline, we show that GroupBrief, a simple task-guided approach that iteratively probes the message history and synthesizes a structured brief without offline memory indexing or graph construction, outperforms ten retrieval and agent-memory baselines in overall coverage with gpt-5.4-mini, while benchmarking nine language models reveals that prospective friction remains harder to recover than past state across model scales. We further use reference-point coverage as a feedback signal for harness optimization, refining GroupBrief's retrieval and synthesis instructions while keeping model weights frozen. On held-out Test projects, GroupBrief (optimized) improves coverage by 2.54 percentage points for gpt-5.4-mini and 12.88 for gpt-5.6-luna, achieving 64.46% and 68.42% preference over no brief in downstream simulations. These results establish our benchmark and evaluation pipeline as a foundation for measuring and optimizing how collaborative agents ground in group cognition.
  </details>

- **[EmoRouter: Adaptive Modality Cascading for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28960)**  `arXiv:2611.28960`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fangxu Yu, Lingzhi Yuan, Dehai Min, Tao Feng, Ge Liu, Furong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) involves detecting the underlying emotion behind each utterance within a conversation, which is essential for emotionally aware human-computer interaction. While omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) demonstrate strong capability for MER by jointly processing modalities including text, audio, and vision, different modality combinations yield different prediction outcomes across utterances: acoustic and visual inputs can help recover annotated labels missed by text-only inference, but can also turn correct predictions into errors. Processing all modalities for every utterance is also computationally expensive. To this end, we introduce EmoRouter, a training-free, model-agnostic framework for adaptive modality cascading that selects an exit tier for each utterance along a predefined modality order. The routing is guided by prediction-uncertainty estimates derived from Conformal Prediction. EmoRouter routes each utterance through a sequence of increasing-cost modality levels and exits at the first level when the model is confident in the emotion. To determine when to exit, we propose Conformal Set Contraction, a structural calibration criterion that calibrates the per-tier CP error budgets used for these exit decisions. Comprehensive evaluations on 4 MER benchmarks and 3 Omni-LLMs reveal that EmoRouter matches or outperforms the full modalities while reducing inference latency by 67.9--94.5%. Furthermore, EmoRouter demonstrates high transferability across different models, validating its robustness. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Legal Is Not Good: A Pre-Registered Benchmark of Low-Cost Language and Decision Models on a New Strategy Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28713)**  `arXiv:2611.28713`  `cs.CL`  
  _Federico Lucca_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Measure Before You Train argued that an agent's decisions should be measured against ground truth, under a protocol fixed in advance, before anyone trains or replaces the model behind them. We carry the method to a second, very different domain, a turn-based strategy game, and ask a deliberately modest question: how well do low-cost agents play under a pre-registered protocol? hexfolk is a minimal two-player 4X game written for this purpose, so no model has seen its rules or positions. A deterministic engine, one agent interface, a referee that never corrects an illegal or late move, and per-decision logs make every game replayable; questions and expected answers were committed before the games, on held-out seeds with mirrored seats and 95% Wilson intervals. Two small open language models (8B and 3B) lose every game to a few-line rule player. Answering in free text they are invalid on 98% of their decisions; constrained to legal moves they never play an invalid one, yet they lose to the uniformly random player (the 8B model wins 10.0 [4.3, 21.4]% of games), refuting our expectation that they would beat it; the result survives a control that shuffles the order of the options. Two native decision models never play an invalid move and both beat random; one loses all 50 games to the rule player, the other is not distinguishable from it in 50 games and beats a one-step search. The paid agents make tens to over a hundred model calls per game; against the same opponents a free 60 ms-per-move search, which makes none, outplays every paid model by the full range of the scale. Two 7-8B models served locally behave the same way. No frontier model is included.
  </details>

- **[BIRD-Persona: A Persona-Aware Benchmark for SQL and pandas Analytics Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28542)**  `arXiv:2611.28542`  `cs.CL`  
  _Davin Hill, Pawan Chorasiya, Bhawani S. Leelar, Oren Avram, Robert E. Tillman, Tamer Soliman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Production text-to-code systems use large language models to turn natural-language analytics requests into executable SQL and pandas code. Existing benchmarks cover both tasks but do not isolate when performance diverges between them. We introduce BIRD-Persona, a persona-stratified extension of BIRD-Python that adds novice and expert rewrites to tasks with matched SQL and pandas ground truth, along with schema variants and task-structure annotations. Across 1,534 tasks, nine models, and 245,592 evaluations, SQL outperforms pandas for eight of nine systems. The gap varies by condition: expert, schema-explicit prompts improve both paradigms but help SQL more; schema representations have mixed, model-dependent effects; and selected-column burden widens the gap while subqueries do not. By making user phrasing a controlled benchmark dimension, BIRD-Persona supports persona-aware and paradigm-aware evaluation of natural-language analytics systems. The dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Muted Distillation: Sealing Reasoning from Teacher Unlocks Token-Efficient Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28445)**  `arXiv:2611.28445`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuwei Zhang, Ian Miao, Wentao Zhang, Chen Qu, Bill Bi, Jingbo Shang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is commonly adopted as one of the components in post-training of large language models (LLMs), but in multi-turn agentic tasks we find that the student model increases their reasoning lengths after training. Our empirical analysis shows that the reasoning token lengths is affected by how much the student can distract the teacher's scores. We introduce Muted Distillation (MD), a simple variant of OPD that hides the student's reasoning from the teacher, so the teacher judges what the student does without the interference of student's reasonings. MD takes a few lines of code and adds no extra data or hyperparameters. On the knowledge-work benchmarks OfficeQA Pro and SpreadsheetBench, it reduces output tokens per episode by 57\% and 53\% compared with standard OPD, making the student more concise than it was before training. At the same time, it matches OPD's accuracy on OfficeQA Pro and improves pass@1 by 4.8 points on SpreadsheetBench.
  </details>

- **[Typhoon OCR 2: From Thai Document Transcription to Multilingual, Multi-Task OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27494)**  `arXiv:2611.27494`  `cs.CL`  
  _Surapon Nonesung, Natapong Nitarach, Sittipong Sripaisarnmongkol, Kunat Pipatanakul, Warit Sirichotedumrong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typhoon OCR 1.5 focused on reading Thai documents. However, in real-world workflows, users often need to extract specific information or preserve the structure of an entire page for further processing. Typhoon OCR 2 handles both tasks in a single model, with prompt-driven extraction and structured full-page parsing. Full-page parsing covers Thai, English, and Chinese. We release compact 2B and 0.8B models adapted from open document parsers through supervised fine-tuning on curated Thai and public multilingual data, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with task rewards and a rule penalizing unfinished responses. Among evaluated open models, Typhoon OCR 2 2B scores highest on Thai MDPBench (80.7 versus 68.9 for Typhoon OCR 1.5) and on ThaiOCRBench (59.91 versus 40.45), which covers recognition, extraction, and document understanding. The 2B model remains close to its base on English olmOCR-bench (84.7 versus 84.4) and on OmniDocBench, which evaluates English and Chinese together (90.97 versus 91.03). On MDPBench, GRPO reduces the mean share of pages with repetition loops for the 2B model from 3.2% to 0.7%, with a smaller and statistically uncertain score gain for the 0.8B model. We release the model weights, Python package, and evaluation suite.
  </details>

- **[Catch It Where It Happens: Real-Time Error Correction During LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13107)**  `arXiv:2611.13107`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rotem Elimelech, Michael Shalyt, Ido Kaminer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted with mathematical, scientific and engineering work, yet their mistakes are often confidently written and hard to spot. A single wrong step tends to propagate, making the model waste inference tokens following a false premise. Existing safeguards either check only the final answer and restart from scratch, or rely on a verifier LLM that competes for GPU capacity and slows down response time. We propose online, deterministic verification: many kinds of reasoning steps can be validated by code, so a step can be verified in real time, as it is written, without any model call or GPU use. Whenever a check fails, we point out the mistake and let the model continue from the same spot. We demonstrate this approach on symbolic mathematics, where derivations already contain explicit expressions that a computer algebra system can check in milliseconds. Tested on the ASyMoB benchmark with three open-weights models, our online validation improves accuracy by 1.4-5.6 percentage points, corresponding to a 1.8-14.9% net reduction in incorrect answers, while increasing token usage by only 6-7%. Because the verifier is independent of the model, the approach complements progress in LLMs rather than competing with it: every new model can benefit immediately, and the benefit grows as deterministic checkers extend to more kinds of reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Towards Foundation Decision Models: Drex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07854)**  `arXiv:2611.07854`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nischay Dhankhar, Alisher Amantay, Zhanibek Datbayev, Darkhan Akhmetov, Yash Shah, Nurmukhamed Ubaidullayev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive approaches with large language models, such as long chain-of-thought reasoning, have become the default for new AI applications because they reach higher accuracy on a wide range of tasks. In production, however, they give inconsistent answers, require parsing of outputs, and add cost and latency. Decision models address this: given a question and a set of options supplied by the user, they return a probability distribution over the options without autoregressive decoding. We present Drex, a family of four open decision models with 8B (DLM), 9B (v1.5), 35B with 3B active (v2.0), and 27B (Max) parameters. Each request consists of a state (a document, record, or agent context, optionally with images) and questions about it, each asking for a choice among options, a yes/no judgment, or a score on an ordered scale. Training combines supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning from adjusted feedback (RLAF), plus vision tuning for Max. Ablations show that RLAF improves accuracy and that consistency losses improve robustness to option order. On Decision Index 0.4.1, Max sets a new state of the art among the decision models we compare (67.52 on the public suite vs. 60.70 for Jev 1.14.0). On Decision Bench v4, it is statistically tied with d3-turbo and GPT-6 Sol and trails Claude Opus 5.5 by 1.6 points, and it has the highest accuracy among decision models on DecideBench. Max answers a typical request in 183 ms, against about 2.0 s for GPT-6 Sol and 3.5 s for Claude Opus 5.5, and its outputs are stable under option reordering. All four models are open source:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CivWorldBench: Evaluating Language World Models in Structured Multi-Agent Sandbox Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16450)**  `arXiv:2610.16450`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ang Li, Haofei Yu, Zhengwei Tao, Liang Chen, Kuan Li, Miaomiao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing use of language models for forecasting motivates a broader evaluation of their world-model capabilities. Such evaluation must go beyond predictive accuracy to assess hidden-state inference and intervention prediction. Real-world forecasting benchmarks offer limited access to hidden-state ground truth and controlled experiments. We introduce CivWorldBench, a benchmark built on a multi-agent strategy game engine with recorded internal states, controlled observations, and paired intervention rollouts. Its 1,900 decision points from 475 worlds support three task levels: association forecasting, belief inference, and intervention prediction. Reference predictors support attributable evaluation of capability gaps, with an expert harness and empirical ceiling estimates contextualizing scores. Stored trajectories make intermediate predictions verifiable, while agent-play experiments test whether world-model capabilities support decision-making. The main panel comprises eleven frontier language models. Composite benchmark scores correlate with real-world forecasting leaderboards on matched model panels, more closely than with a general-capability index, supporting its use as a proxy for real-world forecasting capability. Strong association forecasts do not consistently extend to belief inference or intervention prediction, and effect magnitudes remain poorly calibrated despite informative direction predictions. Process verification reveals errors missed by final-answer evaluation, and structured reasoning improves intervention prediction for the strongest model in the process study. Agent-play experiments identify useful assisted plans and motivate evaluating decision-relevant capabilities beyond association forecasting. These findings identify hidden-state inference, intermediate-state prediction, and effect calibration as priorities for world-model training.
  </details>

- **[The Labeling Problem in Hallucination Detection Benchmarks: An Empirical Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08026)**  `arXiv:2610.08026`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jorma Valjakka, Juhani Kivimäki, Juha Mylläri, Jukka K. Nurminen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, several methods for detecting when large language models (LLMs) hallucinate have been developed. These methods are often benchmarked with open-domain question answering (QA) datasets containing questions and corresponding short reference answers. First, an LLM is used to generate answers to questions within the QA dataset. Then, some automated labeling strategy is used to label these answers as hallucinated or not by comparing them with the reference answers in the dataset. This evaluation setting creates a methodological ambiguity between two criteria: reference faithfulness (whether the answer is fully supported by the reference) and factual correctness (whether the answer is free from contradictions and factually false specific claims). In practice, automated labelers may apply the former criterion even when the intended target is the latter. We study this potential criterion mismatch using 900 human-labeled question-answer pairs spanning three commonly used QA datasets and three generator models, with labels targeting answer-level factual correctness. We evaluate lexical similarity metrics, a reference-entailment NLI baseline, and seven LLM judges under controlled prompt variants as automated labelers. Our experiments reveal substantial disagreement both among automated labeling strategies and between these labels and human annotations. Many strategies also exhibit strong directional error biases, and for most judge-generator pairs, replacing a faithfulness-oriented prompt with a factual-correctness prompt improves agreement with human annotations and reduces false-positive dominance, indicating that automated hallucination labels depend strongly on how the target criterion is specified. Label-source choice should therefore be considered a fundamental part of benchmark design and made explicit, validated, and matched with the benchmark goal.
  </details>

- **[Can Language Models Learn to Forecast Stock Prices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36914)**  `arXiv:2609.36914`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Guo, Suozhi Huang, Shuzhen Li, Yunlong Gao, Zerui Cheng, Jason Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training has been shown to significantly improve language models' performance on tasks with verifiable outcomes, including mathematical reasoning, software engineering, and computer use. However, whether the same approach can improve forecasting in financial markets is much less clear. Compared with tasks with verifiable outcomes, not only are realized returns noisy, but even what constitutes a relevant information set for making effective predictions is not obvious a priori: the model must decide which observations to gather and then commit to a numerical judgment before the outcome is known. We study this question in a chronological stock-price sandbox, where a language model gathers price, volume, relative-performance, and market-context evidence and predicts a future return. We post-train Qwen3-4B with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on tool-use demonstrations, then proximal policy optimization (PPO) with a terminal reward given by the forecast score against the realized return. The resulting AURA-4B more than doubles the starting direction--magnitude score, from 20.94 to 43.31, and is comparable to frontier language models on this benchmark. Conditional magnitude agreement rises from 33.3 to 66.2, while directional accuracy changes from 62.9 to 65.4. SFT expands tool use, and PPO further increases the share of ranking and market-context queries. These results show that post-training can substantially improve financial forecasting performance, together with changes in how the model investigates the market, on this outcome-selected benchmark.
  </details>

- **[From Hidden States to Stated Confidence: Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33290)**  `arXiv:2609.33290`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yadong Xi, Rongsheng Zhang, Tangjie Lv, Ziyang Luo, Ruochen Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with binary correctness rewards trains correctness, not calibrated confidence. The confidence that reasoning models verbalize is overconfident and tracks how willing a model is to commit to an answer rather than how likely the answer is to be right, so post-hoc rescaling rarely transfers. We look inside the model instead. On factual question answering, a linear probe on the hidden state between the chain of thought and the answer has 5-38x lower expected calibration error (ECE) than the verbalized score across four benchmarks and two model families. Since a probe needs hidden-state access, we introduce probe-guided self-distillation (Probe-SD): the probe scores the model's own sampled traces, each trace's stated confidence is overwritten with that score, and the base checkpoint is finetuned on the result, so the model itself states calibrated confidence at test time. On Qwen3-14B, Probe-SD cuts out-of-domain ECE from 0.542 to 0.113, beats post-hoc recalibration and self-distillation with sample-based labels, and keeps the confidence stated while reasoning consistent with the final score. Its question-level variant, Probe-SD-Q, lowers ECE further to 0.099, and the gains carry over to multiple-choice QA with shuffled options. The probe mainly separates questions the model knows from those it does not. As an answer selector it nearly ties majority voting, so we use it as a confidence, not a selector.
  </details>

- **[Made in Hungary: Comments on the performance of generative language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18284)**  `arXiv:2609.18284`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mátyás Osváth, Enikő Héja, Noémi Ligeti-Nagy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, three initiatives have emerged to develop generative language models in Hungary. The motivation behind them is the same. For Hungarian, no model with the given capability existed, or existing English-centric models offered limited proficiency. A detailed examination of the corresponding studies, however, reveals several methodological limitations. First, the reliability of the evaluation protocols is questionable. Contrary to the findings of Csibi et al. [2026], evaluation under the recommended inference settings shows that Qwen3-4B achieves higher scores than Racka-4B, its Hungarian-adapted version. Data contamination is evident in the work of Yang et al. [2025d] and Szentmihályi et al. [2025], potentially biasing the reported results. Second, the training pipelines fall short of current best practices in corpus curation and data mixture, which risks wasting substantial compute on low-quality data. The lack of controlled ablations prevents reliable assessment of these choices. Third, none of the three papers assessed forgetting or capability loss. Testing the adapted models on a subset of the original benchmarks indicates performance decline in all three cases, especially Racka-4B. These observations emphasize the importance of rigorous experimental design in language model development, given the significant computational and financial costs involved.
  </details>

- **[ResKV: Reconstructing Omitted Attention Contributions for Fixed-Budget KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29591)**  `arXiv:2607.29591`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhang Zhan, Lisi Chen, Shuo Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KV cache compression is essential for efficient long-context inference. Existing eviction methods permanently discard unselected tokens and consequently remove their aggregate contribution to attention. Merging-based alternatives preserve more information but can perturb retained keys and values that should remain exact. We observe that the information omitted by cache eviction can be formulated as residual statistics in both the numerator and denominator of softmax attention. Based on this observation, we propose ResKV, which divides a fixed KV budget into an exact main cache and a compact residual cache that reconstructs the contribution of omitted tokens. ResKV lets main-cache tokens and residual entries participate in the same softmax normalization, so residual entries restore both attention numerator and denominator mass rather than acting as a post-hoc correction. A construction-time validation proxy determines residual allocation for each layer and KV head, while a decode-time dynamic gate adjusts residual contributions for individual queries. Comprehensive evaluations on LongBench and RULER, covering query-aware and query-agnostic settings, multiple backbones, cache budgets, and representative compression baselines, demonstrate broad improvements under the same retained KV budget while preserving the practical efficiency of compressed decoding, including peak memory usage and long-context decode throughput.
  </details>

- **[AURA: Intent-Directed Probing for Implicit-Need Surfacing in Situated LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05557)**  `arXiv:2606.05557`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Li, Jiaxiang Liu, Jiang Cai, Mingkun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A situated query like "where is Lin Wei?" often encodes more than its literal content: the user may also want to know whether Lin Wei is free, in a good mood, or worth interrupting now. Standard tool-use agents answer the literal question and stop. AURA inserts an inference step between scene perception and tool use that produces an IntentFrame: a structured estimate of the implicit need with a scalar gap score that controls per-query probe budget and tool selection. On a 100-query four-scene implicit-intent benchmark, AURA improves implicit-need coverage over ReAct-style probing (Delta = +0.07, p < 10^-6); three of four scenes are individually significant, the gain reproduces on a second backbone, and a prompt ablation attributes the lift to gap calibration rather than answer memorisation. A component ablation localises the gain further: the gap-derived budget ceiling alone outperforms the full architecture (p = 0.02), and bin-wise calibration analysis (r = -0.33) identifies the gap score as a probe trigger rather than a calibrated value estimator. On factual lookup the controller trades raw accuracy for 82% fewer probes and zero forbidden-tool violations on a privacy-sensitive slice; scope conditions are detailed in Limitations. Code, simulator, and benchmark are released atthis https URL, with the implicit-intent benchmark also on the HuggingFace Hub (innovation64/aura-implicit-intent).
  </details>

- **[Decompose, Look, and Reason: Reinforced Latent Reasoning for VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07518)**  `arXiv:2604.07518`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mengdan Zhu, Senhao Cheng, Liang Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models often struggle with complex visual reasoning due to the visual information loss in textual CoT. Existing methods either add the cost of tool calls or rely on localized patch-based embeddings that are insufficient to extract semantics in multi-step reasoning. We propose "Decompose, Look, and Reason" (DLR), a reinforced latent reasoning framework that dynamically decomposes queries into textual premises, extracts premise-conditioned continuous visual latents, and deduces answers through grounded rationales. We introduce a three-stage training pipeline and propose a novel Spherical Gaussian Latent Policy, to enable effective exploration in the latent space. Extensive experiments on vision-centric benchmarks show that DLR consistently outperforms strong baselines, including text-only, interleaved multimodal CoT, and latent reasoning methods, while providing superior stepwise interpretability.
  </details>

- **[The Lexical Imprint of Pre-training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10657)**  `arXiv:2602.10657`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeonghoon Kim, Woojin Chung, Woomin Song, Cheonbok Park, Nako Sung, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How much of zero-shot benchmark accuracy is captured by word-level alignment between a pre-training corpus and the benchmark target? We study this question through word-level unigram cross-entropy (UCE), a tokenizer-agnostic, model-free measure of lexical alignment between a reference corpus and a target benchmark. Likelihood training shapes the model's predictive distribution from the pre-training corpus, and zero-shot scores under likelihood-based evaluation depend in part on the lexical alignment between that corpus and the benchmark target. Cleanly tracing how the lexical imprint of pre-training data scales with corpus--benchmark alignment requires controlled from-scratch pre-training that varies corpus identity while holding the training recipe fixed. Across this controlled sweep, spanning $10$ zero-shot benchmarks, $4$ pre-training corpora, and $3$ model scales, we find that corpus--benchmark lexical alignment, UCE, consistently tracks zero-shot accuracy on every benchmark, with corpora more aligned with the benchmark (lower UCE) achieving higher accuracy. The same signal extends from measurement to control at finer scopes. At the document level, strengthening the imprint by selecting training documents to maximize lexical alignment with the benchmark produces the accuracy gains predicted by the mechanism. At the prompt level, matching the imprint per prompt by routing each prompt to the drafter whose domain corpus is most lexically aligned with it produces measurable throughput gains over a generalist-drafter baseline. Taken together, pre-training corpora leave a measurable lexical imprint on base-model zero-shot behavior, and UCE provides a model-free probe of lexical alignment between reference and target data.
  </details>

- **[BMAM: Brain-inspired Multi-Agent Memory Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20465)**  `arXiv:2601.20465`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Li, Jiaxiang Liu, Yusong Wang, Yujie Wu, Mingkun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model-based agents operating over extended interaction horizons face persistent challenges in preserving temporally grounded information and maintaining behavioral consistency across sessions, a failure mode we term soul erosion. We present BMAM (Brain-inspired Multi-Agent Memory), a general-purpose memory architecture that models agent memory as a set of functionally specialized subsystems rather than a single unstructured store. Inspired by cognitive memory systems, BMAM decomposes memory into episodic, semantic, salience-aware, and control-oriented components that operate at complementary time scales, organised as a six-phase memory lifecycle. To support long-horizon reasoning, BMAM organises episodic memories along explicit timelines and retrieves evidence by fusing multiple complementary signals. Experiments on the LoCoMo benchmark show that BMAM achieves 78.45% accuracy, outperforming seven memory-augmented baselines. Pairwise ablations reveal super-additive synergy between brain-region components rather than redundant stacking, and a Soul Portability Test demonstrates 87.5% identity-integrity across full memory export, clear, and restore. A targeted refinement of the temporal-trigger heuristics raises LongMemEval multi-session accuracy from 45.2% to 56.4%, validating the architectural decomposition behind BMAM. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where Autonomy Belongs: A Separation of Powers for Agentic Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15309)**  `arXiv:2611.15309`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhengyang Ai, Tong Liu, Jingxian Tang, Xiaobing Du, Tao Xiao, Fengshi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven program evolution rests on three decisions: govern directs the search from accumulated evidence, propose constructs the next candidate, and judge settles validity and fitness. All three appear in every system, and existing work splits into two lines over who makes each. Controller-centric systems strengthen govern and neglect the autonomy of propose, which caps what evolution can reach. Agent-centric systems let a coding agent take over both govern and propose, at a cognitive and computational cost. Neither line protects the independent authority of judge, so benchmark reliability suffers. These failures share one root: autonomy is misallocated across the three decisions. We address this problem with EvoTrias, a separation-of-powers architecture in which each decision has one owner: Structured Governance for what spans evolution rounds, Open Proposal for construction inside a task, and Protected Judgment behind a private evaluator. These owners exchange typed contracts only, and runtime hooks reinforce the boundaries. This separation also lets EvoTrias run as a plugin that turns an existing coding agent into an evolution system. EvoTrias sets a new state of the art on mathematical, systems, and algorithm-design benchmarks. Three further results show what each owner buys: separating Governance from Proposal cuts token cost by 64%, Open Proposal extends evolution to a 680K-line industrial repository, and Protected Judgment keeps benchmark scores faithful to transfer.
  </details>

- **[Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning via Epigraph-Form Guided Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07066)**  `arXiv:2611.07066`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Sungil Son, Hoseong Jung, Dahyun Oh, H. Jin Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discovering cooperative behavior in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is challenging due to the combinatorial complexity of joint state-action spaces, which hinders the emergence of coordinated behaviors from trial-and-error alone. Intrinsic rewards are often used to aid discovery, but naively combining them with team objectives can distort the learning signal, compromising task performance. In this paper, we propose EFXPLORER, a constrained exploration framework that maximizes exploration objectives subject to a constraint that preserves established task performance. We solve the resulting problem using an epigraph reformulation that introduces adaptive exploration budgets. This approach separates intrinsic rewards from task objectives and regulates exploration through task feasibility. To further encourage diverse and temporally extended exploration, we incorporate a successor distance-based intrinsic reward that captures long-horizon dependencies. Empirically, our method outperforms strong baselines and induces novel cooperative strategies across SMAX, VMAS, and MPE benchmark suites.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Perfect Teammates: Multi-Agent Collaboration with Unreliable Colleagues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16117)**  `arXiv:2610.16117`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Chen Huang, Yiyang Zhang, Yuhao Wu, Yang Deng, Wenxuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent collaboration is increasingly used to solve complex tasks by dividing work among specialized agents and integrating complementary expertise. Yet existing systems and evaluations largely rest on an implicit perfect-colleague assumption: collaborators provide correct and complete information and respond promptly to requests. In realistic teamwork, however, collaborators may return information that does not answer the intended question, provide insufficient detail to support a decision, or fail to respond to a request. To separate task-solving competence from the ability to recover an unreliable evidence-gathering process, we introduce the Unreliable Colleagues Benchmark (UnCoBench), which organizes behavioral failures through Misaligned, Incomplete, and Missed settings. The main agent need to verify contextual correctness, diagnose information gap and track unresolved dependencies in order to obtain the correct evidence for solving the task. We also introduce a verifiable and scalable automated generation pipeline that pairs LLM-based scenario generation with executable checks of answer uniqueness and role necessity. Results across ten models show that similar reliable-setting performance can result in different robustness to unreliable evidence. Trace analysis reveals frequent omissions of corrective follow-up, and the recovery behavior involves inefficient waiting and redundant queries. These findings show that robust collaboration requires not only sound reasoning, but also targeted action to diagnose evidence failures, recover relevant information, and integrate it into decisions. Our data and source code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Memphis: A Case Study in Seeding an Agent Task from a Model's Real-World Attempt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29824)**  `arXiv:2611.29824`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ibrahim A. Ahmed, Ahmed Sameh Khalil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent benchmarks are usually made harder by scaling up the data their tasks are built from, yet models that do well on them still fail at real work. We propose building simulated tasks from a model's own attempt at an open-ended task in the real world, so that the model's failures in the simulation resemble its failures in reality. As a case study, we had GPT-6.1 Sol manage two human workers in Cairo over chat for five shifts, with a budget of 7,000 Egyptian pounds, to build as many USB desk lamps as it could. It built two. From the real-world attempt's transcript, we build Memphis, an evaluation in a model-simulated world. Memphis is hard: GPT-6.1 Sol delivers 1.0 lamp per trial on average and repeats its real-world mistakes, buying too few stands and giving up the search for them. With a one-page hint on where to buy parts and who can wire them, it delivers 9.2. Claude Opus 5.5 delivers 2.2 without the hint and 4.6 with it. We release the task, the trials, the audits, and a redacted record of the real-world evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Dimension Is Free, Horizon Is Not: When Evolution Strategies Rival Policy Gradients for LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29795)**  `arXiv:2611.29795`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qiyao Liang, Kajetan Schweighofer, Xin Qiu, Risto Miikkulainen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Policy gradient (PG) methods such as GRPO dominate LLM fine-tuning, yet recent work showed that Evolution Strategies (ES) also scale to fine-tuning models with billions of parameters. Which method needs fewer samples, and on which tasks, remains unclear. We view the two methods as estimators of the same gradient that differ in where they explore. Because ES perturbs parameters, its noise grows with the number of parameters, and because PG samples actions, its noise grows with the number of generated tokens. Only noise along the directions that change the reward slows learning. The cost of ES then depends on the effective dimension, the number of independent skills a task requires rather than its parameter count, while the cost of PG depends on the effective horizon, which counts only the tokens that share both credit and weights with the reward-carrying ones. We train both methods on a synthetic task where the number of skills and the effective horizon can be varied separately. PG is cheaper when the horizon is short, ES becomes cheaper once it is long enough, and this crossover moves to longer horizons as the number of skills grows. We then measure the effective horizons of 20 NLP, math reasoning, and agentic tasks and compare the two methods with Qwen2.5-7B. PG leads on short-horizon tasks, the two tie on the moderate-horizon math ones, and no task has a horizon in the regime where the theory predicts an ES advantage. Terminal-Bench has a far longer horizon. There, in a preliminary comparison with Qwen3.5-4B, ES without any credit assignment reaches a higher success rate than the PG checkpoint released with Tmax, as the theory predicts. ES should thus have the advantage on long-horizon tasks with few independent skills and a nonvanishing reward signal. Current benchmarks are only at the onset of this regime, so ES should become more competitive as agentic tasks grow longer.
  </details>

- **[Scalar Tuned Lens Summaries Offer Limited Incremental Value for Mid-Generation Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29589)**  `arXiv:2611.29589`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Khurram Khalil, Khaza Anuarul Hoque_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mid-generation abstention aims to stop an unpromising response before spending its remaining computation budget. Intermediate transformer states may help predict eventual correctness, but whether scalar summaries of their vocabulary predictions add useful evidence is less clear. We compare Tuned Lens entropy and divergence features with final-state and raw intermediate-state probes under matched, causal evaluation. Across eight instruction-tuned models on MATH-500, raw intermediate states improve ranking, whereas scalar-only probes usually trail the final-state baseline. Scalar hybrids have positive exceptions, but their advantage over position-matched permutation controls is small. Cross-fitted residual comparisons further show that gains over a final-state probe need not require aligned scalar features. Actual stopping runs on two model sizes and two benchmarks reveal a separate accuracy--computation trade-off, with token savings overstating wall-time savings for scalar policies. The evidence supports a restricted empirical conclusion about these scalar features, not a general negative claim about intermediate representations.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Another Angle: Rotation-Based Test-Time Training for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28972)**  `arXiv:2611.28972`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Changyu Liu, Fei Zhao, Yiyang Liu, Xi Xiao, Tianyang Wang, Cheng Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on vision-language tasks but often remain vulnerable to distribution shifts due to their fixed parameters during inference. While Test-Time Training (TTT) offers a pathway for dynamic adaptation, existing approaches suffer from prohibitive computational overhead due to multiple sampling and the risk of error reinforcement from biased self-generated supervision. In light of this view, we propose RoTTT (Rotation-based Test-Time Training), a simple and efficient TTT approach that enables instance-specific refinement at inference time. For each test instance, RoTTT performs a small number of gradient updates by predicting the rotation angle of the input image. The known rotation angles naturally serve as a reliable self-supervised signal, enabling lightweight and stable adaptation without requiring additional annotations. Extensive experiments across multiple vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that RoTTT consistently improves performance across different MLLMs, providing a practical and efficient paradigm for on-the-fly MLLM adaptation.
  </details>

- **[STEdgeFormer: MCU and NPU Deployable Transformer Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28935)**  `arXiv:2611.28935`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sudhakar Sah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based sequence models have achieved strong performance in language and time-series modeling, but self-attention remains computationally and memory intensive for deployment on resource-constrained edge accelerators. We propose STEdgeFormer, a lightweight, hardware-aware sequence architecture designed for efficient execution on embedded neural accelerators. STEdgeFormer combines polynomial mixture layers and DyT normalization with an accelerator-aware causal memory block, replacing pairwise self-attention with compact causal aggregation. On TinyStories, the fully NPU-supported STEdgeFormer (MW) achieves a perplexity of 7.39 with 4.12M parameters and 6.51 MB RAM, with an end-to-end latency of 40 ms. In comparison, the standard 4.12M-parameter configuration achieves a lower perplexity of 6.79 but requires CPU fallback for unsupported operators, resulting in 1450 ms latency. Vanilla Transformer, on the other hand, achieves a perplexity score of 5.71 using a 4.40M-parameter model, but it cannot be deployed on the hardware. On downstream tasks, STEdgeFormer achieves 94% accuracy on ECG5000 and 93% accuracy on Speech Commands with 1.39M parameters. These results demonstrate that hardware-aware architectural choices and operator compatibility can have a substantial impact on practical inference latency, beyond model size and task accuracy alone. Rather than targeting state-of-the-art accuracy on individual benchmarks, STEdgeFormer is designed to provide a deployable sequence modeling architecture under the memory, computational, and operator constraints of embedded neural accelerators.
  </details>

- **[FormaFin: Verifiable Financial Analysis with Solver-Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28881)**  `arXiv:2611.28881`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bethel Hall, William Eiers_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Formally verifying financial analyses generated by large language models (LLMs) remains challenging as financial rules are largely expressed in natural language and rarely have corresponding formal specifications. Training language models to formalize these rules is hindered by the scarcity of labeled data and reliable training signals, while confidential financial data favor small models that run locally. We introduce FormaFin, a framework that trains small open language models to translate financial rules into reusable SMT formulas. FormaFin integrates teacher distillation with reinforcement learning using an SMT solver to compute rewards from numerical thresholds stated in financial rules. We evaluate FormaFin on corporate loan monitoring, in which lenders check borrowers' financial figures against loan agreements. To evaluate our framework, we curate \bench{}, a benchmark containing 143 loan agreements and 2,527 questions covering compliance, earnings cushion, and debt capacity. Our results show that a 7B formalizer achieves 94.8\% accuracy, outperforming open LLMs up to ten times larger. Across three model families, accuracy ranges from 92.6 to 96.8\%. Our framework also detects 95.6 to 99.7\% of errors in other LLMs' financial analyses. We make FormaFin and \bench{} publicly available.
  </details>

- **[DeCo: Decoupled Traffic State Modeling with Cross-Variable Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28685)**  `arXiv:2611.28685`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Du Yin, Lindong Xie, Xiachong Lin, Ruiyi Yang, Yifan Duan, Hao Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate forecasts of traffic flow, occupancy, and speed are essential for congestion management and travel planning. These variables exhibit distinct spatiotemporal patterns, and their coupled dynamics characterize the evolution of traffic conditions, such as the onset and dissipation of congestion. Existing methods often either neglect this coupling or struggle to capture the distinct dynamics of all three variables within a unified model. In this paper, we propose DeCo, a decoupled traffic state modeling framework with cross-variable coordination for forecasting flow, occupancy, and speed. Specifically, DeCo first trains a dedicated forecaster for each variable to specialize in its unique spatiotemporal dynamics. It then estimates a residual correction for each forecast by jointly leveraging the predicted trajectories and recent observations of all three variables, thereby exploiting cross-variable coupling and mitigating systematic prediction errors. We extend the flow-only PeMS03 and PeMS07 benchmarks to all three variables on their original sensors and release the aligned data. Experiments on PeMS03, PeMS04, PeMS07, and PeMS08 show that DeCo achieves the lowest mean absolute error for every variable on every dataset, up to 4.9% below RAGC, the strongest baseline. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[APPSolver: Adaptive Patch Partitioning for Point-Wise Ship Flow Prediction on Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29355)**  `arXiv:2608.29355`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Wenhua Huo, Fenglei Han, Wangyuan Zhao, Xiao Peng, Chunhui Wang, Jialin Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting high-fidelity flow fields around ship hulls is central to hydrodynamic performance analysis, yet traditional CFD solvers remain computationally prohibitive for rapid design exploration across multiple vessel types and operating conditions. Existing deep-learning surrogates often interpolate unstructured CFD meshes onto regular grids, which can blur near-field resolution, while direct point-level global attention on large unstructured meshes can be computationally expensive. In this paper, we introduce APPSolver, a neural surrogate that learns next-step point-wise flow-field prediction directly on the unstructured free-surface ship CFD mesh without re-gridding. APPSolver is built around Adaptive Patch Partitioning (APP), a quadtree-based strategy that groups unstructured points into local spatial patches according to the non-uniform density inherent in ship CFD meshes: fine near the hull and wake, coarse in the far field. The resulting patch tokens are processed by a Transformer backbone together with optional condition token that encode ship geometry and condition parameters via a frozen large language model, forming a unified token-space model for one-step temporal advancement of velocity and pressure fields. On the ShipBench dataset, APP-Transformer exhibits an accuracy--efficiency trade-off, incurring higher prediction errors than the strongest learned baselines while requiring substantially lower forward computation. Ablation studies quantify the compression-fidelity trade-off of APP and demonstrate that condition token provide preliminary gains in some leave-one-hull-out settings. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Reward Hackability in Code RL Training Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16062)**  `arXiv:2606.16062`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shreshth Rajan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code RL rewards a policy for passing a task's tests, so a test suite that accepts a wrong patch trains the wrong behavior. We measure how often open code RL environments accept wrong patches and how reliably hackable tasks can be detected and repaired, checking each claim by execution against the task's gold patch. First, 22.4% of 49 SWE-bench Verified tasks accept a Docker-verified patch that behaves differently from the gold patch, and 57.8% against a stronger attacker. The gold-patch check is necessary: an attacker not shown the gold patch returns it for 31% of its passing "exploits". Second, across 134 public SWE-bench submissions, models pass hackable tasks 28.6 pp more often than tasks of the same difficulty, mostly because those tasks are passable at all; pooled over three attacker runs, hackable tasks still exceed matched passable ones (p=0.014). Third, repair is harder to verify than detection: 61.9% of LLM-written repair tests fail on the gold patch, and even behind a gate an LLM judge is wrong on 38% of the verdicts execution can check. Finally, of 873 training trajectories behind an open coding agent run beside the gold fix, 19.4% answer differently on a case the issue describes; an LLM review that can only demote these executed differences judges 6.9% to be wrong fixes the tests rewarded (6.4-10.5% across three model reviews), and 5.4% in a second public training set (R2E-Gym). End to end, the gated repair yields verified tests for 290 of 324 wrong fixes in that agent's data and all 9 in 384 rewarded Open-SWE-Traces rollouts (2.3%). One-shot attacks hack 23 of 100 SWE-bench Pro V2 and 9 of 112 DeepSWE tasks; 12 of 1,344 passing DeepSWE leaderboard patches are model-judged wrong. Science graders mostly hold: an agentic red team passes 0 of 61 Terminal-Bench-Science tasks. Audits and repairs of code RL environments should be decided by execution wherever execution can decide.
  </details>

- **[MemFail: Stress-Testing Failure Modes of LLM Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26667)**  `arXiv:2605.26667`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ishir Garg, Neel Kolhe, Dawn Song, Xuandong Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory systems to remain consistent across long-horizon interactions, but little empirical work has been done to understand the specific failure modes and design choices that these systems present. Existing benchmarks report aggregate question-answering accuracy and treat memory systems as black boxes, making it impossible to attribute an incorrect answer to a particular failure mode of the system. We introduce MemFail, a diagnostic benchmark that isolates the failure modes of modern LLM memory systems. We begin by formalizing memory systems as the composition of three canonical operations -- summarization, storage, and retrieval -- and identify the potential failure modes induced by each. Based on these hypothesized failure modes, we construct five datasets spanning four tasks, each adversarially designed to test a specific operation of a memory system. Using these datasets, we evaluate four state-of-the-art memory systems on MemFail and demonstrate how MemFail can be used to empirically understand the tradeoffs induced by differences in memory system architectures.
  </details>

- **[LiteGUI: Lightweight GUI Agents via Multi-solution Guided Distillation and Offline Dual-level Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07505)**  `arXiv:2605.07505`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yubin Wu, Zicheng Cai, Liping Ning, Hua Wang, Zhi Chen, Yaohua Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present LiteGUI, a post-training framework for building lightweight vision-only GUI agents. Visual GUI interaction is challenging for compact models because desktop interfaces expose multiple equivalent action affordances (e.g., context menus, toolbar icons, and keyboard shortcuts) and single-reference imitation quickly collapses into repetitive action loops once execution deviates from a fixed template. LiteGUI addresses these challenges through two-stage optimization over logged GUI states without requiring costly online environment rollouts during training. First, we propose Guided Multi-Solution On-Policy Distillation (Guided-OPD), where the student samples completions from its current policy at logged visual states and receives privileged teacher guidance conditioned on the human-verified valid action most matched to the student's sampled behavior. Second, we develop Multi-Solution Dual-Level GRPO (MD-GRPO), which combines set-valued visual action matching with history-conditioned subtask-plan quality supervision to ground step-by-step progress in screenshot evidence. We further develop a scalable data generation pipeline and a corresponding multi-solution dataset, Lite-Dataset, to support the training and evaluation of GUI agents, addressing a gap in the existing literature. Deployed in live closed-loop GUI environments with zero inference-time judge overhead, LiteGUI-2B more than doubles OSWorld task success over its backbone (from 6.04% to 13.24%), while LiteGUI-30B-A3B (3B active parameters) achieves 58.95% on ScreenSpot-Pro, 22.7% on OSWorld, and 88.75% on Lite-Bench.
  </details>

- **[PACT: Plan-Action Consistency Training by Preserving Correct Plans and Repairing Actions in GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22591)**  `arXiv:2611.22591`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuohan Cai, Zhangxuan Gu, Runze Li, Beitong Zhou, Ming Wen, Hongbo Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI agents can formulate a correct immediate plan in their reasoning yet emit an action that fails to execute it, creating an execution gap. This recurring mismatch undermines reliable task completion, as a single execution error can derail a multi-step workflow. In outcome-supervised reinforcement learning, broadcasting a negative response-level advantage across all generated tokens risks penalizing valid planning together with erroneous execution. To address this credit-assignment conflict, we introduce PACT (Plan-Action Consistency Training), a training framework that combines reference-aligned plan protection and plan-aligned action repair without altering the original scalar action reward. Using annotated reference actions to identify execution gaps, PACT protects the verified plan by masking negative policy-gradient contributions across its token span. Keeping the generated reasoning unchanged as context, it then favors the reference action over the emitted erroneous action through a preference surrogate with stop-gradient pair weights. Compared with the outcome-only baseline, PACT raises AndroidWorld task success from 60.34% to 68.97% (+8.63 pp), while improving rule-based plan-action consistency on AndroidControl High from 83.71% to 89.74% (+6.03 pp). Adapting PACT to desktop agents also improves task success from 40.92% to 44.17% (+3.25 pp) on the full 369-task OSWorld benchmark. Together, these results establish PACT as an effective credit-assignment framework for bridging execution gaps and improving task completion across mobile and desktop GUI environments.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolving Evaluation Environments for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30962)**  `arXiv:2611.30962`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Wenhao Wang, Panrong Tong, Quyu Kong, Pengxiang Li, Haowen Yang, chenglin Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-use agents (CUAs) are rapidly improving, yet their evaluation remains largely static. Existing benchmarks provide a strong foundation, but fixed difficulty ceilings, limited feature coverage, and coarse aggregate scores progressively weaken their discriminative and diagnostic value. We introduce CUA-SEE, a closed-loop evaluation environment that co-evolves tasks and executable verifiers from rollout evidence. It disentangles three objectives: benchmark evolution expands evaluation scope through cohort-calibrated difficulty and coverage growth; diagnostic evolution increases resolution through agent-conditional capability-boundary probes; and verifier evolution improves precision by translating changing requirements into state-grounded checkpoints with adversarial validation. Across multiple agents, applications, and rounds, CUA-SEE generates challenging, measurable tasks while preserving process-level evidence. Our results position adaptive evaluation as a scalable counterpart to self-evolving agents, continuously tracking what agents can do, where their boundaries lie, and how reliably progress is measured.
  </details>

- **[WeMM-Ecom: Advancing Multimodal Content Understanding for WeChat E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30662)**  `arXiv:2611.30662`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Binhui Xie, Jinmin Li, Haomiao Tang, Guangting Wang, Song Jin, Jianwei Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present WeMM-Ecom, a family of multimodal large language models that deliver frontier e-commerce capabilities while achieving state-of-the-art general-purpose performance at their respective scales. The family spans five sizes, from a 0.8B dense model to a 35B mixture-of-experts model with 3B active parameters. Our training pipeline begins with two-stage continued pre-training that progressively raises the proportion of e-commerce data within a diverse multimodal data mixture, followed by hybrid-thinking post-training that combines on-policy distillation and reinforcement learning to support both non-thinking and thinking modes. For evaluation, we construct a comprehensive suite of 28 e-commerce benchmarks (23 in-house and five open-source) covering eight business scenarios and complement it with 64 public general-purpose benchmarks. On the e-commerce suite, WeMM-Ecom-35B-A3B surpasses substantially larger open-source flagship models, including Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, HY4, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. On the general-purpose suite, every WeMM-Ecom model surpasses its similarly sized Qwen3.5 counterpart, with the lightweight variants (e.g., WeMM-Ecom-2B) showing clear advantages over leading lightweight models such as Qwen3.5-2B, MiniCPM-V-4.7-1B, and LFM3-VL-2B. Beyond benchmarks, WeMM-Ecom now powers hundreds of production use cases in WeChat, including catalog management, multimodal product search, and livestream commerce, serving billion-scale requests per day. We publicly release all model weights and detail our data curation, training recipe, and benchmark construction to facilitate future work on domain-specialized multimodal models. We have released the model weights and code to facilitate future research atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MVAgent: Per-Video Agents under Global Coordination for Multi-Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29154)**  `arXiv:2611.29154`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Kewei Wei, XueXiang Wen, Bocheng Hu, Weili Xu, Haiyang Yu, Kaiyue Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-video understanding requires systems to combine evidence from different videos for cross-video reasoning. Answering such questions involves two kinds of decisions. Observation decisions must adapt to each video's content, whereas cross-video judgments require relating evidence from different videos based on the question. Most existing agents make both kinds of decisions in one controller, whose context accumulates observations from every video. We separate them in MVAgent, a training-free Multi-Video Understanding Agent framework with one LocalAgent per video. Each LocalAgent plans observations of its own video and keeps a private observation history. A GlobalAgent coordinates cross-video reasoning by instructing the LocalAgents and combining their reports with joint observation. We evaluate MVAgent on CVBench, MVU-Eval, and CrossVid, using three Qwen3.5 models of different sizes and GPT-6-sol. With Qwen3.5, our framework improves aggregate benchmark scores by 5.1 points on average over Single-pass inference with the same model and by 5.0 points over the strongest prior agent using the same model. With GPT-6-sol, the average gain over Single-pass inference with the same model is 4.3 points. On the same hardware, MVAgent also runs 1.8-2.7 times faster than prior agents of comparable size.
  </details>

- **[torchtsetlin: Fast, Interpretable Tsetlin Machines for PyTorch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28195)**  `arXiv:2611.28195`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Vajira Thambawita, Pål Halvorsen, Ole-Christoffer Granmo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Tsetlin machine learns propositional rules with teams of Tsetlin automata that are driven by reward and penalty rather than gradients, so its models are interpretable by construction and cheap to evaluate. Its software, however, consists of C and CUDA kernels behind scikit-learn-style wrappers that sit outside the deep learning ecosystem. torchtsetlin implements the Tsetlin machine family as ordinary PyTorch modules: vanilla and weighted, coalesced, regression, convolutional over sequences, images and volumes, and dense per-pixel segmentation. The automata are a tensor buffer, so a model moves with .to(device), is saved with state_dict() and learns from a mini-batch with one update(x,y) call. Its core technique is a batched formulation of Type I and Type II feedback, in which automaton transitions become event counts realised as binomial draws, with one commit per mini-batch. On ten benchmarks taken from the existing libraries, it matches their accuracy, and on all but the two smallest it trains 2.4-10.9X faster than PyTsetlinMachineCUDA and 10.3-81.2X faster than tmu on the same GPU. The package is pure Python, MIT-licensed and on PyPI; every number in this paper is reproduced by one script. Code:this https URLpackage:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SAMA: Training-Free Consensus Ridge Tracking for Camera-Based Heart Rate on Five Public Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28185)**  `arXiv:2611.28185`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Sudhakar Mishra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) is usually benchmarked by one heart rate per video, a figure that hides the short windows in which a tele-consultation would actually read the pulse. We present SAMA, a training-free estimator that (i) forms a quality-weighted consensus of POS and CHROM chrominance spectra, (ii) discounts frequencies occupied by pulse-free references derived from the same frames (frame motion, skin-mask geometry and background brightness), (iii) follows the heart rate through time with a Viterbi ridge under a random-walk prior, and (iv) returns a track-filtered, amplitude-normalised waveform with a per-window confidence. No parameter is learned and no dataset-specific tuning is used.
