On 218 recordings from 111 participants in five public datasets (UBFC-rPPG, PURE, SUMS, M3PD and a two-participant subset of MMPD), all processed with the same fixed face box and scored with a re-implementation of the rPPG-Toolbox protocol, SAMA has the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) averaged over datasets at every protocol level: 4.40 against 5.13,bpm for POS per video, 4.80 against 5.43 on 30,s windows and 5.05 against 6.59 on 10,s windows. Pooled over participants, the bootstrap interval of its paired difference from POS excludes zero on 30,s and 10,s windows (Wilcoxon p=0.11 and 0.004) but not per video, and it does not beat POS on SUMS per video or on the MMPD subset. Withholding the 20\% least confident windows lowers the pooled 10,s error from 4.06 to 2.33,bpm. We also show that the per-video protocol has a floor of 1.2--1.6,bpm on datasets where heart rate drifts, and that skin tone cannot be recovered from uncalibrated video, which motivates the instrumented, skin-tone-stratified collection we propose for the Indian population. The method does not surpass the best published neural networks on UBFC-rPPG; we report where it stands against them without re-running them.
  </details>

- **[RealICU: Do LLM Agents Understand Long-Context ICU Data? A Benchmark Beyond Behavior Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13542)**  `arXiv:2605.13542`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Chengzhi Shen, Weixiang Shen, Tobias Susetzky, Chen, Chen, Jun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intensive care units (ICU) generate long, dense and evolving streams of clinical information, where physicians must repeatedly reassess patient states under time pressure, underscoring a clear need for reliable AI decision support. Existing ICU benchmarks typically treat historical clinician actions as ground truth. However, these actions are made under incomplete information and limited temporal context of the underlying patient state, and may therefore be suboptimal, making it difficult to assess the true reasoning capabilities of AI systems. We introduce RealICU, a hindsight-annotated benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) under realistic ICU conditions, where labels are created after senior physicians review the full patient trajectory. We formulate four physician-motivated tasks: assess Patient Status, Acute Problems, Recommended Actions, and Red Flag actions that risk unsafe outcomes. We partition each trajectory with 30-min windows and release two datasets: RealICU-Gold with 930-window annotations from 94 MIMIC-IV patients, and RealICU-Scale with 11,862 windows extended by Oracle, a physician-validated LLM hindsight labeler. Existing LLMs including memory-augmented ones performed poorly on RealICU, exposing two failure modes: a recall-safety tradeoff for clinical recommendations, and an anchoring bias to early interpretations of the patient. We further introduce ICU-Evo to study structured-memory agents that improves long-horizon reasoning but does not fully eliminate safety failures. Together, RealICU provides a clinically grounded testbed for measuring and improving AI sequential decision-support in high-stakes care. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SAEPO: Sparse-Autoencoder-Guided Credit Assignment for Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29936)**  `arXiv:2611.29936`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yusong Zhao, Zihao Zhao, Zirui Zhang, Tunyu Zhang, Ziyan Wang, Yi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has demonstrated strong performance in improving large language model (LLM) reasoning, yet its credit-assignment signal remains coarse. Outcome verifiers provide scalable supervision by judging whether a completed response is correct, but they do not directly indicate which intermediate reasoning tokens contribute to success or failure. As a result, policy optimization can be inefficient, especially when training base models directly from sparse outcome feedback. In this paper, we introduce SAEPO, a Sparse AutoEncoder-guided token-level credit assignment method for outcome-supervised Policy Optimization. Rather than treating correctness only as a rollout-level (sequence-level) signal, SAEPO uses a pretrained sparse autoencoder (SAE) to build token-level semantic representations within reasoning trajectories; it scores each token based on the SAE concepts (e.g., penalizing wrong "final-answer" tokens but rewarding correct "reasoning-process" tokens), learned in an unsupervised manner, to achieve fine-grained credit assignment. SAEPO is a plug-in advantage-shaping module compatible with various policy optimization algorithms. We instantiate SAEPO with both GRPO and GSPO, covering token-ratio and sequence-ratio policy-gradient objectives, and evaluate SAEPO across three LLM backbones on six math and two code benchmarks. Empirical results show that SAEPO improves both the math and code benchmark averages over the matched baselines, with gains of up to 6.63% and 5.57%, respectively. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base, training curves also show faster early accuracy growth. Feature and token-level analyses suggest that the SAE-derived signal favors question-relevant concepts and reasoning tokens.
  </details>

- **[GameAnything: From Any Inspirations to Playable Game Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29238)**  `arXiv:2611.29238`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Wenyi Wu, Siqi Liu, Yuru Feng, Zixuan Song, Aayush Salvi, Sibo Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-driven game development holds the potential to transform diverse human inspirations into interactive experiences. However, existing systems and benchmarks primarily focus on explicit development specifications, with limited support for adapting multimodal references and evaluating actual gameplay experiences. We introduce GameAnything, an open-source agentic platform that transforms multimodal inspirations into playable extensions of existing games through iterative development. GameAnything integrates coding agents with an independent playtesting critic, persistent development state, and reusable experience memory to support continuous refinement. We further present GameAnything-Bench, a benchmark spanning diverse reference modalities, game creation objectives, and gaming environments, with exploratory gameplay testing to assess both functional correctness and gameplay quality. Evaluations of nine coding agent configurations reveal that current agents can adapt reference content but still struggle with playable interactions and precise modifications. Moreover, multimodal references improve content fidelity, while iterative refinement yields substantial but varying improvements across agents. These findings highlight both the potential and remaining challenges of inspiration-driven game development.
  </details>

- **[SkillBuffBench: Measuring What Agent Skills Contribute across Harnesses, Models, Skill Access Modes and Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22017)**  `arXiv:2611.22017`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Gengyue Dong, Ye Wei, Zheng Zhu, Yidong Han, Jiale Miao, Guanghui Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent Skills, file-based packages of procedural knowledge that a coding agent can discover and load on demand, are now shipped by every major agent harness, yet the evidence for how much they help comes almost entirely from settings in which the right Skill is hand-picked for the task and the agent starts from a blank slate. Neither holds in deployment, where an agent chooses from whatever library happens to be installed and frequently carries memory from earlier work. We present SkillBuffBench, a benchmark and paired evaluation protocol that treats Skill access as a three-level factor (no Skill, a curated Skill, and automatic invocation from an installed library), crosses it with memory (off and on), with four agent harnesses and with multiple models (the state-of-the-art proprietary models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI, and the state-of-the-art open-weight models from Chinese providers), every model being run under every harness, and evaluates the result on research tasks contributed by domain experts together with the Skills they use, currently in materials science and biology, with the suite extended on a rolling basis. Every run is marked by a deterministic verifier; uncertainty is reported with a paired, task-level bootstrap; and cost, agent rounds and wall-clock time are reported alongside the score rather than folded into it. This version of the paper describes the design of the benchmark, its evaluation protocol and its current tasks and Skills. The evaluation is in progress: results will be added once the runs are complete, and the task suite, results and analysis will continue to be updated on a rolling basis.
  </details>

- **[From Point Clouds to Engineering Models: Benchmarking Agentic 3D Reconstruction in the Built Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28197)**  `arXiv:2611.28197`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Changyu Du, Stavros Nousias, Syed Salman Ahmed, Kai Dietmair, Mohammad Alrabab'h, André Borrmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can translate 3D measurements into CAD geometry, but reconstructing engineering models for the built environment demands semantic interpretation and consistent construction constraints beyond surface fitting. We formulate scene-level Scan-to-BIM (Building Information Modeling) as an agentic 3D coding task and introduce S2Bench, comprising public and proprietary scans of 128 real-world scenes across five domains, paired with expert-curated reference models spanning diverse spatial scales. A shared framework lets agents measure scans in Python, build class-labeled components in a live CAD/BIM kernel, and iteratively refine their code. We also propose parallel asynchronous collaboration, where a lead delegates versioned tasks, continues modeling while workers execute, and integrates revision-bound proposals. Evaluating five agents and a collaborative team across 1,028 reconstructions shows that scan-to-model residuals directly guide geometric repair, whereas inferring semantic classes and construction relations requires joint geometric, visual, and engineering reasoning that agents struggle to ground and verify. Consequently, the leading agent fits scan geometry closely ($96\%$ coverage within $5\,\text{cm}$) yet reaches only $63\%$ mIoU, and no agent passes all engineering checks on more than $38\%$ of scenes. Coverage and engineering conformance drop with scene scale, whereas running a smaller model as a team improves all three axes with greater coverage gains on larger scenes. The project page is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[When Does Optical Context Compression Pay Off? A Decode-Aware Cost Analysis of Reasoning VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30130)**  `arXiv:2611.30130`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ziqing Ge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rendering long text as images lets a vision-language model (VLM) read a document with fewer input tokens, and evaluations often count mainly these prefill tokens. We show that for reasoning models this accounting is incomplete. Across five models and two long-context benchmarks, reading the rendered document makes the model think longer: decode length grows by 1.5-3.5x on the four general-purpose VLMs tested, and under typical output pricing this decode tax makes image input more expensive than text for two of the five models on QuALITY. Unrecoverable information loss is not sufficient to explain the tax at moderate compression: Qwen's OCR of the same images recovers 98.7% of words, and feeding Kimi-K3 its own transcription as text restores text-mode reasoning length and accuracy. Whether compression pays off reduces to an exchange rate, (1-1/c)/(r-1), that must exceed the workload's price-weighted ratio of output to input tokens; this accounting decomposition shows why Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash can benefit and Qwen and GLM cannot. None of the training-free signals we test predicts the tax before generation. As a practical heuristic, an image-first policy that sends only inputs with a per-input compression ratio of at least 1.6 to images and falls back to text once image-mode reasoning costs as much as the input's prefill saving cuts the bill by 6-14% on Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at an accuracy cost of 0.3-1.2 points; on QuALITY, asking for a reasoning budget on both paths is a stronger lever.
  </details>

- **[DiscernBench: Evaluating Cooperative Discernment of LLM Agents in Institutional Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28787)**  `arXiv:2611.28787`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Disha Sheshanarayana, Tanishka Magar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The deployment of large language models in operational settings demands evaluation of judgment under institutional constraint. We introduce DiscernBench, a benchmark for evaluating cooperative discernment: the ability of an agent to determine whether to comply with, refuse, or escalate a request in an institutional setting. DiscernBench comprises scenarios derived from real-world incident reports across domains such as aviation, law enforcement, and emergency management. We evaluate eight LLMs across three decision-making configurations and analyze both final decisions and the intermediate judgments produced during multi-agent deliberation. Under disagreement, Final Responders exhibit model- and role-dependent aggregation patterns in which a lone specialist recommendation can disproportionately influence the collective decision, a phenomenon we term Minority-Signal Override. Role-specialized multi-agent deliberation raises average over-escalation from 3.79% under direct single-agent judgment to 28.27%, with the direction and magnitude of minority-signal influence varying substantially across models and specialist roles. These results reveal distinct collective decision behaviors that are not captured by final-answer accuracy alone. Trustworthy multi-agent systems therefore require evaluating not only individual specialist reasoning, but also how conflicting judgments are transformed into collective decisions. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25214)**  `arXiv:2611.25214`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fangxu Yu, Tao Feng, Xuying Ning, Haolin Yang, Jiaxuan You, Tianyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SkillForgeBench: Evaluating Agent Reliability Grounded in Agent Skill Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14918)**  `arXiv:2610.14918`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengxi Li, Junyi Zhou, Qiyuan Zhang, Chuhan Wu, Fuyuan Lyu, Le Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating an agent's ability to strictly satisfy diverse and procedurally complex requirements is essential to determining its practical reliability. Conducting such an evaluation demands agentic tasks that reconcile broad domain coverage with deep procedural complexity. Existing paradigms face a fundamental trade-off: expert-curated benchmarks achieve procedural depth but suffer from domain diversity bottlenecks, whereas crowdsourced benchmarks span diverse scenarios but lack execution rigor. To resolve this tension, we present SkillForgeBench, which leverages agent skills as unifying execution blueprints across three co-designed stages: (1) Resource Preparation uses domain-balanced skills to guide artifact retrieval, establishing broad workflow coverage; (2) Skill-driven Task Synthesis employs skills as procedural anchors to partition artifact roles, synthesize naturalistic task instructions, and package sandboxed scenarios for deep procedural execution; and (3) Holistic Evaluation Design translates skill-embedded requirements into fine-grained completion and process rubrics to enable strict requirement-level scoring. Comprising 842 sandboxed tasks across five major professional domains, SkillForgeBench reveals a steep capability hierarchy among eight mainstream agents, led by Opus 4.8 ($40.9\%$ strict completion). Requiring all constrains satisfaction uncovers severe capability bottlenecks across all model tiers that remain masked under individual rubric pass rates ($70.0\%$ to $85.7\%$ vs. $12.6\%$ to $40.9\%$). Crucially, under the external guidance from skills, top-tier agents obtain larger gains than low-tier agents (i.e., $\Delta 19.2\%$ for Opus 4.8 vs. $\Delta 5.8\%$ for Haiku 4.5). Finally, agents satisfy process rubrics far less frequently than completion rubrics (under $3\%$ for Haiku 4.5), proving that completion-only checks overestimate real-world agent reliability.
  </details>

- **[ArgGYM: A Procedural, Engine-Verified Benchmark for Structured Defeasible Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38409)**  `arXiv:2609.38409`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _İbrahim Ethem Deveci, Funda Tan Çalık, Barış Deniz Sağlam, Duygu Ataman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in large language model reasoning has been driven by benchmarks and reinforcement learning environments with automatically verifiable rewards, particularly in mathematics, code, and formal logic. These settings make model accuracy easier to evaluate and optimize, but it remains unclear how far success under fixed problem specifications and stable evaluation criteria transfers to reasoning outside such domains. Real-world reasoning often proceeds under incomplete and revisable information: conclusions may be supported provisionally, defeated by counter-evidence, reinstated by further arguments, or revised when stronger reasons become available. Reasoning of this kind is generally referred to as defeasible reasoning. We introduce ArgGYM, a procedural benchmark and RLVR-compatible training environment for structured defeasible reasoning. ArgGYM decomposes this reasoning into twelve tasks and grounds task-specific scoring in a symbolic argumentation engine that computes the formal states used to evaluate model outputs. It includes a frozen benchmark of 1,440 verified instances across fifteen curriculum configurations, two argument preference orderings, and two set orderings, while the same generators and verifiers can produce fresh instances, reducing dependence on static test sets, for both evaluation and verifiable-reward training. On the frozen benchmark, frontier and open-weight models show sharply different reasoning profiles: they can recover substantial parts of structured answers without solving the complete task, and performance declines in later curriculum configurations with longer dependencies and more interacting structures. Training Qwen3.5-4B with ArgGYM's verifiers as rewards more than doubles complete-task success on held-out instances, with the largest gains on constructive tasks.
  </details>

- **[TraceDance: An Automated System for Building Agent Behavior Benchmarks from Real-World Agent Deployment Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33295)**  `arXiv:2609.33295`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dehai Min, Daoan Zhang, Yiming Zeng, Huayi Zhang, Ziyi Chen, Yan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent can complete a task while exhibiting undesirable behavior during execution. Developers need tests for the specific behaviors encountered in deployment, beyond fixed benchmark suites. We present TraceDance, an agent system that constructs targeted benchmarks from deployment traces for user-specified undesirable behaviors. For efficient construction, Anchor-and-Confirm combines programmable retrieval with candidate-level confirmation by a Flash large language model (LLM), while the Anchor Synthesis Loop generates and revises specifications for custom behaviors. The benchmarks use decision-point continuation to evaluate an LLM's next turn at a recorded decision point with a behavior-specific rubric, without a reference answer or environment replay. Experiments in coding and general tool use draw on 252,557 sessions and produce 107 benchmarks with 4,125 instances, fulfilling 95.3% of build-target requests. Both human annotators confirm the requested behavior in 84% of sampled instances, and the automated grader's agreement with human pass/fail judgments is comparable to that between the annotators. Nine frontier LLMs achieve a mean pass rate of only 26.7%, showing that they still struggle to respond appropriately at the evaluated decision points. Analysis across behavior-specific benchmarks further reveals weaknesses in how current LLMs behave as agents. By turning deployment problems into targeted benchmarks, TraceDance could serve as a key component of the recursive self-improvement (RSI) loop.
  </details>

- **[Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27266)**  `arXiv:2608.27266`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuan Chang, Xiaoqi Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts in IFBench and HotpotQA, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning may partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA despite its much simpler mechanism, while GRPO still performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt-only optimization. We also show that NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when applied verbatim to other student models, especially across models within the same family. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.
  </details>

- **[Towards Adaptive, Scalable, and Robust Coordination of LLM Agents: A Dynamic Ad-Hoc Networking Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08009)**  `arXiv:2602.08009`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Li, Zeyu Zhang, Xiaohe Bo, Quanyu Dai, Chaozhuo Li, Feng Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent architectures built on large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated the potential to realize swarm intelligence through well-crafted collaboration. However, the substantial burden of manual orchestration inherently raises an imperative to automate the design of agentic workflows. We frame such an agent coordination challenge as a classic problem in dynamic ad-hoc networking: How to establish adaptive and reliable communication among a scalable number of agentic hosts? In response to this unresolved dilemma, we introduce RAPS, a reputation-aware publish-subscribe paradigm for adaptive, scalable, and robust coordination of LLM agents. RAPS is grounded in the Distributed Publish-Subscribe Protocol, allowing LLM agents to exchange messages based on their declared intents rather than predefined topologies. Beyond this substrate, RAPS further incorporates two coherent overlays: (i) Reactive Subscription, enabling agents to dynamically refine their intents; and (ii) Bayesian Reputation, empowering each agent with a local watchdog to detect and isolate malicious peers. Extensive experiments over five benchmarks showcase that our design effectively reconciles adaptivity, scalability, and robustness in a unified multi-agent coordination framework.
  </details>

- **[What If TSF: Evaluating Scenario-Guided Multimodal Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08509)**  `arXiv:2601.08509`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jinkwan Jang, Hyunbin Jin, Hyungjin Park, Kyubyung Chae, Taesup Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled time series forecasting to move beyond numerical observations and incorporate external information in multimodal settings. Such information can improve forecasting performance, but accuracy alone may not reveal whether models appropriately respond to it: a model may ignore relevant information, fail to distinguish scenarios with different implications, or overreact to irrelevant or weak signals. We introduce What If TSF (WIT), a benchmark for evaluating whether models effectively respond to future scenarios. WIT constructs controlled scenario sets by fixing the forecasting state while systematically varying future scenarios, enabling three complementary evaluations: Factual Comparison, which measures the predictive utility of factual scenario information; Relational Comparison, which evaluates whether forecasts satisfy expected direction, contrast, intensity, and restraint relations across scenarios; and Grounded Comparison, which provides a complementary empirical assessment of scenario-induced revision trajectories where comparable real cases are available. Experiments show that factual accuracy gains do not consistently translate into appropriate responses to alternative scenarios, demonstrating the need to evaluate multimodal forecasting beyond predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Steer on Injected Uncertainty Tokens, but Fail to Self-Monitor In Situ](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31228)**  `arXiv:2611.31228`  `cs.AI`  
  _Daniel Theron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prompting language models to verbalize stepwise numerical confidence during generation is an increasingly popular approach for triggering backtracking and error correction in agentic reasoning. This strategy presupposes two capabilities: (1) in-situ monitoring fidelity (does confidence drop when an error occurs?) and (2) downstream steering control (does an uncertainty token causally steer the model to repair its error?). We evaluated four open-weights models (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Phi-4, 14.7B) across 15,200 math benchmark attempts, isolating 2,017 reasoning errors within each model's capability frontier. Applying 27,568 counterfactual continuations across these failure points, we demonstrate a consistent decoupling between monitoring and control.On monitoring, all four models fail to detect their own errors. Verbalized confidence does not distinguish mistake steps from sound reasoning, yielding near-chance within-position AUROCs between 0.500 and 0.561. Even at a model's first reasoning error, trajectory confidence remains saturated at 95.1% to 99.9%, with noticeable drops ($\ge 10$ points) appearing on fewer than 9% of errors in Llama and under 1.6% in the other three models. In contrast, models respond readily to external uncertainty: injecting a low-confidence tag (10%) at the error step significantly increases downstream correction across all models (+3.7 to +22.5 points over format-matched 95% certainty; all $p \le 0.0022$), reaching up to 38.5% under active rethinking instructions. However, injecting doubt onto already-correct steps causes models to discard valid reasoning, reducing solve rates by up to 22.8 points. Inference-time self-correction is thus bottlenecked by in-situ error detection rather than downstream repair capacity: models readily steer toward recovery when prompted with doubt, yet fail to self-monitor during generation.
  </details>

- **[Seeking in Space: Benchmarking Purposeful Active Exploration of Vision-Language Models for Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31215)**  `arXiv:2611.31215`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Xinlin Zhuang, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans explore unfamiliar spaces with purpose, deciding where to look until they have gathered the evidence a spatial question requires. However, can vision-language models (VLMs), which reason increasingly well over curated images and videos, also seek in space when the evidence is not handed to them? We present ActSpace-Bench, a benchmark of 994 questions across 234 exploration episodes in 72 real-scanned indoor scenes, where a model starts from a single view, navigates real captured frames, and must gather evidence before answering. To assess whether such exploration is purposeful, we introduce a controlled evaluation protocol that fixes each model as the reasoner and compares its own exploration against a same-budget random walk and an oracle route, separating evidence seeking from spatial reasoning. Every question is verified to require exploration and answered from 3D annotations, masked-evidence questions test whether models know when to abstain, and over 95% of sampled questions are confirmed correct by human verification. Evaluations of 23 proprietary, open-weight, and spatially specialized VLMs reveal that the strongest models do seek evidence with purpose but lose much of it to limited memory, whereas weaker and specialized models explore no better than a random walk or fail to reason over the evidence they are given, pointing to memory and evidence-grounded reasoning as the next targets for active spatial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[LiveLoRA: Exact KV Cache Migration Across LoRA Updates for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31105)**  `arXiv:2611.31105`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyi Huang, Samuel Xu, Ivy Gu, Nathan Huang, Siqi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Updating an LLM adapter should not require replaying the full interaction history of every active session. Yet reusing key-value (KV) cache entries computed under an earlier version mixes model versions, while full-history re-prefill becomes increasingly costly as histories and session fleets grow. We introduce LiveLora, a model-runtime co-design for exact final-block KV cache migration. Frozen lower decoder blocks and fixed K/V adapter down-projections preserve each token's base projections and low-rank coordinates across adapter versions. The runtime reconstructs candidate K/V, reevaluates the latest token through the final block, and commits the adapter, cache, and next-token logits together. We prove continuation equivalence to full-history re-prefill in exact arithmetic under the stated model assumptions. On Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, median paired publication speedups over full-history re-prefill average $68.98\times$ across five runs for 32 active sessions with 8K tokens each. Serving benchmarks show approximately 4.8% decoding overhead relative to native cached decoding. Compared with retained-boundary replay, compact retention gives a $3.42\times$ reduction in additional session storage at 8K, with similar measured publication-plus-decoding cost. LiveLora allows persistent LLM agents to adopt new adapter versions without replaying their full interaction histories.
  </details>

- **[Boltz-Flow: Generating High-Quality Robot Action from Boltzmann Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31038)**  `arXiv:2611.31038`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hengzhi Jiang, Haixu Liu, Jindou Jia, Tinglan Wang, Shaobo Chen, Yiren Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching policies integrate a velocity field from a fixed, observation-independent Gaussian source. However, since the source is unimodal and identical across all tasks, the limited representational capacity of Gaussian distributions constrains how far a policy can scale with growing task diversity. Moreover, the source factorizes over time steps and control dimensions, discarding the coordination that manipulation requires. We introduce Boltz-Flow, which replaces Gaussian initialization with a more powerful, observation- and instruction-conditioned Boltzmann source while leaving the base VLA unchanged. Boltz-Flow parameterizes this source with a conditional Boltzmann machine that assigns an energy value to each binary source state. A listwise objective, supervised entirely by offline demonstrations, aligns this energy with the quality of actions produced after frozen flow integration. At inference, the energy defines an Ising instance whose solution is decoded into continuous noise before a single standard integration. We use a physical coherent Ising machine during training. Experiments show that Boltz-Flow improves $\pi_{0.5}$ across three benchmarks, outperforming the compared strong policy baselines and competing noise optimization methods on LIBERO and RoboTwin 2.0 and ranking second only to LEAP on LIBERO-Plus. Additional experiments show gains under noise-adapted fine-tuning, with action-to-action warm starts, and on the SmolVLA backbone.
  </details>

- **[ComputeWise: Measuring and Fixing GPU Waste by AI Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31027)**  `arXiv:2611.31027`  `cs.AI`  
  _Egor Pakhomov, Nina Pakhomova, Anurag Koul, Shafiq Joty, Erik Nijkamp_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are given GPUs to do AI research on their own. Do they use those GPUs well? A busy GPU does not tell us, because it can be busy with work the answer did not need. This matters, because the tokens the agent spends cost almost nothing next to the GPUs its experiments run on, so using those GPUs better has a huge impact. So we built ComputeWise, an open benchmark of research tasks on which this waste can be measured and reduced. For it we developed our own simulated GPU, which replays recorded runs to the agent's code. With no real GPU needed, we ran hundreds of experiments, far more than would be reasonable on real GPUs. With no help, the agents put only 52% of their GPU time into work the answer needed. Another 22% went to work it did not need, and 26% sat idle. Asking the agent to finish the work faster, telling it to keep every GPU busy, or giving it a job scheduler does not fix this. So we built an open-source harness, part prompting and part deterministic code. Across five models, it removed over 80% of the extra time to the answer.
  </details>

- **[MedUIGym: Evaluating and Training GUI Agents for Clinical Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30983)**  `arXiv:2611.30983`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Qiao, Lei Liu, Yue Shen, Jian Wang, Jinjie Gu, Zhixuan Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating clinical workflows requires vision-language agents that can navigate dense graphical user interfaces (GUIs) in electronic health records (EHRs) and execute precise actions, yet many medical agents still rely on text or structured tool calls. In this work, we introduce MedUIGym, a scalable interactive environment comprising 15,739 tasks from 17 benchmark sources across six clinical task families for training and evaluating clinical GUI agents. In MedUIGym, agents perceive EHR states from screenshots and execute mouse-and-keyboard actions, as well as a paired native-tool interface over identical clinical data. The environment verifies both final answers and required clinical actions, and supports parallel trajectory collection for training. Benchmarking twelve vision-language models shows that current agents struggle across diverse clinical workflows, and simply increasing reasoning effort fails to yield consistent gains. Furthermore, MedUIGym supports offline learning from GUI trajectories and online reinforcement learning from task rewards. After training, both Qwen3.5 models perform better on in-domain clinical tasks and generalize to tasks from external sources. By unifying tasks from existing benchmarks, dual-interface evaluation, and GUI trajectory collection, MedUIGym provides a testbed for studying how vision-language agents complete clinical tasks through the EHR's graphical interface. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GameDojo: Benchmarking Real-Time Interactive Agents in Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30771)**  `arXiv:2611.30771`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kuan Zhang, Zhihao Yang, Yukun Chen, Xingyilang Yin, Mingyu Ouyang, Yue Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive intelligence requires agents to act on observations and adapt to the consequences of actions. Studying these abilities calls for diverse, challenging tasks under controlled conditions. We introduce GameDojo, a controllable interactive gameplay benchmark of 120 boss encounters across 20 games for evaluating model capabilities, learning algorithms, and agent harnesses. Coding agents reconstruct the environments and write gameplay scripts, producing GameDojo-Data with 600 hours of gameplay trajectories. Experiments reveal substantial challenges for frontier models, especially in real-time interaction. Training on GameDojo-Data brings GameDojo-4B to frontier performance, with the strongest real-time performance. We also find joint training improves individual tasks, and learned policies transfer across bosses. Together, these findings establish GameDojo as a challenging and valuable interactive gameplay benchmark for the community. Our project page is atthis https URL, and our code is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[How Many Parameters Does Forecasting Need? A Survey and Code-Level Audit of Lightweight Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30685)**  `arXiv:2611.30685`  `cs.AI`  
  _Issam Ait Yahia, Abdelkader El Mahdaouy, Ismail Berrada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term time series forecasting supports decisions in energy, transportation, weather and finance, where forecasts are produced continuously and often on constrained hardware. Recent work has moved in two directions. One scales Transformers and pre-trained foundation models to millions or hundreds of millions of parameters. The other shows that models with only hundreds to tens of thousands of parameters remain competitive on the standard benchmarks. Existing surveys organize forecasting models by backbone and treat efficiency as a side topic, so the reasons these small models work, and the way their sizes are reported, remain unexamined. This survey focuses on lightweight long-term forecasting. It provides a taxonomy of about thirty-five models in six families, with a common notation and an architecture diagram for each core model. It offers a structured-linear view in which the smallest models are linear maps restricted by frequency truncation or period folding, and it tests each restriction in closed form on eight benchmarks. It complements the literature review with an audit of sixteen official code repositories, which reproduces published parameter counts and reveals reporting gaps of up to 289 times. Finally, it gives practical guidance on model selection and reporting, and summarizes open problems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Black Box: A Diagnosis-Driven Agent for Interpretable MIP Solver Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30670)**  `arXiv:2611.30670`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yidong Luo, Weihuang Wen, Xuanhao Pan, Yinya Wang, Shanwen Pu, Chenghao Lan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tuning a mixed-integer programming (MIP) solver is an instance-specific problem, where a poor configuration can increase solving time by orders of magnitude.Existing Bayesian optimization tuners treat solver tuning as a black-box optimization problem: each evaluation returns a single score, allowing the tuner to learn which configuration is faster, but not why.Inspired by the use of profilers in GPU-kernel optimization, we introduce APEC (Agent for Per-instance Explainable Configuration), a log-grounded tuning agent for MIP solvers.APEC has three layers: a deterministic layer that profiles each log, an interpretation layer that guides hypothesis-driven diagnosis, and a system layer that controls evaluation, enabling more reliable and interpretable solver tuning.On the full MIPLIB 2017 benchmark set, APEC achieves a 1.58X improvement over the default configuration with over 38% fewer parameter changes than the baselines, making the results easier to read.
  </details>

- **[JaxNaviSim: A GPU-Accelerated, Data-Driven Simulator and Learning Benchmark for Maritime Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30600)**  `arXiv:2611.30600`  `cs.AI`  
  _Quang Anh Pham, Janaka Chathuranga Brahmanage, Hue Tse Leong, Akshat Kumar, Tien Anh Mai, Siu Loon Hoe, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coordinating vessels through the shared waterways of busy ports grows more challenging as vessel traffic increases. Port authorities need realistic simulations to assess how arrival schedules, anchorage assignments, and routes affect vessel throughput and navigation safety. Existing AIS-based trajectory models predict a vessel's track from its past trajectory in an open-loop fashion. They do not simulate step-by-step responses to other ships or test whether a vessel collides or reaches its goal. To address this problem, we introduce JaxNaviSim, a data-driven simulator and learning benchmark coupling Automatic Identification System (AIS) data with fast, controllable traffic simulation, in which learned models steer vessels through recorded traffic. First, we release a dataset of ~141 million AIS records from over 30K vessels across eight high-density regions of the Singapore Strait, among the world's busiest waterways, with ~6.7 million vessel-hours. Unlike several existing AIS datasets which mostly cover open seas, ours captures close-quarters vessel traffic in the narrow strait. Second, we define a navigation task for each recorded vessel trajectory. A model steers that vessel every 10 s from its recorded state to its anchoring point or region exit, while surrounding ships follow their recorded tracks or are steered by the model. We implement a JAX simulator that runs these tasks in large batches on a GPU. We also incorporate multiple vessel dynamics models and sea surface currents. Finally, we benchmark imitation learning (IL), offline and online RL, and include Predictive Action Refinement (PAR) as a safety baseline that cuts collision rates to 1-3%. The comparison shows that low tracking error does not ensure arrival, and learned policies can reach most destinations yet collide in 20-45% of episodes.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Change: State-Verified Reinforcement Learning for Educational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30559)**  `arXiv:2611.30559`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shengyao Wang, Siyuan Su, Siyu Song, Mengsong Wu, Yiming Luo, Wenbo Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persistent tutoring agents do more than answer questions: they manage course files, practice records, and learner profiles that govern future lessons. In such stateful settings, knowing what to teach does not guarantee knowing what to change. We identify a pervasive failure mode, content-state decoupling, where agents deliver pedagogically sound responses while silently corrupting workspace state through unwarranted updates, missed prerequisites, or unintended side effects. Text-level judges overlook these state errors, whereas isolated tool-call checks break down because whether a write action is valid depends jointly on the learner request, prior state, and execution order. We propose State-Verified Reinforcement Learning, which supervises online policy optimization directly through verifiable workspace transitions. Within isolated sandboxes that snapshot state around every tool call, a deterministic verifier audits tool effects, workflow ordering, and native auto-sync side effects, pairing graded partial credit with a hard gate that zeroes episode reward upon any critical violation. Whenever no critical violation occurs, an evidence-grounded judge scores actual saved artifacts rather than agent claims. We pair 1,000 verifiable training instances with an independently authored, human-reviewed evaluation benchmark of 60 tasks spanning 11 educational categories and 16 native tools. After online GRPO training, Qwen3.8-27B raises category-weighted strict success on our benchmark from 51.47% to 80.74% (+29.27 points), cutting content-pass-only execution failures from 45.6% to 13.7% and required state-check failures from 63 to 1 across 180 trials. The resulting policy outperforms seven reference baselines by over 20 points while reducing output tokens by 17-39% across seven of eight external benchmarks without regressing on general reasoning or tool use.
  </details>

- **[Reproducible by Seed? Not on GPUs: Batch-Dependent Non-Determinism in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30545)**  `arXiv:2611.30545`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javier Conde, Xiuyuan Qi, Shanshan Liu, Pedro Reviriego_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In text to image AI models, fixing the prompt and the random seed is widely assumed to fix the image. We show that it does not on GPUs. We evaluate six text-to-image models (diffusion and autoregressive) under four numerical precisions and quantizations on three GPU types (RTX A6000, RTX 6000 Ada, and H100 NVL), across up to 100 prompts per set for all six models. For each configuration we compare, with the prompt and seed fixed, the image generated alone with the image generated inside a batch, and we also compare across GPUs. Every replay control reported here is bit-exact, so the variation is not random noise: the execution setting, mainly the batch composition and the GPU, decides the output. The effect is large and model dependent. Structural similarity between the single-image and the batched output falls to 0.580 for an autoregressive model in bf16 and to 0.551 with int8. For a second autoregressive model in bf16 it falls to 0.261 with sampled decoding and to 0.229 with greedy decoding, and for an fp8 diffusion model to 0.68. A diffusion model in bf16 stays close to the batch-1 output (0.96-0.98) but changes every image. In most cases the batched image is a different but equally valid image; in some, such as prompts with digits and counters, it is visibly worse. Cross-GPU differences are of the same order as the batch effect: for one autoregressive model in bf16 compared across two GPU architectures (RTX 6000 Ada versus RTX A6000), no image is bit-identical and the cross-GPU LPIPS (0.355 to 0.383) matches the batch effect within one GPU (0.347 to 0.381). The consequences are direct: seeds do not make results reproducible, benchmarks and image-quality comparisons that mix batch sizes or hardware can be biased, and safety audits and A/B tests on generated images need the serving setup reported.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting LLMs as Context Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30494)**  `arXiv:2611.30494`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junrui Zhang, Zeyu Xu, Runchuan Zhu, Jie Peng, Long Zhang, Zixin Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are used in more and more new tasks, context engineering becomes a fundamental method to efficiently steer LLMs by constructing instruction prompts or metadata without intensive training. A growing number of LLM context engineering (LCE) approaches automate the task by casting the LLM itself as the engineer that draws generalizable instructions or experiences from offline trials as context. Although these methods resemble how humans learn from experiences, it remains unclear \textbf{when and how} LLMs can engineer effective context from data. To answer the question, we first establish a benchmark composed of datasets with different reasoning patterns. We compared different LCE methods with different LLM families in 8B size, showing that there is no single winner for all tasks, even compared with simple baselines like chain-of-thought. Even when scaling model capabilities via more parameters or generation iterations, LCEs do not consistently boost existing performance. Across all datasets and models, the performance gains of LCEs are negatively correlated ($r=-0.5, p\le 0.05$) with the diversity of the failure modes of reasoning trajectories. Investigating how LCEs work, we find that (1) the improvement in instruction adherence can explain some success of LCEs; (2) one major bottleneck is whether LLMs can extract effective experiences out of natively diverse failures. Through this study, we uncover the limited capabilities of LCEs in abstracting principled contexts from diverse failure modes and understand how LCEs work, providing guidelines for future work.
  </details>

- **[HWRE-Bench: Can LLM Agents Understand Hardware They Did Not Design?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30422)**  `arXiv:2611.30422`  `cs.AI`  
  _Peilong Zhou, Cangyuan Li, Kaiyan Chang, Ying Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hardware benchmarks for LLM agents all run the design flow forward, from a specification to RTL, a layout or a bug fix. We ask the reverse question: given an implemented artifact, such as a layout, a gate-level netlist or a protected simulation model, can an agent work out what it does well enough to act on that knowledge? We introduce HWRE-Bench, a benchmark for agentic hardware reverse engineering built on one observation. Each step of the forward flow discards information (names, hierarchy, word-level structure, generator parameters, the programming model), and a reverse-engineering task asks the agent to recover what was lost and to prove it with an executable deliverable that the artifact alone cannot supply. Since layout extraction is now mechanical and a correct netlist is already a cycle-exact model, every task carries a mandatory copy-the-netlist control that must score zero. HWRE-Bench spans five tracks (Extract, Abstract, Audit, Interoperate, Modify), four information-loss levels and white- versus black-box access, all on original designs. We report a pilot of 148 rollouts from two agents on four seed tasks under 16 conditions, with stage-annotated trajectories. Agents built their own layout extractors and audited a sanitized layout as reliably as a netlist, at about 27% more turns. Keyless, equivalence-preserving obfuscation changed neither pass rates nor defect-repair scores. Whether agents read the structure at all depended on task framing rather than on necessity, so pass rate alone does not show understanding. Provider safety filters stopped 16% of one agent's runs on the audit tasks. These results argue for deliverables beyond equivalence and for reporting run validity explicitly.
  </details>

- **[TamperCert: A Benchmark for Detecting Text Tampering in Degree Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30419)**  `arXiv:2611.30419`  `cs.AI`  
  _ChiFung Kwan, Kangzhong Wang, Youqian Zhang, Weijie Chen, Jiaqi Gao, Yongjian Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Degree certificates play a critical role in high-stakes decisions such as university admission, immigration, and hiring. The issuing institution vouches for the holder, so a forged certificate may let its bearer pass these checks. Degree certificate forgery is increasingly prevalent. Yet, many assume that it is easy to spot. Reported cases show otherwise: in 2024, the business school of the University of Hong Kong found that about 30 master's students had enrolled with forged academic credentials. Few studies have looked at how to detect forged degree certificates, partly because public benchmarks are scarce. We present TamperCert, a benchmark built from 400 real degree certificates issued by 38 universities. Each certificate was subjected to 11 publicly available tampering methods to alter the holder's name and further processed with four additional post-processing techniques, resulting in over 20,000 forged images. We evaluate 13 detectors, a commercial multimodal large language model (MLLM), and detectors trained onthis http URLfindings reveal that no detector is reliable for every tampering method. Consequently, a determined forger may still evade detection. Moreover, the commercial MLLM misses most forgeries made by general-purpose generative models, and post-processing, such as resizing an image or photographing it with a camera, severely degrades detection.
  </details>

- **[DMC-5: Dual-Model Collaboration with Cache-aware Context Compression for Long-horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30412)**  `arXiv:2611.30412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haochen Li, Xiaobing Tu, Yinggui Wang, Jinkui Ren, Xiantao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents repeatedly feed an ever-growing interaction trajectory to a specialized agent model. Prefix caching reuses computation over repeated history, but cannot prevent context growth or remove information irrelevant to the current decision. This is especially costly when the specialized agent lacks the cached-input discounts available to general-purpose models, making trajectory compression a natural alternative. However, existing compression methods on selective context risk losing global information, recursively rewriting summary leads to semantic drift, while repeatedly recompressing the full history shifts the growing cost to the compressor. Therefore, we introduce DMC-5 (Dual-Model Collaboration with CaChe-aware Context Compression), an asymmetric framework that assigns full-history integration to a general compression model and action generation to a specialized agent model. DMC-5 maintains the raw trajectory as an Append-only Canonical View, preserving global access while allowing the compressor to reuse a stable historical prefix instead of recursively rewriting or recomputing prior context. The agent operates only on a compact Decision View with recent raw interactions, reducing repeated input while retaining the information needed for the next action. DMC-5 further incorporates YAMATO (integritY-Aware Memory Abstractions for Tool Operations) to preserve execution-critical information during lossy compression. Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and 8-objective QA show that DMC-5 reduces agent model input by 38.5-57.0% and end-to-end cost by 14.5-28.0% with 78.0-85.0% compressor cache-hit rates, while improving task performance by 1.0-5.8% over the uncompressed baseline.
  </details>

- **[MMMem: Self-Improving Agents with Task-Aware Multimodal Experience Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30287)**  `arXiv:2611.30287`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Chen, Changwei Lv, Yunfei Li, Zhongjing Du, Kehui Tan, Zijun Liao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents accumulate conversations, images, and documents through long-term interactions with users. Answering later questions often requires connecting evidence across these records, yet the resulting understanding is rarely retained, allowing mistakes to recur on related questions. Existing multimodal memory methods largely focus on storing and retrieving records, with limited mechanisms for learning from completed tasks and consolidating task-specific experience. We therefore propose MMMem, a task-aware multimodal memory framework that extracts and consolidates experience from completed tasks through four complementary strategies. First, source-linked multi-view memory organizes original text, images, and rendered pages alongside generated scene images that combine clues across records for retrieval. Second, source-generated tasks support knowledge alignment at two levels: warm-up consolidates source knowledge into task-relevant experiences in external memory, while self-training adapts the model's parametric knowledge to task demands. Third, the same vision-language model distills completed tasks into experiences that record the task context, conclusion, and support links. Finally, task-aware retrieval recalls source views and relevant experiences for answering and verification, while source-supported reuse guides long-term retention. Across three memory benchmarks and two vision-language readers, MMMem outperforms eight baselines. With Qwen3.5-9B, MMMem achieves relative gains of 36.9%, 17.6%, and 15.2% over the best-performing baselines on LoCoMo, Mem-Gallery, and LooGLE v2, respectively. Ablations show consistent gains from training and memory warm-up, while case studies illustrate how individual components work in practice.
  </details>

- **[Learning Heterogeneous Reasoning in Large Language Models: From Mode Compatibility to Local Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30281)**  `arXiv:2611.30281`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sunbowen Li, Dandan Song, Shengkun Ma, Xuanning Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training large language models routinely pools reasoning trajectories from diverse teachers or algorithms, yet it remains unclear whether standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can learn such heterogeneous strategies without explicit mode conditioning. We study the learnability and local bottlenecks of heterogeneous reasoning through a formal mixture formulation and controlled experiments. We first formalize the representational compatibility of the optimal autoregressive model with latent reasoning mixtures: the target next-token distribution weights modes by their prefix posteriors and concentrates on a single mode once alternative modes assign negligible likelihood to the prefix. In a verifiable symbolic graph-search environment with paired breadth-first and depth-first search trajectories, no consistent disadvantage is observed for unlabeled mixed SFT relative to homogeneous or labeled baselines under matched token budgets while preserving both modes. Scaling across 0.6B--14B models and data budgets largely eliminates global mode confusion, shifting the primary bottleneck to local state-tracking transitions. Because a local mistake affects the final outcome only when subsequent reasoning is sensitive to that step, we find that only a small fraction of local errors are outcome-determining \emph{Critical Reasoning Nodes (CRNs)}, and that single-step repairs at these nodes rescue failing trajectories across symbolic search and natural language benchmarks whenever the model has sufficient downstream competence to complete the remaining derivation. Our environment is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CLIP-FMoE: Scalable CLIP via Fused Mixture-of-Experts with Enforced Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30233)**  `arXiv:2611.30233`  `cs.AI`  
  _Luong Tran, Lan-Cuong Nguyen, Huynh Dang Nguyen, Dat Nguyen Cong, Dung D. Le, Van Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a promising approach for scaling deep learning models while maintaining computational efficiency. However, existing MoE adaptations for Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) models suffer from significant computational overhead during sequential training and degradation of zero-shot capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose CLIP-FMoE, a novel approach that integrates MoE architecture into CLIP fine-tuning. Our method uses Isolated Constrained Contrastive Learning, a pipeline that trains specialized experts on cluster-based data partitions to accelerate expert specialization. Additionally, we introduce a Fusion Gate mechanism to mitigate catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained knowledge. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves consistent improvements on downstream tasks while preserving zero-shot capabilities. Furthermore, our method demonstrates robust performance across varying context lengths, making it particularly suitable for diverse real-world applications.
  </details>

- **[VeriWorld: A Verifiable Visual SWE-Bench for Spatial Reasoning in 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30226)**  `arXiv:2611.30226`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yan Zheng, ShengYun Peng, Xuyao Liang, Xiaoyan Cong, Nuo Chen, Bangya Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used for spatial tasks such as navigation and manipulation, where they must infer geometry and object relationships from visual input in order to act correctly. Existing benchmarks usually test perception and reasoning in isolation, making it hard to tell whether failures come from visual understanding, downstream reasoning, or the bridge between them. We introduce VeriWorld, a benchmark for spatial reasoning in interactive 3D environments with executable actions and deterministic verification. For task families where all three channels are well-defined, we evaluate matched visual, structured, and combined input conditions on the same instance, environment, and verifier; families without a clean visual/structured/combined split are reported separately. This design supports controlled comparisons of perception, reasoning, and their interaction. Using this setup, we uncover two diagnostic findings. First, models often perform much better when task-relevant information, such as object identity, current state, or target location, is given explicitly than when it must be read from the rendered scene, revealing a recurring visual-to-structure gap. Second, action-space and harness design can change outcomes even under the same task and information condition, showing that interaction protocol is itself a confounding variable. VeriWorld is open-sourced to support reproducible and fine-grained evaluation of spatial reasoning in VLMs.
  </details>

- **[AutoSECO: Automated Skill Evolution for Cross-Problem Combinatorial Optimization with General-Purpose LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30139)**  `arXiv:2611.30139`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Zhu, Cong Zhang, Ni Zhang, Kuan Xu, Zhiguang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose large language model (LLM) agents can develop complete solvers for combinatorial optimization problems. Their specialization, however, often relies on workflows engineered for individual problems, limiting the reuse of development expertise. We propose AutoSECO, an automated skill evolution method that learns reusable solver-development guidance from experience. The challenge is that external evaluation measures the completed solver, while the skill guides decisions during development. AutoSECO connects this feedback to guidance deficiencies by tracing performance shortfalls through solver code and recorded development decisions. Revisions are expressed as general instructions and retained only if a fresh solver developed under the revised skill achieves a lower mean evaluation gap. Evolved solely on the traveling salesperson problem (TSP), the skill guides fresh agents in developing solvers for capacitated vehicle routing, job-shop scheduling, and three-dimensional bin packing without further skill evolution. On TSP, the final mean benchmark gap is 0.52\%, compared with 0.98\% for the skill-evolution baseline SkillOpt. Across all four problems, the evolved skill achieves better aggregate results than no skill guidance, the initial skill, and SkillOpt. The same frozen skill also supports solver development under alternative model--harness configurations. These findings suggest that solver-development experience can be retained as reusable expertise for general-purpose LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[WASP: Continual Weight Adaptation for Streaming LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30134)**  `arXiv:2611.30134`  `cs.AI`  
  _Linxi Xie, Chonghua Liao, Mengxuan Wu, Guandao Yang, Zhangyang Wang, Kai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized large language models (LLMs) should continually adapt to users' evolving preferences. Yet extracting preference changes from noisy interaction histories while supporting inexpensive updates remains challenging. We introduce WASP (Weight Adaptation for Streaming Personalization), a recurrent framework that captures fine-grained preferences directly from raw user interactions and generates personalized LoRA adapters without expensive per-user optimization. Specifically, WASP achieves this by training a shared hypernetwork that maintains a compact user representation and outputs adapter weights in a single forward pass. As new interactions arrive, WASP updates this representation without reprocessing full interaction history, enabling continual adaptation with per-update costs independent of history length. Experiments on personalization benchmarks and chronological interaction streams show that WASP sustains high-quality personalization over long horizons under fixed compute and storage budgets. Together, these results support efficient streaming personalization and facilitate privacy-preserving on-device deployment.
  </details>

- **[Could This BE Any More Wrong? Teaching VLMs to Know What They Don't Know in Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30119)**  `arXiv:2611.30119`  `cs.AI`  
  _Varad Srivastava, Victor Casas Hernandez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models can extract structured fields from scanned financial documents, yet they provide no indication of when a field might be wrong. We derive per-field confidence estimates from the token log-probabilities the model already produces, via a token-to-span alignment procedure that maps logprobs onto each extracted value within the structured JSON output. Evaluated on two benchmarks (CORD v2 receipts, Kleister NDA legal contracts) with three VLM families, the minimum token probability per field achieves Area Under the ROC Curve (AUROC) 0.74-0.91 for error detection, far exceeding verbalized confidence (0.53-0.56). The uncertain fields form a natural training curriculum: fine-tuning via Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) improves accuracy from 78.5% to 87.5% on CORD (67 examples) and from 70.7% to 75.1% on multi-page NDA contracts (259 examples), while substantially improving error detection on Kleister NDA (AUROC 0.70 to 0.85, Area Under the Accuracy-Rejection Curve (AUARC) 0.78 to 0.92). The loop requires no annotation beyond the training-split ground truth, demonstrating that confidence-guided curriculum selection transfers to complex multi-page documents when combined with native multi-image training. A frontier model (Claude Sonnet 4) used as a pseudo-label judge achieves ~92% reward agreement with ground truth on both benchmarks, suggesting this loop can operate without human annotation.
  </details>

- **[Post-Training Agents for Long-Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30090)**  `arXiv:2611.30090`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sushant Mehta, Logan Ritchie, Edwin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Professional documents are a long-context workload that retrieval-style benchmarks do not capture. In a ten-domain enterprise collection, a task's evidence is spread across a source document of median length 27 pages (mean 48.8, maximum 873); a third of tasks rest mainly on charts, drawings, and maps; and 70.9% require two or more reasoning hops. We post-train Kimi-K2.7, an open-weight foundation model, at its full context window on 1,465 expert-authored tasks with atomic rubrics from that collection, training rank-32 adapters with one round of privileged-context on-policy self-distillation followed by two rounds of rubric-reward reinforcement learning. We render each PDF as text for training and as extracted text plus selected page images at evaluation. The smoothed RL training reward rises from 0.37 in the first tenth of the run to 0.67 in the last. On the 100-taskthis http URLholdout, served in a 262,144-token window, strict pass (all rubric criteria met) more than doubles after the second round, from 11.0% to 24.5%, with gains across input lengths up to 240K tokens. Mean tokens generated stay flat (9,803 to 9,733), so the gain does not come from longer outputs. GPT-5.6 Sol's leaderboard strict pass is 30.7% at a measured 3,422 tokens. On 220 out-of-domain GDPval tasks, where the model runs as a long-horizon tool-using agent, mean rubric reward rises from 0.571 to 0.746 after the third round and zero-credit tasks fall from 54 to 10, while messages per task fall 18%, from 70.9 to 58.2. In paired trajectories, the third round replaces a context-thrashing retry loop with one up-front check of all sources, and verification extends from checking that files exist to checking what they contain. We argue that long-context evaluation of agents should report accuracy jointly with tokens and turns, and that datasets of long documents should ship text and image renderings alongside each PDF.
  </details>

- **[EVD-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for EEG-to-Image Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30061)**  `arXiv:2611.30061`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kieren Yu, Qian Zhang, Ziyang Liu, Chang Huang, Jintai Chen, Kaishun Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EEG-to-image decoding recovers visual information from non-invasive brain recordings, providing a window into human perception. However, comparisons across systems are confounded by a variable that is rarely reported as such: which participants' data the system was allowed to use. The same Top-1 score on a 200-way gallery may come from a per-participant system, a pooled model over all participants, or a model that never saw the test participant---cases that differ by more than most architectural changes between the methods being compared. We present \bench, a benchmark on THINGS-EEG2 that standardizes the gallery and EEG input, matches training budgets across systems within each regime, and compares three participant-access regimes: within-subject, joint-subject, and strict leave-one-subject-out, with unlabeled target calibration reported separately. All three settings evaluate concept-level zero-shot retrieval: the 200 test concepts are disjoint from the 1,654 concepts used for EEG--image fitting and validation. We compare retrieval across access regimes and use zero-EEG and matched-wrong EEG controls to assess dependence on correct EEG--stimulus pairing. We vary trial averaging, gallery size and difficulty, and training data coverage, and compare target adaptation and visual feature targets. We also assess sensitivity to EEG window length and channel count, robustness to input perturbations, and confidence calibration. These results establish participant-access regime as a necessary reporting dimension alongside gallery, repetition, and visual target.
  </details>

- **[Reward Blindness: A Theoretical Framework for Diagnosing Reinforcement Learning Reward Limitations in Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29930)**  `arXiv:2611.29930`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lakshmi Muraleedharan Nair, Shiqi Huang, Fangyu Lo, Yihua Xu, Dingyan Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems let users without programming expertise query relational databases in natural language. Reinforcement learning has become the dominant way to train such systems, yet progress is still judged by a single number, execution accuracy, which counts correct queries but says nothing about which kinds of semantic error survive training. This paper develops a theoretical framework that examines text-to-SQL from the perspective of reward design. We introduce Reward Error Sensitivity, a measure of how strongly a reward function separates queries that contain a given error type from queries that do not, and prove a Reward Blindness theorem: when this sensitivity is zero, policy-gradient training carries no systematic signal for removing that error. Using fourteen published finegrained error signals for four text-to-SQL systems on the BIRD benchmark, we build error profiles and estimate sensitivities for common reward designs. Predicate and result-level errors are the most frequent, firing on up to 27 percent of queries, while structurally deep errors such as suboptimal joins persist at 5 to 12 percent. Each reward family has a distinct and predictable set of blind spots, and inexpensive database-level checks that need no language model calls detect 45 to 60 percent of semantic errors. Controlled training simulations confirm that executiononly reward leaves output-preserving errors untouched, whereas a signal-grounded reward removes them even when the detectors are noisy.
  </details>

- **[Weight Averaging at Small Budgets Confounds Formula-Labeled Pretraining in Microstructure-Property Regression: A Pre-Specified Study with Post Hoc Rechecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29876)**  `arXiv:2611.29876`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jonghyun Lee, Soojung Kang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-form physics formulas can label synthetic microstructures at no cost, so supervised pretraining on those labels is an alternative to self-supervised pretraining when labels are scarce. One pre-specified protocol tests this on two public simulated benchmarks, two-phase conductivity and short-fiber stiffness: a ConvNeXt-Small is pretrained on 150,000 synthetic images by formula regression or masked reconstruction and fine-tuned on 100, 400, or 1,600 labels. Five fine-tuning seeds from one formula checkpoint have mean residual correlations of 0.994 (conductivity) and 0.999 (stiffness). On conductivity the formula network compresses its predictions (slope 0.64) and has four times the 400-label error of ImageNet initialization. Two confounds interact: the generator renders the low-conductivity phase with the opposite contrast polarity, and the exponential moving average (EMA; decay 0.995, no bias correction) still holds 16% of the initial weights after the 400-label schedule. Without the EMA the slope is 0.97, and over three subsets and five seeds the mismatched checkpoint has 1.4 times the ImageNet error (0.0119 versus 0.0084). Three pretraining runs per polarity leave the comparison inconclusive: matched means (0.0086, 0.0111, 0.0118) fall below mismatched means (0.0119, 0.0120, 0.0123) in aggregate (0.0105 versus 0.0121) but overlap on two of three subsets, and the matched spread (0.0032) exceeds the mismatched spread (0.0004). On stiffness without the EMA, formula pretraining lowers the 100-label error by 18% versus ImageNet over five seeds, including a re-initialized head, while ImageNet seeds also correlate at 0.992. Weight averages that start at the pretrained weights can dominate small-budget comparisons and should be rechecked at the last weights.
  </details>

- **[Who Sent It or How It Moved? Sender Shortcuts in Cryptocurrency Anti-Money-Laundering Benchmarks, Audited on Elliptic2 and Elliptic++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29744)**  `arXiv:2611.29744`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammad Nasir Uddin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two public benchmarks dominate graph-based anti-money-laundering (AML) research on Bitcoin: the transaction-level Elliptic dataset, extended as Elliptic++, and the subgraph-level Elliptic2. In both, a label is assigned according to the entity that sends the funds. We ask whether models trained on these benchmarks learn how laundering moves or merely who sent the money, and we propose a diagnostic protocol to tell the two apart: a sender lookup baseline, a novelty baseline, a sender-disjoint split with a leakage check, and a decomposition of performance into sender, receiver and intrinsic signal. On Elliptic2 the task largely reduces to classifying the sender. Under the standard random split, a no-learning lookup of sender history reaches a PR-AUC of 0.954, against 0.975 for gradient boosting on 456 features; our reimplementation of RevClassify reproduces its reported 0.97 and falls to 0.126 without sender information. When senders are held out, performance drops to 0.746, and the subgraph's own structure, transactions and receivers fall from 0.619 to 0.186. On Elliptic++ the same protocol finds the shortcut negligible: sender history reaches only 0.340 against 0.986 for the full model (3.5 against 10.1 times the test prevalence), adding it to the full model raises PR-AUC by less than 0.004 in every seed, and the transaction's own features keep 0.883 when sending addresses are held out. We trace the difference to the unit of sender identity: Elliptic2 senders are entity clusters that recur across many labeled subgraphs, whereas Elliptic++ senders are individual addresses, and fewer than half of test transactions have a sending address seen in training. A shared sender-based label rule thus produces a severe shortcut in one benchmark and a negligible one in the other, so the diagnostic must be run on each benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Fin-RSI: A Unified Library of Financial Skills, Checks, and Self-Improvement for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29716)**  `arXiv:2611.29716`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanghao Ye, Shwai He, Sihan Chen, Yiting Wang, Bowei Tian, Guoheng Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) become more capable, financial agents need tools they can readily discover and use. Yet financial software is fragmented across libraries with different interfaces and conventions, making it difficult to build reliable agent workflows. We present Fin-RSI, a unified Python library of financial skills, checks, and self-improvement designed for LLM agents. It brings together tools for market analysis, forecasting, portfolio construction, and trading research through shared interfaces across models and agent frameworks. Agents can find suitable tools from natural-language requests, reuse tested implementations, and improve generated programs through checking and feedback. Developers can extend the library with new tools without rebuilding integrations for each model. Across 20 open-weight models on six trading-position tasks, tool discovery increased the proportion of episodes producing valid programs from 9.7% to 29.2%; for models with at least 14 billion parameters, it rose from 23.6% to 64.6%. We release the library and benchmark as a shared resource for the community and plan to expand its tools and task coverage as financial agents evolve.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Closed-Ended Medical VLM Evaluation: From Stable Wrongness to Counterfactual Answer Sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29592)**  `arXiv:2611.29592`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qi Wu, Xiaoyu Liu, Ao Wang, Rongsheng Wang, Wenting Chen, Wenxuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As medical VLMs are increasingly evaluated for clinical decision-support scenarios, knowing when to trust their predictions is critical.
