Closed-ended medical VQA has become a common benchmark format because it enables automated scoring. Yet, systematic investigation into whether this format changes the reliability problem remains scarce. We identify a failure mode that we call stable wrongness: models can produce incorrect yes/no answers that remain confident and repeatable even when patient-specific visual evidence is removed or mismatched. This failure violates the premise behind sampling-stability uncertainty methods, including recent hallucination-aware calibration (HAC) variants: disagreement is informative only when errors are unstable. When shortcut-driven errors are themselves stable, sampling agreement can remain deceptively high. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce Counterfactual Answer Sensitivity (CAS), which shifts reliability estimation from answer stability to evidence sensitivity. CAS uses cross-modal counterfactual interventions, such as text-only, black, and corrupted images, to audit whether a committed yes/no answer remains supported when visual evidence is invalidated. Across 7 VLMs and 3 benchmarks, CAS outperforms the best HAC-family scores across all evaluated settings, achieving a macro-averaged AUROC gain of +0.150 for error detection. Mechanistic analysis further shows that CAS recovers hidden errors in the HAC-confident region and that its strongest standalone signal is counterfactual decision-boundary shift rather than hard prediction stability. Our findings suggest that closed-ended evaluation should test visual dependence, not only answer stability, in safety-critical medical contexts.
  </details>

- **[Layer-Level Control of Language Model Execution in Agent Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29519)**  `arXiv:2611.29519`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ran Guan, Yiqi Lin, Xiangxi Tian, Yige Luo, Mi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent frameworks typically orchestrate language models through prompt-and-response interfaces. We propose a harness design that exposes intermediate model states and layer-level execution controls to the agent runtime. By connecting state collection, policy fitting, and execution, the design enables policies to allocate computation within and across models based on intermediate states. We demonstrate the approach with a prototype that learns stitching bridges and state-conditioned execution policies from paired model states, then applies them during inference to select among continue, exit, and stitch. For dispatcher supervision, we select the least-computation action whose full-vocabulary next-token prediction matches the source model's full-depth prediction, using continue when neither early action matches. We train these components on a frozen proxy corpus of 5,000 typed-decision items authored using source texts sampled from WildChat conversations, then evaluate the prototype on JevBench measuring task accuracy, agreement with full-model predictions, and end-to-end latency including runtime overhead. Preliminary results show an approximately 15% reduction in mean latency while maintaining task accuracy comparable to full-model inference.
  </details>

- **[Neural Agent Compiler: Learning to Schedule Soft Dependencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29262)**  `arXiv:2611.29262`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Yan, Peng Wang, Zihan Chen, Ziwen Kan, Zhen Tan, Huiyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents accomplish complex tasks by coordinating multiple reasoning steps and tool operations, which form dependencies among their operations. Existing orchestration methods primarily consider hard dependencies that determine when an operation can legally begin, but overlook soft dependencies, i.e., information that is not required for execution but may improve an already executable operation. We identify soft-dependency scheduling as an important problem in agent execution. To this end, we introduce Neural Agent Compiler (NAC), a learned scheduler that predicts the task-quality and resource consequences of three execution modes and selects the highest-utility legal mode at a scheduling boundary. NAC represents the executable continuation, pending information event, and resource state in a shared memory, and uses a utility function to adapt the consequence predictions to different resource preferences. Across AppWorld, WebShop, and $\tau^2$-bench, NAC achieves 276/417 AppWorld task completions, a 74.69 WebShop Task Score, and 59/100 $\tau^2$-bench successes. Additional studies show that the learned scheduler reduces execution work, establishing soft-dependency scheduling as a promising solution for orchestrating tool-using agents.
  </details>

- **[The Retry Tax: Why Static Same-Task Retries Can Cost More per Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29133)**  `arXiv:2611.29133`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei-Jung Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrying an agent after failure can complete more tasks while increasing the cost per completion. We study a static policy that stops at verified success or an attempt cap and otherwise resets the original prompt and workspace without passing feedback to the next request. Under stationary task success probabilities, independent attempts conditional on the task, and a common attempt cost, easier tasks tend to succeed earlier. When task success probabilities differ, the unresolved mix shifts toward harder tasks. Specializing classical survivor selection, we show that cost per completion is at least as high as in one-shot serving and that marginal cost per additional completion is nondecreasing wherever the completion increments are positive. These are different quantities: one averages spending over all completions, while the other prices the completions added by another attempt. We then arrange each task's existing benchmark runs in different orders, stop each sequence at verified success or the attempt cap, and average the resulting completion and spending at each supported cap. For one OpenHands configuration, replay estimates completion of 59.7 percent with one attempt and 70.0 percent with four, while cost per completion rises from about USD 0.70 to USD 1.32. The recorded trade-off describes fixed configurations on the available tasks. Choosing a cap requires comparing the additional spending with the application's value for the additional completions.
  </details>

- **[PruneNAS: Constraint Aware Neural Architecture Search based Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28964)**  `arXiv:2611.28964`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sudhakar Sah, Nikhil Chhabra, Honnesh Rohmetra, Andy Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Architecture Search (NAS) has achieved state-of-the-art performance across diverse tasks, but its high computational cost remains a major barrier. We introduce PruneNAS, a novel framework that integrates structured pruning into NAS to efficiently explore high-performing architectures while leveraging pretrained models. Unlike conventional NAS methods that train each candidate from scratch, PruneNAS generates a candidate pool by pruning pretrained networks at multiple granularity levels and formulates the selection as a global optimization problem using training-free importance metrics. This approach enables rapid evaluation of candidate architectures under strict resource constraints, dramatically reducing search time from hundreds of GPU hours to mere minutes. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that PruneNAS discovers compact, high-accuracy architectures that nearly halve the computational cost (MACs) of prior methods on ResNet-50, while also showing strong generalization on object detection tasks with YOLO. Our method bridges structured pruning and NAS, offering a practical solution for resource-constrained model deployment.
  </details>

- **[OmniDecisionBench: Evaluating and Benchmarking the Multimodal Jev-Style System One Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28924)**  `arXiv:2611.28924`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianxiang Gao, Jinzhe Li, Yue Zhou, Dandan Guo, Qi Wang, Diana Inkpen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal System One models answer visual tasks with typed decisions and native probabilities, yet no holistic evaluation compares them with general VLMs or maps either class's capability boundaries. We introduce OmniDecisionBench, a holistic benchmark of multimodal System One models. Its main evaluation assigns 45 datasets (192,975 records) to five capability families and twelve primary angles across four typed interfaces, scoring accuracy and candidate-probability calibration. A validation layer adds three single-factor diagnostics varying visual presentation, textual evidence sufficiency, and target dataset. Across three System One models and two general VLMs, three regularities emerge. First, strengths depend on angle and metric: System One models lead discrete tasks overall with lower ECE15 on most perception, reading, and reasoning angles (Jev 1.14: 80.14% accuracy, 4.50% ECE15), while general VLMs lead ordinal grading and outperform Jev 1.14 among the three models tested on direct Box grounding. Second, multi-image and abstract reasoning bound every model, where confidence rather than accuracy separates them. Third, reliability shifts with the input: rendering only options nearly triples Jev 1.14's selective risk at nearly flat mean signed bias, and donor risk cutoffs often exceed target risk across unseen datasets and task families. OmniDecisionBench thus maps which model class suits each angle and where reliability ends. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Guarding with Judgment: Learning to Intervene for Multimodal Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28849)**  `arXiv:2611.28849`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jipei He, Cheng Cheng, Liuguo Wang, Zihan Wen, Changlin Yu, Wentao Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When visual context makes an ordinary request unsafe to answer as asked, a blanket refusal may leave the user's need unresolved. Choosing how to help requires distinguishing an established hazard from a condition that still needs verification, a distinction that binary safety labels leave unspecified. We formulate this decision through four actions: allow, warn, review, and refuse. To ground these decisions in verifiable evidence, we build a corpus of 20,126 training audits and the JudiBench benchmark, connecting each action to diagnostic judgments, source evidence, and controlled context changes. Our guard, JudiAct, separately states what the source establishes, what condition governs the help, and how the rules apply. It then checks these three brief review items against the original inputs to predict diagnostic labels and a handling action. Supervised training learns individual judgments while comparing label assignments across related cases. A second stage swaps one review item at a time, using the resulting audit feedback to refine review generation. On JudiBench, JudiAct improves request and answer Macro-F1 by more than 28 percentage points over its 4B backbone. Across nine public benchmarks, it outperforms state-of-the-art guards on the mean delivered-answer score in both pre-generation and post-generation settings, cutting MM-SafetyBench attack success from 40.78% to 25.30%.
  </details>

- **[EA-Bench: Measuring information and action efficiency under imperfect instructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28846)**  `arXiv:2611.28846`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xu Wan, Wenyue Xu, Shenjie Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personal agents help users complete everyday work across digital applications. Many standard benchmarks assume that users provide sufficient information to solve a task. However, agents often encounter incomplete, distracting, or inconsistent information in practice. To systematically study agent inefficiency under such imperfect information, we introduce Efficient Action (EA)-Bench, which evaluates both tool calls and user interactions in work-related tasks. To our knowledge, it is the first benchmark to jointly evaluate tool-call efficiency and user-interaction efficiency under controlled missing, redundant, and conflicting information. We construct 63 base-task instances adapted from existing work-related benchmarks. To guide perturbation design and quantify task requirements, we represent workflows as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) and examine node-level dependencies to identify where information can be withheld, contradicted, or supplemented with distractors. We use a two-stage validation process combining AI-assisted checks with expert screening to select 232 conditions from 792 initial candidates. We organize these tasks into Easy and Hard tracks by task complexity. Experiments show that imperfect information can reduce accuracy and increase tool-use and user-interaction costs. More concerning, several models obtain apparently correct outcomes by guessing user-specific values without confirmation, revealing potential execution unreliability in current models under imperfect user information.
  </details>

- **[Agent Money Benchmark: Can AI Agents Be Trusted to Spend Money?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28610)**  `arXiv:2611.28610`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Chen, Emma Hsieh, Vicky Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Giving an AI agent a wallet lets it buy data and services on a client's behalf. Trust then depends on how it spends money and what that spending achieves. We introduce Agent Money Benchmark (AMB) to test whether agents spend a client's money well, starting with purchases of information for financial decisions. We link seller quotes and payments to final answers, evaluating 12 models on 80 payment tasks with simulated sellers and 76 research questions using live data. Purchases settled in USDC on Solana mainnet via x402, a protocol for paying per web request. Three of the 12 models passed 79 of 80 payment tasks without payment or budget errors. Nine fell for a seller's trap at least once; repeat charges and above-list quotes accounted for 79% of scam events. We estimate waste from data and inference costs, counting failed payment tasks in full and research answers in proportion to missed points. With spending split equally between tracks, estimated waste ranged from \$21.0 per \$100 for Claude Fable 5.2 to \$70.8 for GPT-6 Luna.
  </details>

- **[X-AQA: Unifying Action Quality Assessment with Multimodal Large Language Models and Multi-Domain Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28548)**  `arXiv:2611.28548`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaili Zheng, Wei Ding, Yuchen Ding, Kaiwen Wang, Xun Zhu, Chenyi Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action Quality Assessment (AQA) aims to evaluate the quality of human actions, yet existing methods are predominantly developed and evaluated on individual datasets with different task formulations and assessment criteria. We introduce X-AQA, the first unified training and evaluation framework for general-purpose AQA with multimodal large language models (MLLMs). We construct X-AQA-40K, a training corpus comprising 40,163 samples from 22 data sources across four domains: competitive sports, fitness exercises, rehabilitation, and general skills. We convert heterogeneous annotations into a unified question-answering format, incorporating dataset-specific annotation guidelines and official scoring rules when available. Using this corpus, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B to develop a single MLLM capable of handling diverse AQA tasks. We further establish X-AQA-Bench, which evaluates both in-distribution (ID) performance on datasets represented during training and out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization on four held-out datasets. The resulting model outperforms all evaluated open-source MLLMs on X-AQA-Bench and achieves competitive performance relative to proprietary MLLMs. Ablation studies further demonstrate that multi-domain joint training and explicitly providing assessment criteria in prompts both improve OOD generalization. X-AQA establishes a unified foundation for advancing general-purpose AQA across diverse real-world domains. Data and models are released at \hyperlink{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[Diversity over Scale: Recursive Self-Improvement with Ensembles of Small Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28420)**  `arXiv:2611.28420`  `cs.AI`  
  _Navapat Nananukul, Mayank Kejriwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of large language models (LLMs) has significantly enhanced the capabilities of agents across various tasks. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) loops, in which a language model repeatedly rewrites a program and builds on its previous version, now perform well in machine-learning tasks, but they rely on frontier models and hundreds of calls per run. Small open-weight models are far cheaper, yet when used alone, their programs often crash and their improvements stall. We propose DiVE (diverse models evolve), an ensemble method for RSI that aims to improve creativity and diversity of generated solutions. DiVE uses three components: a shared memory, a failure log, and best-idea-wins rounds. This allows models to write candidate solutions from the same context and retain the best idea at each iteration. On 22 MLE-bench Lite competitions, an ensemble of five 8-12B open-weight models has 25.4% less test error than its average member and 16.7% less than Llama 3.3 70B, and comes within 7.1% of Qwen 3.5 122B-A10B at 75% of its cost. Diversity among small models can thus stand in for part of the scale that RSI relies on.
  </details>

- **[Schedule, Rank, Profile: Continuous Evaluation of LLM Agents in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28368)**  `arXiv:2611.28368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Martyna Plomecka, Yao Yan, DJ Sterling, Jon Lipovetz, John Schultz, Michael Aaron, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Games are a robust test for large language model agents: they require sequential decisions, adversarial or cooperative interaction, state tracking, reasoning under uncertainty, and long-horizon planning. However, a one-time game benchmark can go stale as models, prompts, environment implementations, and evaluation policies change, and refreshing it is costly: full round-robin evaluation scales poorly in the number of model pairs, and the resulting outcomes live on incompatible per-game scales. We present a framework for continuous evaluation of LLM agents in game settings, organized around three coupled components. First, an adaptive scheduler that maps pairwise Bradley-Terry uncertainty to reduced per-pair match targets, concentrating budget on comparisons that remain unresolved. Second, a Unified Leaderboard (ULB) that reduces heterogeneous outcomes to pairwise evidence and fits a game-balanced Bradley-Terry model by minorization-maximization with bootstrap intervals, so that high-throughput games cannot dominate the aggregate ranking. Third, a game skill-profile methodology that scores each game along diverse skill dimensions, making the capability coverage of the suite explicit and turning game selection into a design decision rather than an artifact of availability. In this paper, we specify the research design and outcome reduction of the current implementation and evaluate the framework on a ten-game suite spanning perfect-information board games, imperfect-information games, and social/language settings, reporting LLM capability profiles, evaluation-cost savings across model-refresh cycles, and an empirical Pareto frontier relating model performance to inference cost. As deployed, the scheduler saved 61.7% of episodes; it counted each episode in the two team games as four records, overstating their savings, and the other eight games save 61.9%.
  </details>

- **[SMAD: A Semantics-Guided Multi-Agent Framework for Unsupervised Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28348)**  `arXiv:2611.28348`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qinghua Liu, Junpeng Wang, Mingzhi Hu, Menghai Pan, Shubham Jain, Yuzhong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) lacks a one-size-fits-all detector for heterogeneous data and anomaly types. Existing automation is limited by implicit objectives, potentially unreliable historical knowledge, or high computational costs. More fundamentally, it selects how to detect anomalies without explicitly defining what to detect. We propose SMAD, an LLM-based multi-agent framework that translates anomaly intent into executable, testable detection pipelines. Rather than selecting detectors against an implicit target, SMAD establishes a natural-language semantic contract grounded in measurable time-series properties, specifying target event patterns, expected score responses, and validation questions. This contract governs both detector construction and evaluation: LLM agents propose pipelines, while contract-matched synthetic interventions provide label-free evidence from the target series to assess semantic coverage and guide iterative self-refinement. A semantics-aware ensemble integrates validated candidates and attributes each alert to contributing detectors and supporting evidence. On univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks, SMAD improves detection performance over the strongest baseline by 11.5% and 9.6%, respectively, while outperforming general-purpose coding agents at 2-5x lower language-model token cost.
  </details>

- **[A Nepali-Accented English Evaluation Dataset for Automatic Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28263)**  `arXiv:2611.28263`  `cs.AI`  
  _Santosh Dahal, Kiran Chandra Dahal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems perform strongly on native-English benchmarks, yet their accuracy degrades sharply when the input speech comes from under represented non-native accents. Nepali-accented English is particularly under-served: existing resources either focus on native Nepali speech, cover broader multi-accent settings without dedicated Nepali evaluation, or provide only limited Nepali-accent coverage. This paper presents a Nepali-accented English evaluation dataset designed to support robust ASR benchmarking under accent mismatch. The corpus was collected through a web-based platform that did not collect directly identifying metadata and contains recordings from 57 speakers. Each session follows a fixed 22-prompt protocol consisting of 11 phonetic prompts, 10 domain prompts, and 1 spontaneous prompt, providing complementary coverage of pronunciation, topical vocabulary, and natural speaking style. In addition to transcribed speech, the dataset includes participant metadata for coarse exploratory subgroup analysis and speaker-level manual recording-quality labels. Manual quality assessment shows that 50.9% of sessions are clean and 42.1% contain only mild noise. As a descriptive reference, open-source ASR baselines are substantially worse on this corpus than the corresponding LibriSpeech test-clean values reported in official model cards, reaching 38.15-55.00% WER on the collected set versus reported 2-4% WER on LibriSpeech test-clean. These baseline results position the corpus as a practical held-out resource for evaluating accent robustness and out-of-distribution generalization on Nepali-accented English.
  </details>

- **[Demystifying the Autoresearch Harness: How Context and Skills Shape Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28244)**  `arXiv:2611.28244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kelvin Kiu-Wai Tam, Newt Hue-Nam K. Nguyen, Tianshi Zheng, Yunxiang Mo, Weihan Li, Zhongwei Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoresearch agents, which autonomously design, run, and refine experiments, are accelerating scientific progress, particularly in AI research. However, existing agents rely on harnesses that combine multiple mechanisms, making it difficult to determine how individual components contribute to performance. We systematically study harness design for long-horizon, self-evolving autoresearch agents along two dimensions: context management, which determines what an agent retains from its past attempts, and skill management, which governs how it stores and reuses lessons from those attempts. Across MLE-Bench and KernelBench with gpt-oss-120b, gpt-6-luna, and gpt-6-sol, we isolate the effects of individual harness design choices by evaluating each against a recency-truncation baseline within a shared agent framework. For context management, we find that preserving individually accessible records of past attempts is more effective than maintaining a rolling summary, with context folding performing best across all six settings and improving upon the baseline by up to 19.2%. For skill management, selectively discarding persistently unused lessons is more effective on average than consolidating them or promoting them to standing rules. Building on these findings, we combine context folding and skill deletion in MLEvolver, which achieves a 72.7% medal rate on MLE-Bench Lite and a 94% $\mathrm{fast}_1$ score on KernelBench Level 3, substantially outperforming AIDE and OpenEvolve under the same inference budget. Overall, our study distills design principles for preserving experimental history and curating reusable lessons in autoresearch harnesses, paving the way for agents that turn accumulated experience into sustained progress.
  </details>

- **[FinEvo: Recursive Self-Improvement for Long-Horizon Financial Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28239)**  `arXiv:2611.28239`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengjun Pan, Shichun Liu, Zekai Zhou, Shihan Dou, Jiahang Lin, Chengsong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in large language models (LLMs) have expanded their role in high-value productivity tasks, including financial analysis and modeling, while enabling models to play an increasingly active role in their own improvement. This motivates two complementary lines of inquiry: can financial modeling capabilities provide a foundation for model self-evolution, and can self-evolution, in turn, advance financial capabilities? We introduce FinEvo, a self-evolution framework that integrates autonomous experience construction with financial capability improvement. FinEvo prepares financial models, iteratively evolves challenges through self-play, and learns from the resulting interactions using executable rewards, relying on the same model throughout. An end-to-end execution with Qwen3.6-27B shows that solving feedback supports both increasing reasoning demands and identifying specification issues that executable checks can miss. Learning from these experiences raises whole-task success from 22.73 to 29.09 on held-out within-source tasks and from 13.13 to 19.19 on SpreadsheetBench 2 Financial. Comparable gains across task sources and evaluation settings, together with improvement on a task category absent from training, suggest that FinEvo strengthens underlying financial modeling capabilities shared across tasks. These findings support self-evolution as a path toward broader financial competence, advancing the empirical exploration of recursive self-improvement (RSI) in professional domains. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoPILOT: A Visual Agent Harness for Real-Time Gameplay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28202)**  `arXiv:2611.28202`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mark Zhao, Pengcheng Wang, Rui Yang, Jiarui Yao, Xirui Jiang, Yuxi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal foundation models can play video games well when the game waits for them, but often fail when it keeps running. We find that much of this gap lies in the harness, which decides what the model sees, what it remembers, and how its outputs become actions over time. Existing harnesses such as VISTA improve the first two, yet turn each model call into at most one action, so the agent acts only as often as its model thinks. We introduce AutoPILOT, which changes the third: in each model call, the model can send a primitive action, start an open-loop macro, or write a closed-loop controller that keeps playing from the screen without further model calls until it hands control back. To evaluate agents in real-time games, we build AFK-Bench, 120 diverse browser games grouped by how they respond to inaction and playable in real time or with the clock paused. With GPT-6.1 Sol under the same budgets, AutoPILOT reaches 68.6% real-time success against 38.3% for VISTA, and on GameWorld it keeps 80.0% in real time while VISTA drops from 84.1% to 42.9%. Its gains hold across models, and it reaches VISTA's final success rate at a fifth of its API cost. Our results suggest that how a harness turns model outputs into actions over time is a central yet underexplored part of agent design, and we hope AutoPILOT and AFK-Bench will serve as a foundation for agents in a world that does not wait.
  </details>

- **[WePM: Hierarchical Conflict Reconciliation for Unified MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27365)**  `arXiv:2611.27365`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqiang Yuan, Qiwei Yan, Liwei Yang, Jinzhe Liu, Yang Liu, Zhongyan Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bringing multimodal intelligence from the cloud to users' devices keeps sensitive data local and reduces inference cost, but deploying a specialized model per capability duplicates vision-language capacity under tight memory and compute budgets. We formalize heterogeneous capability density (HCD), measuring how broadly and effectively a model serves heterogeneous task families per unit of resident inference memory. High HCD across representation, generation, and decision raises two challenges: these capabilities need visual evidence at different spatial granularities under a fixed token budget, and separately collected task corpora yield conflicting updates to shared parameters, while cross-task paired supervision is costly and ambiguous for open-ended inputs. We present WePM, a family of pocket models unifying representation, generation, and decision within one vision-language backbone. Multi-Granularity Visual Budgeting (MGVB) allocates a fixed visual-token budget across global, regional, and fine-grained banks; Unified Listwise Knowledge Distillation (ULKD) enriches separately collected data with soft teacher guidance, avoiding cross-task paired annotation; Stable and Efficient Multitask Optimization (SEMO) regulates each task's contribution through explicit loss weights and exposure schedules, with sequential gradient accumulation under memory constraints. In the joint representation-generation setting, WePM outperforms all baselines across five evaluation groups, raising the Joint score from 38.15 to 52.74. In single-task evaluation, WePM-4B attains the best average score over 30 embedding, 39 generation, and seven typed-decision benchmarks (72.86, 62.41, 61.11). Against the separate-model stack with the highest HCD, WePM achieves slightly higher balanced performance with 61.9% less resident weight memory, a 2.64-fold higher HCD.
  </details>

- **[AppSci-Bench: Can LLMs Build AI Agents and Make Them Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25360)**  `arXiv:2611.25360`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sanjari Srivastava, Manpreet Kaur, Cheng Chang, Sofía Ramós Anton, Oumaima Essamadi, Rishu Garg, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EverydayBench: Evaluating Satisfactory Completion of Live User-Facing Digital Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25146)**  `arXiv:2611.25146`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fangrui Huang, Bingyang Wang, Chuhan Li, Yijiang Li, Arpandeep Khatua, Austin Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Everyday digital tasks, from selecting products and planning trips to conducting research and creating artifacts, require AI agents to deliver useful outcomes rather than merely retrieve web information. Yet evaluating these outcomes on the live web is challenging, as a single request may admit multiple valid results, and changing web information can alter which results remain valid over time. Although existing benchmarks address aspects of this setting, they do not fully integrate predefined success criteria, current evidence verification, and deliverable-quality assessment for agent-selected outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce EverydayBench, a benchmark of 77 human-authored tasks spanning eight domains of everyday life and work. Each task specifies success criteria before execution without prescribing an answer or workflow, allowing agents to select their own outcomes. To evaluate submissions under fixed success criteria, EverydayBench combines (1) deterministic checks of explicit constraints, (2) verification of claims against agent-cited webpages retrieved at evaluation time, and (3) rubric-based assessment of final deliverable quality. We further validate the protocol through human judgments, repeated scoring under fixed evidence, and controlled grounding failures. Across 23 reported agent-model configurations, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1 tie for the highest strict pass rate at 54.55%; a controlled GPT-6 Sol effort sweep ranges from 29.87% at low effort to 45.45% at max effort. Reliability varies substantially by task type: GPT-5.6 Sol leads lookup, Fable 5.1 leads planning, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 tie on comparison, and GPT-6 Astra and Sonnet 5.5 tie on customized deliverables. Together, these findings highlight the difficulty of delivering complete, well-supported, and usable outcomes for everyday user requests.
  </details>

- **[CompoundingKernel: Self-Improving from Zero to First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19230)**  `arXiv:2611.19230`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qian Qu, Hongyang Wang, Wenbo Chen, Yichuan Li, Jingguo Liu, Xian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present CompoundingKernel, a self-improving agent system that writes GPU kernels by search. As of 2026-11-11, its generated kernels rank #1 on the public L1, L2 and Quantization boards of SOL-ExecBench (NVIDIA B200). They have held #1 on L1 since 2026-10-27, albeit a short loss on 2026-11-07. All 235 problems have a site-verified correct kernel; on the 209 public-board problems they are 4.40x faster than the benchmark's reference baseline. The search starts from zero: agents get each problem's PyTorch definition but no reference kernels or official scores. A per-problem archive outside the model holds every correct kernel and its lineage. An LLM controller reads it, picks a parent or a new design, and gives each parallel child agent one direction. Checks outside the model decide correctness. At equal wall time, it beat a coded UCB style tree on 35 of 38 problems by making 2.4x as many correct attempts. Over a thousand agents ran at once, and one warm restart fix raised their evaluation rate 3.6x. We find that the rate of reliable measurements, not the number of agents, limits how far such a search scales. Our code and kernels are available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reglos: A Self-Evolving Personal Memory Agent with Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06049)**  `arXiv:2611.06049`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sen Yang, Boqin Yuan, Jiaduo Lin, Zehong Huang, Wen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personal agents draw on past conversations to tailor their responses to each user. Their memory systems store and update what each user says, and coding agents now also rewrite the harness that turns this memory into the model's context. Such patches risk overfitting, because one tested only on the failures it targets can fix those cases and help nowhere else. We present Reglos, a personal memory agent that tests whether harness changes help beyond the failures they were written to fix. Its self-evolving memory adds to each user's append-only record with every conversation, as new preferences supersede old ones and facts can be forgotten. Its harness improves through recursive self-improvement, in which a coding agent proposes patches from failure traces and a critic from a second model family screens each one for benchmark gaming. After the harness is frozen, each further patch is tested on users the loop has not seen. With the same backbone behind every system, Reglos outperforms state-of-the-art memory systems such as Mem0, MemOS and EverOS, reaching 67.7% and 84.0% on PersonaMem-v2 and LongMemEval-S with Qwen3.8-27B, and 62.1% and 87.2% with GPT-5.6 Luna.
  </details>

- **[Towards Robust Long-Lived Agents: Benchmarking Experience Lifecycles in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05450)**  `arXiv:2611.05450`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shijue Huang, Hangyu Guo, Hengheng Zhang, Guo An, Zhitao He, Qi Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agents become more capable, they are moving beyond one-off tasks toward sustained assistance across successive user goals. Such assistance requires agents to discover how unfamiliar environments work, reuse what they learn across tasks, and keep that experience current as environments evolve. Existing benchmarks increasingly study experience reuse and adaptation, but provide limited insight into which parts of accumulated experience agents revise, retain, or lose. To address this gap, we introduce ReScopeBench, a benchmark for the experience lifecycle across 18 interactive environments. It distinguishes structural experience about how an environment is organized and accessed from rule experience about reusable principles and constraints that guide decisions across tasks. To study how experience is acquired, reused, and revised, we propose an experience-first construction approach. It builds task streams around shared experience and introduces silent environmental changes. We evaluate task performance and track changes in saved experience throughout each stream. Across 20 models, we find that retained experience improves task performance, but agents struggle to keep it current as environments change. Even when the required experience is correctly recorded, agents do not always complete tasks successfully. These findings identify selective maintenance and effective use as complementary challenges for long-lived agents.
  </details>

- **[AI Data Analysis: A New Approach - Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00807)**  `arXiv:2611.00807`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jianfeng Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI data analysis today is three fragmented worlds: LLMs understand semantics but cannot compute; BI aggregates precisely but is stateless; ML processes large-scale data on a stateful kernel but lacks semantics. This paper proposes a combination mechanism, named Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis, that fills a still-open gap - the multi-engine joint context: the data context is embedded into LLM conversational analysis, where the LLM serves only as the semantic, reasoning, and orchestration layer (not computation) and connects four engines through the 1M context window - Python and R ML kernels, SQL over relational databases, Cypher over graph databases - yielding two multiplications, "semantics and precise computation" and "relational and graph", under enterprise-grade governance. The novelty is a four-dimensional coordinate system: horizontal (engine kinds), vertical (state flow - the watershed), spatial (business generalization), temporal (iterative convergence); a deterministic grounding method (one-shot injection ofthis http URL+this http URL, M+S) carries the spatial and temporal dimensions. Empirically, all four dimensions are substantiated: horizontally, four engines are quantified - SQL, Cypher (96.5% on CypherBench movie), ML in Python and R (TabReD weather); vertically, state flows across engines and grounding is reused across kernels; spatially, SQL generalizes across three business databases - AdventureWorks 91.3%, financial 95.3%, formula_1 94.6% (average), about 20-23 points above single-shot SOTA 74.45%; temporally, accuracy converges monotonically as notes accumulate. The core invariant - the separation of semantics from computation plus a cross-data-domain persistent context - does not expire as LLMs and agents evolve or engine forms change.
  </details>

- **[FrontierMat: Benchmarking Language Models at the Frontier of Materials R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41162)**  `arXiv:2610.41162`  `cs.AI`  
  _Feng Chen, Jianguo Huang, Yushan Xiao, Yue Li, Bijun Tang, Xuyu Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To accelerate materials discovery, language models must be able to address problems at the research frontier. Yet textbook- and examination-derived benchmarks primarily assess established knowledge, leaving performance on frontier research problems largely untested. We introduce FrontierMat, a benchmark of 330 problems drawn from frontier academic and industrial research in fields including aerospace, semiconductors, embodied intelligence and nuclear fusion. Every problem is validated by leading experimentalists and industry experts in its field. Each problem presents a real study and asks the model to infer a withheld outcome or draw a conclusion from selected experimental evidence. Problems are annotated by functional domain, material system and the scale of the governing mechanism; 130 require cross-scale reasoning. The strongest general-purpose model scores only 53.7%, and on matched problems, cross-scale problems score 8.7 points below single-scale ones. Half of the problems are solved by at most one model, and models fail chiefly by endorsing what is plausible rather than what was observed. A domain-adaptation study with meta-weighted continued pretraining and scale-modular self-distillation raises an open-weight model from 36.7% to 55.4%, matching the strongest proprietary model, with improvements on public materials benchmarks and no decline in aggregate performance on general benchmarks. FrontierMat provides a testbed for measuring progress toward language models that support frontier materials R&D.
  </details>

- **[The View from Nowhere: Population-Centered Agent Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36660)**  `arXiv:2610.36660`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuo Yan, Ningning Hou, Haoyu He, Haochen Pei, Shang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-improving AI systems can revise the designs that govern their own behavior. Yet existing approaches center improvement on selected parent agents and their evolutionary experience, leaving the broader population largely underused. We identify a substantial gap between population coverage (i.e., the tasks solved by at least one evolved variant) and the performance of the best individual agent. This gap shows that useful capabilities and diagnostic evidence emerge across the population without being consolidated into its leading agents. These observations motivate the view from nowhere, a principle for agent evolution: an agent should be improved by interpreting its behavior through population-wide patterns of success, failure, and prior self-modification. We instantiate this principle as Population-Centered Evolution (PCE). PCE distils task records and modification histories from the population into a population block, which captures recurring failures, complementary successes, and the effects of previously attempted modifications to guide improvements to a selected parent's harness. Under matched models, initial harnesses, and evolution schedules, PCE achieves 72.1% success on SWE-bench Verified and 96.7% on Polyglot, improving over a strong group-evolution baseline by 3.2 and 5.9 percentage points. Across independent runs, PCE reduces the gap between population coverage and best-agent performance by 30% on SWE-bench and all but closes it on Polyglot. These results suggest that treating the population as evidence, not merely as candidates for selection, provides a complementary principle for agent evolution.
  </details>

- **[PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29467)**  `arXiv:2610.29467`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jian Zhong, Zeyu Liu, Pingchuan Cao, Rongduo Han, Shunye Tang, Guohuan Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon dialogue agents need to keep user memories stable enough for personalization while adapting when the user genuinely changes. Existing memory systems often append every observation or overwrite old entries by heuristic rules, which makes transient noise hard to distinguish from real belief drift. We introduce PreCoMem, a memory consolidation framework inspired by predictive coding that represents memory as confidence-weighted beliefs about the user's state. Its core signal, Effective Surprisal, combines semantic deviation and contradiction evidence, then downweights this signal when retrieval is diffuse and unreliable. According to effective surprisal, a two-threshold gate maps each turn to one of three deterministic updates: MAINTAIN reinforces supported beliefs, PROFILE stores ambiguous cues as low-confidence hypotheses, and CORRECT softly decays reliably contradicted facts. The PROFILE stage acts as an explicit waiting room, preventing premature commitment to noisy evidence. Experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and PersonaMem-v2 show consistent state-of-the-art accuracy; our new DiMoBench confirms that PreCoMem adapts to non-stationary belief drift without over-reacting to noise. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Code to Playable Systems: CEGame-Bench for Long-Horizon Game Creation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26098)**  `arXiv:2610.26098`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Dong, Yanghai Wang, Hanyan Bian, Jiaxi Tao, Junze Li, Jiadong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A game ships when a player can launch it, finish it, and return to a saved world, none of which a successful build guarantees, yet game-creation agents are mostly tested on one engine or on one-prompt browser games. We present CEGame-Bench, the first long-horizon benchmark for complete-game creation across five production engines: Defold, Godot, Roblox Studio, Unity, and Unreal Engine. Agents build each game from an empty project and a design document in runs averaging up to 295 steps and 65M tokens; the document doubles as a contract, and a hidden evaluator accepts a game only by real-input play. Under four scaffolds, agents earn most integration points but usually under half the gameplay points, and their games reach the ending in only 16% of runs, while references built by engineers with coding agents score near the maximum, which places this artifact-to-play gap in the agents. No two engines rank configurations alike (Kendall's tau 0.14-0.62), and the same game usually scores higher on Unity and Unreal Engine, which check more before play. We release Game Completion Boost, an agent skill for requirement-driven playtesting and repair that raises the execution-based score of three models by 14.1-24.3 points.
  </details>

- **[JockeyBench: The Racing Analyst's Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19589)**  `arXiv:2610.19589`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhongwei Xie, Xingsu Li, Jingqiao Yu, Zihao Wang, Hong ChiTat, Zhaowei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating LLM agents in noisy sequential forecasting is challenging: strong performance may reflect a capable initial predictor rather than successful online revision, while accurate forecasts may still fail to deliver economic value after costs and market frictions. Existing benchmarks rarely isolate revision gains, compare reusable model-written predictors with direct LLM forecasting on the same tasks, or assess forecast quality alongside research costs and realized returns. To address these gaps, we introduce JockeyBench, a leakage-controlled replay benchmark built from 12 years of Japanese horse-racing records. Agents acquire evidence at a cost and seal probabilistic forecasts before outcomes are revealed. To isolate the effect of revision, we pair each adaptive code-as-policy agent with a frozen copy of its initial predictor. We also compare code-as-policy, which executes reusable prediction programs, with LLM-as-policy, which forecasts each race directly. We evaluate 29 models on 324 races. The strongest initial predictors outperform a conditional-logit baseline trained on 20,730 races. The two top-ranked models retain their initial predictors throughout evaluation. Revision helps weaker predictors correct overconfidence, but updates driven by noisy outcomes can hurt performance. Code-as-policy generally produces more accurate forecasts at lower research cost, while models that struggle to write reliable predictors benefit from forecasting directly with LLM-as-policy. Yet all 29 models, including Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, have negative mean returns in retrospective settlement. JockeyBench thus offers a demanding testbed for current LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Cross-Modal Hallucinations by Correcting Modality Misrouting in Omnimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18141)**  `arXiv:2610.18141`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Mi, Licheng Pan, Jiayi Zhu, Qirui Liu, Yitong Qiao, Changtong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omnimodal large language models (OLLMs) are susceptible to cross-modal hallucinations under audio-visual interference. We find that, for a substantial fraction of incorrect joint predictions, the question-relevant unimodal branch can still recover the correct answer, suggesting that task-relevant evidence can remain recoverable from the relevant modality even when it is not reflected in the joint prediction. We refer to this recoverable failure pattern as \emph{modality misrouting}. We propose CaRe (Conflict-aware Residual Intervention), a training-free inference-time method for identifying and correcting modality misrouting. CaRe uses a text-only modality probe to identify a candidate modality-specific counterfactual branch, and flags potential misrouting when its prediction conflicts with the original joint prediction. For such cases, CaRe computes a residual direction from the discrepancy between the counterfactual and fused representations and uses it to steer the original multimodal state toward the relevant modality evidence, while retaining both modalities in the original audio-visual inference path. The intervention strength is adapted based on the confidence of the counterfactual branch. Experiments on CMM and AVHBench across two OLLMs show that CaRe consistently outperforms the evaluated inference-time hallucination-mitigation baselines.
  </details>

- **[AdaC-GRPO: Towards Mastering Long-Horizon Visual Sudoku Puzzles via Adaptive Curriculum Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13184)**  `arXiv:2610.13184`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sida Li, Yuanxin Liu, Linli Yao, Shicheng Li, Xu Sun, Haoli Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR) enhances reasoning in large language models, its efficacy in long-horizon multimodal tasks remains underexplored. Visual Sudoku puzzles pose a representative challenge, requiring multi-step deductive reasoning over both local constraints and global dependency chains. To this end, we introduce FG-Sudoku, a fine-grained benchmark categorized by grid size and required deductive steps. Evaluations reveal that frontier MLLMs fail on complex long-range dependencies (e.g., 9*9 puzzles). During post-training, this near-zero success rate causes severe reward sparsity, halting standard RLVR. We propose Adaptive Curriculum Group Relative Policy Optimization (AdaC-GRPO) to overcome this. Unlike rigid or prior-based schedules, AdaC-GRPO dynamically constructs training batches by tracking the model's "Zone of Proximal Development" via online rollout accuracy. Experiments demonstrate that AdaC-GRPO enables Qwen2.5-VL-7B to approach the performance of Gemini-2.5-Flash while bridging 62.2% of the overall accuracy gap to Gemini-2.5-Pro, with its generalizability further validated across InternVL-3.5 and Qwen3-VL.
  </details>

- **[PLCWorld: Benchmarking LLM-Generated PLC Programs in Closed-Loop Plant Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02982)**  `arXiv:2610.02982`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunji Kim, Yunseok Lee, Hyunwoo Seo, Jaerim Choi, Woojin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Programmable logic controllers (PLCs) coordinate industrial equipment through sensor inputs and control commands. Evaluating LLM-generated PLC programs requires observing device and workpiece states to assess task completion and safety. We introduce PLCWorld, a closed-loop execution environment and benchmark coupling Structured Text (ST) execution with simulated plant responses and sensor feedback. Grounded in control relations from industrial PLC programs and engineering documentation, PLCWorld contains 100 synthetic tasks and 473 registered task-condition pairs across Motion Control and Material Handling, with difficulty defined by control-dependency scope. It reports Task Success and Safety Violation separately. Execution Gap measures the fraction of cases with task failure or observed safety violation among those assigned to programs passing a common submission-profile and I/O check. Validation combines practitioner review, reference and alternative programs, 542 targeted counterexamples, alignment checks, and independent ST runtime comparisons. Across six LLMs and four adapted generation-and-verification workflows, direct GPT-5.5 achieves 83.96% Task Success on Easy cases but 24.51% on Hard cases. The workflows show no consistent plant-level gains, and failures persist after upstream acceptance. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SkillCome: Group Contrast Skill Optimization with Dual Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37128)**  `arXiv:2609.37128`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haolin Li, Feng Hong, Ang Li, Chilin Fu, Weichang Wu, Ya Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill evolution improves the capabilities of large language models by analyzing trajectories generated under a given skill and modifying the skill accordingly. Existing approaches typically generate a single trajectory per question. However, this provides insufficient optimization signals since it requires inferring effective skill edits from a solitary path. It is difficult to pinpoint which actions caused the failure in a failed trajectory, or to determine which actions in a successful one should be incorporated into the skill. Furthermore, they rely on a local batch of trajectories for analysis, making the optimization direction susceptible to noisy evidence. To address these, we propose SkillCome, a Skill-evolution method based on group Contrast optimization with dual memory. For each question, SkillCome generates trajectories and performs group contrast analysis to precisely identify key behavioral divergences between successful and failed trajectories, offering reliable optimization signals. The dual memory system further accumulates evidence from historical steps to track patterns shared across different groups, leading to more generalized optimization directions. Together, SkillCome builds a systematic optimization process that transforms experience from observed successful trajectories into reusable skills. Extensive experiments on six benchmarks spanning question answering, reasoning, and agentic tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method. SkillCome consistently outperforms baselines across five models of varying families and scales, with gains up to +5.69 points. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stale Does Not Mean Unsafe: Measuring Commit-Guard Precision for Tool-Using LLM Agents in Simulated and Transactional Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29522)**  `arXiv:2609.29522`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zihao Zheng, Jiayu Long, Baichuan Li, Junyi Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language models act on state that can change while they plan. A version check detects that change, but an audit update and a revoked approval have different consequences for a pending action. We study how commit guards distinguish changes that preserve an action's safety conditions from changes that invalidate them. ShiftGuard pairs both types of race across sixteen infrastructure operations and replays identical proposals under alternative guards. The primary simulator grid contains 3,456 frozen proposals from three models, with a separate 1,152-proposal replication on a fourth. We implement the same operations on PostgreSQL and score persisted effects with a requirements-based checker. Among benign races in the scripted PostgreSQL benchmark, the runtime-held version and global-epoch guards block 80% and 100%, respectively, while the row-local predicate guard blocks none. The predicate guard records zero unsafe writes on the declared benchmark conditions, and all five additional hazards outside its declared contract escape it. A version guard using the model's expected version permits unsafe writes on 4.9% of primary replay decisions. The runtime-held version guard records zero in the same replay. These results identify two design choices for agent tools: bind freshness tokens to runtime observations, and represent safety conditions explicitly at the point where the effect is applied.
  </details>

- **[When to Use Subagents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759)**  `arXiv:2609.19759`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yishuo Yuan, Yibo Wu, Yihan Zhang, Minyuan Sun, Shenliang Li, Xinkai Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When should a task be delegated to subagents? We answer this at the granularity of individual trajectories. Modeling a trajectory as a dependency graph, we identify bridge edges, whose removal separates the trajectory into independent components, and show that the agent orchestration pattern should match this bridge-edge structure. Our experiments on long-horizon benchmarks indicate that subagents tend to benefit sparse bridge-edge structures, whereas tightly coupled trajectories are often better handled by a single agent. We instantiate this principle as SAIGE, a lightweight mechanism that spawns subagents only at predicted bridge edges and resolves their connections lazily, achieving a favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance where the structure admits decomposition. We further observe that scaling the agent pool or recursion depth does not reliably help, in line with the view that orchestration should follow the bridge-edge pattern.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Evaluation Framework for Trustworthy Large Language Models, Agentic AI, and Multimodal Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19524)**  `arXiv:2609.19524`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shaina Raza, Ahmed Y. Radwan, Imran Liaquat, Kathryn Hume_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark scores alone provide an incomplete basis for assessing the trustworthiness of modern artificial intelligence systems. Large language models (LLMs), agentic systems, and multimodal models (MLLMs) require different forms of assessment, yet their evaluation evidence must remain interpretable for development and oversight. We propose a unified framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment through eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, accompanied by uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. A meta-evaluation layer examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself. Multidimensional profiles expose strengths and weaknesses, while safety-critical overrides prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. Mappings to governance frameworks, international standards, and European Union regulatory requirements connect technical assessment with oversight needs. The framework provides a structured basis for assessing both system performance and the credibility of the evidence supporting it, with empirical validation across deployment contexts remaining an essential next step.
  </details>

- **[ASCII Attack: Recontextualising Harmful Requests as Artistic Critique in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02215)**  `arXiv:2609.02215`  `cs.AI`  
  _Da Cheng Gu, Yifei Dong, Xinghao Yang, Yongshun Gong, Wei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment trains large language models (LLMs) to refuse harmful requests. However, a request can keep its operational content and still change how the model interprets it, and refusal training covers such "recontextualised" requests only weakly. To study this gap, we propose the ASCII Attack, a single-turn black-box jailbreak. It embeds a fully legible harmful request in an assembly of ASCII-art characters, presents it as artwork, and asks the model for "artistic feedback". ArtPrompt masks a sensitive word that the model must decode. In contrast, the ASCII Attack hides nothing and changes only the social frame of the request. In order to separate the effect of this frame from topic, model and decoding effects, we pair every framed prompt with a matched direct-question control on the same request. The control also omits the scene description and the missing-information list, so the contrast measures the framing bundle rather than the frame alone. Across eleven models and eight harm topics, the JailbreakBench classifier judges 62% of framed prompts harmful against 42% of their controls. With a single query, the ASCII Attack matches or exceeds ArtPrompt and DeepInception under four of five harm judges. We further show that the effect tracks the model more than the topic and does not fall with scale, which is consistent with mismatched generalisation.
  </details>

- **[The Illusion of What If: Evaluating the Breakdown of Counterfactual Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27953)**  `arXiv:2608.27953`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yucheng Wang, Yuetian Du, Zhengyi Liu, Rongyu Zhang, Bing Zhao, Boyu Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual reasoning requires models to reason beyond the observed world and explain how altered conditions propagate through downstream consequences. Existing benchmarks largely target bounded settings with fixed variables or single gold outcomes, overlooking open-domain scenarios requiring causal-process evaluation. To this end, we present $\textbf{WhatIfBench}$, a diagnostic benchmark for open-domain, open-form, long-horizon counterfactual causal reasoning, containing 220 what-if questions across STEM, HSS, and Hybrid scenarios. To evaluate free-form responses, we further propose $\textbf{PRISM}$, which first converts each natural-language explanation into a Response-Derived Semantic Causal Graph of events, states, and mechanisms. On top of this graph, PRISM then jointly applies a Process Metric assessing graph-level causal validity and a Rubric Metric assessing answer-level explanatory adequacy. Evaluating six frontier LLMs with this framework, we find that WhatIfBench remains far from saturated: even the strongest model reaches only a 64.62% final score. Further analysis reveals persistent causal gaps, premise drift, and topology fragmentation, suggesting that fluent counterfactual narratives often mask fragile causal processes. The benchmark, code, and evaluation scripts are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Competing at Every Price Point with Agentic Evolution over a Menu of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16207)**  `arXiv:2608.16207`  `cs.AI`  
  _Andrew Borthwick_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Consider a firm that surveys its competition for a particular agentic task and seeks to offer superior accuracy at every price point. A firm that Pareto-dominated its competitors would leave no rational customer a reason to buy elsewhere. This paper shows a path to this kind of capability by evolving multi-LLM Python agents from training pools of at most 100 examples. Given a priced menu of nine LLM endpoints; brief documentation of the task, objective, and API; a simple seed agent; and an operator-chosen per-problem cost target--usually set at an incumbent's own price--RoboPhD, an evolutionary meta-agent, evolves complete agent programs that attack the public frontiers of two semantically dissimilar tasks point by point: DS-1000 (execution-checked code generation) and PaperFindingBench (LLM-judged scientific document retrieval). On public leaderboards for each task, the evolved agents hold every Pareto-frontier slot on PaperFindingBench and all but one on DS-1000, including Pareto domination of both the top-scoring and the lowest-cost competing points.
  </details>

- **[Do LLM Agents Negotiate Rationally? A Mechanism-Design Framework for Verifiable Multi-Agent Interaction over A2A/MCP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14613)**  `arXiv:2608.14613`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wael Albayaydh, Rui Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern LLM-agent frameworks increasingly interoperate through standards such as Anthropic's Model Context Protocol (MCP) for agent-to-tool access and Google's Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol for agent delegation and negotiation. However, these protocols specify transport and discovery rather than strategic correctness and do not guarantee efficient, individually rational, or strategy-proof outcomes.We introduce a framework that (i) encodes classical negotiation mechanisms, including alternating-offers bargaining and Vickrey-Clarke-Groves-style auctions, as constraints over A2A message schemas; (ii) provides a lightweight runtime verification and repair layer that checks messages against protocol invariants; and (iii) offers a benchmark of negotiation and allocation tasks with known optimal solutions for measuring deviations from game-theoretic predictions.We evaluate multiple LLM backbones using unstructured dialogue, structured protocols, and structured protocols with verification. Across negotiation trials (N=30 per condition), verification reduces outcome variance, while structured protocols achieve 100 percent success for both models. After correcting parser artifacts, audited unstructured baselines achieve approximately 97 percent and 93.3 percent success.In auction experiments (N=30 per model), both models achieve 100 percent efficient allocation but differ sharply in truthful bidding: one bids its exact valuation in every trial, whereas the other does so in only 3.3 percent of trials. Thus, mechanism-level incentive compatibility does not automatically transfer to LLM-agent behavior. A three-party fair-allocation task produced only 4.2 percent usable outcomes; we report this negative result with a diagnosis. This work bridges classical multi-agent systems theory and modern LLM-agent infrastructure and defines verifiable interaction at the A2A protocol layer.
  </details>

- **[PATH-Bench: Path-Dependent Evaluation of Lifelong Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01149)**  `arXiv:2608.01149`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xidong Yang, Xingyi Zhang, Wenhao Li, Wenyan Liu, Junjie Sheng, Yun Hua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lifelong LLM agents increasingly adapt through external learning states that store past interactions as retrievable memories or reusable skills, yet existing benchmarks rarely account for how the path of accumulated experience shapes what agents transfer and retain. In this work, we establish PATH-Bench, a benchmark for path-dependent evaluation of lifelong agents. PATH-Bench estimates directed task relationships via multi-model in-context learning, constructs probe-centered sequences with controlled helpful and interfering histories, and repeatedly evaluates probe tasks to measure average performance, forward transfer, backward transfer, and forgetting. We evaluate eight representative agents on single-turn code generation and multi-turn tool-use tasks under positive- and negative-dominant histories. Benchmark results show that experience utility depends jointly on how experience is represented and on the task's interaction structure, that strong transfer does not ensure retention, and that later experience can reshape gains acquired earlier in the learning path. Based on these findings, we propose Selective Experience Use (SEU), an agent harness that regulates how path-accumulated experience influences each new task, admitting helpful items while filtering out potential interference. SEU consistently reduces forgetting while improving forward transfer in the majority of settings. The PATH-Bench provides both a controlled evaluation framework and actionable guidance for designing more selective and robust lifelong agents. The benchmark data and evaluation code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Leaderboard: A Synthesis of Tool-Use, Planning, and Reasoning Failures in Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05775)**  `arXiv:2607.05775`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wael Albayaydh, Rui Zhao, Ivan Flechais_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly evaluated on their ability to use tools, plan multi-step tasks, coordinate with other agents, and operate over extended horizons. Reported benchmark gains often obscure recurring failure modes documented across otherwise unrelated evaluation efforts. This paper synthesizes 27 benchmark, taxonomy, and audit papers (2023-2026), spanning 19 distinct benchmarks, into a cross-cutting taxonomy of agent limitations. To our knowledge, this is the first synthesis that integrates evidence across tool use, planning, long-horizon reasoning, multi-agent coordination, safety, and measurement validity into a single, unified taxonomy of LLM agent limitations. We identify six failure clusters: (1) tool invocation and parameter-level errors, (2) planning and constraint-satisfaction failures, (3) long-horizon degradation from context accumulation, (4) multi-agent coordination failures, (5) safety and security failures under adversarial or underspecified conditions, and (6) measurement validity problems. The taxonomy was derived iteratively by grouping independently reported error categories into themes corresponding to distinct stages of the agent reasoning-to-action pipeline. Across the literature, we find that failures compound nonlinearly with task length, that strong performance on individual sub-tasks does not reliably translate into end-to-end success, and that additional scaffolding does not consistently improve reliability. At the same time, substantial progress has been demonstrated in single-turn tool use, short-horizon web navigation, and narrowly scoped coding tasks.
  </details>

- **[Fixed-Point Reasoners: Stable and Adaptive Deep Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18206)**  `arXiv:2606.18206`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sajad Movahedi, Vera Milovanović, Shlomo Libo Feigin, Alexander Theus, Thomas Hofmann, Valentina Boeva, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped architectures provide an inductive bias toward learning step-by-step procedures for tasks that require compositional reasoning. Increasing the effective layers of looped architectures can improve their reasoning capabilities. Similar to deep architectures, looped architectures are prone to a signal propagation problem induced by depth as the halting decision is postponed. In this paper, we address the signal propagation issue by using pre-norm layers and residual scaling. By improving signal propagation, we achieve competitive performance without hierarchical looping, resulting in a simpler architecture than previous state-of-the-art models evaluated on puzzle reasoning tasks. Building on these modifications, we propose FPRM: a Transformer-based Fixed-Point Reasoning Model that uses fixed-point convergence as an end-to-end halting mechanism in a looped architecture. We show that fixed-point halting allows FPRM to adapt its compute to the difficulty of the task. FPRM proves effective on common reasoning benchmarks, namely sudoku, maze, state-tracking and ARC-AGI.
  </details>

- **[GTBench: A Curriculum-Grounded Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs as Mathematical Research Assistants in Graph Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03144)**  `arXiv:2606.03144`  `cs.AI`  
  _Noujoud Nader, Ibrahem Aljabea, Patrick Diehl, Deepti Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as self-study assistants in technical disciplines, yet their reliability as mathematical reasoning assistants remains poorly understood. We introduce GTBench, a curriculum-grounded benchmark for evaluating LLMs as mathematical research assistants in graph theory, comprising 63 problems organized into three groups of increasing difficulty: undergraduate definitions and basic properties (Group 1), algorithm tracing and structural reasoning (Group 2), and graduate-level proof construction (Group 3). Problems are sourced from verified academic materials including Diestel's Graph Theory. We evaluate five frontier models -- GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite, Llama 3.3 70B, and Mistral Large 3 -- under zero-shot and chain-of-thought prompting, using exact-match and LLM-as-judge evaluation for Groups 1 and 2, and a hybrid human expert and LLM-as-judge protocol for Group 3. Our results reveal a pronounced performance hierarchy: GPT-5 approaches ceiling on Group 1 (95.8% zero-shot) and maintains meaningful accuracy on graduate proofs (82%), while all other models degrade substantially with difficulty, with Llama achieving 0% under human evaluation on Group 3 zero-shot. Failure mode analysis shows that correct algorithm, wrong execution errors dominate Groups 1 and 2, while Group 3 additionally surfaces incomplete reasoning failures and reveals systematic disagreement between human evaluators and the automated judge, particularly on verbose or near-complete proofs (kappa = 0.48-0.83 across human pairs). GTBench provides the first curriculum-grounded evaluation framework for graph-theoretic reasoning in LLMs, with direct implications for the governance of AI tools in mathematical education and scientific research.
  </details>

- **[SpatialEpiBench: Benchmarking Spatial Information and Epidemic Priors in Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06530)**  `arXiv:2605.06530`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruiqi Lyu, Alistair Turcan, Bryan Wilder_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate epidemic forecasting is crucial for public health response, resource allocation, and outbreak intervention, but remains difficult with sparse, noisy, and highly non-stationary data. Because epidemics unfold across interacting regions, spatiotemporal methods are natural candidates for improving forecasts. Despite growing interest in spatial information, no standardized benchmark exists, and current evaluations often use simple chronological train-test splits that do not reflect real-time forecasting practice. We address this gap with SpatialEpiBench, a challenging benchmark for spatiotemporal epidemic forecasting in realistic public-health settings. SpatialEpiBench includes 11 epidemic datasets with standardized rolling evaluations and outbreak-specific metrics. We evaluate adjacency-informed forecasting models with widely used epidemic priors that adapt general models to epidemiology, but find that most methods underperform a simple last-value baseline from 1 day to 1 month ahead, even during outbreaks and with these priors. We identify three major failure modes: (1) poor outbreak anticipation, (2) difficulty handling sparsity and noise, and (3) limited utility of common geographic adjacency for epidemiological spatial information. We release benchmark data, code, and instructions atthis https URLto support development of operationally useful epidemic forecasting models.
  </details>

- **[Claw-Eval: Towards Trustworthy Evaluation of Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06132)**  `arXiv:2604.06132`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bowen Ye, Rang Li, Qibin Yang, Yuanxin Liu, Linli Yao, Hanglong Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents for multi-step workflows in real-world software environments. However, existing agent benchmarks are limited by trajectory-opaque grading, underspecified safety and robustness evaluation, and narrow coverage of modalities and interaction paradigms. We introduce Claw-Eval, an end-to-end evaluation suite addressing these gaps with 300 human-verified tasks spanning 9 categories across three groups: general service orchestration, multimodal perception and interaction, and multi-turn professional dialogue. To enable trajectory-aware grading, each run is recorded through three independent evidence channels: execution traces, audit logs, and environment snapshots, yielding 2,159 fine-grained rubric items. The scoring protocol evaluates Completion, Safety, and Robustness, with Average Score, Pass@k, and Pass^k across three trials to distinguish genuine capability from lucky outcomes. Experiments on 14 frontier models show that: (1) Trajectory-opaque evaluation is systematically unreliable, missing 44% of safety violations and 13% of robustness failures detected by our framework. (2) Capability does not imply consistency, with Pass@3 remaining stable under error injection while Pass^3 dropping by up to 24 percentage points. (3) Agent capability is strongly multi-dimensional, with model rankings varying across task groups and metrics, indicating that our heterogeneous evaluation coverage is essential. Claw-Eval highlights directions for developing agents that are not only capable but reliably deployable.
  </details>

- **[SYNAPSE: Sharing Tool-Use Experience Across Organizations with Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00911)**  `arXiv:2602.00911`  `cs.AI`  
  _Abhijit Chakraborty, Vivek Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent with a large tool catalog first retrieves a few candidate tools for each request. Examples of how people used each tool help this step, but usage records are private and stay with the organizations that hold them. We present SYNAPSE, a way for organizations to share tool-use experience without sharing records.
