They jointly train a request generator under differential privacy (DP) and release noisy per-tool usage counts, which set how many requests the generator writes for each tool. The generated requests become each tool's usage field in a shared index, scored beside public documentation. For fixed scoring settings, each release is record-level DP if its noise stays secret, at least three of five clients are honest and secure aggregation holds; choosing the settings on a second release doubles the budget. Our benchmark simulates the mechanism with public noise and five clients drawn from one pool, so it measures utility, not privacy. On TOUCAN, a catalog of real tools with model-written requests, at $\varepsilon = 4$ with the Jina encoder, Hit@5 on test queries whose correct tools have usage records rises from 32.3% with documentation alone to 57.2%. Gains are smaller with a second encoder and on a second catalog, APIBench. On these queries the privately trained generator beats a public generator given the same request counts, and on TOUCAN it also beats public documentation expansion. In post hoc tests, adding the noisy counts to the public generator's requests beats SYNAPSE on the same queries. Tools without usage records lose about two points on TOUCAN, a loss our intervals cannot bound within two points.
  </details>

- **[PRISMA: A Schema-Coordinated Multi-Agent Framework for Open-Domain Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05465)**  `arXiv:2601.05465`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Liu, Wenxiao Zhang, Cong Cao, Fangfang Yuan, Hao Peng, Yanbing Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Constructing reliable evidence chains across massive corpora is a core challenge in open-domain multi-hop question answering. Single-pass retrieval struggles to locate the bridge passages required for chained reasoning. In multi-step pipelines, early retrieval errors can cascade across hops, and trajectory-level training makes it hard to attribute errors to specific stages. We propose PRISMA, a multi-agent framework with a Plan-Retrieve-Inspect-Solve-Memoize Architecture that reflects expert information seeking behavior. PRISMA uses structured generation as an inter-agent communication schema. Each agent's output constrains its reasoning and defines the input interface for downstream agents. An Inspector audits the evidence chain at each hop and triggers targeted recovery. We adopt a decoupled training strategy with agent-specific rewards. Stage I specializes the Planner and Solver as task experts. Stage II freezes the trained pipeline and trains the Inspector on trajectory-conditioned verification. A recovery-gain analysis quantifies the net contribution of verification. Across all ten benchmarks, PRISMA consistently outperforms all trained baselines and delivers overall performance competitive with larger closed-source API models, supporting efficient real-world deployment. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ArchPilot: A Proxy-Guided Multi-Agent Approach for Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.03985)**  `arXiv:2511.03985`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuowen Yuan, Tao Liu, Yang Yang, Yang Wang, Feng Qi, Kaushik Rangadurai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent LLM-based agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in automated ML engineering. However, they heavily rely on repeated full training runs to evaluate candidate solutions, resulting in significant computational overhead, limited scalability to large search spaces, and slow iteration cycles. To address these challenges, we introduce ArchPilot, a multi-agent system that integrates architecture generation, proxy-based evaluation, and adaptive search into a unified framework. ArchPilot consists of three specialized agents: an orchestration agent that coordinates the search process using a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS)-inspired novel algorithm with a restart mechanism and manages memory of previous candidates; a generation agent that iteratively generates, improves, and debugs candidate architectures; and an evaluation agent that executes proxy training runs, generates and optimizes proxy functions, and aggregates the proxy scores into a fidelity-aware performance metric. This multi-agent collaboration allows ArchPilot to prioritize high-potential candidates with minimal reliance on expensive full training runs, facilitating efficient ML engineering under limited budgets. Experiments on MLE-Bench demonstrate that ArchPilot outperforms SOTA baselines such as AIDE and ML-Master, validating the effectiveness of our multi-agent system.
  </details>
